Private addresses still need `gateway.allowed_private`. | Members type the host at connect time; one derived package per instance (works today, but every org maintains a fork); wildcard zones for customer domains | Registering where credentials may go is an admin trust decision, like approving a package; one signed built-in then serves the SaaS host and every self-hosted deployment, and the gateway keeps the exact-host injection rule tenant hosts already have. |
| D-79 | **P2-INST implementation choices for D-74** (no change to the owner's decision): (a) deleting an instance first disables its connections (`status_reason = instance_deleted`) and clears their `instance_id`/`oauth_client_id` in the same transaction (the restrict FKs then allow the delete); such a connection cannot be re-enabled (C-247). (b) A static claim of another app on a registered instance host **shadows** the instance in the gateway (fail closed: nothing is injected for it); the compile-time `09 §7` conflict decision for later-activated packages is not built. (c) The API honours `gateway.allowed_private` for instance probes and connect requests only. (d) `GITLAB_API_URL` is removed from the GitLab package (agent env cannot be per-instance); approval links use `{request.host}`. (e) `app_instance.create\|update\|delete` are written in the change's transaction. | Keeping `instance_id` on deleted-instance connections (needs soft delete); a compile-time claim for instance hosts | Smallest change that keeps SEC-04/SEC-59 true on every path. |
| D-75 | **P2-CAT-0 built-in catalogue framework** (owner-confirmed via the coordinator, 2026-11-08): (a) the `builtin` source is wired: the API scans `plugins.builtin_dir` (default `/usr/share/aurora/plugins`, C-210) next to `plugins.dir`, and that source is **fail-closed**: only archives verifying against the compiled-in project key (publisher `aurora`) load; unsigned (even with `plugins.allow_unsigned`), other-key, trusted-key and invalid archives are refused and logged. Dev/e2e load the repository packages unsigned through `plugins.dir`, where they wait as `pending_approval`. (b) The image ships archives (`<id>-<version>.aurora-plugin`), built and signed in the Containerfile stage `plugin-archives` from the BuildKit secret `aurora_project_signing_key` (CI: environment `release`, 0600 temp file, deleted afterwards; skipped with a warning when absent); the signing script verifies the result against `AURORA_PROJECT_PUBLIC_KEY`. (c) Package layout follows 09 §2 (`docs/setup.md`, `assets/icon.png`, `tests/*.yaml`), not a top-level `guide.md`. (d) Checks (`scripts/plugins/check-builtin.sh`, run in CI `rust-build`): validate, test with fixture coverage derived from the runner report (every host entry, every tool positive + `negative <tool>:` case, every summary template and its fields), scopes (read-only defaults, write marked, reads are GET/git fetch/graphql query), token (reauth codes + refreshed token injected), docs (rendered twice, identical, regenerated rather than committed). The fixture runner cannot replay token-endpoint responses, so the refresh exchange itself is covered by gateway tests, not package fixtures. (e) Link checks run in the non-blocking `plugin-links` workflow (weekly/manual/PR). (f) The live-token guard `scripts/guards/no-live-tokens.sh` is part of `run-all.sh`. (g) GitLab ships for gitlab.com only; self-managed instances arrive with P2-INST. | Archives signed by a separate job and handed to the image build; unsigned built-ins accepted under `allow_unsigned`; committing rendered docs | Signing where the archives are baked leaves one artifact and no hand-off; the builtin directory is the only auto-activation path, so it must not have a dev escape hatch. |
| D-76 | **P2-006 implementation choices for D-50** (no change to the owner's decision): (a) the freeze transaction does all database effects itself — flag, abandoned approvals, rejected action approvals (`error = frozen`), turns ended `aborted` with `error_code = frozen` (follow-ups are promoted and ended in the same loop; a running turn is ended in the database at once instead of waiting for a supervisor report, because the sandbox is parked in the same poll) — and the audit row and the gateway `aurora_gateway_invalidate`; a due-work sweep (`freeze::service::sweep_frozen_turns`, ahead of arm 4) ends any turn created in a race with the freeze; (b) every door that creates a turn is refused in `create_turn_capped` (423 `frozen`), with explicit earlier checks where the door must answer differently (webhooks 410, schedules `skipped_frozen`, channel presences a rate-limited "paused" reply, peer messaging a tool error); (c) the gateway reuses P2-025's `TunnelRegistry`: every live tunnel is now registered (not only BYO ones) and re-resolved on `agent`/`workspace`/`org`/`credential` invalidations, and a frozen credential resolves to `ResolveError::Frozen` (403 `frozen`, never cached past an invalidation); (d) the `level` reported is the broadest set flag (org, workspace, agent); `Agent.frozen` and `Workspace.frozen` carry the effective record, the freeze/unfreeze response the flag of the scope acted on; (e) the `FREEZE_REPLY_INTERVAL` limiter is in-process (per replica); (f) the operator list `GET /v1/admin/orgs` was added so the *Organizations* admin page can reach the kill-switch (names and slugs only); (g) the freeze/unfreeze authority of a caller is the highest applicable on the route: org owner/admin → `org_admin`, workspace owner → `workspace_owner`, instance admin on `/admin/orgs/…` → `instance_admin`; (h) FU-P2-021a: the frozen check precedes `automation_authorized` in `schedules::fire` and `webhooks::receive`, so a freeze never disables an automation and holds for both anchors. `API_CONTRACTS C-200..C-209`. | Ending running turns only through the supervisor; a separate gateway tunnel mechanism; storing the freeze on the credentials | One transaction keeps the state, the effects and the audit consistent, and one tunnel registry means one place that closes live connections. |
| D-77 | **P2-CAT-H hook-capable built-ins (refines D-75, I-02)**: (a) a built-in with hooks keeps its guest as a standalone cargo project in `plugins/builtin/<id>/hooks/` (`crate-type = ["cdylib"]`, `aurora-plugin-sdk` by path, committed `Cargo.lock`, its own `[workspace]`); a prebuilt `hooks.wasm` next to `plugin.yaml` still works for fixtures, but not together with `hooks/`. (b) `aurora plugin build <dir>` now compiles it (`cargo build --release --locked --target wasm32-wasip2`, `RUSTFLAGS` cleared, the single cdylib `.wasm` taken from cargo's JSON artifact list) and packs it as `hooks.wasm`; there is **no `cargo component`**: a cdylib on `wasm32-wasip2` already is a component (the Phase 0 fixture builds the same way). The only extra toolchain is the `wasm32-wasip2` rust-std of the builder's own rustc, installed with `rustup target add` in the `chef` stage (cached across source changes; rustup verifies the SHA-256 of the download against the toolchain's channel manifest; the toolchain version is the builder image's, i.e. the `RUST_IMAGE` pin); it exists in build stages only, the `api` runtime image is unchanged. (c) `aurora plugin test` runs `hook:` cases (until now reported as skipped) through the embedded hook-host runtime: the real `aurora-hookhost` runtime (wasmtime, memory/time limits, the egress rules, output validation against the manifest) with `fetch` answered from a recorded `http_cassette` after the same https/egress checks (`FetchPolicy.cassette`, never set in production); recorded responses that are never requested fail the case. (d) `check-builtin.sh` builds each package once and checks that archive; hook coverage rule: every declared point has a passing success case and, except `approval.summarize`, a passing failure case, every egress host is reached by a recorded request, and one case shows egress outside the list being denied (`egress_override` + `egress-denied`); hook cases for undeclared points fail. It also checks the test-only `plugins/fixtures/hook-example` (a `token.mint` + `approval.summarize` package in built-in shape, never shipped). (e) In the image build, compiling runs **before and apart from** signing: `build-builtin.sh --build-only` (hook compile, third-party build scripts, no secret) then `--sign-only` (no cargo), so the signing key never meets build code; the signed result and the verification against the compiled-in key are unchanged (D-75). The build step also compiles the hook example, so a broken toolchain fails the image build before the first hook lane. (f) Fixing `fixture-hooks` with the now-running cases: the continue-url host must be in `connect.exchange` egress (the host rejects it otherwise), and a denied fetch surfaces as `egress-denied`. | Shipping `cargo component`/`wasm-tools` (not needed); a separate hook-build stage; running hooks against a live mock server in `aurora plugin test`; signing in the same step as compiling | Reuses the production hook host so fixtures cannot drift from it; the build-then-sign split removes the only way a hook dependency could read the project key. |
| D-78 | **Owner answers to the planning review (2026-11-08)**: the recommendations for Q1–Q15 in `PROGRESS.md` §Remaining work plan are accepted, with these changes. (a) Q9: the contract migration dropping `users.password_hash` and `email_tokens` (CONTRACT-001) is the first phase-3 task and needs no further confirmation, because there is no user data yet. (b) OPS-021 (Dependabot) and the Slack connector (Q14) move to **phase 5**. (c) Q15 (single-tenant Entra registrations) stays deferred until the owner reports the need. (d) New phase-2 exit bar: the system is deployable and usable, proven by the P2-E2E-1 first-run journey on the Helm-deployed stack, dashboard only (new user → LiteLLM key → hello → reply from the mock LLM through the gateway with the real harness); `19 §Phase 2`, `PROGRESS` exit criterion 12. | Keep the columns until a later release; Dependabot in phase 4; exit phase 2 on component tests alone | The owner wants to deploy and use the system at the end of phase 2, so the exit gate is a real user journey rather than a sum of component suites. |
| D-80 | **P2-DBL-1 gateway database lane, choices within `21 §Phase 2 — Database lane`** (no change to the spec's contract): (a) classification is a separate crate `aurora-sqlclass` (libpg_query via `pg_query` 18, MIT; `cargo deny` accepts it), a dependency of the gateway only; it walks the whole parse tree (serialised from the protobuf) rather than relying on the crate's table-oriented walker; (b) **policy is applied at `Execute`** (and at `Query`), classification at `Parse`: parsing has no effect, and each execution then is one decided request with its own rate-limit hit, approval and log row; (c) beyond the spec's lists the always-refused set also covers `COPY` to/from server files, `set_config`, `LOAD`, parser-affecting `SET`s (`standard_conforming_strings`, `backslash_quote`, `escape_string_warning`, `client_encoding`), FDW/server/user-mapping DDL, `CREATE EXTENSION dblink|postgres_fdw|file_fdw|adminpack` and fast-path function calls — each lets the server read or run something other than what was classified, or reaches a file or another host; (d) "SET of non-session settings → admin" is read as: a short list of client preferences (`extra_float_digits`, `application_name`, `timezone`, `datestyle`, `intervalstyle`, `bytea_output`, `client_min_messages`) and `SET TRANSACTION` are `transaction`, every other `SET`/`RESET` is `admin`; `EXPLAIN ANALYZE` is `admin` (it executes), `EXECUTE` is `admin`, `PREPARE` takes its body's class; (e) a gateway-side refusal puts a server transaction into the aborted state with a swallowed failing statement, so the client's and the server's transaction state agree; (f) a missing `sslmode` is `require`, `allow` behaves as `prefer`, and `require` with a stored `ca_pem` verifies the chain (as libpq); (g) the platform database cannot be a target (SEC-06), so the e2e scenario starts its own PostgreSQL; (h) direct-TLS (`sslnegotiation=direct`) is not supported yet; (i) the approval card's statement is redacted lexically (also covering `CREATE ROLE … PASSWORD '…'`, which a parser-based normaliser keeps). `API_CONTRACTS C-220..C-225`. | Policy at `Parse`; the crate's own AST walker; relaying the server's authentication to the agent (would expose the database password exchange) | The classifier must see everything the server will run and may only fail toward refusal; the server's own error protocol is the least surprising behaviour for drivers. |
| D-81 | **P2-011A implementation choices for D-56** (no change to the owner's decision): (a) the effective timeout comes from one function (`limits::effective_approval_timeout_secs`): the rule-level minimum clamped to `[60 s, approvals.max_timeout_secs]`, else `approvals.timeout_secs` unclamped (so a test/e2e default below 60 s still works); `approvals.max_timeout_secs` is validated 300–14400 and `< hosted.turn_ceiling_secs − 900` at startup of API and gateway, and `approvals.timeout_secs` must not exceed it; (b) an out-of-range timeout answers `approval_timeout_out_of_range` whenever any field of the draft is a range violation (all violations in `details.fields`), a timeout on a rule that does not require approval answers `policy_invalid`; (c) the stall exemption holds only while the hold is `pending` **and** inside its `deadline` (a hold past its deadline is expiring and no longer protects the turn); `resolved_at` is also set by freeze abandonment and the API sweepers, and migration back-fills terminal rows from `decided_at`/`deadline`; (d) a watcher is a user's open `approvals/stream` or held-open `pending?wait=` call, upserted per (replica, user, workspace they can decide) at open and every 15 s, pruned after 90 s; (e) the e-mail recipients are the explicit workspace owners/approvers (active org members), else org owners/admins, minus disabled users, opted-out users and users who cannot see the approval's `direct` conversation; approvals of one `batch_id` produce one mail; `approval_notifications` gets a `delivery` column (`mail`, `batch`, `digest_mail`, `digest`) so the hourly cap of 20 counts real mails and at most one digest mail goes out per user per hour (two replicas racing on different approvals may rarely send two digests); claims older than 7 days are pruned with the daily pass; (f) action approvals are e-mailed (title = their one-line platform summary) but not pushed on `approvals/stream` and do not exempt turns; (g) the quota notices of RFE-015 (FU-P2-015b) also e-mail the org owners through the same mailer when `approvals.email_notifications` is on; (h) `GET /v1/instance` carries `approval_email` since the admin overview route does not exist yet; (i) the browser notification runs in the app shell on its own stream, only when the user opted in on that browser. Contracts `API_CONTRACTS C-260..C-268`. | Clamping the default timeout too; one digest per sweep; pushing action approvals on the SSE stream (needs a new bus event kind) | A hold past its deadline is about to deny, so protecting the turn longer would only delay a failure that is already decided; the claim table keeps the cap and the idempotence in the database, not in memory. |
| D-83 | **P2-008 implementation choices for D-52** (no change to the owner's decision): (a) the set is the list `SECURITY_CRITICAL_ACTIONS` in `aurora-core::audit`, using the action names the API **already emitted** (no rename, so audit consumers, saved filters and the other lanes keep working): `grant.create\|delete` for `grant.put\|delete`, `member.add\|update\|remove` for `org_member.*` (add = SSO just-in-time provisioning; role and status changes, including ownership hand-over, are `member.update`; there is no separate `org.transfer_ownership` action), `workspace_access.set\|remove`, `admin.runner_create\|rotate\|delete`, `admin.connector_create\|rotate\|approve\|delete`, `secret.value_update`, `trusted_key.create\|revoke`, `reach_grant.decide`, `agent_link.policy`, `org.app_availability.update`, `plugin.host_claim.decide`, `admin.user_update` (instance-admin grant/revoke, disable/enable) and `user.delete`; additions beyond the table: `invitation.accept` (also on first sign-in), `connection.reconnect`, `cli_login.consume` (it mints the key), `member.add`; `approval.batch_decide` is not emitted (a batch writes one `approval.decide` row per decided approval, `batch: true`, all in the batch's transaction). (b) The mechanism is one helper pair in `aurora-api::audit`: `begin` and `commit_critical` / `commit_critical_many` (insert the row(s) with the existing `insert_tx`, map a failure to 503 `audit_unavailable`, drop the transaction, then commit). `audit::record` is for the best-effort class only (debug assertion on critical successes). Instead of the spec's "repository functions take the audit entry as an argument", the repository functions take the caller's connection and the service function that owns the change commits through `commit_critical`; the coverage test scans the sources so a critical action written through `record` or outside a transactional writer fails. (c) `audit_log.critical` is derived inside `repo::audit::insert` from the action and `outcome = success`, never passed by callers; the migration backfills existing success rows. (d) Channel doors (button click, `approve <code>`, `unlink`) retry the inbound event on audit failure; the click token was consumed, so the user presses the button again on a fresh card. (e) Denied attempts and `approval.expire` / `approval.abandon` stay best-effort rows with `critical = false`. | Keep spec names and rename emitted actions; give repository functions an audit-entry parameter | Renaming churns tests, the audit UI filter and parallel lanes for no safety gain; a connection-taking repository plus one commit helper gives the same "no row, no change" guarantee with far fewer signature changes, and the source scan plus fault injection keep it from regressing. |
| D-82 | **P2-CAT-T token lifecycle in built-in package tests** (coordinator decision as spec owner; dev-tooling format, no wire change): (a) `09 §12` gains a case-level `token: { upstream, http_cassette, expect: { outcome, request_url?, credentials?, retried? } }`, `connection.oauth_client` (fake fields of the registered OAuth client) and `form` (subset match) on a cassette request; `outcome` is `refreshed`, `reauth_required` or `unavailable`. (b) The pure halves of `TokenManager::exchange` are extracted in `aurora-gateway` `refresh.rs` (`build_exchange`, `interpret_exchange`) with no behaviour change; `exchange()` calls them and `check-snapshot` replays the cassette through the same functions. (c) `check_builtin.py` requires, for every `oauth2_refresh` app, a replayed `refreshed` and a `reauth_required` case (instance-bound apps: on the default host and on a registered instance host); mint-by-hook is covered by P2-CAT-H hook cases (no declarative mint exists). (d) GitLab icon replaced by a neutral generic branch glyph. (e) `aws-keys` reviewed against AWS docs: endpoints, API versions and IAM actions confirmed; setup guide gains the `CopyObject` source-permission note. | A separate `token-fixtures` file format; replaying through the real `TokenManager` (needs Postgres and the vault in a developer tool) | The replay must run offline in `aurora plugin test` yet exercise the gateway's own request-building and answer-interpretation code. |
| D-84 | **Indico package (P2-CAT-7), owner decisions 2026-11-08**: an `indico` built-in in phase 2, instance-bound with **no default host** (D-63, D-74). Two tiers: the **official** tier uses Indico's documented HTTP API; the opt-in **extended** tier uses the endpoints Indico's own web UI calls (favorites, cancel booking, real availability search, recurring bookings, full search), which Indico does not promise to keep compatible (verified against 3.3.14) and which need `read:everything` / `full:everything` tokens. Event creation is **prepare and hand off**: the agent prepares the event and the person finishes it in Indico through a pre-filled link (no automation of the creation form). The broad token is accepted with mitigations (the token is injected only on eight Indico path prefixes, writes are held for approval, tested), and a phase-4 design item **P4-GRANTS** adds an app-level "custom grants only" option. User search (names, e-mails, affiliations) is included and named in the setup guide. Legacy signed API keys are unsupported; contribution favorites and category info are deferred. `docs/tasks/P2-CAT-7.md`. | Official API only; automate the creation form; block `full` grants before shipping | The owner asked for favorites, cancellation and availability search, which only the extended tier can do; keeping event creation as a hand-off keeps a person in the loop where Indico has no API. |
| D-90 | **FIX-011 runner sandbox defaults reach the resource profile** (phase-2 exit, found by the nightly `first-run`): `RunnerCapabilities` gains optional `defaults { cpu_millis, memory_mib, pids, home_gib }`, filled by the runner at registration from `runner.sandbox_cpus × 1000`, `sandbox_memory_mib`, `sandbox_pids`, `home_gib`; `boot::profile` resolves agent override > runner `defaults` > constants equal to the docs/15 defaults (1000 / 2048 / 512 / 10, were 2000 / 4096 / 1024 / 10), then clamps to the runner bounds as before. The org override named in docs/11 is not in the code and is left out. The Kubernetes backend logs the last blocking reason when the capacity wait expires. Requests = limits, the namespace quota and LimitRange are unchanged. | A fixed API-side constant change only; a new config key on the API | The spec already says "runner default" and the four keys already exist and validate; the API cannot know the runner's setting unless the runner says so. Operators size sandboxes on the runner; a 2 CPU / 4 GiB default made the Pod unschedulable on a 4-vCPU CI node. |
| D-88 | **P2-E2E-1 first-run journey on the kind dev stack** (coordinator renumbers on conflict): (a) the `--llm-mock` profile runs hosted agents on the **real claude-code harness**: `kind-dev.sh` builds `scripts/e2e/llm.Containerfile` on the dev sandbox image as `aurora-sandbox-llm`, loads it and `values-llm-mock.yaml` sets `runner.image` to it (closes FU-OPS-017a); the profile without the mock keeps the fake-harness `aurora-sandbox`. (b) The honest first-run experience is the **default** one: the default agent's harness is `hosted.default_harness` (`claude-code`) and its model is the endpoint's `default_model` (`scn-pong` on `litellm-mock`), so the user adds a key, grants it and chats; no Instructions step. (c) `values-dev.yaml` sets `gateway.advertised_proxy_url: https://aurora-gateway:4102`: sandboxes have no DNS and the Kubernetes runner aliases exactly that name to the proxy Service (it is on the proxy certificate); the derived default (`localhost:4102`) made every real-harness model call fail with `API Error: 502` while the fake harness never noticed; guard `scripts/guards/dev-gateway-address.sh`. (d) `AURORA_DEV_PORT` moves the published host port and `externalUrl` of a kind dev cluster, so a second cluster (`AURORA_DEV_CLUSTER`) can run beside the owner's. (e) The journey is `web/e2e/first-run.spec.ts` via `scripts/e2e/first-run.sh`; CI is the new `nightly.yml` (never per PR, owner Q3). | Fake harness for the journey; a Playwright step that picks the model; patching the derived default in the API | The bar is what the owner will actually do after `kind-up`; the real harness is the part nothing else proved inside Kubernetes; the sandbox address is a deployment setting, not code. (Superseded in part by FIX-010 / D-87: the sandbox address becomes its own config key.) |
| D-86 | **FIX-009: default-host connections of instance-bound apps, and header-absence fixtures** (no change to the owner's D-74; no wire shape changes, no contract numbers). (a) A connection of an instance-bound app with `instance_id = NULL` is the **default-host connection**: the gateway resolver sets its `{instance}` host to the app's `instances.default_host`, so token refresh (`https://{instance}/oauth/token`), the `token.mint` hook input and the instance guard all use it (the API connect, probe and OAuth-URL paths already did). Before, the host was missing, so a gitlab.com connection could never refresh (`unavailable`). (b) SEC-04/SEC-59 stay exact: the instance guard now also requires a default-host connection's request host to equal `default_host` (421 `host_mismatch` naming it), a default-host connection is still never injected on a registered instance's host, and an instance connection never on `default_host`. (c) Dev-tooling fixture format (approved by the coordinator): `expect.upstream.headers_absent: [<header names>]`, case-insensitive, fails the case if any listed header is on the upstream request (injected or client-sent), documented in `09 §12`; a fixture case without `connection.instance_host` against an instance-bound app is the default-host connection, and `scripts/plugins/check_builtin.py` counts it as the default-host token case. | Resolving a missing host to nothing (status quo, never refreshes); a separate `default_host` column on connections | One rule (no instance means `default_host`) used by every path keeps the two host kinds symmetric, and the guard change narrows where a credential is injected. |
| D-87 | **FIX-010 separate sandbox gateway address** (phase-2 exit, found by P2-E2E-1): new config key `gateway.sandbox_proxy_url` (optional, `https://host[:port]` only) is the proxy address in the boot document (`gateway.proxy` and `server_name`, `11 §The boot document`); `gateway.advertised_proxy_url` keeps its meaning (what BYO agents and `container_config` get; default external host + `:4102`). Unset default by `runner.backend`: `kubernetes` → `https://aurora-gateway:4102` (the host alias the runner writes into each sandbox Pod; sandboxes have no DNS), `podman` → the advertised proxy URL (unchanged behaviour), `fake` → unused (resolves like podman). The sandbox verifies the gateway certificate exactly as before; the host of the sandbox address is always in the proxy certificate SAN set (`aurora-gateway` already was, the chart lists it in `gateway.proxy_tls.names`). The dev-profile workaround of D-88(c) is removed (`values-dev.yaml` no longer sets `advertised_proxy_url`); the guard `scripts/guards/dev-gateway-address.sh` now checks the dev profile does not override either address. | A runner-side rewrite of the boot document; a sandbox-only TLS name list; keeping the dev override | The same URL served two audiences with different reachability; in a real Helm install port 4102 of the external host is usually not reachable from inside the cluster, so hosted agents would fail while the fake harness hid it. The default needs no operator input, the override exists for clusters with a different Service DNS name or a mesh. |
| D-91 | **P2-CAT-1: `github` and `github-app` built-in packages** (coordinator correction replacing the webhook item of the brief): (a) **Webhooks are out of scope**. `09 §8.2` has no inbound-webhook hook point and agent webhooks are core (`10 §Webhooks`, `hmac_sha256` over the raw body); `github-app` uses `token.kind: hook` with a `token.mint` hook instead. GitHub signs deliveries with `X-Hub-Signature-256` (`sha256=` + HMAC-SHA256 of the raw body); matching that against core's verification is a follow-up to confirm, not part of the package. (b) **Two apps per package** (the `github` / `github-app` split is superseded by D-195(a), D-214..D-216: the GitHub App is a connect method of `github` and `github-ghes` now; the SaaS / GHES split stays): an instance-bound app is injected on exactly one host (`instance_guard`, D-74/D-86) and github.com needs two (`api.github.com`, `github.com`) with REST paths that differ from GHES (`/api/v3`), so `github` / `github-app` (SaaS, static hosts, no `instances`) and `github-ghes` / `github-app-ghes` (GHES, `instances` without `default_host`, `{instance}` hosts under `/api/v3`, `/api/graphql` and git, per-instance OAuth client `github-ghes` for the OAuth package). (c) **`github-app` mint**: the guest signs an RS256 app JWT with the pure-Rust `rsa` 0.9 + `sha2` (no RNG at signing time; builds for `wasm32-wasip2` with only `aurora:plugin/host` imports), `iat = now − 60`, `exp = now + 540` (≤ 600), `iss` = app id or client id, `now` from `now-unix-seconds`, then `POST /app/installations/{id}/access_tokens` with `Authorization: Bearer <jwt>`; the expiry is GitHub's `expires_at` capped at one hour (and by `max_lifetime_secs`). `connect.exchange` takes the form (app id, installation id as metadata, private key `.pem` as a credential) and mints once to validate it. The key is never put in metadata, logs, summaries or errors. (d) **`ghes_host` metadata field (interim)**: hook inputs do not carry the connection's instance host, so the GHES app asks for it in the connect form; the guest validates it (bare `host[:port]`, DNS name only) and calls only `https://<ghes_host>/api/v3`; the hook host's egress pins the call to the connection's registered instance (`{instance}`), so any other value fails closed (`egress denied`, "does not match the registered instance"). The SaaS app never reads `ghes_host`; the GHES app never calls `api.github.com`. Egress is declared per package, so the list is `[api.github.com, {instance}]` and the app boundary is enforced by the guest and fixtured. Follow-up (next contract revision): pass `instance_host` in hook inputs; `ghes_host` is then derived and the field removed. (f) `scripts/plugins/check_builtin.py`: a hook egress entry `{instance}` counts as reached only by a passing case of the same point whose app declares `instances`, with a non-null `egress_override` (the resolved list) and a recorded request to a host of that list that no static egress pattern of the point covers. (g) Throw-away fixture keys: `scripts/guards/fixture-keys.allow` lists the files exempt from the PRIVATE KEY pattern of `no-live-tokens.sh` only (each must exist and carry the marker line `# aurora-fixture-key: throw-away, generated for fixtures, never valid upstream`, else the guard fails); `check-builtin.sh` runs `cargo test --locked` for every `plugins/builtin/*/hooks` crate with tests. (e) OAuth scopes: GitHub has no read-only repository scope, so `repo`, `public_repo` and `workflow` are `access: write`; defaults are `read:user` and `read:org`. | A single app with `default_host`; a GHES-only `github-app`; changing the WIT or the hook host | One injection host per instance-bound connection is a gateway guarantee (SEC-04/SEC-59) that must not be weakened; the hook ABI is frozen for contract 1 and the interim field needs no core change while keeping egress fail-closed. |
| D-92 | **P2-CAT-2: `atlassian` built-in package** (Jira and Confluence). (a) **Four apps, one OAuth client**: `jira` and `confluence` (Atlassian Cloud, one `atlassian` OAuth 2.0 3LO client: `auth.atlassian.com`, JSON token body, `audience=api.atlassian.com` and `prompt=consent` as `authorize_params`, `offline_access` for rotating refresh tokens, no PKCE because the docs do not describe it) and `jira-dc` / `confluence-dc` (self-hosted, `instances` without `default_host`, personal access token as bearer on `{instance}` under `/rest`), following D-91 because the Cloud paths (`api.atlassian.com/ex/<product>/<cloudid>/…`) and the Data Center paths differ. (b) **Cloud id**: the connect probe calls `GET https://api.atlassian.com/oauth/token/accessible-resources` and stores the **first** site (`/0/id` as `cloud_id`, `/0/url` as `site_url`, `/0/name` as the label). The connection is not pinned to that site: tools match `/ex/jira/*/…` and a token valid for several sites works on all of them; the guide says so. (c) **Shared lookup path**: the probe needs a host entry covering `/oauth/token/accessible-resources` in both apps, and the gateway resolves one (host, path) to one app (longest prefix, then the smaller app id). `jira` claims the exact path and owns the `list_accessible_sites` tool; `confluence` claims `/oauth/token`, so a Confluence-only connection cannot use the lookup through the gateway (its agent text says to ask for the cloud id). A package may overlap itself (`claims.rs`), so this is not a host conflict. Follow-up: connection-aware resolution for same-package overlapping claims. (d) **Scopes**: Jira uses the classic `read:jira-user`, `read:jira-work` (default) and `write:jira-work` (write), because Atlassian recommends them and its granular Jira scopes are beta; Confluence v2 documents only granular scopes (`read:space|page|comment:confluence` default, `write:page|comment:confluence` write) plus the classic `search:confluence` for the v1 search endpoint (v2 has none). (e) **Left out**: deletes, Jira `POST /search/jql` (a read over POST), inline-comment creation, attachments, admin, Jira Software/Service Management, Confluence DC `/rest/api/content/*/child/*` beyond comments, and DC instances behind a context path (an instance is a bare host; superseded by D-231 for registered instances: a registration may carry a base path). (f) `scripts/guards/no-live-tokens.sh` gains the Atlassian API token pattern `ATATT…`. | A single `atlassian` app with both products; Cloud-only; granular Jira scopes; claiming `/oauth/token` per app with distinct paths | The apps sharing one host need path-disjoint tool prefixes and one shared client; Data Center cannot reuse Cloud hosts or paths, and an instance-bound app is injected on one host only. |
| D-93 | **P2-CAT-6: `sentry`, `notion` and `linear` built-in packages.** (a) **Sentry, four apps** (D-91; **superseded by D-243, phase 5: one app, the regions are preset instances**): `sentry` (`sentry.io`), `sentry-us` (`us.sentry.io`), `sentry-de` (`de.sentry.io`) and `sentry-self-hosted` (`instances`, no `default_host`), because a tool names exactly one host and the docs say to use the organization's region domain. Auth token (bearer) in a connect form with `org_slug` (required) and `project_slug` (optional) as metadata; the probe is `GET /api/0/organizations/{fields.org_slug}/projects/` on the app's host (documented, needs only `project:read`). Tools (read): organizations (`sentry.io` and self-hosted only; the docs show the list on `sentry.io`), organization, projects, project, issues, issue, issue events, issue event, releases, release; write: `update_issue` (PUT). Left out: issue comments (no documented endpoint), deletes, bulk mutation, release writes. (b) **Notion**: public integration, authorization code, `client_auth: basic`, JSON body, token endpoint `https://api.notion.com/v1/oauth/token`; `Notion-Version: 2026-03-11` (latest in the versioning reference) injected with the `header` mechanism; refresh tokens rotate and no `expires_in` is documented, so refresh follows a 401. **Notion has no OAuth scopes**: the method declares `scopes: []` and access is chosen on Notion's consent screen; `check_builtin.py` accepts empty `default_scopes` only for a method that declares `scopes: []` explicitly. Read-only operations over POST (`/v1/search`, `/v1/data_sources/*/query`) ship as **write** tools held for approval, because read tools must be GET (follow-up FU-P2-CAT-5a: parameter/body-level matching). (c) **Linear**: OAuth (PKCE, form body), access token 24 h, rotating 30-day refresh token; one `/graphql` endpoint with `graphql_query` (read) and `graphql_mutation` (write) as in `github`. No probe (probes carry no body and the endpoint is POST-only). Linear documents `scope` as comma separated while Aurora joins with spaces, so the guide tells admins to pick one scope (`write` covers all changes). (d) `no-live-tokens.sh` gains `sntrys_`/`sntryu_`, `ntn_`/`secret_`/`nrt_` and `lin_api_`/`lin_oauth_` patterns. | One Sentry app with a wildcard `*.sentry.io` host (injects on docs/ingest hosts); a fake Notion scope; Notion search as a read tool | One host entry per tool host keeps injection narrow; inventing a scope would send an undocumented parameter to Notion; read-as-GET is a framework rule. |
| D-89 | **P2-DBL-2 sandbox files and the `postgresql` package** (no change to D-80): (a) the **supervisor** writes the libpq files (`.aurora/pg/`), not the API: it alone knows the sandbox's gateway address (the boot document, so FIX-010's `sandbox_proxy_url` is followed with no code here) and its gateway credential; the home sync only carries `databases: [{service, connection_id}]` (C-231). (b) **No gateway credential is ever readable by an agent process** (coordinator decision, SEC-01, `12 §Loopback proxies`): there is no password file. The supervisor runs a loopback PostgreSQL relay (`127.0.0.1:3130`, only while databases are granted) that answers the gateway's cleartext password request itself with the credential the HTTP forwarders use (boot token; the turn context credential while exactly one turn is active, so audit attribution matches); the client leg is plaintext loopback (`sslmode=disable`, the same documented exception as the HTTP forwarders) and never sees an authentication request. The database password is never in the sandbox either. (c) The relay opens TLS to the gateway verified against `ca_pem` with the server name `<connection_id>.db.aurora.internal` (the gateway presents a CA leaf for that SNI, so the name is verified, not the gateway's own host name); a verification failure sends no credential and never relaxes. `CancelRequest` is forwarded over the same TLS (verified against the gateway's server name, since a cancel carries no connection id). (d) The service name is derived from the connection label by one pure function shared by API, sandbox and dashboard (C-230/C-231); a name shared by two granted connections gets the id prefix on both. (e) `Connection.database` is the only API change; the dashboard shows the name and `psql service=<name>` on the connection row. (f) The linter lets database apps use the synthetic tool host without a `hosts[]` entry, and `aurora plugin test` classifies SQL fixtures with `aurora-sqlclass` (C-233). (g) The sandbox image gains the `postgresql` client package. (h) With several concurrent turns a relay session cannot be attributed to one turn and uses the boot token (a database login is not tied to a conversation by any marker the client could send); the request log still names the agent and the connection. (i) A pgpass or root.crt written by an earlier version is deleted on the next sync. | A password file with the boot token (any agent process can read and exfiltrate it; `aub_` is not source-bound); the API rendering the files (credentials in the sync); `sslmode=verify-ca` | The sandbox's secrets stay with the supervisor's loopback forwarders; the relay is the third forwarder of the same kind. |
| D-94 | **P2-CAT-3: `google-workspace` built-in package** (Gmail, Calendar, Drive). (a) **Three apps, one `google` OAuth client** (authorization code, `client_auth: body`, form, token endpoint `oauth2.googleapis.com/token`): `gmail` (`gmail.googleapis.com` `/gmail/v1`), `calendar` (`www.googleapis.com` `/calendar/v3`) and `drive` (`www.googleapis.com` `/drive/v3` and `/upload/drive/v3`). Calendar and Drive share a host, so each claims its own path prefixes (D-92). `authorize_params` are `access_type=offline`, `prompt=consent`, `include_granted_scopes=true`. **No PKCE**: Google's web server flow page documents none (`pkce: false`). Refresh tokens are not rotated; a 401 triggers the refresh and `invalid_grant` needs re-authorization. (b) **Scopes**: defaults are `gmail.readonly`, `calendar.readonly`, `drive.readonly` (all `read`). Optional read: `calendar.events.readonly`, `drive.metadata.readonly`. Write: `gmail.send|compose|modify`, `calendar.events|calendar`, `drive.file|drive` (`drive.file` is write: it grants file creation and change). The guide states Google's sensitive/restricted classes (Gmail: all but `gmail.send` restricted; Calendar: sensitive; Drive: `drive` and `drive.readonly`/`drive.metadata.readonly` restricted, `drive.file` not sensitive) and the verification each needs (sensitive: verification; restricted: plus an annual security assessment); the classification was **not** verified against Google's FAQ page in this lane, so the guide tells the operator to confirm it on the consent screen. (c) **Drive hook**: `approval.summarize` only (no egress, no `fetch`), referenced by `summarize: hook` on the Drive `approvals` (reads `get_file` for `alt=media` and `export_file`, and every write). The guest reads depth-1 JSON members tolerantly (a body prefix cut mid-string yields only complete values), the first JSON part of a `multipart/related` body (never the media part), `x-upload-content-*` and `content-length` headers, and query parameters; a body it cannot read is `invalid-input` (the manifest template is then used) and a metadata `get_file` is `unsupported`. Summaries never carry file content or `emailMessage`. (d) **Tool ids** are path-based: `get_file` covers metadata and `alt=media` (a tool cannot match a query parameter), so a download is a read that is summarized only when something holds it. (e) `calendar.freeBusy` (a read over POST) ships as a **write** tool held for approval (D-93, FU-P2-CAT-5a). (f) **Left out**: Gmail `/upload/gmail/v1` endpoints, attachments, history, settings, `trash`, `delete`, label create/update; Calendar ACL, settings, watch, move/import/quickAdd, calendar create/delete; Drive `files.delete`, `emptyTrash`, comments, revisions, shared drives, `files.download` (POST) and `files.watch`; the resumable-upload `PUT` of content (the session start is summarized); `content.googleapis.com` (the docs name no other download host). (g) `no-live-tokens.sh` gains `ya29.` access tokens, `1//` refresh tokens and `AIza` API keys. | A Google package per product; PKCE without documentation; `drive.file` as the read default; a separate `userinfo` scope; Calendar free/busy as a read tool | One path claim per app on the shared Google host is the D-92 rule; the docs describe no PKCE for web clients; `drive.file` cannot read existing files; `userinfo` is not needed because the probes read the account from each API. |
| D-95 | **P2-CAT-5b: `aws-role` built-in package** (assume-role minting through a `token.mint` hook; no manifest or WIT change). (a) **Connect form** (`kind: hook`, `authorize: false`): role ARN and region as metadata, optional `session_duration` cap (900..43200 s, default 3600) as metadata, the **source** access key id and secret as credential fields (`source_access_key_id`, `source_secret_access_key`). The guest validates the ARN (`arn:aws[-a-z]*:iam::<12 digits>:role/<path/name>`; only the `aws` and `aws-us-gov` partitions, because the others use endpoints outside `amazonaws.com`) and the region name. (b) **Mint**: the guest SigV4-signs `POST sts.<region>.amazonaws.com/` (`Action=AssumeRole`, `Version=2011-06-15`, `RoleArn`, `RoleSessionName = aurora-<org_id>` from the `org_id` context variable, `DurationSeconds` = the cap, **`ExternalId` = the `org_external_id` context variable**) with a pure-Rust HMAC-SHA256 SigV4 over `sha2` (checked against AWS's published `get-vanilla` vector and the IAM `ListUsers` example). A missing external id fails closed (`internal`, no call). It returns `access_key_id`, `secret_access_key`, `session_token` and `expires-at` = STS `Expiration` capped at 12 h. (c) **Hosts** (`s3.*`, `sts.*`, `ec2.*` of `amazonaws.com`, as `aws-keys`) sign with `aws_sigv4` using the session credentials and `session_token` only; the source key is used only inside the hook and is never injected. Egress for `connect.exchange` and `token.mint` is exactly `sts.*.amazonaws.com` (regional, the narrowest pattern the validator allows); `permissions.context: [org_external_id, org_id]`. (d) `connect.exchange` mints once to validate; errors carry AWS's error *code* class only (never AWS's message, which names principals, and never a key); `AccessDenied` and key errors are `reauth-required` on `token.mint` and `invalid-input` on the form. (e) **Left out**: the global `sts.amazonaws.com` endpoint, AWS China and isolated partitions, temporary source credentials (role chaining), MFA (`SerialNumber`/`TokenCode`), session policies/tags. The package claims the same S3 and EC2 hosts as `aws-keys`; the host conflict is an admin decision (09 §7), so an instance enables one of the two for those hosts (both guides say so). (f) **No STS for agents (SEC-01), in both `aws-role` and `aws-keys`**: the `sts.*.amazonaws.com` host entry and the `sts_call` tool are removed, because STS answers to `AssumeRole`, `GetSessionToken` and `GetFederationToken` carry usable AWS credentials and tools cannot be narrowed to `GetCallerIdentity` until parameter-level matching exists (FU-P2-CAT-5a). The hook egress `sts.*.amazonaws.com` stays (the hook host calls STS, the agent does not). Fixtures in both packages prove a request to `sts.<region>.amazonaws.com` is claimed by no app and gets no `authorization`, `x-amz-security-token` or `x-amz-date`. `aws-keys` is bumped to 1.0.1; its guide points to `aws-role` for assumed roles. (2026-11-10) (f) superseded for GetCallerIdentity by D-124 (P4-GRANTS): STS host claimed again with only sts_get_caller_identity and grants.block_untooled; every other STS action is blocked before rules. | A declarative mint mechanism; an `sts_call` tool held for approval; `ExternalId` typed in by the operator; injecting the source key; a global STS endpoint | The hook ABI is frozen for contract 1 and already carries the context; the org external id is server-owned, so a tenant cannot supply another tenant's value and the role's trust condition does the confused-deputy check. |
| D-99 | **Built-in package icons** (owner, 2026-11-08; P2-CAT-ICONS). (a) Every built-in ships a real vendor icon, chosen in this order: **Simple Icons** (CC0, one pinned release, glyph in the brand colour), then the **vendor's official source** (brand or press page, official icon set, or the official repository of an open-source project), unaltered and within its stated terms. A neutral monogram is used only when neither exists. Unofficial redraws and third-party icon sites are never used. (b) `assets/icon-dark.png` is shipped where the brand glyph disappears on dark surfaces. (c) Provenance is recorded per package in `plugins/builtin/ICONS.md`: source URL or Simple Icons slug and version, sha256 of the source, date, and terms. Only rendered PNGs are committed. (d) Packages whose icon changes get a patch version bump. (e) First application: Simple Icons 16.37.0 for atlassian, github(-app), gitlab, google-workspace (the "Google" glyph; no Workspace icon exists), linear, notion, postgresql and sentry; the AWS Architecture Icons cloud logo for aws-keys and aws-role (AWS Trademark Guidelines recorded; the owner reviewed their §4 approval clause and chose to keep the official logo, 2026-11-08); the official `indico_square.png` from the indico repository; a monogram for microsoft-365, because Microsoft offers no Microsoft 365 mark under terms that allow a connector listing. | Monograms only; drawing logos; third-party icon packs | Users must recognise the product they connect to (SEC-33 clarified); CC0 or the vendor's own files keep the licensing clean, and recorded provenance makes each icon auditable. |
| D-97 | **P2-FU-2: requests caught by a freeze or BYO tightening are answered before the tunnel closes.** (a) The tunnel registry, when its re-check finds the credential frozen or its posture `disabled`, first publishes the refusal a new request would get (403 `frozen` / `byo_disabled`, `details.level`) and then fires the close. (b) A request still in the pipeline (held for approval, or any step before forwarding) is refused with that answer: the pipeline future is dropped, which abandons the approval (`resolved_at` set) and never releases the request upstream. The answer is counted in `aurora_gateway_frozen_refused_total` / `aurora_gateway_byo_refused_total{reason="posture"}`; a BYO refusal also writes the usual rate-limited refusal log row. (c) The tunnel then shuts down gracefully (answers flushed) with a 2 s hard stop; other closes (source address, context required, revoked) keep the immediate drop. WebSockets die at once. (d) Absolute-form requests register in the tunnel registry for the length of the request so they get the same answer; the response carries `Connection: close` on HTTP/1. (e) Database sessions are out of scope. | Answering only after the connection reset; releasing held requests; a new metric | The spec (06 §BYO admission 1, C-207) promised an answer and an abandon; a reset left the client guessing and the row `pending` until expiry. |
| D-96 | **P2-FU-1: phase-2 spec gaps** (no change to an owner decision). (a) **Quota e-mails** (FU-P2-015b): P2-011A's mail path was verified and tested; one deviation fixed: recipients are the org's **owners and admins** (`02` `ATTACH_QUOTA_NOTICE`), not owners only, and a drop from `full` to `warning` no longer re-sends the 80 % mail (only a rise from below 80 % does). The de-duplication state stays the `attachment.quota_notice` audit row (C-176), so no migration. (b) **Expired chip** (FU-P2-015a): the events page carries `purged_attachments` (C-196) rather than a new route or a flag on every event, because transcript events are durable payloads written before the purge. (c) **Caller address** (Q7 = yes): `Me.client_address` on `/v1/me` (C-195), since the Connect tab already needs the session and the field is the caller's own. (d) The two indexes are plain partial `CREATE INDEX` (migrations run in a transaction; both tables are small), documented in `03`. (e) `presence.provision` carries the absolute avatar URL or `null` (C-198). (f) `AppIcon` renders the light and the dark `<img>` and lets CSS pick by `data-theme` (no theme state, no flash); only the apps tab shows app icons today. | Probing the content route for 410; a metadata route for attachments; owners-only e-mails; `CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` | One wire addition per gap, each the smallest that lets the dashboard show the truth. |
| D-102 | **P2-UI-5: Chat v2, a messaging layout for the agent Chat tab** (owner request, 2026-11-08, inspired by the Flowbite chat-room layout but built only from Aurora tokens and patterns). (a) One fixed-height `ChatPanel` with a conversation list pane and a thread pane. The page does not scroll; the list and the transcript scroll inside the panel, and the composer is pinned. Below 768 px the list and the thread are two views, with the selected conversation in the URL. (b) List rows are built only from existing `Conversation` fields: `kind` (icon and label), `title`, `last_activity_at`, and `active_turn` (a "Working" dot). There are no previews, unread counts or presence. The Active / Archived switch maps to `archived`; the title filter runs on the client. (c) A thread header carries the visibility, retention and archived notes in one meta line, a Live / Reconnecting pill, and an actions `Menu` (Archive or Unarchive, two exports, Delete). This replaces the retention banner, the visibility banner and the row of four buttons. (d) The transcript groups messages by sender under a meta line with name, time and door chip, and adds date dividers. Person turns use `primary` bubbles and agent turns use `subtle` bubbles, each with a 4 px corner. Tool calls fold into one `ToolCallGroup` per turn. Inline approvals, attachment cards, a typing or activity row, redacted rows, error blocks with a fix link, and queued follow-ups are styled as `14 §Visual design` › Agent chat describes. (e) Per-message actions (Copy, Redact…) appear on hover or focus. **No API change**; behaviour (RFE-009/010/012/015/018) is unchanged. Mock-ups: `docs/design/ui-refresh/Agent*.html`. | Keep the stacked layout and restyle it; a third pane for conversation details (the details fit in the header meta line); agent text without a bubble; adding previews or unread counts to the API | The old tab spread the conversation actions, two banners and a "Reconnecting…" line above an unbounded transcript, so the composer moved down the page as the transcript grew. A pinned two-pane layout is the convention users expect from chat. Every element maps to data the API already returns. |
| D-100 | **P2-OPS-1: ops and supply-chain follow-ups.** (a) `hookImage` stays the third-party `alpine/k8s:1.31.2` but is pinned by its multi-arch index (manifest list) digest `sha256:d489e3c7…` (amd64 + arm64), resolved with `skopeo inspect --raw … \| sha256sum` and cross-checked with `skopeo inspect --format '{{.Digest}}'`. Recommendation for later: an Aurora-built tools image (`images/tools/`, only `kubectl` + busybox) removes the third-party dependency, but it needs a build, publish and CVE-rebuild pipeline; the digest pin gives the supply-chain guarantee (immutable content) at no cost, so it was implemented. (b) The evaluation Postgres stays pre-install hook resources and is documented (what is left after uninstall and how to remove it). Rendering it as normal chart resources is unsafe: an existing release holds them as hooks, so `helm upgrade` would fail with "exists and cannot be imported", and the `migrate` pre-install hook needs the database before regular resources exist. `helm upgrade` from the previous chart was run on kind. (c) sccache 0.10.0 → 0.19.0 in `images/services/Containerfile`; both SHA-256 values equal the release's published `.sha256` files and were re-checked against our own downloads. (d) `scripts/plugins/deny-hooks.sh` runs `cargo deny check` per hook crate with a `deny.toml` in each; `github-app` alone waives RUSTSEC-2023-0071 (`rsa`, Marvin timing attack: it signs a deterministic RS256 JWT in a wasm guest with no clock, network or RNG, so timings are unobservable); the script runs in CI's `rust-deny` job, not in `run-all.sh` (those guards are offline, cargo-deny needs the advisory database). (e) The `mattermost-conformance` job is added to `nightly.yml`. (f) `scripts/e2e/llm.Containerfile`'s `dnf install` gets `retries=10`, `minrate=100`, `timeout=120` to tolerate a slow mirror; no verification is weakened. (g) Real-cluster install: checklist in `docs/operator/install-helm.md`; not run (no cluster available). | Aurora tools image now; Postgres as chart resources or a subchart; waiver in the shared workspace `deny.toml`; deny in `run-all.sh` | Cheapest safe options that keep `helm upgrade` working and the supply chain verifiable. |
| D-107 | **API-P3: phase-3 contracts** (coordinator, 2026-11-09). (a) The wire details the phase-3 spec left open are settled as written in `API_CONTRACTS.md` C-310..C-385 and the `15` Vault rows, among them: `Page` for the agent-token lists; `expires_at` on BYO creation too; `Agent.byo.token`; `Idempotency-Key` validated after authorization; `Followup.promoted_turn_id`; mismatch `details.{turn_id, followup_id}`; `Skill` keeps `scope` next to `tier`; `skill_exists` (409) and 400 `invalid_request` for names in every tier; `overridden_in`; effective skills `{ items, shadowed }`; `impact` on every pending version; approve returns `PluginPackage` plus `connections_reauth`; the `default_rule_skipped_llm_host` trace entry; a separate deprovision payload schema; seal failure on delete → 503, nothing deleted; Vault reference refs stored as `@latest` (`08 §2` grammar); `gateway_degraded` reason `snapshot_not_loaded`. (b) **Vault `reference_scopes` are keyed by the immutable org id (UUID), never the org slug**; a non-UUID key refuses startup. `05`, `08 §2` and `21` are amended to match. | Proposals left open per lane; keying scopes by `org_slug` (the earlier `08` example) | Lanes implement one agreed shape. A slug can be changed, freed and taken by another org, which must never inherit Vault scopes (SEC-61). |
| D-108 | **Gateway LISTEN heartbeat** (P3-014, RFE-014, SEC-54). The gateway detects a LISTEN connection that is open but no longer delivers: every `GATEWAY_DB_PROBE_INTERVAL` (5 s) it NOTIFYs `{"scope":"heartbeat","id":<instance>,"seq":n}` on `aurora_gateway_invalidate` through the pool and expects it on the LISTEN connection; two consecutive misses mark the listener down (state `degraded` / `listener_down` at once, no second grace) and run reconnect-then-flush as for a visible loss. Heartbeat payloads are ignored by every cache (and by other replicas). The gateway runs its own LISTEN loop on sqlx `PgListener` (aurora-db's `Listener` hides the connection loss it would have to report). | Trusting the TCP connection to report its own death; relying on TCP keepalive | A freeze or BYO tightening closes live tunnels only through invalidations: a dead-but-"up" listener would leave a frozen agent's tunnels open indefinitely. sqlx 0.8 exposes no TCP keepalive option, so the heartbeat is the only line of defence at the driver level (kernel keepalive can be set by the deployment). |
| D-118 | **Per-provider `trusted_email_domains`** (owner, 2026-11-09, FIX-018; amends RFE-004's "always refused" in `04 §OIDC` and SEC-42). An `email` whose domain exactly (case-insensitively, no subdomains) matches an entry counts as verified even when the ID token has no `email_verified = true`, or has `false`. It is computed once at the token exchange, so sign-up, linking to a pre-provisioned user, invitation acceptance and `instance_admins` all see the same answer. Entries must be exact domains (no `@`, wildcard, empty label or single label); empty by default, so every other deployment keeps requiring the claim. | Trust the provider's every address (`trust_email = true`); ask the IdP operator to send the claim (no Aurora change) | CERN SSO documents no `email_verified` claim, so no CERN user could sign in. CERN SSO also federates Google, GitHub, eduGAIN and guest accounts whose addresses CERN does not control, so trusting the whole provider would let such an account claim an address and link to an existing user or invitation. Holding a `@cern.ch` address at another IdP requires that mailbox, so an exact-domain trust keeps the guarantee for the domain the provider owns. |
| D-128 | **Managed connectors from the dashboard** (owner, 2026-11-09: "let me do this from the UI just like any other app, but at the instance level"; first part of phase 4, P4-MCON; spec `13 §3.1`, C-450..C-459, SEC-69, SEC-70). (a) A core service `aurora-connector-host` (Helm `connectorHost`, on by default) runs the built-in connector types in-process with the SDK runtime; an instance admin adds a managed connector by choosing a type, reviewing its manifest, filling `instance_setup` settings and secrets; the reviewed digest is approved in the same request; no token is shown. (b) The host authenticates with one `auh_` token (chart-generated, bootstrapped like the runner token) and acts for a managed, enabled connector via the `Aurora-Connector` header; no per-connector token exists for managed connectors. (c) Connector-level settings come from a new manifest key `instance_setup` (widening when it grows) with the new field type `generated_secret`; secret values are sealed (`connector.settings`, nil org), write-only, delivered only to hosts. (d) Callbacks are served under `/connectors/<id>/` behind the normal edge, `public_url` defaults from `external_url`. (e) One managed connector per type in phase 4 (id = type id). (f) Integration settings (Mattermost server URL and admin token) stay **per org** (owner, 2026-11-09). Self-hosted connectors and their token flow remain. | One Deployment per connector created by the API through the Kubernetes API (needs cluster write access from the API and still an image choice); per-connector `aun_` tokens minted and sealed for the host (more secrets at rest for no gain); instance-level server credentials (owner chose per org) | The pain was the hand-carried token, the extra deployment and env settings per connector. Running compiled types in a chart-managed host removes all three without letting the dashboard choose what code runs (SEC-69), and reuses the connector contract, manifest approval and widening unchanged. |
| D-119 | **P4-CNT (phase 4, SPEC-P4a): shared rate-limit counters** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09; supersedes the "in memory per replica" half of D-05 and D-57 from phase 4, D-05's default stays). (a) A **`CounterStore`** trait (crate `aurora-counters`; `fixed_window_peek`, all-or-nothing `fixed_window_consume`, `bucket_take`, `health`) with backends **`memory`** (default; today's behaviour, no infrastructure), **`postgres`** (`UNLOGGED` tables `rate_limit_windows`, `rate_limit_buckets`, dedicated small pool, no new infrastructure) and **`redis`** (optional; TLS, credentials from environment variables, no Cluster). (b) Used by the gateway rule quotas and the API limiter (including the unauthenticated, rejected-bearer and webhook limits); the stream concurrency cap, CLI polling and BYO admission stay as they are. (c) **Unavailable backend**: per class (`security`, `management`, `gateway`) `local` (default: per-replica in-memory counting until recovery) or `closed` (503 `counter_unavailable`); **no `open` value exists** and one in config refuses startup. (d) The evaluator stays pure: counts are passed in (peek), consumption is a separate atomic store call that is authoritative; windows and bucket times come from the store clock; e-mail and IP keys are keyed hashes (`ratelimit_key` HKDF purpose). (e) Metrics `aurora_counters_*`, Helm `counters.*`, SEC-62, tests in `18 §Shared rate-limit counters`. `15`, `05`, `06`, `04`, `07`, `03`, `08`, `17`. | Redis as the only shared backend; fail-open on backend errors; a bundled Redis in the chart | Replicas can then enforce one limit without adding required infrastructure; a counter outage must never turn a brute-force brake or a quota into "unlimited"; availability is kept by the per-replica fallback, which is exactly today's strength. |
| D-120 | **P4-S3 (phase 4, SPEC-P4a): S3-compatible blob store** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09; the "BlobStore seam stays the long-term answer" of D-54). (a) A second **`BlobStore` backend `s3`** for **attachment bytes only** (avatars and plugin blobs stay in Postgres) beside `postgres`; `blobs.backend` picks where **new** bytes go, `attachments.blob_backend` (already per row) says where each row's bytes are, so nothing breaks and nothing moves unless the operator runs **`aurora-api blobs migrate --to s3\|postgres`** (resumable, verified, compare-and-set; there is **no filesystem backend** and so no migration from one: phase 0–3 stored attachments in Postgres). (b) Key `<prefix>attachments/<org_id>/<attachment_id>`, no filename or metadata in the object, `If-None-Match: *`, SHA-256 checksum on every put, one `PutObject` per file (limits unchanged, no multipart), bounded upload concurrency. (c) **Server-side encryption requested on every put (`AES256` default) and confirmed by a startup probe**; `none` is an explicit opt-out. (d) **No pre-signed or public URLs**: bytes are always streamed through the API's authorization, 410 and headers. (e) Credentials: workload identity (`provider_chain`, recommended) or environment variables named in config, from a Secret; never inline; API only. (f) A versioned bucket is refused unless `allow_versioned_bucket` (then every version is deleted on purge); Object Lock is never accepted. (g) Store failure → 503 `blob_store_unavailable`, no row, no quota; never 404/410; `readyz` unchanged. (h) Quota, retention, delete, visibility and the sweep are D-54/D-73 unchanged; the orphan sweep lists the prefix with `S3_ORPHAN_GRACE`. `10`, `15`, `03`, `05`, `16`, SEC-63. | Pre-signed download URLs (offload); client-side sealing of attachments now; automatic bulk migration at startup; moving avatars | Pre-signed URLs would bypass retention, the 410 and membership changes; per-row backends make the switch safe without a big-bang copy; verifying SSE and refusing versioned or locked buckets keeps "deleted means deleted" (SEC-47) true. |
| D-121 | **P4-LLM (phase 4, SPEC-P4a): additional LLM providers** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09; extends D-16). (a) Registry entries **`openai`**, **`azure_openai`** (key auth, the OpenAI v1 API only), **`bedrock`** (`aws_sigv4` or Bedrock API key), **`vertex`** (`gcp_service_account`), **`openai_compatible`**; enabled only through `llm.enabled_providers` (default unchanged), so existing configs are untouched. (b) **Cloud credentials stay in the gateway**: Bedrock requests are re-signed by the gateway (agent `Authorization`/`X-Amz-*` removed) and Vertex tokens are minted by the gateway from the service-account key (stateless, per-replica single-flight cache, `token_uri` pinned to Google); the sandbox gets only "skip auth" switches and placeholders, never a key. (c) New dialects `anthropic_bedrock` and `anthropic_vertex` (claude-code declares them); provider-native dialects with no harness consumer are not declared. (d) **Inference-only injection**: per-provider path sets, Vertex project/location pinned, refusals `llm_path_not_permitted` before policy (SEC-65); the carve-out of the Default Rule covers **exact configured hosts only**; plugins cannot claim endpoint hosts. (e) Governance, key origin (account id via STS, GCP project, OpenAI org/project), model allow-lists and posture apply unchanged; static model lists for Azure and Vertex. (f) One error-classification table shared by gateway and adapters; usage stays harness-reported but provider/endpoint/model are stamped by the API; the gateway counts no tokens. (g) Not in phase 4: Entra ID, assume-role/gateway-identity AWS auth, Converse/Gemini dialects ((phase 5, P4-LLM spec batch) Entra client credentials and assume-role added by D-261; gateway identity, Converse and Gemini stay out). `08 §Phase 4 providers`, `06`, `07`, `09`, `12`, `15`, `16`, SEC-64, SEC-65. | Letting the sandbox sign (placeholder AWS keys, ADC files); injection on any path of the endpoint host; wildcard cloud hosts in the carve-out; token counting in the gateway | A stored IAM key or service account is a cloud credential: keeping signing and minting in the gateway extends "no real credential in a sandbox" to them, and the path set keeps an agent from using it for anything but inference. |
| D-122 | **P4-HAR (phase 4, SPEC-P4a): harness adapters and selection** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09; implemented after P4-LLM). *[Amended by D-138: P4-LLM and what rested on it moved to phase 5.]* (a) A **`codex` adapter** (pinned OpenAI Codex CLI, `codex exec` JSON events) declaring `openai_responses` and `openai_chat`, `steer = false`, `AGENTS.md` instructions, state under `CODEX_HOME` in the home; configuration rendered per start with placeholders only. (b) **Explicit selection**: `agents.harness`, restricted to `hosted.enabled_harnesses` (default `["claude-code"]`, so nothing changes until an operator opts in), chosen only by the API, stamped in the boot document, instantiated from a compile-time supervisor registry; unknown or missing runtime fails the boot (`harness_unsupported`), no runner offering it fails the turn (`harness_unavailable`). (c) Dialects are declared per harness in preference order; the dialect used is the first of the harness's list that the endpoint serves. (d) **Switching** an agent's harness bumps every conversation's session epoch, purges the old sessions with the old adapter, drops incompatible model overrides; refused during a turn. (e) **Image**: one sandbox image with both runtimes by default (`SANDBOX_HARNESSES` build argument to slim it), label `org.aurora.harnesses` read by the runner (absent = `claude-code,fake`, so old images work). (f) Codex's own sandbox and approvals are off because Aurora's sandbox and gateway are the boundaries; ChatGPT-account sign-in is not supported. `12`, `11`, `10`, `05`, `15`, `16`, SEC-66. | Per-turn harness choice; harness chosen by the sandbox; a separate sandbox image per harness; supporting ChatGPT-subscription auth | One harness per agent keeps sessions, instruction files and skills coherent; an API-only choice keeps "nothing in the sandbox can pick its own runtime"; an opt-in default keeps every existing deployment unchanged. |
| D-123 | **P4-GRANTS (phase 4, SPEC-P4a): app-level "custom grants only"** (owner, D-84 Q-C, Q10). (a) Optional manifest key **`apps[].grants { custom_only, block_untooled }`**, defaults false, additive in contract 1 (a core that does not know it rejects the package; packages using it declare `min_core`), so no existing package changes. (b) `custom_only`: `full` grants refused (422 `full_grant_forbidden`) on every path; evaluator defence in depth (a target without `tools` never allows or selects such an app's connection). (c) `block_untooled`: a request on that app's connection matching no catalogue tool is blocked before rules (`app_untooled_blocked`), unoverridable. (d) **Activation that introduces `custom_only`** converts existing full grants to custom grants with **exactly today's tools** (`allow_tools` = whole catalogue, no new asks) in the activation transaction, audited per grant, and holds the upgrade for admin approval with the count (`converts_grants`), as RFE-022 does for connections; nothing is converted back. (e) Restrictions only tighten and compose by the usual strictness order. The Indico package adopts them at implementation. `09`, `07`, `05`, `14`, SEC-67. | Converting full grants to ask-for-writes; suspending existing full grants on upgrade; user-settable per-grant restriction; a rule-based (compiled) `block_untooled` | The risk of a broad token is exactly what a full grant adds (untooled requests, future tools); converting to the same tools removes it without surprising agents with new approvals or bricking them; manifest-level flags cannot be switched off by the people the restriction is for. |
| D-124 | **Q8 specified: parameter-level tool matching** (phase 4, SPEC-P4a; Q8 had proposed deferral, the owner then selected every phase-4 item, Q10). (a) Optional tool key **`params`** (1–4 AND-ed conditions: `source` `query` \| `form` \| `header` (`x-amz-target` only), `name`, `match` `equals` \| `in` \| `prefix`), a classifier beside `graphql` and `git`; additive in contract 1, existing packages untouched. (b) **Fail closed**: parameter must occur exactly once, no query/body conflict, urlencoded uncompressed valid-UTF-8 untruncated body, else the tool does not match and classification falls through; doubt never yields `read`. (c) Compatibility: a gateway that does not know the classifier refuses the snapshot (previous kept), like an unknown mechanism, so a `params` tool never runs as path-only. (d) Body buffered only when a `form` tool may match; summaries via the existing `form` decoder; linter requires negative fixtures. (e) Out of scope: regexes, JSON-body fields, numeric matching. AWS packages adopt it later (`ec2_describe`, `sts_get_caller_identity` become read tools; splitting an existing tool makes the new id arrive blocked in existing custom grants). `09 §3.4`, `06`, SEC-68. | Keep `Describe*`/STS reads as held write tools forever; a hook-based classifier; JSON pointer matching now | The owner wants read-only AWS use without approvals for every `Describe*`; a closed, linter-provable matcher with fall-through-on-doubt keeps the safe default whenever the request is not clearly the read it names. |
| D-136 | **FIX-019: hosted model readiness, `no_default_model`, and LiteLLM team verification** (owner report from the production cluster, 2026-11-09: a granted, verified key on endpoint `aigw` (`provider: litellm`, `base_url` and `key_origin.method: endpoint` only) failed every turn with `no_model_key`, so the sandbox never started; `litellm_team` "couldn't get it to work"). Cause: `08 §Model selection` resolves the model only from `default_model`, the provider default, the allow-list or static `models`; a LiteLLM endpoint configured with none of them has a usable key and **no model**, and the dispatcher reported that as "no key". (a) **Per-key readiness**, computed by one function used by the dispatcher and the new `GET /v1/agents/{agent}/model-readiness` (`can_own`): for every LLM key granted to the agent, `usable` or the first failing reason in the order `not_active`, `endpoint_unknown`, `provider_disabled`, `scope_not_permitted`, `origin_unverified`, `origin_not_compliant`, `no_shared_dialect`; plus `default: { endpoint_id, model_id } \| null`. (b) A new turn error **`no_default_model`** when at least one key is usable but neither an agent model override nor an endpoint default resolves a model; `no_model_key` stays for "no usable key" and its message names the reasons (codes and counts, never key names, since participants who are not owners read the transcript). Both fail before any sandbox start, as today. (c) **No automatic pick** from the live model list: a LiteLLM listing mixes chat, embedding and image models and its order is not meaningful, so the choice stays explicit (the agent's model on Instructions, or the operator's `default_model`). (d) Config: a `litellm` (or other listed-model) endpoint without `default_model` starts with a **warning** naming the endpoint and the consequence (existing configs keep starting); `15` and the operator docs recommend `default_model`. (e) **`litellm_team`**: the verification error carries the actual cause (HTTP status of `/key/info`, unreachable, or `key_reports_no_team`) to the LLM key row; a key reporting no team verifies with `team_id = null` when no `allowed_team_ids` applies (instance or org), and is refused `key_reports_no_team` only when team ids are required; config validation of an unknown `key_origin.method` suggests the nearest valid value (`litellm-team` → "did you mean `litellm_team`?"). The call shape is re-checked against the LiteLLM version deployed in the kind mock with a **team key** (not only the master key). `08`, `10`, `05`, `14`, `15`, `18`, C-500..C-502. | Pick the first model of the live listing; make `default_model` mandatory for LiteLLM (breaks existing configs at start); keep one `no_model_key` code with a longer message | Users need to see *why* a granted key does not work, and the fix for a missing model is different from the fix for a missing key; an arbitrary model choice would surprise users and could pick a non-chat model. |
| D-137 | **FIX-020/FIX-021: give agents access from the credential, and deep-linkable Credentials tabs** (owner request, 2026-11-09: "when a user defines an LLM key (or an app or whatever), let them give access to one or more agents right after"). (a) Credential-side grant routes: `GET`/`POST /v1/secrets/{id}/grants` and `POST /v1/connections/{id}/grants` (bulk, 1–`GRANT_BULK_MAX` = 50 agents). Each agent passes **exactly** the checks of the agent-side `PUT /v1/agents/{agent}/grants/…` route (same service function: `can_own` on the agent's workspace, credential in scope for the agent, P4-GRANTS restrictions, frozen-agent rules); the request is **all-or-nothing** in one transaction with one audit row per grant, idempotent for grants that already exist (an existing connection grant is **not** modified: the response marks it `existing`). Removal stays the agent-side `DELETE`. Lists only show agents the caller can see. (b) The dashboard offers an **"Give agents access"** step right after an LLM key, a secret or a connection is created (skippable, nothing preselected) and an **Access** action on every credential row listing the agents that have it (add/remove). For a connection the step grants `full`; for an app with `custom_only` it asks once for the allowed tools (every other tool `block`) and applies that custom grant to every selected agent. (c) The Credentials page tabs are addressable (`?tab=secrets\|llm\|proposals\|apps\|connections\|oauth`), so error deep links land on the right tab (`no_model_key` → `?tab=llm`). (d) The agent Access tab's connection-grant editor that `14` already specifies and the dashboard never had is FIX-022. `05`, `14`, SEC-77, C-503..C-506. | Client-side loop over the agent-side route (no atomicity, N audit round trips, partial failures); a replace-the-set `PUT` (could silently remove grants in workspaces the caller cannot see for org credentials); preselecting the workspace default agent | Granting where the credential is created is the natural moment; reusing the agent-side checks per agent keeps one authorization path (SEC-77), and add-only semantics cannot remove access the caller does not see. |
| D-130 | **P4-GRP-1 (phase 4, SPEC-P4b): manual directory groups** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09). (a) Flat org groups (`groups`, `group_members`, `group_workspace_access`, `03 §Planned changes`) that hold **workspace roles** (`owner`/`approver`/`member`) and an optional **org-role mapping** `admin`/`member`; **`owner` can never be mapped**. (b) **Effective role = strongest of direct and group-derived**, never a sum, never lowering a direct role, implemented once in `aurora-db::authz` (`effective_access`) so `can_use`/`can_own`/`can_approve` and everything built on them (conversation visibility, deciders, fire-time authority, API-key liveness) follow without special cases. (c) A group **never creates, reactivates or removes an org membership** (composite foreign keys to `org_members`); a suspended member derives nothing. (d) Managed by org owners/admins (membership, mapping); bound to workspaces by `can_own` of the workspace; everything critical-audited; limits 500 groups per org, 5 000 members per group, 100 groups per user. (e) Evaluated from the database on every request (no authorization cache); the impact view and `via_groups` keep offboarding honest. (f) Not in phase 4: nesting, JIT group assignment, import/export. `04`, `03`, `05`, `14`, `17`, SEC-71. | A role table separate from workspace roles; groups as org members' only access path; a cache of effective roles with a TTL; letting a group be mapped to `owner` | Directory-driven access is what organizations with an IdP expect, but ownership is a human decision and the last-owner invariant must hold; "strongest of" is the rule people already assume; reading per request makes removal immediate without invalidation bugs. |
| D-131 | **Group identities in policy and invalidation** (phase 4, SPEC-P4b, P4-GRP-1; extends D-49). (a) Rule identity `{ "kind": "group", "id" }`: matches only when the request has a **speaker** who is currently in the group; the evaluator's principal set is `{agent_id} ∪ speaker_group_ids`, resolved by the gateway at credential resolution (same moment and `can_use` check as the speaker) and passed in, so `aurora-policy` stays pure. (b) **No speaker, no group**: boot, BYO, schedule, webhook, watch, peer, system and bot turns never match a group identity, exactly as for `user` (SEC-44). (c) **A deleted group never widens a rule**: rules are not rewritten; a dead id matches nobody; an empty `identities` array stays the only way to say "anyone"; the warning `rule_names_removed_group` tells the author. (d) Invalidation: new gateway scope **`user`** (membership change) plus `workspace`/`org`; new `aurora_auth_revoked` kind **`access`** re-authorizes open SSE streams and long-polls; sessions are not revoked. (e) Authoring validates the group is in the scope's org (422 `unknown_identity`). `06`, `07`, `05`, SEC-71. | Group ids evaluated against the workspace roster; rewriting rules on group deletion; invalidating the whole workspace for one membership change | Consistency with the RFE-005 speaker binding is what makes person-scoped privileges safe; rewriting or emptying a rule on deletion is the one way a deletion could turn a restricted rule into a public one. |
| D-132 | **P4-GRP-2 (phase 4, SPEC-P4b): SCIM 2.0 provisioning** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09).
