(a) `/scim/v2` Users and Groups, the subset Entra ID, Okta and Keycloak use (single `eq` filters, `startIndex`/`count`, PATCH with and without path, no Bulk/ETag/sort/enterprise extension, no `roles`), SCIM-format errors, on the API port, **404 unless `scim.enabled` and the org owner enabled it**. (b) **Token `aud_`** per org, created and revoked only by an org **owner** (session, recent re-authentication), shown once, **hash only**, always expiring (default 365 d, max 730 d), five per org, valid only on `/scim/v2`. (c) A SCIM user is a **link** (`scim_users`) to a global `users` row found **by e-mail only**; a new one is a **pre-provisioned, unverified** user (`email_verified_at` null) that can do nothing until the OIDC sign-in with the verified e-mail (D-118 included) binds it; `externalId` is correlation only, never linking. (d) Provisioned e-mails must be in the **owner-configured domains**; SCIM never changes `users.email`/`display_name` of a person who has an identity. (e) Org role is always `member` (no `roles`); **owners are never mutated by SCIM**; manual groups, mappings and bindings are out of its reach; SCIM groups grant nothing until an admin maps or binds them. (f) Optional org `exclusive` mode refuses JIT/invitations without a SCIM link; off by default. (g) Audit as the dashboard's critical rows with `actor_kind = service` and `metadata.via = "scim"` (no new `audit_log.source`). `04 §SCIM`, `16 §W15`, `03`, `05`, `15`, SEC-72. | Linking by `externalId` or any SCIM-asserted attribute; SCIM-assigned roles; a tenant-wide SCIM token; non-expiring tokens; letting SCIM reach owners | The IdP is trusted for directory state, not identity: linking by verified e-mail keeps the OIDC sign-in the only way in; owners and mappings are the deliberate human decisions SCIM must not be able to forge; hash-only expiring tokens limit the damage of a leaked one. |
| D-135 | **SCIM deprovisioning effects** (phase 4, SPEC-P4b, P4-GRP-2; extends D-132). (a) States `active` → `suspended` → `removed` (tombstone). Suspend = membership `suspended` (role kept); remove = membership deleted (bindings and group memberships go with it); the `users` row and other orgs are never touched. (b) **Sessions** are revoked only when no other active membership remains. (c) The org's **API keys of the user are revoked** (not just dormant), so reactivation never revives a leaked key. (d) Speaker turns of the user in the org are **aborted** (`user_deprovisioned`); schedules/webhooks follow the existing creator re-check (RFE-021), which SCIM cannot reassign, so the offboarding view lists them. (e) **No BYO token rotation, no agent deletion**: an IdP hiccup must not break live services; the audit row and dashboard list the BYO agents the person created. (f) **A tombstone or suspended link blocks JIT, invitations and sign-in re-admission** to that org. (g) **Reactivation only when SCIM itself suspended** (`suspended_by_scim`): a human suspension wins; aborted turns and revoked keys stay so. `04 §SCIM`, `10`, SEC-73. | Hard-deleting the user; reviving keys on reactivation; auto-rotating BYO tokens; letting the IdP override an admin's suspension | Offboarding must be immediate and irreversible where secrets may have been seen, but an IdP outage or a mis-mapped sync must not destroy agents or undo a decision an administrator made on purpose. |
| D-133 | **RFE-011b (phase 4, SPEC-P4b): approval park-and-resume** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09; `80 §RFE-011` Decision). *[Completed by D-264/D-265 (P4-011B spec batch): the switch governs creation only, LLM traffic never parks, access removal and session resets revoke grants, one follow-up per decision transaction, request-log and settings rules.]* (a) **Opt-in** (`approvals.park.enabled`, default false). A request whose effective approval timeout exceeds `approvals.park.threshold_secs` (900 s) is **refused at once** (`403 approval_parked`, `x-should-retry: false`, **not sent**) and its approval stays `pending` until its deadline (up to `approvals.park.max_timeout_secs`, 24 h default, 7 d max); nothing is suspended, the turn ends, the sandbox parks normally (**not a keep-awake reason**). (b) The decision becomes a **single-use grant**: salted `SHA-256` fingerprint of agent, conversation, method, host:port, exact path and query, body hash, requiring rule and winning credential; consumed by one compare-and-set when the agent repeats the identical request within `approvals.park.resume_window_secs` (30 min); policy is re-evaluated, the grant only replaces `needs_approval`. (c) The agent learns by a **platform-composed system follow-up** in the same conversation (the action-approval mechanism); expiry only posts a notice. (d) Only **hosted-context, fully buffered (≤ 256 KiB), speaker-independent** requests park (the resume turn has no speaker, D-49/D-131); everything else is held and clamped to `approvals.max_timeout_secs`. (e) Caps against approver flooding, deny cooldown, freeze abandons pending and revokes ready grants; parked approvals do not exempt a turn from stall detection. (f) Same deciders, audit and cards as holds; the stale-holder sweep and SIGTERM skip parked rows. `07`, `06`, `03`, `10`, `11`, `12`, `13`, `14`, `15`, SEC-74. | Suspending the harness process or container for the wait; a pre-approval token the agent can present for any request; parking BYO and streaming requests; unbounded deadlines | Hours-long approvals should cost a database row, not a container, and the agent must not be able to turn an approval into a general permission; byte-identical single-use grants keep the human's decision exactly as wide as what they saw. |
| D-134 | **RFE-011b (phase 4, SPEC-P4b): Web Push** (owner, Q10, 2026-11-09). *[Completed by D-266/D-267 (P4-011B spec batch): `push.*` stays in Helm, material in the `inline` store, session revocation deletes and same-person rebind, key identity by public key, unlinkable `Topic`, the service worker's limits and the loopback-only test seam.]* (a) **Opt-in at three levels**: `push.enabled` (default false, needs outbound HTTPS from the API to the push services), a deliberate per-user per-browser registration (session only; API keys cannot register), per-device pause/remove. (b) A subscription is **bound to the session that registered it**: delivery only while that session is live (sign-out and "sign out others" end it), and an endpoint re-registered by another user **moves**. (c) **Payload**: end-to-end encrypted, ≤ 1 KiB, fixed fields; **never** a title, summary, rule, host, path, body, token or link; agent and workspace names only on a device that opted in (default off); no action buttons. (d) Sent only to deciders at send time (groups, visibility), without a live watcher, for still-pending approvals, after a 5 s grace, 30 per user per hour then a digest; one reminder for parked approvals; **e-mail is independent and not suppressed**; no retraction. (e) **VAPID**: ES256 key generated by the first API replica, **private key sealed** (`webpush.vapid`), API-only, operator-managed alternative via an environment variable; rotation keeps each subscription on its own key until the dashboard silently re-subscribes, retired key destroyed after 30 days. (f) **Subscription endpoint and keys sealed** (`webpush.subscription`); endpoints must be https on an operator allow-list (defaults: FCM, Mozilla, Apple, WNS), re-validated before every send, delivered through the destination guard with no redirects. (g) A static service worker with no fetch handler that shows text built from fixed fields and opens only same-origin `/o/…` paths. `07 §Web Push`, `05`, `08`, `14`, `15`, `16 §W16`, SEC-75, SEC-76. | Push without a session binding; titles or summaries in the payload for convenience; a generated key only; trusting the subscription URL as a destination; approve/deny buttons in the notification | The point of push is to wake a decider, not to disclose or to act: everything sensitive stays behind the signed-in dashboard, and a browser-supplied URL is the classic SSRF vector, so it is allow-listed and guarded. |
| D-138 | **Phase-4 scope trimmed** (owner, 2026-11-09): SSH front door (P4-SSH-1, P4-SSH-2), additional LLM providers (P4-LLM), evals (P4-EVAL), S3 blob store (P4-S3), and approval park-and-resume with Web Push (P4-011B) **move to phase 5**; the rest of phase 4 is managed connectors (P4-MCON-1..3), the owner's fixes (FIX-019..FIX-022), shared counters (P4-CNT), custom grants and parameter matching (P4-GRANTS, Q8), harness adapters (P4-HAR), groups and SCIM (P4-GRP-1, P4-GRP-2), port previews (P4-PRV-1, P4-PRV-2) and the git skill library (P4-GSL), then AUDIT-004; work stops after phase 4 until the owner starts phase 5. The moved items keep their ids and their already-written specs (their "(phase 4, …)" tags name the spec batch, not the implementation phase). P4-HAR no longer waits for P4-LLM (`litellm` endpoints already serve `openai_chat` and `openai_responses`); P4-PRV-2 no longer waits for P4-SSH-2. | Keep all of phase 4 | Owner's call on scope and order (Q10 follow-up). |
| D-139 | **P4-IDP-1: roles from IdP claims** (owner, 2026-11-09, after the first production sign-in: "the oidc setup should include a way to specify which users are instance admins, owners, etc., based on a role they get from IdP; the user group to role mapping will be done by the IdP server"; "roles will probably have to be configured in helm values"). (a) Per OIDC provider: `role_claim` (dot path), `role_claim_source`, `require_role`, `role_mappings` (exact claim value → `instance_admin`, org role incl. `owner`, workspace roles). These live in the Helm values only; the dashboard shows them read-only. (b) **Synced at every sign-in**: claim-sourced assignments are added and removed. Assignments from other sources are never touched; the strongest source wins; the last owner is never removed. Missing orgs and workspaces named by a mapping are created. (c) Claim-derived rows are read-only in the dashboard with a "From IdP role" badge; a claim debugger shows on the account page and the admin Users page. (d) Amends D-130(a)'s "no directory change can create an owner" for **operator configuration only**: groups and SCIM still cannot map `owner` or instance admin. `04 §OIDC role claims`, `15`, `14`, SEC-78. | Group-claim mapping in Aurora (owner: the IdP maps groups to roles); mappings editable in the dashboard (an instance admin could mint more admins through the UI); sync only at first sign-in (roles would never be revoked) | The IdP is the organization's source of truth for who administers what. Exact values and sources keep the sync from touching anything a human set, and keeping it in Helm values keeps admin creation an operator act. |
| D-140 | **P4-ONB-1: new-user placement** (owner, 2026-11-09: "all users will get their own org and be owners, which is not at all what the flow should be … the instance admin has to be able to configure a default org and workspace for new users, including a dynamic workspace for each user"; "everything else should be through the interface"). (a) Admin → Sign-up & placement, **dashboard only** (no config key): mode `shared_org` \| `personal_org` (today's behaviour) \| `none`; default org and role; shared workspaces; **personal workspace** with name template (claims, fallback chain) and role; **starter grants** (org credentials granted to each new personal workspace's default agent). (b) Applied once when a user is created, after invitation, claims and JIT; turning personal workspaces on later gives existing users theirs at the next sign-in. (c) The personal workspace is bound to `personal_owner_user_id`, never found by name; collisions get numbered slugs (SEC-79). (d) Upgrade-safe: unsaved placement behaves as today, with an admin warning; on a fresh instance non-admin sign-ins under `open` wait until the admin saves it (setup wizard). `04 §New-user placement`, `14`, SEC-79. | Placement in config (owner: through the interface); a personal org per user (the current behaviour, wrong for shared instances); finding a user's workspace by slug (collisions and renames would hand workspaces to the wrong person) | Matches how a shared instance is run: one org for users, one workspace each, a working agent on day one. |
| D-141 | **P4-ONB-2: onboarding** (owner, 2026-11-09: "some sort of guided wizard-like workflow for new users, showing them around and explaining where to add an LLM key, how to hook it up to an agent … this already partially exists, but it needs to be better"). (a) **Instance setup wizard** for instance admins (shown until a placement is saved; Admin → Setup afterwards): roles, placement, LLM access (endpoints, `default_model`, org key + starter grants), runners, optional apps, try it. (b) **Welcome page** at a user's first sign-in, with real names for org, workspace and agent, and a **getting-started guide** whose steps complete from real state (model readiness, grants, conversations). Steps already satisfied (e.g. an org key from starter grants) are collapsed. It is role-aware: it names who can do a step the user cannot. (c) **Tour** of the navigation (coach marks, keyboard, 390 px) and **teaching empty states**; errors link to the guide step that fixes them. (d) Progress is stored server-side per user; everything is skippable and replayable. `14 §Onboarding`. | A one-time modal tutorial; client-only progress; a guide driven by clicks instead of state | The failure the owner hit (key stored, not usable) is invisible to click-driven checklists; state-driven steps and named next actions turn it into a guided fix. |
| D-142 | **Connector-host registration honours `plugins.allow_unsigned`** (coordinator, 2026-11-10; P4-MCON-2 finding, task P4-MCON-4). With the development-only flag on, a host type with an **empty** signature enters the catalogue marked `signed: false` (dashboard badge "Unsigned (development)"); a present-but-invalid signature, an id mismatch or an invalid manifest is still rejected; with the flag off (default, production) nothing changes. Release images sign the built-in manifests with the project key (CI `release` environment). | Keep rejecting unsigned types everywhere (development installs then list no connector types at all); accept invalid signatures under the flag | Parity with the self-hosted connector path, where the same flag already auto-approves unsigned manifests; a forged or corrupted signature is never accepted. |
| D-144 | **P4-PRV-1 (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): preview shares** (`21 §Phase 4 — Port previews`; D-138 kept previews in phase 4). (a) **Opt-in at three levels**: instance (`previews.enabled` plus a validated `previews.domain`, which needs wildcard DNS and a wildcard TLS certificate), org (owner/admin switch `settings.previews_enabled`, default off, audited `org.previews.update`), share (a person). Nothing else changes for a deployment that sets none of them: no relay, no DNS, no certificate. (b) **One share per (hosted agent, port)**, hostname `p` + 24 random base32 characters under `previews.domain`: stable for the share's life, containing no agent, org or port information, **never reused** after deletion. (c) **Visibility is `private` only** (live dashboard session of a user for whom `can_use(workspace)` holds); no public, org-wide, link or per-person visibility and no API-key or agent access in phase 4; the column is CHECK-constrained so widening is a new decision and an additive migration. (d) **Create, pause, change, delete = `can_use`**, never an agent tool (an agent cannot publish itself); BYO agents have none; limits 10 per agent, 200 per org. (e) Port 1024–65535 excluding the supervisor's loopback listeners; the allow-list reaches the supervisor only as the `previews` home-sync part and lives in its memory only. (f) Audit `preview.share.create/update/delete` and `org.previews.update` are security-critical; the start command text is never audited (hash prefix only). (g) Frozen agents' shares are inert (no wake, no start, 423), kept, and work again on unfreeze. `11 §Port previews`, `03 §Planned changes`, `04`, `05`, SEC-80. | Visibilities `org`/`public`/signed link now; a global switch only; agent-created shares; derived hostnames such as `<agent>-<port>.<domain>` | Previews put a browser-reachable path in front of a sandbox: the narrowest audience (people who can already read the transcripts), random hostnames (no enumeration, no takeover of a recycled name) and default-off at every level are the cheapest secure choice; every wider mode can be added later without changing stored data. |
| D-145 | **P4-PRV-1 (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): wake, start command and keep-awake for previews.** (a) A request to a parked share is a **wake demand** (`sandboxes.preview_wake_at`, due work for 60 s, rate-limited to 12 wakes per share per hour, never for a frozen agent) handled by the ordinary start arm and start backoff. (b) A share's optional **start command** is run **on demand** only: after the boot and the `previews` sync, if the port is not listening and a request waits, the API enqueues `preview.start { share_id }`; the supervisor looks the command up in its own list by port (no command text on the wire) and runs it as process `preview:<port>` as the agent user with the container environment; at most 3 launches per 10 min per share. A share without a command starts nothing. (c) A preview **never creates a turn** or stamps `last_engaged_at`. (d) Keep-awake reason **`preview`**: the relay heartbeat refreshes `preview_active_until` (120 s) while a stream is open or recent, capped at 4 h per boot (`preview_awake_since`), so an open WebSocket cannot pin a sandbox forever. (e) The relay waits at most 45 s for a woken port, then shows a self-refreshing 503 page. `11 §Port previews`, `05`, `12 §Previews`, `16 §W5`. | Starting the command on every boot; a preview counting as engagement (`last_engaged_at`); no cap on stream wakefulness | Running a user-supplied command only when someone opens the link costs nothing otherwise and keeps unattended execution tied to a human action; engagement is a person-and-turn concept and a held tab must not defeat the idle and cap rules. |
| D-146 | **P4-PRV-2 (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): preview authentication and origin isolation** (D-138: no longer waits for SSH). (a) **`aurora-relay` is a stateless, database-less, key-less service** (port 4112 behind a wildcard edge host, 4193 ops) that makes no authorization decision: the API does, on every uncached resolve (cache ≤ 15 s). (b) Preview origins are **disjoint** from the dashboard: `previews.domain` is never the dashboard host, a parent or child of it, of any trusted origin or of the gateway host (startup validation, both directions); the session cookie is **host-only** (an invariant). (c) A preview has its **own credential**: cookie `__Host-aurora_preview` (`auv_`, hashed, one share, one dashboard session, 8 h absolute, 1 h idle, revoked with the session, membership, share, freeze), obtained by a **sealed, single-use, 60 s grant bound to share, user, session and a browser-held state nonce** (the OAuth state pattern: login CSRF fails) through a session-only `GET /v1/previews/authorize` on the dashboard origin whose redirect target is built from the share's label; the relay strips the credential before the app sees the request. (d) **Fetch Metadata** (`Sec-Fetch-Site`) refusal of same-site/cross-site non-navigation requests at the relay, WebSocket `Origin` equal to the preview, upstream `Set-Cookie` `Domain` removed, and — only while previews are on — the same refusal for cookie-authenticated dashboard API requests. (e) Unknown, disabled and no-access are one 404; open streams are revalidated every 15 s. (f) Three prefixes: `aul_` (relay anchor, chart-generated like `auh_`), `auv_` (preview session), `aux_` (stream grant). `04 §Preview access`, `16 §W17`, `15 §Port previews`, SEC-81, SEC-85. | A signed cookie scoped to a parent domain (`Domain=.previews.example`: shared by all previews and tossable); handing the dashboard session to previews; trusting the relay with authorization; one global preview credential | A per-host `__Host-` credential cannot be set, read or replaced by another preview or by a parent domain, and the grant exchange is the same proven pattern as OAuth with state; keeping the relay dumb means its compromise leaks traffic of users who are already inside a session, never access. |
| D-147 | **P4-PRV-2 (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): byte streams and allow-lists** (`21 §Phase 4 — Port previews`). (a) **Leg 1, relay → runner**: one WebSocket per upstream connection to the runner's own **stream listener :4108** (separate from 4105 and 4106, reachable from the relay only), authenticated by a **single-use 30 s `aux_` grant** that carries no port or sandbox: the runner redeems it at the API and uses only the binding it returns; backpressure is TCP's, bounded by one window. (b) **Leg 2, runner ↔ supervisor**: multiplexed over the existing W6 channel by `stream.*` JSON frames **outside the seq/ack lineage** plus **binary data messages** `[0x01][stream_id u32][≤ 16 KiB]`; per stream and direction a **credit window of 128 KiB** (so ≤ 64 streams × 128 KiB = 8 MiB in flight per sandbox), round-robin between streams, control frames never blocked; a W6 reconnect ends every stream (never resumed). (c) **Allow-list at both ends**: the runner from the API binding, the supervisor from its in-memory `previews` share list (never from discovery, never from a file in the home), loopback only (`127.0.0.1`, then `[::1]`), ports 1024..65535 minus its own listeners; an empty list refuses everything. (d) Limits: 32 streams per share, 64 per sandbox, 64 per user per relay replica, idle 120 s (600 s upgraded), lifetime 4 h, request body 100 MiB, 5 GiB per share per day. (e) TLS on leg 1 is optional (`runner.stream_tls`); default plaintext in-cluster is a documented residual. (f) The relay forwards the original `Host`, keeps HTTP/1.1 to the app and passes WebSockets as upgraded streams without inspecting frames. `16 §W18`, `11`, `12`, SEC-82. | HTTP/2 multiplexing or a sidecar tunnel in the sandbox (a listening socket); the relay dialling the sandbox network; Postgres or Redis as the data path; a port parameter in the stream request | Reusing the authenticated supervisor channel adds no listening socket and no network path into the sandbox; letting the API bind a grant to one share and port makes every hop's authority explicit and independently enforced; credit windows bound memory per sandbox without head-of-line blocking on the channel. |
| D-148 | **P4-GSL (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): git skill library — what is stored, who decides, how it composes** (`21 §Phase 4 — Git skill library`; RFE-020, SEC-56). (a) A library is an **org-scoped app connection** of a git-hosting package plus repository/ref/path, created by org owners/admins; a library is a **source, not a tier**: skills are *added* to the agent, workspace or org tier by that tier's existing write authority (agent `can_use`, workspace `can_own`, org owner/admin; never an agent tool), so SEC-56 and precedence (agent > workspace > org) are unchanged and the projection and wire format do not change. (b) **Only added skills are stored**, as ordinary `skills`/`skill_files` rows **pinned** to a commit (blob ids and content hash recorded); browsing stores nothing; an add must quote the commit the person saw. (c) Library skills are **read-only** (people may enable/disable, remove, or **detach** into an editable skill; agent tools cannot touch them). (d) A refresh **never applies**: it writes **proposed updates** (`changed`/`removed`/`invalid`); applying needs the tier's authority, a session, and the proposal's `content_sha256` after reading the **full diff**; no auto-update or branch-tracking mode in phase 4. (e) Repository entries that are symlinks, submodules, oversize or invalid are refused; budgets per tier (agent 1 MiB / 25, workspace 2 MiB / 50, org 4 MiB / 100) and 4 MiB of open proposals per org. (f) A failed connection or deleted library leaves pinned skills as they are; deleting a library detaches its skills. `10 §Skills`, `03`, `04`, `05`, SEC-83. | Tracking the branch head (a push changes agents silently); a library tier with its own precedence; storing the whole repository; auto-applying "safe" updates; letting agents add or update library skills | The only way to keep SEC-56 true with an external source is to make the repository a *proposer* and a human with the tier's authority the *decider*, over the exact bytes that will be projected; pinning also makes agent behaviour reproducible and independent of the git host's availability. |
| D-153 | **P4-HAR (phase 4): implementation choices D-122 left open** (worker, 2026-11-10; contracts C-420..C-429). (a) **The pinned Codex CLI is 0.167.0** (release `rust-v0.167.0`, static musl build, SHA-256 verified in the image build); it **refuses `wire_api = "chat"`**, so by D-122(c) ("a version that drops it removes it") the adapter and `GET /v1/harnesses` declare **`openai_responses` only**; a chat-only endpoint is `no_shared_dialect` for Codex. (b) **A queued purge remembers its harness** (`session_purges.harness`, `session.purge.harness`, optional on both): after a switch the *old* adapter (compile-time registry) deletes the files, and such a purge is not held back by the conversation's new turn. (c) **A supervisor that cannot run the boot document's harness sends `sandbox.status failed harness_unsupported` instead of `ready`** (one new supervisor → runner frame kind) and exits; the runner needs no new code path and the existing start-failure mapping fails the queued turns. (d) **`runner.image_harnesses`** names the image's runtimes for the Kubernetes backend, which cannot read `org.aurora.harnesses`. (e) **`harness_unavailable` fires only while at least one live replica exists and none lists the harness** (an outage keeps turns waiting, as before). (f) **Codex adapter details**: one `codex exec` child per turn, the Codex thread id is the session reference, skills live in `~/.codex/skills/aurora`, the vendor-service features and web search are disabled, `auth.json` is removed at every start, and the provider base URL is the provider's placeholder-only `OPENAI_BASE_URL` client variable when it has one, else the endpoint base URL. (g) **Known limit**: Codex keeps a thread metadata row in its SQLite index after `purge_session` deletes the rollout files; the conversation content is gone, the index row (thread id, title, timestamps) stays until Codex rebuilds it. `12`, `11`, `15`, `16`, `03`, SEC-66. | Declaring `openai_chat` for Codex anyway; a harness field on every `session.purge` consumer; a new runner → API status call for boot refusals; failing turns with `harness_unavailable` when no runner is online | Each choice is the smallest change that keeps D-122's properties (API-only selection, no fallback, no credential in the sandbox, nothing changes for a default deployment). |
| D-149 | **P4-GSL (phase 4, SPEC-P4c): library access path and refresh.** (a) Packages opt in with an additive manifest block **`library`** (declarative `GET` templates for `resolve_ref`, `list_tree`, `get_blob` with pattern-checked placeholders and JSON-path extractors; no hooks, no new mechanism; instance-admin approved and re-approved on change), so the core stays integration-neutral (SEC-33) and REST is used instead of a git client (no pack parsing, bounded per-file reads, nothing cloned). (b) **The gateway performs the reads** through `POST /internal/v1/library-fetch` on the internal port: it builds the request from the template, requires an org-scoped active connection of the named org, injects the credential with the host entry's mechanisms and refreshes OAuth, uses the guarded resolver, never follows redirects, caps size and time; policy/grants/approvals do not apply because no agent is involved, and no agent request can reach the route; the API never holds a plaintext token. (c) The refresh job runs every 15 min (minimum 5) per library **under a per-library lease**, only for libraries with added skills, one `resolve_ref` call when nothing changed, with error backoff 15 min → 1 h → 6 h and 4 concurrent refreshes per replica. (d) `skills.library.enabled` (default `true`) is a kill-switch; the feature is invisible until an org admin connects a repository and needs no new infrastructure. `09 §3.4 library`, `06 §Library fetch`, `05` jobs, `15 §Git skill library`, SEC-84. | The API calling the git host with the token (a second place for plaintext and refresh logic); `git clone` or a git library in the API; hard-coding GitHub/GitLab adapters in the core; webhooks from the git host (a new inbound, signed-delivery surface) | Reusing the gateway's credential custody, host guard and refresh keeps credentials in one place, and a data-only manifest block gives GitHub, GitLab and later hosts the same path without core changes; polling under a lease needs no inbound path. |
| D-151 | **P4-ONB-1: placement details the owner's D-140 left open** (implementer, 2026-11-10, for the coordinator to confirm). (a) The name template's fallback chain is `\|`-separated inside one string (`{preferred_username}\|{email_local}`); one field in the form, one column. (b) The admin preview has no stored token to read: Aurora keeps no claims, so "your own token" is the caller's profile (display name, e-mail local part) and a typed sample stands for every simple variable; claim-only variables are reported as unresolved. (c) `open` + placement `none` admits only by invitation, role claim or `jit` org (`04`: "users enter only by invitation, role claims or JIT"). (d) Existing users outside the default organization are not moved and get no personal workspace; a member of it gets theirs at the next sign-in. (e) A starter-grant credential that vanished is skipped and audited, never a sign-in failure. (f) "Personal orgs from before" = organizations named `<name>'s org` with one member, who owns it and has it as default org (no new column). (g) Unsaved + fresh = no placement saved and no organization: only listed or claim-mapped instance admins sign in (C-527). `04 §New-user placement`, C-520..C-529. | Storing the last token's claims for the preview (retains personal data for a cosmetic feature); failing the sign-in on a stale starter grant (one deleted key would lock everyone out); a marker column for old personal orgs (no value after the first migration) | Smallest surface that keeps D-140's promises and SEC-79 intact. |
| D-154 | **P4-GSL implementation details that complete D-148/D-149** (worker, 2026-11-10; no change of a security property). (a) **The API reaches the gateway's internal port through a new key `api.gateway_internal_url`** (default `http://aurora-gateway:4103`, validated like `api.hookhost_url`; the internal secret ring authenticates, as for the hook host). (b) **Fetch route shape**: the response carries the git host's status and the body **base64-encoded** (`encoding: "base64"`: a raw blob may be binary), only `link` and `retry-after` are returned as headers, a 3xx is returned as data and never followed; a paginated tree adds an integer `page` parameter (2–200) to the template's query, read by the API from the `Link` header's `rel="next"` `page` value, so a response URL is never followed; the placeholder set gains `{ref\|urlencode}` (GitLab refs containing `/`); a step may carry an optional `method` that the linter accepts only as `GET` (`library_method`). (c) **Default-branch fast path**: without a `ref` the API asks for `HEAD` in the one `resolve_ref` call (both built-in hosts resolve it) and falls back to the `default_path` template, so an unchanged library costs one call. (d) **Refresh states**: `error` and `over_budget` libraries keep being refreshed (the 15 min → 1 h → 6 h backoff needs it; the job re-checks `over_budget` when space may be free); only `paused` stops; `last_head_sha` is the last *compared* head (an add of another commit clears it). (e) **`content_sha256`** is a length-prefixed SHA-256 of description, body and files (path, executable bit, bytes) for `changed`, and of a marker (+ reason + blob set) for `removed`/`invalid`, so an identical repository state hashes identically: that is how a rejection is remembered and a duplicate proposal avoided. (f) **At most 50 files per skill directory** (bounds the blob reads of an add; the skill total of 32 000 characters did not bound the count of empty files); a wrong-case `skill.md` beside `SKILL.md` is refused; repository text is stripped of control, bidi and zero-width characters (the body keeps its indentation) and `CRLF` becomes `LF`. (g) **Three small additive columns and one DTO field each**: `skill_libraries.refresh_failures` and `check_requested_at`, `skill_library_updates.bytes` (`03`); `serves_library` on the app DTOs and `library_host` on a plugin's apps so the dashboard can offer eligible connections and the permissions review can say what a `library` block does. `API_CONTRACTS.md` C-570..C-579. | Following a `Link` URL from the response (a redirect-like request line chosen by the host); a `raw` marker per file instead of base64; refreshing only `active` libraries (an erroring library would never recover) | Each item removes an ambiguity the first implementation hit while keeping D-149's guarantees: the request line is still built only from the approved templates and checked parameters. |
| D-143 | **P4-IDP-1: a mapped role claim admits a new user in `invite_only` and `sso_jit`** (coordinator, 2026-11-10; gap in D-139: the owner's first production sign-in has no invitation, `jit` block or listed e-mail). A new user is admitted when the validated claim **of the provider that signed them in** maps, under **that provider's** `role_mappings`, to at least one actual grant (`instance_admin`, an org role or a workspace role). Unmapped values, an absent claim, a mapping that grants nothing or a value mapped only under another provider never admit. Every other sign-in check applies first and unchanged (validated ID token, verified e-mail incl. D-118 `trusted_email_domains`, `allowed_email_domains`, disabled-user refusal); a mapping never bypasses them. `open` mode is unchanged. The admission is audited (`user.admit_by_claim`: provider id and matched values, never the token) in the user-creating transaction. Also recorded: claim-sourced rows are tracked on the rows themselves (`idp_role`/`idp_source`/`base_role`, `users.idp_admin_source`); the claim may only **raise** a manual role and the manual role is restored when the claim goes; a claim-sourced role cannot be edited or removed in the dashboard (409 `managed_by_idp`); `auth.instance_admins` is a manual source and wins over a claim. `04 §OIDC role claims`, `§Sign-up semantics`, C-516. | Claims never admit (the first administrator would need an invitation or the e-mail list, defeating D-139); any provider's mapping admitting (cross-provider escalation) | The operator who writes `role_mappings` already decides who administers the instance; admitting exactly the people the IdP gave an Aurora role keeps "the IdP is the source of truth" without widening who can enter. |
| D-156 | **IdP-linked groups** (owner, 2026-11-10: "CERN SSO gives group information too … we can rely on this coming from the IdP (e-groups)"; P4-GRP-1). A group may have `source = idp`: an org owner/admin links it to one provider and one claim value; at every sign-in through that provider the user's memberships in that provider's IdP-linked groups (orgs they are an active member of) are set from the validated claim (`group_claim`, `group_claim_source` with the P4-IDP-1 `sub`-binding rule); members are read-only (409 `managed_by_idp`); the sync never creates an org membership; freshness is "next sign-in" (residual, bounded by session limits). For CERN SSO the recommended claim is `cern_roles` (roles the application owner assigns to e-groups in the Application Portal; nested e-groups expanded by CERN); the `groups` claim also works once CERN enables it (ServiceNow request; CERN discourages it as the default, its value format is undocumented). Manual and SCIM groups stay. | Only manual groups (lists maintained twice); create a group automatically for every claim value (unbounded, names chosen by the IdP); live membership queries to the CERN Authorization Service API (phase 5 at the earliest: needs an API client and permissions) | E-groups are already the organization's source of truth; linking by an explicit admin choice keeps the set bounded and reviewable, and reusing the P4-IDP-1 claim extraction keeps one validated path for every claim. |
| D-158 | **FIX-025: a turn never hangs on "Sandbox starting" — end-to-end readiness deadline and a human reason for every start failure** (owner report from the production cluster, 2026-11-10: a turn was stuck on "Sandbox starting" for 20+ minutes; the pod was `Running` but the supervisor never connected to the runner, and no view named why). (a) **End-to-end deadline** `hosted.sandbox_ready_timeout_secs` (60–1800, default 300, `15`): from "a turn needs a sandbox" (the start is claimed; the runner's capacity wait counts inside it) to "the supervisor is ready", enforced by an API readiness sweep (10 s interval) that fails the attempt with a code naming where it stopped — `runner_unavailable` (no runner claimed the start), `pod_refused` (the cluster refused pod creation), `pod_failed` (the pod ended while starting), `home_volume_unbound` (the PVC never bound), `image_unavailable` (the image pull failed), `supervisor_unreachable` (pod running, no supervisor connection to the runner within `RUNNER_SUPERVISOR_HELLO` = 60 s, message names the runner endpoint and the Cilium hint), `supervisor_boot_failed` (connected, boot unfinished), and the existing `pod_refused`/`harness_unsupported` paths. Every failure ends the queued turns with a **human, sanitised reason** (no secrets, `aurora_core::sanitize::reason`) and a Computer-tab-visible start attempt (phase, detail, elapsed, pod Warning events, C-584). (b) **Start attempts are bounded**: automatic retries stop after 3 failures (`start_failures`); the next message retries once the backoff has passed. Failures are recorded per runner (`sandbox_start_failures`, pruned after 30 days) for Admin › Runners (`GET /v1/admin/runners/{id}/start-failures`, C-586). (c) **Visibility**: chat error blocks (`10 §Failure semantics`), the agent header pill ("Sandbox failed" + reason tooltip), the Computer tab's "Last start attempt" and the admin Runners page all show the same reason. (d) **Cilium**: the sandboxes-namespace NetworkPolicies match by pod identity (namespace + pod labels, Cilium-native), so the egress rule sandbox → runner :4105 in the chart needs no CiliumNetworkPolicy; the run evidence is in `15`/operator docs. `10 §Failure semantics`, `11 §Lifecycle`, `14`, `15`, `18`, C-580..C-589. | Keep "Sandbox starting" without a deadline and rely on pod events alone; a per-stage timeout without an end-to-end bound (a slow capacity wait + slow pull could exceed each stage but hang the turn); exposing raw kube error strings (secrets can leak into reasons) | The owner's incident had no deadline and no reason anywhere: the turn waited forever and every view said "starting". One end-to-end bound with per-path codes gives every failure a name and a place to look, and pod-identity NetworkPolicies keep the chart Cilium-compatible without a second policy kind. |
| D-159 | **Helm: `config.runner.image` defaults to the service registry/tag** (owner report from the production cluster, 2026-11-10: sandboxes never started because the default bare name `aurora-sandbox:<version>` resolves to `docker.io/library/aurora-sandbox`, which no registry hosts; the operator had to duplicate the registry/tag of `image.registry`/`image.tag` into `config.runner.image` by hand). The chart now derives `[runner] image` as `<image.registry>/aurora-sandbox:<image.tag>` when neither `runner.sandboxImage` (new) nor the legacy `config.runner.image` is set; `image.registry: ""` keeps rendering bare names (kind loads `aurora-sandbox:<tag>` into the nodes). The sandbox image is published under the same tag scheme as the services (CI `images-sandbox`, `type=sha`), so one tag bump retargets every component. | Keep the bare default and document the extra value (the first-install trap this decision fixes); template interpolation inside `values.yaml` (Helm values cannot reference each other) | One place names the registry for everything the cluster pulls; the kind path and the legacy key keep working; an explicit `runner.sandboxImage` stays for a genuinely different sandbox image. |
| D-161 | **P4-TERM: a web terminal into the agent's sandbox — wterm with the Ghostty core, over the preview-stream machinery** (owner, 2026-11-10: "build a web terminal interface to the agent's sandbox using ghostty wterm"). (a) **Frontend**: wterm (Apache-2.0) with its `@wterm/ghostty` core (libghostty compiled to WASM, 599,368 bytes, vendored under a version-named file with its sha256 recorded, and served same-origin) — DOM rendering keeps native selection, browser find and screen-reader support (the dashboard's a11y posture holds without reimplementing terminal accessibility), IME, OSC 8 links, alternate screen; a React tab on the agent page. The CSP gains `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` in `script-src` for WASM instantiation; nothing else loosens. (b) **Transport**: the P4-PRV-2 stream path reused, not forked — the API upgrades a same-origin WebSocket (`GET /v1/agents/{agent}/terminal`, session cookie, `can_use`), mints a single-use `auy_` grant (hashed in `terminal_stream_grants`, 30 s), redeems it at the runner's existing :4108 listener via the `preview-streams/open` route with `kind: "terminal"`, and the runner asks the supervisor over the existing W6 binary-frame extension with new `pty.*` frames (tag `0x02` beside W18's `0x01`); credit-based flow control, limits and the close-code table are W18's. (c) **Supervisor PTY**: the image shell in a PTY as the agent user, cwd `/workspace`, environment minus every `AURORA_*` variable and the boot document (SEC-01/SEC-34); resize by `TIOCSWINSZ`; never a turn, never a transcript event. (d) **Posture**: operator opt-in (`terminal.enabled`, default off, like previews), dashboard origin only (no relay, no previews domain, no org toggle), frozen/parked semantics mirror previews (wake on open, keep-awake reason `terminal` capped 4 h), bytes never logged, audit records opens only, no agent tool can touch a terminal (SEC-87). `14`, `11 §Web terminal`, `12 §Terminal`, `16 §W19`, `15`, `18`, C-616..C-625. | xterm.js (canvas: no native selection/find/screen-reader support, reimplemented a11y); a relay/domain path (an interactive shell does not need the preview origin isolation, and the relay's session model is share-shaped); an exec API with polling instead of a PTY (no TUI, no resize, terrible latency); letting agents open terminals (a shell is people's window into the sandbox, an agent already has one); SSH as the transport (P4-SSH-2 moved to phase 5, D-138 — and the certificate machinery buys nothing for a same-origin dashboard surface) | The stream path P4-PRV-2 already specifies is a general sandbox byte channel: its grant → :4108 → W6-frames spine carries a PTY as well as a TCP port with one new frame kind, one new grant kind and one redemption field; wterm's DOM rendering is the only emulator approach that meets the dashboard's accessibility bar without new work; operator opt-in keeps "interactive shell into sandboxes" a deliberate deployment choice like previews. |
| D-160 | **FIX-029: retry a failed sandbox start; a start log and per-agent start-failure history** *[(a) amended by D-164(b)]* (owner feedback on D-158's shape, 2026-11-10: "there's no way to retry without deleting the agent … better logs somewhere on what's going on, at least for the admin"). (a) **`POST /v1/agents/{agent}/sandbox/retry`** (same authority as restart, `can_use`; only from `failed`, 409 `sandbox_not_failed` otherwise or while a lifecycle lease is live, 423 `frozen`): clears the failure fields, **resets `start_failures` to 0** (the C-583 bound exists to stop failure storms, not to gate recovery after an operator fix), claims the ordinary lifecycle lease, appends a retry note to the start log, best-effort audit `agent.sandbox_retry`, and notifies due work — the retried start is the **ordinary due-work start arm** with a fresh `boot_id`, bounded by the same readiness deadline (C-580); no new work-item kind, no second boot-minting path. In chat, Retry now re-posts the failed turn's text as a new message after the API call (the failed turn stays failed). (b) **A per-boot start log**: `sandboxes.start_log`, a bounded jsonb ring (30 entries, 8 KiB, oldest dropped, sanitised at write, reset by every boot) fed by the fenced phase reports, the start failures and the retry note, served in `SandboxView.start_log` and rendered on the Computer tab — a live trail of a starting sandbox and a frozen record of a failed one, neither of which C-582's last-attempt fields keep. (c) **Per-agent history from the existing rows**: `GET /v1/agents/{agent}/start-failures` (`can_use`, 20 rows) and `GET /v1/admin/agents/{agent}/start-failures` (IA, with `runner_id`) read `sandbox_start_failures` (C-587) — no new table, retention unchanged; Admin › Runners links each failure to the agent's history. `10 §Failure semantics`, `11 §Start readiness deadline`, `05`, `14`, `03`, C-610..C-615. | Deleting and recreating the agent (loses conversations, sessions and grants to work around a transient failure); auto-retrying unboundedly (what C-583 deliberately stops); a new work-item kind or API-side boot minting (two start paths to keep fenced); streaming sandbox stdout (credentials and unbounded noise; the pod events + supervisor reports are the diagnostics) | Retry is an operator/owner action that resets a deliberate circuit breaker, so it must exist as an explicit, audited action that goes through the one fenced start path; the start log turns the single stored phase into a trail an admin can read without kubectl, and the per-agent history reuses the table FIX-025 already writes. |
| D-162 | **FIX-045 (phase 5): the personal-workspace name template defaults to the OIDC-unique `sub`** (owner, 2026-11-11: "preferred_username is not guaranteed to be unique. It's best to use whatever field oidc says identifies a user uniquely"). (a) `{sub}` becomes a first-class template variable beside `{preferred_username}` (C-523; rendered at placement time from the validated ID token; `user_identities.subject` already persists it, unique `(provider_id, subject)` — no new storage). (b) The default template, the unsaved C-520 view and the wizard's recommendation become **`{sub}|{email_local}`** (`preferred_username` is a display name: providers recycle aliases, users rename themselves, two providers can both have "bob"). (c) The fallback chain stays a *missing-value* mechanism (D-151(a)): a collision never falls through to the next alternative — that remains SEC-79's numbered-slug business, unchanged; a chain with `preferred_username` before `sub` would never reach `sub`. (d) The admin preview resolves `{sub}` from the caller's stored identity row (lowest `provider_id` when several) and leaves it unresolved in the typed-sample path; no new claims retention (D-151(b) stands). (e) Nothing migrates: existing workspaces, slugs and saved templates stay; a null-template placement silently starts naming new personal workspaces from `sub`. Admins who prefer readable names keep typing `{preferred_username}` or `{claim:cern_upn}`. | A configurable "unique claim" setting (OIDC defines the unique field; `{claim:…}` covers exotic IdPs); migrating or renaming existing workspaces (a personal workspace is created once, C-524); a collision-aware chain (mixes two mechanisms — missing-value fallback and SEC-79's collision policy) | Honors the owner's rule — name from what the IdP guarantees unique — at the cost of one variable, one default and one recommendation line; readable names stay one keystroke away, and SEC-79 keeps every template safe. |
| D-250 | **P5-ASRC: addon sources — third-party app-package repositories** (owner brief, 2026-11-10, renumbered from the WIP's D-164/D-159 on 2026-11-14, brief `docs/tasks/P5-ASRC.md`). (a) **Trust model**: an addon source is a git repository publishing a versioned manifest (`aurora-addons.yaml`, `api: 1`, strict parse, ≤ 1 MiB over https) whose `signing_key` (base64 ed25519) is the **trust anchor**: registration is TOFU — the admin sees a review screen (repo info, publisher, the signing-key fingerprint prominently, the addon list) and confirming creates the source row **and a `trusted_keys` row** for the publisher's key in one audited transaction, so verification composes with the existing machinery (`09 §2`); a manifest whose key differs from the registered fingerprint never auto-trusts — new versions are refused, the source is flagged `signing_key_changed`, and an admin must re-confirm (old + new fingerprint shown, the `trusted_keys` row updated, audited). Package signatures always verify against the manifest-published key through that `trusted_keys` row — a repo cannot silently ship packages signed by a different key. (b) **Packages are ordinary packages**: ingested through the existing pipeline with `source = "addon"`, `source_id = <addon_sources row>`; addon permissions always require instance-admin approval (never auto-activated on first sight, unlike `builtin`); updates never auto-activate (the ordinary version-activation rules run on click: identical/narrower activates, widened or major goes through the existing review, C-343/P4-GRANTS holds apply); versions removed upstream never touch installed packages (listed "removed upstream"); `min_core` above the running core lists the addon but does not ingest it (reason shown). (c) **Polling**: one instance setting `plugins.addon_sources.update_interval` with exactly the values `6h`, `12h`, `1d`, `1w`, `2w`, `1mo`, `never` (default `1d`; `never` = manual only) — one setting for all sources, no per-source override in v1. The background job claims one source at a time under a per-source lease (`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, the P4-GSL pattern), re-fetches the manifest, compares digests, and ingests every version newer than the newest ingested one; any error (unreachable, parse, bad signature, digest mismatch) records `check_failed_at` + a sanitized reason and changes nothing else — last known state keeps serving. The poll path is shared with the manual buttons: `POST /v1/admin/addon-sources/{id}/check` (one source) and `POST /v1/admin/addon-sources/check-all` run exactly this path with `trigger = manual|scheduled` in the `addon_source.check` audit row, lease-protected so a manual click during a scheduled run never double-fetches; both are synchronous with per-fetch timeouts, a hanging source fails its own check and never blocks the others. (d) **Fetch guard (SEC-86)**: manifest and package fetches are https-only through the API's existing outbound guard (`06 §Destination guard`: public addresses only, no IP literals/loopback/link-local, timeouts); the manifest URL is fetched **without** redirects (it is a raw file); package URLs follow **at most one** redirect (GitHub release-asset CDN pattern), still https, still public — the redirect target is not re-judged by the destination policy beyond that check; the manifest digest checked before parse and the package digest checked before parse remain the integrity anchors; packages ≤ `PACKAGE_MAX` (20 MiB). (e) **Removal**: refused while packages of the source are active; otherwise packages stay and polling stops (stated in the confirm). (f) **Limits**: `ADDON_MANIFEST_MAX` 1 MiB, `ADDON_ADDONS_MAX` 100, `ADDON_VERSIONS_MAX` 50 per addon; the interval enum and default `1d` are normative. `03 §addon_sources`, `05 §Instance administration`, `09 §10`, `14 §Admin`, `15`, `17` SEC-86, `22-addon-repos.md`, C-590..C-609. | Trusting packages by URL (a moved or re-pointed URL would mint arbitrary packages); per-source schedules (one setting is enough for v1); auto-activating updates (updates can widen permissions); auto-trusting a changed signing key (a compromised or transferred repo would mint arbitrary packages); fetching packages without the digest-first rule (a partial or spoofed body could reach the parser) | The manifest-published key plus TOFU registration and audited re-confirm keeps third-party trust explicit and reversible; the shared, lease-protected poll path makes manual and scheduled checks the same audited code; reusing `trusted_keys` and the existing ingest/approval pipeline means addon packages are governed by exactly the rules every other package already has. |
| D-251 | **P5-CERN-ADDONS: the gateway LDAP lane** (owner D-235(d), 2026-11-14: "LDAP through a gateway lane"; brief `docs/tasks/P5-CERN-ADDONS.md`). (a) **Shape**: a package declares an `ldap` block on an app — `{ server, port (636), base_dn, attributes[], max_entries (50, ≤ 500) }` — and exactly one `hosts[]` entry for the synthetic host `<app id>.ldap.aurora.internal` (the one exception to the reserved-host rule, SEC-16) with no injection and no declared credentials. Its tools are ordinary tools on that host (`POST /search`), so grants, rules, approvals, rate limits and the request log work unchanged. (b) **The lane**: an admitted request is not forwarded; the gateway runs **one LDAP search** against the approved server and answers JSON. LDAPS only, the server certificate verified against the configured name (system roots + `gateway.extra_ca`), the dial through the destination guard (SEC-06) or the egress proxy, an **anonymous** simple bind (no credential exists to send), then the search, then an unbind. The client is hand-written BER limited to those three operations, so **no write operation can be expressed** — the lane is read-only by construction, not by filtering. (c) **Confinement**: the request body is `{ filter, attributes?, scope? (sub|one|base), base?, limit? }` (≤ 16 KiB, unknown keys refused); the filter is an RFC 4515 string parsed strictly (≤ 1 KiB, depth 8, 64 terms, no extensible match) whose attributes must all be on the app's list (a filter on an unlisted attribute is an oracle for it); the requested attributes are a subset of the list and only they are returned; `base` must be the configured base DN or below it (escaped commas never count as separators); aliases are never dereferenced, referrals never followed; at most `max_entries` entries come back with `truncated`; values that are not UTF-8 or over 4 KiB are dropped; the directory's own diagnostic text never reaches the agent. (d) **A grant, not open egress**: the lane answers only a request that won a **granted connection** of that app over TLS to the claimed port; anything else on an LDAP host answers `403 ldap_no_connection` and reaches no directory. (e) **Approval**: the `ldap` block is part of the permissions an instance admin approves; a new block, another server/port/base, a wider attribute list or a higher cap is a widening (`Widening::Directory`, shown as "can read directory entries from <server>:<port> under <base>"), a narrower block is not. (f) **Tool granularity**: the policy catalogue is one read tool (`search`); person/manager/e-group lookups are recipes in the package's agent instructions (a manager chain is one search per hop, at most five), because a gateway tool executes exactly one directory operation. (g) `06 §LDAP lane`, `09 §3 ldap`, SEC-110, C-760..C-763. | The sandbox-side `ldap_search` binary (brief's fallback: the sandbox would need a network path to the directory and could speak any LDAP operation, so the gateway could not promise read-only); filtering write operations out of a general LDAP client (a denylist can miss an operation or a control); a manifest `hosts[]` entry for the real directory host (it would turn the directory into an HTTP host the agent could dial directly); returning every attribute the server sends (a server-side default can include credentials such as `userPassword` hashes) | A fixed, tiny BER client plus a strict filter grammar makes "read-only" a property of the code, not of a policy; reusing the HTTP pipeline for classification keeps grants, approvals, rate limits and logging identical; binding the lane to a granted connection keeps the capability explicit and auditable. |
| D-163 | **FIX-046 (phase 5): shell alignment corrections from the owner's 390 px screenshot** (owner, 2026-11-11). (a) The two stacked selectors align: "the Workspaces drop-down doesn't need to be invented under the organization one, the hierarchy is clear enough as is", clarified on this brief's first draft ("Do NOT delete the workspace selector!! Simply don't indent it visually in the menu, have it span the same width as the organization selector") — FIX-027's stacked pair stays, the Workspace selector loses the `pl-8` indent (caption and control), nesting conveyed by order and caption, not indentation. (b) The narrow-screen drawer opens from the side its button is on: "the hamburger menu is on the right, but it opens the panel on the left. This makes no sense" — the top bar keeps the button top-right and the panel switches `left-0` → `right-0`. | Removing the workspace selector (the owner wants it); moving the button to the left (the button's place is right); a wider shell restructure | Geometry should match the shell's own cues: equal-width controls under each other, a flyout under its trigger. |
| D-166 | **FIX-052: the gateway's upstream hop must never carry a `Host` header next to `:authority`** (owner incident 2026-11-12: with gateway auth repaired (FIX-050), every LLM call still failed `400 Bad Request` — `request_log` showed `decision=allow, injected=1, upstream_status=400` with no gateway error, and the body was an **nginx HTML page** from `aigw.cern.ch`, not litellm JSON; identical rows from 2026-11-10 19:51 prove the endpoint never worked through Aurora — the failure was hidden behind the FIX-050 407 wall). Root cause proven by reproduction against the live endpoint: `Upstream::send` unconditionally re-inserts `Host` (`upstream.rs:311-323`), so on an ALPN-h2 hop the wire carries `:authority: host:443` **and** `host: host` — RFC 9113 §8.3.1 forbids the pair, nginx (trac #2268) rejects it with a bare 400; hyper never strips it (its h2 strip list is connection headers only) and rust's h2 encodes what it is given (python-h2 refuses the same block client-side, which is why only rust clients ship it). hyper-util synthesizes `Host` on h1 hops with byte-identical semantics (default ports dropped), so the manual insert was redundant there and fatal on h2. Fix: delete the insert; refuse a `host`-targeting injection header at snapshot load (it cannot work on h2); pin both protocols with a raw-h2 upstream it-test (the old mock was hyper, which accepts the pair — that is why every test passed). `06 §Per-request pipeline` step 13, C-633. (Task file renumbered FIX-050 → FIX-052: the owner's merged test-only runner-streams commit had already taken FIX-051.) | Fixing it only for h1 (the manual insert was *only* ever needed by h1); a config key to toggle the header instead of conforming to RFC 9113; trusting hyper-based mock upstreams to catch wire-shape bugs (they are lenient where nginx is strict) | A proxy must not add headers the protocol forbids: the request that reaches the upstream is the request the agent sent plus exactly the declared injections — nothing the transport invented. |
| D-165 | **FIX-050: the IdP claim sync never leaves an organization without an active owner, and gateway CONNECT-auth refusals are observable** (owner incident 2026-11-12: every LLM call from every sandbox failed `ERR_PROXY_TUNNEL`; root cause an org created by the claim sync on 2026-11-10 with **zero active owners** — `ensure_org` removes `create_organization`'s bootstrap owner unconditionally and the mapping granted only `member`, audit `member.add … "created_org": true`; the gateway's credential lookup requires an active owner, so every CONNECT of the org was refused 407 with no log line and no request-log row, while message delivery kept working). (a) **Owner-integrity floor**: after the sync's org step, an org an org-role mapping of the provider touched that ends the sign-in transaction with no active owner gets its syncing user promoted to `owner` (claim-derived `idp_role`/`idp_source` retained; audited `member.update` with `reason: "ownerless_org"`; suspended/absent members are never promoted; the existing last-owner guard then protects the floor and the claim dissolves it once it grants a real owner). (b) **Visibility**: `GET /v1/admin/overview` reports `ownerless_orgs` and the Admin dashboard warns; operator docs carry the one-UPDATE repair. (c) **Observability**: every CONNECT-auth refusal logs `connect_auth_refused` with a reason class (`unknown | revoked | expired | no_active_owner | frozen | store_unavailable`), credential kind and source address — never token material — plus metric `aurora_gateway_connect_refused_total{reason}`; refusal answers unchanged. `04 §OIDC role claims`, `06 §Credential authentication`, `14`, C-630. | Trusting the IdP mapping to include an owner (a typo silently kills every sandbox's egress); auto-promoting a suspended member; logging credential material to make auth failures debuggable; a second org-creation path (the floor is a post-sync invariant, not a change to `create_organization`) | An org whose owner comes from data cannot lose it to the same data; the state that silently breaks every sandbox must be either impossible or loud; and a refusal that took an afternoon of SQL to diagnose takes one grep once it is logged. |
| D-164 | **FIX-049: a transient PVC provisioning race must not fail the start, and Retry now/Restart must work from `failed`** (owner report from the production cluster, 2026-11-12: every start failed `home_volume_unbound` with `ProvisioningFailed: Error saving claim: … the object has been modified`, yet the claim was `Bound` one minute after the runner gave up — the in-tree persistentvolume-controller lost one optimistic-concurrency race stamping the provisioner annotation; the manila CSI provisioner recovered it). (a) **The runner's fail-fast distinguishes a bind failure from a slow provisioner**: `FailedBinding` always fails fast (never recovers by waiting); a `ProvisioningFailed` warning fails fast only when no Normal `Provisioning`/`ProvisioningSucceeded` event supersedes it (same or newer timestamp) **and** it is older than `PVC_PROVISION_GRACE` (90 s); otherwise the start keeps waiting with `provisioning` progress, bounded by the readiness deadline as always. The failure message names the claim's actual storage class when it pins one, and the `runner.k8s.storageClass`/cluster-default hint is appended only when the claim has no explicit class. (b) **An explicit Retry now overrides the failure-backoff lease** (amends D-160(a)): the backoff stops *automatic* failure storms (C-583), not human recovery after an operator fix, and a live lease on a `failed` row fences no in-flight start (an in-flight start leaves the row `starting`) — `retry_failed_start` drops its lease-live 409. (c) **Restart from `failed` performs the same recovery** through the C-610 path (423 `frozen` included, audit `via: "restart"`), no second start path; from `stopped`/`unprovisioned`/`stopping` it answers 409 `restart_not_applicable` naming the state instead of a bare 202. The Computer tab's Restart button is disabled where the API would answer 409 and enabled on `failed`. `11 §Start readiness deadline`, `10 §Failure semantics`, `05 §Agents and sandboxes`, `14`, docs/operator/runners.md §Troubleshooting, C-610 amended, C-626. | Treating every `ProvisioningFailed` as fatal (fails every first start on clusters whose provisioner races); a visible recovery button that 409s for the whole backoff window; a Restart that 202s and does nothing | A warning event is evidence, not a verdict — the claim's own newer events and a short grace separate "will not bind" from "binding"; the retry bound is deliberately bypassable by the person it exists to help, and Restart must either act or refuse honestly. |
| D-167 | **FIX-062: SCIM updates never move a link to another account** (code review 2026-11-13: `apply_update` resolved a new `userName` to another global user — or inserted a new pre-provisioned one — and re-pointed the link, so the person who had signed in kept an active, unlinked membership and a later IdP suspend/delete deprovisioned the other user). On `PUT`/`PATCH` of a SCIM user the link's `user_id` is immutable. (a) If the linked user has an identity or a verified e-mail ("signed-in") — or the membership is an owner's — a new `userName`/primary e-mail is stored on the link only; `users.email`/`display_name` are untouched (`04 §SCIM` Updates, SEC-73). (b) If the linked user is a still-unbound pre-provisioned user (no identity, e-mail unverified): if the new e-mail is free globally **and the placeholder has no membership and no `scim_users` link (any state) in any other org**, `users.email` is updated in place; otherwise — the e-mail belongs to another global user, or the placeholder is shared with another org — the link is moved exactly like a `POST` would link it: to that user, or to a **new** pre-provisioned user created like a `POST` (adopt/insert its membership, seat cap; `active: false` through the C-474 path); only this org's membership of the placeholder is removed (its SCIM-group memberships follow the link) and the placeholder's `users` row is **never deleted or changed** (it stays an unbound, membership-less pre-provisioned user; SCIM never deletes a user, SEC-73). (c) Consequences: a `POST` that revives a tombstone keeps the tombstone's pinned `user_id` (no global e-mail re-resolution; the link's `userName` may differ from `users.email`); deprovisioning cancels this org's pending invitations addressed to the link's `userName` or the account's `users.email`. Org-level uniqueness of `userName` (incl. FIX-040's tombstone-pinned 409) stays as is; FIX-036's adoption arm survives only for case (b). `04 §SCIM` Updates, C-473, SEC-73. | Re-resolving the link by every asserted e-mail (deprovisioning then misses the real person; an IdP could re-point a link onto anyone with an address in its domains); deleting the orphaned placeholder (contradicts SEC-73 "SCIM never deletes a user row" and would cascade other orgs' tombstones away); renaming a placeholder another org also links (a cross-org write) | The link is the org's handle on one person: once the person signed in, the IdP's e-mail is directory data on that handle, never a pointer to re-aim; only a placeholder nobody ever used can follow its address, and then only within the org that asserts it. |
| D-170 | **Code Mode is not adopted; its two transferable ideas become "app guides on demand"** (owner question and decision, 2026-11-13; `24 §1`). Code Mode (Anthropic "Code execution with MCP", Cloudflare, UTCP `code-mode`, Skarn) removes the cost of MCP tool definitions loaded up front and of intermediate results passing through the model, by letting the model discover tools on demand and call them from a sandbox. Aurora apps are not MCP tools: an app is a declarative package, the agent calls the app's HTTP API from its own sandbox and the gateway injects the credential, so an Aurora agent already executes code against APIs with intermediate data kept out of the context. What is adopted: **progressive disclosure** of what an agent is told about an app (a folder per app in the home, one line in the instruction file) and **result discipline** (one preamble paragraph). Measured 2026-11-13: plugin notes of the 23 built-in apps are 465–1 369 chars (mean 743), about 250 tokens per connected app per session (estimate at 4 chars/token); the platform's own MCP catalogue is about 35 tools, an estimated 2 000–3 000 tokens, and stays as it is. | Embedding Skarn (an MCP gateway with a QuickJS isolate and `search`/`read_tool_docs`/`execute` meta-tools: no downstream MCP servers exist here; its own `SECURITY.md` puts hostile multi-tenant isolation out of scope; essentially one maintainer, v1.0.1 of 2026-06-24 and no later commit visible on 2026-11-13; its "compression" is lossy filtering of shell output, unrelated to apps); another JavaScript isolate in the supervisor or the API; converting platform tools to a code API; doing nothing | A second sandbox inside the sandbox adds attack surface and removes nothing, because apps have no tool definitions or servers; the token saving of the adopted part is modest today and grows with addon sources and large APIs, which cannot be described in `AGENT_SNIPPET_MAX` at all. |
| D-171 | **Agent guide design** *[(a), (b), (f), (g) amended by D-453 (owner, 2026-11-22): apps ship skills, the guide files and recipes are removed]* (P5-GUIDE-1..4; `24`, SEC-117, SEC-91, C-640..C-649). (a) **Files in the home, not tools and not skills**: `.aurora/apps/<app_id>/` with `guide.md` and `tools.md` written by Aurora and the package's reference files; agents search it with `rg`. (b) **Additive package format**: `agent.summary`, `agent.guide[]` and `agent/<app_id>/<file>` (UTF-8 text, bounded), `agent.instructions` kept as the essentials. (c) **Inline or index by one threshold**: the instruction file is unchanged while the notes of all connected apps total at most `AGENT_NOTES_INLINE_MAX`, and lists one line per app above it. *[(c) amended by D-295 (owner, 2026-11-15): always the index; the threshold is removed.]* (d) **Access from the grant row**, not from the effective view. (e) **Guide text is not a permission**: a change does not need re-approval, but is shown in the review and audited. (f) **One renderer** in `aurora-registry`, used by the API and by `aurora plugin guide`. (g) Home sync resends guide files like skills, bounded by `AGENT_GUIDE_SYNC_MAX`. | (a) A `search_apps`/`get_app_guide` platform tool (two more always-loaded tool definitions and a round trip through the control plane for static text); harness skills (harness-specific, `codex` may have none, loading decided by the harness, and account lists must not depend on that). (c) Always index (every agent with two small apps pays a file read per app and conversation for no saving); per-app thresholds (does not bound the total). (d) The effective view (depends on the speaker; policy publication does not resync homes; a stale "allowed" from policy would mislead more than a documented grant-level list). (e) Re-approval on every documentation fix (third-party updates through addon sources are already manual; the text is labelled untrusted data and grants no capability). (g) Digest-based skipping (state on both sides for a bounded cost; deferred) | Works for every harness with no new wire verb beyond one sync part kind, keeps small agents exactly as they are, gives authors room for a real reference, and shows an agent what it may do before a request is refused, without creating a second source of authorization truth. |
| D-172 | **Large APIs and MCP-only apps: direction recorded, not built** (owner question, 2026-11-13; `24 §10`, `§12`). (a) For large HTTP APIs (Zoom, OpenSearch, OpenStack) the *search* half of Code Mode is the agent guide (an operation index plus per-area reference files, generated by `aurora plugin import-openapi`, P5-GUIDE-5) and the *execute* half is the agent's sandbox; nothing else is needed. (b) An app whose **only** interface is an MCP server is the one case where Code Mode proper applies; its shape is a gateway tool classifier for JSON-RPC `tools/call` beside `graphql` (so grants and approvals see the MCP tool name), the server's tool schemas projected into the app's folder, and an in-sandbox client so the agent calls those tools from code while the harness never loads the server's tool list. It needs its own specification and a first real app without an HTTP API. (c) OpenStack additionally needs endpoints on several hosts and ports per cloud, which `instances` (one DNS name, no port, `09 §3.4`) cannot express; that is a package-model change to specify with that app, not part of the guide. | Attaching third-party MCP servers to the harness (every tool definition in every session, and policy would see only `POST /mcp`); building the MCP lane now without an app that needs it | Keeps this phase small and makes sure the folder layout is the discovery surface a later MCP lane would also use. |
| D-175 | **FIX-082 (REVIEW-5, owner 2026-11-13, Q-R5-1): SCIM never makes an existing account an active member on an asserted e-mail.** `04 §SCIM` step 2(b)/3 linked any existing account by the e-mail the IdP posts and created an **active** membership at once, while SEC-73 says a person is linked only through the OIDC-verified e-mail; on an instance with several organizations an org owner who lists a domain could pull any account on that domain into their org. Rule: (a) when a SCIM `POST` (or a `PUT`/`PATCH` that moves a link, D-167(b)) resolves to an existing account that is **not already a member of the org**, the link is created and the membership is created with status **`pending`** (role `member`): it counts against the seat cap, can be placed in SCIM groups, and confers nothing — every effective-access view, key, grant, group-derived access and gateway identity treats `pending` like a non-member. (b) It becomes `active` in the sign-in transaction of that person's **next verified OIDC sign-in** (any provider of the instance; the account is the proof, the link's e-mail is not compared again), audited `member.add` with `via: "scim"` at that moment; no activation ever happens without a sign-in. (c) `active: false` from the IdP, or `DELETE`, before activation removes the pending membership (nothing to deprovision). (d) An account that is already a member is adopted exactly as today (D-135, C-473); a newly created pre-provisioned user keeps the existing rule (active membership that can do nothing until its first sign-in binds it). (e) SCIM responses report the IdP's own `active` value (the directory's intent), and the Directory view shows "Waiting for first sign-in". `04 §SCIM` Users, C-473, SEC-73; `org_members.status` gains `pending` with FIX-082's migration (`03` is updated in the same change). | Verified domains assigned to an org by the operator (every multi-org install needs an operator step; a domain shared by two orgs is unrepresentable); keep immediate adoption and document it (leaves SEC-73 false) | The verified sign-in is the only evidence Aurora has that the person and the account are the same; a directory's assertion can prepare a membership, never grant one. |
| D-176 | **FIX-098 (REVIEW-5, owner 2026-11-13, Q-R5-2): the dashboard's CSP allows inline style *attributes* so the terminal can render colours** (amends D-161 and C-622's "nothing else loosens"). The Terminal tab renders through wterm's DOM renderer, which sets each coloured run's and wide cell's style as a `style="…"` attribute; the production policy `style-src 'self' 'nonce-…'` drops those, so the terminal had no colours outside development. The policy gains exactly **`style-src-attr 'unsafe-inline'`**: `<style>` elements and stylesheets stay bound to `'self'` and the nonce (`style-src-elem` falls back to `style-src`), scripts are untouched. A style attribute cannot run script; with `img-src 'self' data:` and `connect-src 'self'` it cannot fetch from another origin either. A Playwright project that runs the production build asserts the header and a coloured cell. `14` §Security headers, §Terminal tab; C-622. | Patch or wrap the renderer to apply styles through CSSOM (a maintained fork of a vendored dependency for no security gain); scope the directive to the terminal route (the dashboard is one document — a client-side navigation keeps the first policy); accept a monochrome terminal | The loosening is the narrowest one that makes the specified feature work, and it is stated instead of being discovered in production. |
| D-177 | **FIX-105 (REVIEW-5, owner 2026-11-13, Q-R5-4): the inference-path restriction of SEC-65 applies to `litellm` and `openai_compatible` endpoints now, in phase 4** (amends D-138, which moved all of P4-LLM to phase 5). LiteLLM endpoints have been in production since FIX-019, and without the restriction the sanctioned key is injected on every path of the endpoint host, including LiteLLM's management API and model-invoking routes the allow-list never sees. For these two endpoint kinds the gateway injects the key only on the endpoint's inference path set (the six routes of `08 §Providers` plus an operator-declared `allowed_paths` list per endpoint); any other path is refused 403 `llm_path_not_permitted` before policy and before any credential is opened, and on a model-invoking route outside the set the model allow-list fails closed. The other providers' path sets, the Vertex and Bedrock clauses and the rest of P4-LLM stay in phase 5. `17` SEC-65, `08 §Providers`, `15` (endpoint keys). | Keep it in phase 5 and tell operators never to use a key with management rights (a documentation control on a credential boundary) | The boundary is cheap to enforce for the one provider family that is live, and the alternative depends on every operator reading a warning. |
| D-178 | **REVIEW-5 open questions answered (owner, 2026-11-13: "all remaining recommendations are accepted").** Each of Q-R5-3 and Q-R5-5..Q-R5-23 (`PROGRESS.md` §Decisions needed from the owner) is decided as its row recommends: (Q-R5-3) skill-library browse/add accept only the library ref's current head and heads recorded by refresh → FIX-096; (5) start-failure members' view = failure class + hint, events/detail for instance admins and workspace owners → FIX-093; (6) approving a proposal of a paused library answers 409, rejecting stays possible → FIX-096; (7) database-lane `sslmode=require` verifies against system roots by default, explicit per-connection opt-out for a private CA without `ca_pem` → FIX-106; (8) one connector-host token per host, bound to the connector types it announced → FIX-120; (9) channel approval cards show title, agent, rule and expiry only; details on the dashboard or by DM to approvers → FIX-112; (10) admin Runners gains capacity display and token rotation, provisioning stays in Helm/CLI and `14` says so → FIX-121; (11) the evaluation Postgres creates the gateway's least-privilege role automatically, an external database refuses to render without it unless an explicit shared-login value is set → FIX-106; (12) per-provider `link_existing_users`, off by default for providers relying on `trusted_email_domains` → FIX-108; (13) D-165(a) kept (an ownerless org is worse); the promotion is visible in the audit log and in the admin overview once that page exists; (14) a blocked response names workspace rules, answers generically for org rules → FIX-104; (15) the owner checks `_sqlx_migrations` on the production cluster and, if it ran an earlier numbering, a repair step goes into `docs/operator/upgrades.md` before the next upgrade; (16) CLI-minted keys expire after 90 days by default, configurable → FIX-108; (17) BYO defaults kept, no change; (18) Indico's connect form offers `read:everything` and the specific write scopes only → FIX-118; (19) server stays permissive for directory-managed members' roles, the dashboard disables the control with an explanation → FIX-100; (20) replay protection of signed webhooks belongs to the sender (documented); optional signed-timestamp checking later → FIX-110; (21) secret origin and creator returned to org admins only → FIX-115;
