(22) `agent.terminal.close` audit (close code, duration, never bytes) → FIX-092; (23) one schema-hardening migration (composite workspace/org FKs, append-only `audit_log`) in phase 5. | Leaving the questions open and building to the briefs' current text | The briefs already name each follow-up; deciding all at once lets the lanes carry them without stopping. |
| D-180 | **FIX-104 (REVIEW-5, coordinator decision, 2026-11-13): when a credential is injected.** (a) A **package host claim** (an app's `hosts[].match`) or a generic secret's host pattern injects only into a request that leaves **over TLS to the claimed port** (443 when the claim names none); plain HTTP or another port is an uncredentialed request to that destination, decided by policy as such. (b) An **operator-configured endpoint** (an LLM endpoint's `base_url`, `litellm_base`, any instance-configured upstream) injects only to the **exact scheme, host and port the operator configured**: plain `http://` in-cluster is legitimate there because the operator chose it; any other scheme or port of that host is uncredentialed. (c) Tests only: `gateway.insecure_loopback_injection` (default `false`) relaxes (a) when the destination is a loopback address (`127.0.0.0/8`, `::1`, `localhost`); the Helm chart never renders it (`config.gateway.*` is refused too, `no-deployment-defaults` guard), the gateway refuses it inside a Kubernetes cluster, and `15`/`23` say it is test-only. Gateway ITs run with it, at least one without it to prove (a). Spec: `06 §Plain HTTP`, `09 §7`, SEC-04/SEC-01/SEC-17. | Plain HTTP and any port for claimed hosts; refusing in-cluster `http://` LLM endpoints | A claim names a host the package author controls, so only TLS to the claimed port protects the secret; an operator's own endpoint is the operator's decision. |
| D-179 | **FIX-080 (REVIEW-5): every path that creates an `org_members` row passes the org's SCIM admission gate and seat cap.** `04 §SCIM` named the gate and the cap for JIT and invitations only; IdP role-claim sync and new-user placement inserted memberships with neither (a person the directory removed or suspended, or never provisioned into an `exclusive` org, was re-admitted at the next sign-in). Rule: invitation acceptance, JIT, new-user placement and role-claim sync decide under the org lock the SCIM writers take, with the membership re-read under it; a refusal is a denied `member.add` row (`path`, `reason`) written on its own connection, and never fails the sign-in itself — except JIT's directory refusal, which keeps failing it with `scim_refused` (JIT is then the only way in). A refused invitation stays pending (403 `scim_refused`; seat cap 422). SCIM sets `suspended_by_scim` only when it is the one suspending, so a human suspension is never lifted by the IdP. | Failing the sign-in on a refused role claim or placement; leaving placement and claims ungated | A person with a valid account whose org membership is refused still signs in (there may be other orgs); the audit row is what the admin needs. |
| D-181 | **FIX-106 (REVIEW-5, coordinator decision, 2026-11-13): gateway transport hardening choices.** (a) **Tunnel bounds** (`06 §Tunnel limits`, `02 §Limits`): inner TLS handshake and open-lane dial 10 s, request head inside a tunnel 30 s, idle limit for every tunnel kind 600 s (the WebSocket value; a MITM tunnel is idle only with no bytes **and** no request in flight, so a request held for approval is never cut), 64 concurrent tunnels per credential and 4 096 per gateway (the next `CONNECT` is 429 `rate_limited`, `retry-after: 5`). Constants in `aurora_core::limits`, injectable through `ByoParts.tunnel_limits` for tests only (no configuration key: these are abuse bounds, not tuning). (b) **Own addresses**: a listener bound to the wildcard covers loopback, the unspecified address and every address of the host's own interfaces, read once at start-up from `/proc/net/fib_trie` and `/proc/net/if_inet6` (no `unsafe`, no new dependency; where unreadable the list is empty and the previous behaviour remains). (c) **One range table** (`aurora_core::ranges`, `06 §Destination guard`) serves the gateway guard and the API probe guard; an address that embeds an IPv4 address (mapped, NAT64, 6to4) is judged by the embedded address, `::/96`, Teredo, site-local, local-use NAT64 and the benchmarking, IETF-protocol and reserved IPv4 blocks are refused outright, and an IPv4 `allowed_private` CIDR never opens a translated form of its range. (d) **Egress-proxy mode**: the local name check resolves the name with the system resolver and refuses when **any** resolved address is refused, before the proxy is contacted; a name that does not resolve locally at all is left to the proxy (split-horizon DNS; the kind/e2e fixtures rely on it); the proxy's second resolution (rebinding window) is accepted and stated. (e) **Q-R5-7 mechanics**: `sslmode=require` without a stored `ca_pem` verifies chain **and host name** against the system roots (a public CA vouching for any name would make chain-only verification worthless); with `ca_pem` it verifies the chain against it (libpq `verify-ca`); `allow`/`prefer` stay opportunistic. The opt-out is the connection's non-secret metadata `tls_verification = "off"` (a `store: metadata` field of the `postgresql` package 1.1.0, shown only under `require`), because metadata is what the API can audit and the dashboard can show without opening the sealed login: the `connection.create` / `connection.reconnect` audit row carries it, `Connection.metadata` exposes it (the connection card warns), every session using it logs and counts `aurora_gateway_db_tls_unverified_total`; a stored `ca_pem` wins over it and `verify-ca`/`verify-full` ignore it. (f) **Database login errors** are a fixed text per class (28P01 / 08006 / 08001); detail goes to the log. (g) `api_keys` leaves the gateway grants and `migrate` revokes the grant earlier releases made. | One configuration key per bound; opt-out as an `sslmode` value or a sealed credential field; chain-only verification against system roots | Abuse bounds are the same everywhere and a key invites loosening them; an `sslmode` value or a sealed field is invisible to the API's audit and the dashboard; the host-name check is what makes the system roots meaningful. |
| D-182 | **FIX-106 (REVIEW-5, coordinator decision, 2026-11-13): how the chart makes the gateway's least-privilege role the rule (D-178 Q-R5-11).** (a) **Bundled evaluation Postgres** (`postgres.enabled`) with neither the role pair nor `database.allowSharedLogin`: the chart creates the login role `aurora_gateway` (fixed name; no attribute beyond `LOGIN`) with a generated password. The generated Secret gains `POSTGRES_GATEWAY_PASSWORD` and `AURORA_GATEWAY_DATABASE_URL` (the existing `secrets` hook, only-absent-keys rule); a `<release>-gateway-role` hook Job (weight -1, pre-install and pre-upgrade, the Postgres image's `psql` as the bootstrap owner) creates the role if missing and always re-sets its password, so a database that predates this gets the role on upgrade and the password always matches the Secret; `database.gateway_role` is rendered so `migrate` (weight 0) grants it; the gateway's `AURORA_DATABASE_URL` env reads the new key. The Job pod carries the `migrate` component label so the existing migrate NetworkPolicies admit it. (b) **External database**: the pair is required; without it `helm template` fails with a message naming both ways out (the pair, or `database.allowSharedLogin=true`). `allowSharedLogin` (default `false`) is the explicit shared-login value: it contradicts the pair (render fails), switches the automatic role off with the bundled Postgres, and adds a NOTES warning. (c) Consequently a default-values render now fails; the chart guards and `check-vault.sh` pass `--set database.allowSharedLogin=true` because they are not about the role, and `deploy/helm/check-gateway-role.sh` (run by `run-all.sh`) is the `helm template` matrix: bundled, pair, external without, external shared, contradictions, bundled opt-outs. (d) The kind e2e and the dev profile keep working unchanged: `values-e2e.yaml` sets the explicit pair (its rendered workloads are identical; only the `secrets` hook script gains an inert branch, exercised against a fake `kubectl` for the automatic case), `values-dev.yaml` takes the automatic role. | A fixed init script on the data directory; a chart-created role for external databases; making the shared login a silent default | An init script runs only on an empty data directory (no upgrade path, no password rotation); the chart cannot create roles on a database it does not own and the API user must not hold `CREATEROLE`; a silent default is the defect REVIEW-5 found. |
| D-195 | **Owner production feedback, 2026-11-13 (Q-FB-1..Q-FB-6).** (a) **One app per service** (Q-FB-1, FIX-123): `github-app` and `aws-role` (and the GHES pair) become connect methods of `github` and `aws-keys`; the manifest gains a per-connect-method `token`, method presentation (`label`, `description`, `recommended`) and `tools[].methods`; connections record their `connect_method`; existing connections are migrated and the two packages retired; partly supersedes D-91(b). (b) **Instance settings live in the app** (SPEC-SETTINGS): every setting that does not need different Kubernetes configuration, secret material or dev/test switching moves from Helm into the database and the Admin pages, applied live, security-critical audited, behind recent re-authentication. (c) **A value set in Helm is locked** (Q-FB-5): the dashboard shows it read-only with a note that it is configured in Helm and the API refuses changes; a value Helm leaves unset is edited in the dashboard; the chart therefore renders no default for a movable key. (d) **Secret stores follow (c)** (Q-FB-2, FIX-124), including store definitions, the default store, reference scopes and migrating values from the dashboard. (e) **All of (b)–(d) is phase 5** (Q-FB-6). (f) **No instance-level policy rule scope** for now (Q-FB-4); FIX-131 closed without a build, its read-only Governance view superseded by SET-2. (g) Mattermost setup simplification accepted in principle (Q-FB-3); FIX-127's plan to be fleshed out before a lane takes it. | Separate packages with per-agent conflict resolution (a); Helm seeds and the database wins, or Helm always wins with no dashboard editing (c); a read-only secret-stores page (d); SET-1/SET-2 in phase 4 (e); an instance rule scope (f) | The owner could not tell the paired apps apart and saw conflicts on a fresh install; instance admins should run the instance from the dashboard, while values an operator pins in Helm must stay authoritative and visibly so. |
| D-196 | **Owner, 2026-11-13 (Q-FB-7, amends D-178 / Q-R5-18; and FIX-138).** (a) **Indico keeps `full:everything` as an explicit opt-in**: on the OAuth connect form it is unticked by default, listed last and named for what it enables (extended writes: recurring bookings, cancellations, favorites, event preparation through Indico's internal endpoints); defaults stay `read:user` + `read:legacy_api`; D-84/D-123 mitigations unchanged (`custom_only`, `block_untooled`, writes held for approval). Q-R5-18's "specific write scopes only" assumed a narrower Indico scope that does not exist. (b) **Terminal shell**: bash by default, zsh selectable per person and agent, carried as an enumerated value (`bash`\|`zsh`), never a path; `/bin/sh` only as a last-resort fallback with a notice (FIX-138). | Dropping `full:everything` (loses every extended write) (a); the passwd shell only, or a free shell path (b) | Indico offers no finer scope for those endpoints, and the existing mitigations already bound the broad token; an enumerated shell keeps the PTY spawn free of client-chosen paths. |
| D-190 | **FIX-092: the stream listener's abuse bounds and where idle is judged.** The runner's per-supervisor-connection outbound queue is bounded (512 frames); a full queue closes the offending leg, or the whole connection for a sequenced frame (the replay buffer redelivers). The stream listener holds ≤ 256 unauthenticated connections per replica and ≤ 10 s for the whole request head, caps live legs per sandbox at 64 streams + 2 PTYs, and coalesces browser `pty.resize` to the latest size. Idle (600 s) resets on any byte **or ping/pong**; keep-awake stays byte-driven. The API pings the browser every 20 s and enforces the idle clock itself (4504), because the upstream socket answers the runner's pings on its own and would otherwise keep the runner's clock alive for a vanished tab. A browser text frame > 256 bytes closes 4502 unforwarded; writes toward browser/runner stall ≤ 30 s. `?wait=true` long-polls `terminal.wake_wait_secs` for `running`. The `terminal` boot capability needs both the operator flag and the runner's advertisement. `agent.terminal.close` (D-178) records close code and duration. | Unbounded queues; judging idle only at the runner; forwarding browser pongs upstream (the auto-pong still resets the runner's clock) | A supervisor sits beside untrusted code and the runner serves every sandbox on its node: bounds must hold however a peer behaves; liveness must reflect the browser, not the hop in between. |
| D-194 | **FIX-128 (owner production feedback, 2026-11-13): private notices name a space by its label, and the shared bot's "via" line is for shared spaces.** (a) A private notice that falls back to a DM (the platform has no ephemeral posts, or the connector reports `ephemeral: false` without an admin token) is prefixed with the space it concerns only when that adds something: nothing in a 1:1 DM (the notice arrives in the very DM), `In #<label>:` for channels, `In your group message with <label>:` for group DMs, and nothing when the label is empty; never `<#space:ID>` or any other id (the owner saw `In ~<dm channel name>:`). (b) The `Agent · via Bot` attribution is dropped in a 1:1 DM (`direct` thread link or DM target) and kept in shared spaces (`13 §5.2`). (c) The bot's name in that line is the bot record's `display_name` (`GET /bots/{id}`), re-read whenever the integration is validated, falling back to the bot's username below two characters; the user record's `first_name` was the source of "via R". The default name "Daneel" stays only as the name Aurora sets when allowed. | Keeping the prefix and rewriting the id into a name at render time (the renderer cannot know a DM's label); dropping the via line everywhere a single agent is bound (the shared bot's name is not the agent's) | The prefix and the via line exist to disambiguate; where nothing is ambiguous they are noise, and an internal channel id is never meaningful to a person. |
| D-193 | **FIX-113 (REVIEW-5 CONN-1/2/4..14): how the Mattermost connector orders events, routes callbacks and guards its server URL.** (a) Socket events are handled through a per-channel queue (`lanes.rs`): one event of a channel finishes before the next starts, channels run in parallel, events with no channel share one queue; a worker ends after 60 s idle. (b) A slash command resolves to the integration **whose command token matches** among those serving the team; an unknown team, an unknown token and a wrong token all answer 403 alike. A team holds one command per trigger, so the integration with the smallest id keeps `/aurora` and the others register `/aurora-<last 8 hex of their id>`. (c) The server URL may point only at public addresses: literal loopback/private/link-local/CGNAT/ULA/unspecified/multicast addresses and `localhost` are refused at validation and open time, and a custom resolver refuses names that resolve only to such addresses; `AURORA_MM_ALLOW_PRIVATE_NETWORKS=true` is the operator's opt-in (the e2e and conformance runs set it, their Mattermost is on loopback). The range helper of FIX-106 lives in the API crate, which a connector must not depend on, so the connector carries its own small `is_public`. The target's `message` is never echoed (logged at debug). (d) `AURORA_TLS_EXTRA_CA_BUNDLE` (the file mounted as `tls.extra_ca_bundle`) is trusted by the REST client and the WebSocket in addition to their roots; the chart does not set this variable for connectors yet. (e) The SDK closes a replica's integrations when the core has been unreachable for longer than the `lease_secs` of its last configuration. (f) A WebSocket authentication reply without an error code is no longer a revoked token; reactions are team-scoped like posts; managed connectors in `pending_approval` get no assignment (C-456a); the bootstrap upsert never touches a managed row (the reported attach did not reproduce, see the brief's Analysis). | One task per event with sequence numbers and a reorder buffer (more state for the same guarantee); routing a team's commands to every matching integration | A queue per channel is the smallest structure that preserves what Mattermost already guarantees per channel; the token is the only thing that says which integration a command was registered for. |
| D-192 | **FIX-112 (REVIEW-5 CHAN-1/4/6..9, WEBX-10/11; D-178 Q-R5-9): linking confirms the chat account, prompts are throttled, shared-channel approval cards carry no request details.** (a) `GET /v1/channel-links/preview?token=` (session only, caller must be an active member of the integration's org) returns the integration label, the platform reference, the display name the platform gave (`link_requests.platform_display_name`, migration `20261201000080`) and the e-mail hint; the confirm request repeats `platform_user_ref` and a mismatch is 422 `link_reference_mismatch`, nothing consumed. The token leaves the URL on arrival (kept in `sessionStorage`; the login return URL is the bare page). (b) A person is prompted at most once per 10 minutes per (integration, person): `insert_throttled` takes a per-person advisory lock and inserts nothing, and no DM goes out, while an unused, unexpired request younger than 10 minutes exists (message, command and button paths alike). The token is stored hashed, so "reuse" means "the earlier link is still the one"; the refusal notice is limited to one per (integration, agent, person) per 10 minutes per replica (in memory, like the freeze notice). (c) `approve <code>`/`deny <code>` find the approval in SQL (`substring(sha256(uuid_send(id)) from 1 for 5)` equals the five bytes the typed code decodes to), so the newest of any number of pending approvals is decidable; a retried event reuses its `file.fetch` through the dedupe key `file:<event>:<index>:<attempt>` (the first attempt whose op did not end `failed`/`dropped`); a DM menu card records its addressee (`channel_cards.addressee_ref`, migration `20261201000081`, cards whose space was already learned fail closed); `unbind` writes `space_binding.delete` in the deactivation's transaction (not security-critical, atomic); detaching a presence and removing a space binding ask for confirmation. (d) **Q-R5-9**: an approval card posted into a space whose thread link is not `direct` shows the title, agent, rule and expiry only; the summary fields (method, host, path, body keys) are on the dashboard page the card links to. | A code the chat user types to the bot instead of a sign-in link (stronger, noted for the owner, not in scope); throttle by a time bucket column (a fixed window allows two prompts across a boundary); deleting the old token on a new prompt | The page must show whom the person is binding, or a forwarded link binds the attacker's account to a victim; the throttle bounds how many DMs and rows a hostile member can create; details in a channel are visible to people who cannot use the agent. |
| D-191 | **FIX-094: a bootstrap token is re-presentable until the boot has held a channel; the podman ops listener defaults to loopback.** `sandboxes.channel_held_at` (migration `20261201000019`) records the first `channel/hold` of a boot; `channel/claim` is idempotent while it is NULL and answers 409 `bootstrap_spent` afterwards (SEC-25). The runner claims once per connection and, after a successful claim, admits with the welcome even when `channel/hold` fails retryably (retry in the background). The supervisor port caps unadmitted connections (256) and times the upgrade (`hello_timeout`). A failed Kubernetes API read during a start is retried at the next poll until the start deadline; a `Lost` home claim is `home_volume_unbound`. The final report flush retries until `drain_timeout`. On podman `runner.ops_bind` defaults to `127.0.0.1` and a non-loopback overlap with `server.bind` is a startup error. `verify_network` also reads `dns_enabled` from podman's libpod network JSON and refuses DNS-on networks. The runner Role loses `get`/`watch` on Secrets; `sandboxes/check` answers only for the caller's own sandboxes. | A `used`-flag reset by a timer; a retry loop inside the claim; refusing the overlap only with a warning | An idempotent claim bounded by a durable "held" mark loses no boot to one lost response and still spends the token the moment a channel exists; the other items remove operator-visible footguns the review found. |
| D-183 | **FIX-108 (REVIEW-5 AUTH-1..AUTH-6, D-178 Q-R5-12/Q-R5-16): sign-in and sessions.** (a) **JIT never restores a membership an admin removed**: the admin removal writes a marker `org_member_removals (org_id, user_id)` (migration `20261201000020`), JIT skips an org with a marker, and **every** `org_members` insert (invitation acceptance, admin add, claim, SCIM pending) clears it in the same statement; removals before the migration leave no marker (not reconstructible). Claim-created and SCIM removals do not write one (SCIM has its own tombstone; a claim that goes away is not an admin's decision). (b) A re-authentication needs `auth_time` within 60 s past or future; a missing claim is `reauth_stale`. (c) The auth cache takes a generation before its database read and `put`s only if no invalidation happened since, under the map lock; `PATCH /v1/admin/users/{id}` notifies `aurora_auth_revoked {user}` whenever `is_instance_admin` changes (or the user is disabled). (d) `user.admit_by_claim` is security-critical. (e) CLI approval: `GET /v1/auth/cli/pending?code=` (session only) returns hostname (unverified), start time, a coarse address (IPv4 /24, IPv6 /48 stored in `cli_logins.requested_addr`) and the key lifetime; the screen says approval creates a key acting as the approver. (f) `auth.cli_key_ttl_days` (default 90, `0` = never, max 3650) is the expiry of CLI-minted keys; keys minted earlier have no expiry and are left as they are (no retroactive expiry: revoking them is the owner's choice). (g) `auth.oidc_providers[].link_existing_users` (unset = true unless the provider lists `trusted_email_domains`): when false, a first sign-in does not link to an account that already has an identity at **another** provider (`email_linked_elsewhere`); accounts with no identity (bootstrap admin, SCIM-provisioned) still bind by their e-mail. | Marker on every removal path; reconstructing markers from the audit log; refusing every trusted-domain link including to identity-less accounts | The marker follows exactly the case the review found (an admin's decision); identity-less accounts must keep binding or the bootstrap admin and SCIM pending memberships (D-175) could never be activated. |
| D-187 | **FIX-107 (REVIEW-5 PLUG-1..PLUG-11): what an upgrade may do without an instance admin looking.** (a) A version is a first install (`pending_approval`, reason shown) when its `package.publisher` differs from the installed package's (`publisher_changed`), when it is lower than the highest approved version of that id (`version_downgrade`), or when the id belongs to the built-in source and the version does not (`builtin_id_reserved`); built-in versions from the built-in source are exempt from the last rule only. (b) **`key_id` is the canonical standard-base64 key**; any other spelling is `signature_malformed`; migration `20261201000070` rewrites older `signer_key_id` rows and revocation compares spelling-insensitively as a safety net. (c) Static OAuth token and authorize endpoints must lie on a claimed host or a host in the new `permissions.token_hosts` (exact hosts, not reserved or private); a new token-endpoint host or `token_hosts` entry is a widening (`token_host`). Built-in packages that use a login host of their own list it. (d) The credential-vocabulary check (SEC-57) runs for every activation over an active version, not only majors; a minor that would flip connections is held as `breaks_connections`. (e) Hook host: module pull and compile is single-flight per digest with the map lock held only to read or insert; an authorize URL host must be declared; an unreadable random source fails the call; `plugin.yaml` ≤ 256 KiB (`PACKAGE_MANIFEST_MAX`). (f) The "standing warning banner" for `plugins.allow_unsigned` is dropped from the spec (the API logs a startup warning); the metrics sentence no longer promises fuel. | Allowing only same-publisher, same-or-higher versions to auto-activate and leaving the rest to the approval screen's free text; a banner flag in the instance payload | Every case is an installed package acquiring different code or a different credential destination without a human decision; the approval screen already shows reasons and widenings, so the fix is to route these cases there. A downgrade check against the *highest approved* version (not the installed one) also stops a rollback through a superseded row. The banner was never built and has no one to read it in the dev-only mode that enables it. |
| D-188 | **FIX-118 (REVIEW-5 PKG-1..PKG-12): approval cards show what leaves the account; the classifier and the CLI protect credentials.** (a) **Summary decoders, not hooks, where a template can do it**: `json` takes `*` path segments (scalar leaves joined with `, `), new `json_count`, `json_text` (every `text`/`content` string below a node: Atlassian document format, Notion rich text) and `mime_attachments`; `mime_header` joins every header of that name. Mail approvals (Gmail, Microsoft 365) list every To/Cc/Bcc, a clamped body preview and the attachment count; calendar invitations list attendees; GitHub issue/PR/file writes, Jira descriptions and comments, Confluence and Notion page content show a clamped preview. A `summarize` hook stays the answer where no template can express it. (b) The SQL classifier treats an operator with a non-`pg_catalog` schema and a cast to a non-built-in type as `admin`; views stay uninspectable (setup note). (c) The Confluence entry on `api.atlassian.com` keeps the `/oauth/token` prefix: the connect probe must lie on a declared entry of the app (`probe::declared_host`) and narrowing it to the probe's exact path ties with Jira's entry, which goes to the smaller app id (`confluence`) and would take `list_accessible_sites` from Jira. Left open for the owner. (d) `aurora run --forward` requires a per-run secret (proxy basic auth in the child's proxy variables, 407 before any upstream connection); the CLI refuses a cleartext server URL except loopback and drops stored agent tokens when the login URL changes; server-supplied strings are printed without control characters; `logout` deletes the local file and says the API key was **not** revoked (no endpoint revokes the device-flow key by itself). (e) Indico (D-178 Q-R5-18 as amended by D-196): `full:everything` stays an explicit opt-in, unticked, last, named for what it enables. (f) Built-ins that use a login host of their own list it in `permissions.token_hosts` (D-187). | Hooks (wasm crates) for every package; removing the Confluence entry; a server endpoint to revoke the CLI key | Decoders keep the controls auditable in YAML and fixtures, need no new wasm crates and cover every case the review listed; removing or narrowing the Confluence claim breaks the connect probe or Jira's tool; the key-revocation endpoint is an API change outside this brief. |
| D-189 | **FIX-120 (REVIEW-5, owner D-178 Q-R5-8): one connector-host token per host, bound to the connector types it announced.** `connector_host_tokens` gains `name`, `prefix`, `type_ids` (null = the shared bootstrap token), `created_by`, `last_used_at`, `revoked_at`; `connector_hosts.token_id` records the bound token that registered an instance (migration `20261201000071`). A bound token registers only its types (403 `type_not_bound`), owns the instance ids it registered (403 `host_instance_mismatch` for another token), is served assignments and decrypted settings only for its types, reports status only for its types, and acts under C-458 only for its types. Instance admins issue (shown once), list and revoke them (C-690..C-692, audited, security-critical). **Upgrade path:** existing rows stay the shared unbound token and keep working; the shared token is refused from the moment the first live bound token exists, and works again if every bound token is revoked; a host still presenting it keeps its running set (failed polls keep it) until rolled, and a rolling update starts the new pod before stopping the old one (`15 §Upgrades`). The instance id is not bound to the token at issue time (it is a per-process id that changes on every restart and differs per replica): it is bound at first registration instead. | Binding the token to one instance id at issue; refusing the shared token only for hosts that registered with a bound token (needs a host identity the shared token cannot carry); a grace window | A token per Deployment with several replicas and restarting processes cannot name an instance id in advance; ownership of an id by the first registering token gives the same isolation and survives restarts. |
| D-197 | **FIX-102 (REVIEW-5 OPS/DEP): chart posture.** (a) The Kubernetes API server addresses for the runner's and the `secrets` hook's egress come from the `discovery.k8s.io/v1` **EndpointSlice** `kubernetes` in `default` (one helper, `aurora.kubeApiCidrs`), not the core `v1` Endpoints (deprecated from 1.33: every `helm upgrade` warned); with nothing readable (`helm template`, no `get` on endpointslices) the rule is **omitted**, never `0.0.0.0/0` — `networkPolicy.kubeApiOpen: true` says "any" on purpose. (b) An **upgrade** whose generated Secret is absent refuses to mint new encryption keys (`secrets.allowRegenerate` overrides): the API does not check keys at start, so a restore without the Secret would silently lose every stored credential. (c) Readiness is `httpGet /readyz`, liveness `/healthz`, where the workload has an ops router; PodDisruptionBudgets only from two replicas; hook Roles name their Secrets and each hook Job has its own ServiceAccount; the migrate Job's policies are hook resources; the previews wildcard is its own `HTTPRoute` (`HTTPRouteRule` has no hostnames); scraping is opt-in (`networkPolicy.monitoringFrom`, `monitoring.podMonitor`). |
| D-198 | **FIX-101 (REVIEW-5 OPS/TEST): a skipped suite is a failure in CI.** An env-gated suite calls `aurora_testkit::require_or_skip(gate)`: gate set → run; unset and `CI` not `true` → `SKIPPED:` and return; unset and `CI=true` → panic. The e2e scripts fail on a `SKIPPED:` line under `CI=true` unless the job declares an exception (`AURORA_E2E_ALLOW_SKIPPED`, commented in the workflow). The nextest `ci` profile keeps the gated suites out of the shards; the CI job `gated-suites` runs them with their gates (pinned Redis, runner podman). The schema guard compares type and nullability (not constraints, indexes, defaults), with a shrink-only drift list; a permission-matrix INCONCLUSIVE row fails unless listed. Performance budgets stay manual on an idle machine (no workflow). |
| D-199 | **FIX-119 (REVIEW-5 SPEC): documents say what runs.** (a) A `docs/02` limit nobody reads is wired as the default/reader it describes or removed with its row rewritten to what runs (`TERMINAL_AUTH_CACHE`: the 10 s `ACCESS_RECHECK`; the 60 s turn progress interval: the supervisor's 10 s) — the limits ratchet lost its `unread` entries. (b) `audit_log` has no database-level UPDATE/DELETE protection: recorded as a residual risk in `17` (a trigger plus an insert-only login is the follow-up), not built. (c) The attachment-quota "dashboard notice" is an audit row, an owner e-mail and the Admin › Storage badge; an app-shell banner is an open follow-up. (d) A guard ties the `docs/05` route catalogue to `web/openapi.json` (or the "outside the OpenAPI document" table, or a phase tag). |
| D-200 | **Owner, 2026-11-13 (phase 5, P5-AGENT): the agent page is regrouped and chat-first.** The thirteen agent tabs become five sections: Chat (hosted only) · Behaviour (Instructions, Memory, Skills) · Triggers (Schedules, Webhooks, Channels, Peers) · Computer (Computer, Terminal, Previews; BYO: Computer: Connect) · Access. A section with several pages shows them as a segmented row; a section whose pages are all unavailable is hidden. The URL keeps `?tab=<page>` with the existing page ids, so every deep link (`?tab=access`, `?c=<id>`, C-506) still opens the same page; the section is derived from the page. A hosted agent opens on Chat (was Instructions); BYO on Instructions. The header is one line (avatar, name, `@handle`, sandbox state, section switcher, Agent actions); Freeze and Delete live in the Agent actions menu, and the Delete zone under Instructions goes. Design: the "Agent page redesign" canvas (direction A, chosen over an in-page agent sidebar (B) and a separate activity timeline (C)). Supersedes `14`'s phase-2 agent header and tab strip, and the agent-strip part of FIX-129 once built (FIX-129's `Tabs` overflow stays a primitive). | Keep thirteen tabs with an overflow menu (FIX-129 alone); an in-page agent sidebar (B); moving tool calls out of the transcript into a timeline (C) | Most visits are to talk to the agent; the configuration pages fall into four groups people already name; keeping the page ids keeps every link and test that names a tab. |
| D-201 | **Owner, 2026-11-13 (phase 5, P5-AGENT): a collapsible status rail beside the chat.** In the Chat section the chat panel gains a right column with five parts in this order, each with a fixed icon: Needs you (`CheckCircleIcon`), Doing now (`ActivityIcon`), Recent tool calls (`WrenchIcon`), Computer (`MonitorIcon`), About (`BookIcon`). **Expanded** (288 px) it shows them in full; **collapsed** (56 px) it is a strip of those icons, each with its state (count, dot) and an `aria-label` that says it in words; the approvals icon opens a pop-over with the pending items, the others expand the rail at that part. The choice is stored per browser (like the theme, never sent to the API); with nothing stored, the rail starts expanded at a viewport of 1440 px or more and collapsed below. When the chat panel is narrower than 1024 px the expanded rail overlays the thread (focus-trapped, Esc closes) instead of pushing it. Below 768 px there is no rail: a "N approvals waiting" banner (approvers, N > 0) and a Status button open the same five parts in a sheet. Needs you is shown only to people who can approve (`can_approve`); an approval of the open conversation links to its inline card instead of repeating the buttons. Data comes from existing routes plus one filter (C-700): pending gateway approvals and action approvals by agent, conversations' `active_turn`, the open conversation's stream, the request log by agent, the sandbox endpoint. | A fixed rail (A); a rail that is only an overlay; tabs inside the rail (A2); a per-account preference stored server-side | People who watch an agent want its state on screen and people who talk to it want the width; a per-browser setting matches how the theme works and needs no API. |
| D-217 | **FIX-127 (D-195(g)): `credentials.validate` gains a probe mode and fields may take `choices: "from_validate"`** (contract v1, additive; C-710, C-711). `mode: "probe"` validates what it can, has no effect on the platform, stores nothing and returns per-field choices and warnings; `POST /v1/orgs/{org}/channel-integrations:probe` (org owner/admin, `write` class) runs it once and never stores or logs the credentials. Mattermost uses it to list the bot's teams. Create stays the single authoritative validation. Setup fields also gain an optional `advanced` flag (C-715). | A new op type `teams.list` (every connector change would need the op registered and old connectors would not know it); a free-text team (today); listing teams with the instance admin's credentials | Probe reuses the correlated validate path, the scrubbing of plaintext payloads and the unsupported-op handling that exist, so older connectors degrade to "type the team" instead of breaking. |
| D-218 | **FIX-127: availability and dashboard binding.** (a) Connector availability is a **separate** route `GET /v1/connector-availability` (C-712) so `GET /v1/connectors` keeps meaning "active connectors with schemas"; it returns a per-type `setup_state` and the instance admins' display names only (≤ 10; no ids or e-mails). (b) Bindings can be created from the dashboard (`POST /v1/agents/{agent}/space-bindings`, C-713) by a workspace owner **only for a space whose members include the caller's linked chat account** and into which the bot has already been invited (409 `bot_not_in_space` + hint); direct conversations are not bindable; effects, audit and announcement equal the `bind` command. `GET /v1/integrations/{id}/spaces?mine=true` (C-714) lists the candidates. `13 §5.3` amended. | Adding `setup_state` to `/v1/connectors` (mixes admin-ish state into the user catalogue); binding any space the bot is in (an owner could attach an agent to a conversation they are not part of, breaking the §5.3 consent rule) | The trust model is unchanged: instance admins add connectors, org admins hold bot credentials, workspace owners attach agents and bind spaces they themselves are in. |
| D-219 | **FIX-127: no auto-add of connectors; one-click add for all-optional types.** An instance admin adds a connector explicitly (it exposes public callback endpoints and approves a manifest); a type whose `instance_setup` fields are all optional or generated is added from the choose step with one button, review inline and settings under Advanced. Admin › Setup/Overview show an "available — add it" callout for an announced, not-added built-in type. `14` says so. | Auto-adding built-in connectors at install (connectors would expose callbacks nobody approved; built-in app packages auto-activate because they expose nothing) | Keeps the manifest approval an explicit instance-admin act while removing the four-screen detour. |
| D-184 | **FIX-116 (REVIEW-5 PLAT-4/6/7/8): development switches, credentials and the setup gate.** (a) `runner.dev_allow_external_network`, `plugins.allow_unsigned`, `hosted.allow_fake_harness`, `connector_host.test_types` and `ratelimit.api.enabled = false` are refused unless `server.external_url` is a loopback origin (the chart refuses them too); (b) credentials never ride in `email.smtp_url` or `gateway.egress_proxy` (userinfo refused); the SMTP password is `AURORA_SMTP_PASSWORD` (or `_FILE`) next to `email.smtp_username`; (c) log-only mail logs recipient and subject, and the link (a token) only with `email.log_links = true` (dev values set it); (d) the fresh-instance gate is a persisted marker (`instance_setup.placement_pending`, set by the migration on an instance with no organization and no placement, cleared by the first saved placement), so an administrator's own sign-in no longer opens it; (e) each limit key is read: `terminal.awake_cap_secs` and `terminal.wake_window_secs` drive the terminal keep-awake on their own clock; one SCIM token ceiling, 730 days. | Keep warning-only for the dev switches; keep "no organization exists" as the gate; leave the SMTP password inside the URL | A key that is parsed and ignored, a production switch behind one warning line and a token in a log line each break a guarantee the documents make; the marker is the only state that survives the first sign-in. D-142's wording is corrected: the self-hosted connector path never accepts an *empty* signature outside `plugins.allow_unsigned` either. |
| D-210 | **FIX-096 (REVIEW-5 SKILL-1/3..6, D-178 Q-R5-3/Q-R5-6): skill libraries are bounded against repository text and the git host.** (a) `SKILL.md` frontmatter containing a YAML anchor or alias (a token starting `&` or `*`) is invalid before the parser runs (nested aliases multiply past serde_yaml's jump bound), and only `name` and `description` are read (typed). (b) An approval re-runs the refresh-time validation on the stored proposal (paths, 50 files, file sizes, description and body rules; 422 `library_skill_invalid`). (c) A refresh's head write is compare-and-set on `skill_libraries.head_epoch`, which an add that clears the head bumps. (d) Sum and insert of open proposals run under the organization's advisory lock. (e) A cold listing is single-flight per library under a 90 s deadline. (f) A git-host 429/503 is not a failure: no `refresh_failures` increment, the next attempt after the host's `Retry-After` (delta-seconds, default 300 s, capped 1 h). (g) **Heads:** browse and add accept only the ref's current head or one recorded in `skill_library_heads` (refresh, browse, create and patch record; the newest 20 are kept); anything else is 422 `commit_not_in_library`. (h) Approving a proposal of a **paused** library is 409 `library_paused`; rejecting stays possible. (i) `required_access` accepts only `read`. | Keep any fetchable commit; keep aliases and bound the expansion; count a 429 as a failure | Repository text is untrusted (SEC-84): refuse what multiplies before parsing, re-validate what is stored, and never let a race or a throttling host change what an organisation reviewed. |
| D-211 | **FIX-121 (owner D-178 / Q-R5-10): Admin › Runners shows capacity and rotates runner tokens with a grace period; provisioning stays in Helm/CLI.** (a) The runners list already carried the capacity numbers; the dashboard now shows them (C-694). (b) A rotation keeps the replaced `aur_` token working for `grace_secs` (default **600 s**, 0..86400; `runners.previous_token_hash`/`previous_token_expires_at`, migration `20261201000101`, one previous token at most), then refuses it (C-695); the replaced token is also what `find_by_token_hash` accepts, nothing else changes on the runner plane. (c) `docs/14` says adding and removing runners is Helm/CLI work; the create/delete routes stay for the CLI. (d) A runner still on the old token during the grace does not recognise sandboxes the API labelled after the rotation (the label is the new token's hash); the runbook says to roll within the grace (`docs/operator/runners.md`). | A fixed grace; a rotation that refuses the old token at once; provisioning from the dashboard | A runner has to be rolled (secret updated, Deployment restarted) after the token changes; an immediate cut-off turns every rotation into an outage, and an unbounded overlap keeps a leaked token alive. |
| D-214 | **FIX-123 (D-195(a)): connect methods carry their own token, presentation and tool filter.** (a) Manifest contract 1, additive: every `connect[]` method accepts optional `token` (overrides the app's for connections made with it; `AppEntry::token_for(method)`), `label` (≤ 60), `description` (≤ 400) and `recommended` (at most one per app); `tools[].connect_methods: [method ids]` restricts a tool to connections of those methods. The brief called the last one `tools[].methods`; that key already means the tool's HTTP methods, so it is `connect_methods`. (b) `app credentials` are the union over methods; a required credential read by host templates must be produced by every `fields`/`oauth2` method (hook output is invisible to the manifest), else declared `optional`. (c) A tool restricted by `connect_methods` is hidden from pickers and not classified for connections of other methods; a connection with no recorded method (legacy, or ambiguous at backfill) hides nothing. (d) `app_connections.connect_method text null` records the method; reconnect keeps it unless the person picks another. | A separate `connect_method` table; keeping two apps and a "pick one" note; reusing `methods` for connect methods | The manifest already allows several methods per app; only the token kind and the tool list were app-wide, so the smallest additive change is a per-method override plus a tool filter. |
| D-215 | **FIX-123: superseded apps are moved in the activation transaction of the app that replaced them.** `github-app` → `github`/`github_app`, `github-app-ghes` → `github-ghes`/`github_app`, `aws-role` → `aws-keys`/`assume_role` (a table in the API, `09 §10`). Activating `github` 2.0.0 or `aws-keys` 2.0.0 (an instance-admin approval, because the hooks and egress are a widening) moves the old apps' connections (ids, credential refs, grants, rules unchanged; tool ids carried over), instance registrations (merged into an existing registration of the same org and host), skill-library references and org availability; deletes the old pair's host-claim decisions; disables the retired package (`superseded_by_<package>`, never re-enabled); writes one `plugin.supersede` audit row with counts. Idempotent; nothing is deleted from history (`request_log`, `approvals` keep the old app id). | An SQL migration (the package rows are not in the schema's control); a CLI command run by the operator; deleting the retired packages | The move needs the new app to be active in the snapshot, so it belongs to its activation, which already is the transaction that flips connections and converts grants. |
| D-216 | **FIX-123: built-in packages after the merge.** `github` 2.0.0 gains the `github_app` method on both of its apps (`github`, `github-ghes`; hooks `connect.exchange` and `token.mint`, the egress of the old package, recommended for teams), `aws-keys` 2.0.0 the `assume_role` method (recommended, with `org_external_id`/`org_id` context); `github-app` and `aws-role` are no longer shipped, their hook crates and fixtures move into the merged packages; the OAuth/token methods stay `oauth`/`token`, the AWS key method `keys`. Fresh instances start with one tile per service and no built-in host-claim conflict. | Keeping the retired packages one more release | The upgrade step needs them only in an existing database, not in the shipped set. |
| D-225 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (answers Q11): P4-NARR (live narration cards) and P4-UTOK (`user_tokens` capability) move to phase 5.** Phase 4 closes at AUDIT-004; neither has spec text yet (`21 §Other later items` only), so each needs a spec task before it is built. | Building them in phase 4 (no spec, would delay the exit); leaving them unscheduled | Phase 4 ends with a clean scope; the items keep a home in the phase-5 table. |
| D-226 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-142): SEC-18 relaxed for an agent's own messages on chat platforms.** (a) An agent's message body (turn answers mirrored to a thread, `send_message` text) is rendered as **safe Markdown**, not escaped: formatting kept; links only `http`/`https`/`mailto`, with the destination host appended as text when the label is not the URL; images never inline (a link with the host); raw HTML as text; neutral tokens (`<@user:…>`, `<#space:…>`, `<@here>`) never formed from the text. Labels, card text, notices, approval summaries and other people's text stay `markdown_as_data`. (b) **Mentions work** (owner: "@here or @username"): platform `@username` passes through; a standalone `@here` outside code becomes the core's `<@here>` token, so the platform notifies. `@channel` and `@all` stay defused (coordinator default: they notify every member, offline ones too; the owner can extend this). When a `send_message` held for approval carries `@here`, the approval summary says so. | Keep escaping everything (owner: unreadable); pass model Markdown through unchanged (disguised links, image exfiltration, forged tokens); allow every broadcast | The dashboard already renders the same text as safe Markdown (`14`, FIX-137): chat platforms get the same rules. `@here` reaches only the space the agent may already post in, so the risk is noise, not reach; it goes through the core's token so connectors keep defusing every broadcast word in other text. |
| D-227 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-137, FIX-142): Markdown renders like GitHub, in the dashboard and on chat platforms; formatting changes only for listed security reasons.** Owner: "make sure all standard+github markdown renders correctly. there have better be very serious security reasons to break some formatting!" (a) **Target**: CommonMark 0.31, the GFM spec (tables, task lists, strikethrough, extended autolinks, tag filter) and github.com's additions (footnotes, alerts, emoji shortcodes, highlighted code, math, Mermaid, heading anchors, GitHub's raw-HTML allowlist). This is the neutral content format (`13 §4.3`) and the dashboard renderer's target (`14`). (b) **The only exceptions**, everywhere: images from another origin are never loaded inline (a link instead; zero-click data exfiltration); links only `http`/`https`/`mailto` (plus same-origin relative in the dashboard; script execution); a link whose label looks like a URL or domain of another host shows the real host (the one link form that lies); raw HTML outside the allowlist is removed keeping its text (XSS); typed neutral mention tokens never form (forged mentions, D-226(b)); Mermaid `securityLevel: "strict"` and KaTeX `trust: false`, falling back to code only if the CSP cannot host them. This **narrows D-226(a)**: ordinary link labels get no host suffix, and allowlisted raw HTML is kept (not shown as text). (c) **Platform limitations are conversions, not exceptions**: a connector passes native constructs through byte for byte and converts the rest to the nearest equivalent per a table in its spec; content is never dropped. (d) The core edits only the byte ranges of (b) and verifies by re-parsing; it does not re-emit the message. (e) Conformance tests run the CommonMark and GFM spec examples; differences other than (b) are bugs. | Keep a subset (owner: no); re-emit Markdown from a parse tree (risks altering formatting); show every link's host (noise; only deceptive labels need it) | The security exceptions cover zero-click leaks, script execution, forged mentions and lying links; everything else is presentation and must match what people expect from GitHub. |
| D-228 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5, P5-CHAT): the agent chat becomes a messaging client in the Mattermost mould, built on the P5-AGENT redesign.** Owner: "something more like mattermost, allowing me to reply to a specific message (helps the agent understand the context better), add formatting, attach files, etc." (a) **Reply = quote-reply inside the one linear conversation**, not Mattermost's branching threads: an agent's conversation is one session, so a side thread would have no session of its own to go to. A message may name one earlier message of the same conversation (`reply_to`, C-725); the transcript shows the quote above the bubble and jumps to the original; the agent receives a platform-composed quote block before the text (C-725), so it knows which message is meant even after compaction. (b) **Formatting = Markdown with a formatting bar** (Mattermost and GitHub style: buttons and shortcuts insert Markdown, a Preview tab renders it with the D-227 renderer); no rich-text editor (WYSIWYG would need a second document model and its own sanitiser). (c) **Files**: drag and drop onto the thread, paste from the clipboard, several at once, and a message may be files only (C-726). (d) Also in scope: per-conversation drafts (browser storage), emoji shortcodes and picker, a spreadsheet paste (tab-separated) becomes a Markdown table, "Jump to latest" with a new-message count, a message permalink. Out of scope here: reactions, editing a sent message, Mattermost-side replies; message search was out of scope here and is now P5-CHAT-4 (D-229). (e) **Sync with P5-AGENT**: designed as new boards on the same "Agent page redesign" canvas, built after P5-AGENT-2 in the composer and transcript files P5-AGENT-2 does not own, after FIX-137 (renderer). | Mattermost-style side threads (no session for them); a rich-text editor; leaving the composer as it is | Quote-reply gives the agent the context the owner wants without a second conversation model; Markdown plus a toolbar matches what the agent writes back and what D-227 renders, in the dashboard and on chat platforms. |
| D-229 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5, P5-CHAT-4): message search.** Owner: "we're probably going to need that search API too, right?" (a) **Scope**: what people and agents said in the conversations the caller can see — person and door messages, agent messages, file names and captions; not tool calls, approvals, notices or thinking. One workspace route (C-727) with agent, conversation, author, file, date and archived filters; the chat panel searches "This agent" by default and "All agents" on request. (b) **Engine: Postgres full-text search, no extension**: a separate `transcript_search` table (one row per searchable event, FK cascade to `transcript_events`) holding a `tsvector` of the workspace's memory language (stemmed) plus `simple` (exact words, any language), GIN-indexed; written in the event's transaction; removed by redaction, delete and retention in theirs; existing events backfilled by a resumable job. (c) **Safety**: the visibility law inside the query (`05 §Conversation visibility` already requires it); our own query parser builds the tsquery (user text never reaches `to_tsquery`); snippets are plain text with highlight ranges, never HTML. (d) ~~Agents do not get a search tool here~~ — superseded by D-230: agents search their own conversations. | `pg_trgm` or an external engine (OpenSearch, Meilisearch): an extension the least-privilege roles cannot create, or a new service to run and secure; a generated column on `transcript_events` (rewrites the largest table on upgrade); query-time `to_tsvector` as memory search does (no index: too slow on transcripts) | Postgres is already there and its full-text search covers words, phrases, prefixes and stemming; a side table keeps the upgrade cheap and makes removal a cascade. |
| D-233 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-147): one permissions editor everywhere.** Owner: "The Agent's own Access edit page is way better! Use that one for everything. Don't reimplement multiple ways of configuring this stuff." The Access tab's grant editor becomes a shared controlled `GrantForm` (Full / Custom, Read and Write groups with a group-wide Allow / Ask / Block, per-tool decision, ask timeout) used by the Access tab and by "Give agents access" after a connection is made (which also gains Full/Custom for ordinary apps and Ask for every app). Lists of scopes (the connect window's requested scopes, FIX-136's declared token scopes) use the same grouped-list primitive (`PermissionGroups`) with On/Off rows. No other tool or scope picker may exist (guard test). | Improve the "Give agents access" checklist in place (a second editor to keep in step) | One component means one behaviour to learn, test and extend (FIX-136 adds scope availability once). |
| D-230 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (P5-CHAT-4 part C): an agent can search its own conversations.** Owner: "shouldn't the agent be able to search its own conversations? that would improve memory". (a) Two platform tools on the D-229 index: `conversation_search` and `conversation_read` (C-728). (b) **Scope is set by the person the agent is talking to, never wider**: only the calling agent's conversations, and of those, the **current conversation**, the **current speaker's own direct conversation** with this agent, and the agent's **non-direct** conversations (channel threads, group, schedule, webhook, peer, api) that the speaker can see (`can_use`). **Other members' direct conversations are never searchable by the agent, even when the speaker is a workspace owner**: owners may read them in the dashboard, where that is visible and audited, but an agent repeating them would break the preamble's rule ("Do not quote or describe what someone told you in a direct conversation in another conversation…", `10`). A turn without a person speaker (schedule, webhook, peer, system) searches the current conversation and the agent's non-direct conversations only. Evaluated at call time with the access law of that moment. (c) Results are other people's words: returned as quoted, labelled data ("earlier messages, untrusted", as conversation history is in `10`), bounded (≤ 20 hits, ≤ 40 messages per read), redacted text never returned. (d) No automatic recall from past conversations at turn start in this task (memory recall stays as it is); the tools are used when the agent chooses. Automatic recall is a later decision if the tools prove useful. | No agent search (D-229(d)); the dashboard's visibility law as-is (an owner speaker would let the agent quote other members' direct chats); automatic turn-start recall from transcripts (token cost and noise before we know it helps) | The agent gets the conversation history its users already share with it, with no path for one person's private chat to reach another person through the agent. |
| D-231 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5, FIX-145): a self-hosted app may be served under a path.** The owner could not register CERN's Jira (`https://its.cern.ch/jira`): a registration was a bare host (D-74), and D-92(e) and P2-CAT-7 G7 left out servers under a path. (a) A registration gains an optional **base path** (`/jira`), validated (unreserved characters, no `.`/`..` segments, ≤ 8 segments, ≤ 128 chars, trailing `/` dropped, `/` = none). It cannot be changed after registration, like the host. (b) **Manifests are unchanged**: for that registration, every `https://{instance}/…` (probe, connect probes, OAuth URLs, refresh, mint, library fetch) resolves to `https://<host><base_path>/…`, through one helper. The hook host request carries `instance_base_path`, and hook egress stays per host. (c) **Gateway**: a request belongs to a registration when the host is equal and the path is the base path or under it (segment boundary, raw normalized path, longest base path wins). Manifest matching (host-entry prefixes, tools, `{path.N}`, params) uses the path with the base path removed. Upstream, audit and approval display keep the full path. Outside every base path nothing is injected (fail closed). SEC-59 becomes "only on the instance's host and under its base path". (d) **Several registrations may share a host** when their base paths do not overlap (`/jira` and `/confluence`), for one app or different apps. Overlap (either none, equal, or segment prefix) is `instance_host_conflict`, including on promote. Static claims, LLM hosts and Aurora's own addresses stay host-level. (e) `default_host` stays a bare host. (f) The agent sees each self-hosted account's address `https://<host><base_path>` (FIX-144 adds `address`). (g) Dashboard: an optional Path field; a full URL typed into Host is split on the client. Supersedes D-92(e) and P2-CAT-7 G7 for registered instances. Contract C-731. | Keep bare hosts (CERN's Jira and any server under a path cannot be used); a per-manifest path template such as `{instance_path}` (every package would have to change, and an omission would send requests to the host root); one registration per host whatever the path (Jira and Confluence under one host would need two hosts) | The path is a fact about the deployment, not the app, so it belongs on the registration. Removing it before matching keeps every manifest and tool working unchanged. Binding credentials to the host and the path is stricter than today, never looser. |
| D-232 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5, FIX-146): request tracing end to end.** The owner wants to follow one request across services, as Sentry does. Today `traceparent` reaches few calls and only when OTLP export is set, no log line has a `request_id`, and a turn's work starts a new trace after the database queue. (a) **Request id = W3C trace id, always**: every service installs the propagator and a tracer provider. The exporter is added only with `telemetry.otlp_endpoint`. Every log line inside a span carries `request_id`, so `kubectl logs … \| grep <id>` follows a request across services with no tracing backend. Sampling uses one rule, the same in Rust and the browser: a trace is sampled when the trace id's rightmost 7 bytes, read as an integer, are < `telemetry.sample_ratio` × 2^56 (0.0–1.0, default 1.0). It applies to roots and to browser-adopted contexts. Internal and machine adoption is parent-based. (b) **Server span and one `info` access log line per request** (method, route template, status, duration, request id) on every router, except `/healthz`, `/readyz` and `/metrics`. (c) **Trust (SEC-88)**: incoming trace headers are adopted parent-based only on internal listeners and on public routes for token-authenticated machine callers (runner plane, connector host, relay; a fixed list). On the other `/v1` routes, the browser's trace id and parent span id are kept, but `tracestate` and the sampled flag are ignored and the rule in (a) decides, so the dashboard's span is the API's parent but a browser cannot force sampling. Sandboxes (gateway proxy), SCIM, webhooks and callbacks start a new trace. Aurora sends `traceparent` only to its own services, never to third parties. (d) **Turns cross the queue through `turns.trace_context`**, written at insert. The runner (via its work item), the API (supervisor reports), the gateway (via the turn in the request identity), holds and hooks record their work as children of that context, read from the database or a trusted peer, never from the sandbox. (e) **Span attributes are an allowlist**: ids, route templates, statuses, hosts, model names, decisions, error codes, durations. No paths, queries, headers, bodies, message text, prompts, tool data, secrets or personal data. (f) Public API responses carry `x-request-id`, which the dashboard shows as a copyable reference on errors. (g) **Database spans (owner, same day)**: every sqlx query inside a sampled span becomes a child span, built from sqlx's own query event and back-dated by its elapsed time, so no call site changes. It carries the operation, table, parameterized SQL (≤ 4 KiB, never bind values) and row counts. Queries outside a request or job span (listener, empty polls) make no span, and a background job opens a root span only when it claims work. (h) **Browser tracing (owner, same day)**: when tracing is enabled, the dashboard lazily loads a small hand-rolled browser tracer (not the OpenTelemetry web SDK, D-253). It records navigation spans (route templates), API call spans named by the `openapi-fetch` route template (and sends `traceparent`), stream connects, action spans (send message, approval decision, app connect) and errors (type and stack, no message). It exports same-origin to `POST /v1/telemetry/traces`, so the CSP is unchanged. The API never trusts that payload: it requires a session, applies limits, replaces the resource, drops spans failing the sampling rule, keeps only allowlisted attributes, removes id-like route segments, clamps timestamps, then exports. (i) The chart gains `networkPolicy.telemetryTo` (collector egress; default-deny blocks it today), and kind dev gains `--tracing` (Jaeger, dev only). Sandboxes export nothing. Contract C-732. | Trace ids only when export is on (logs from different services stay unlinked without a backend); adopting `traceparent` from anyone (a browser or sandbox could force sampling or graft spans onto another request's trace); a new trace per hop joined by links (no single view of a turn); a vendor SDK such as Sentry's (OTLP reaches Jaeger, Tempo, a Collector, and vendors that accept OTLP); the browser exporting straight to a collector (a public collector, a CSP change, and nothing between the untrusted client and the backend); honouring the browser's sampled flag (any client could force full sampling); the SDKs' `TraceIdRatioBased` sampler (Rust and JS compute it differently, so the browser and API spans of one trace could disagree); wrapping every query call site for DB spans (hundreds of edits, and new queries could skip it) | One id ties logs and traces together, with or without a backend. Taking the turn's context from the database keeps the untrusted sandbox out of the trace graph, matching how the gateway already trusts only its own identity lookup. Browser spans pass through the API so they get the same allowlist as server spans. |
| D-240 | **FIX-133 (phase 5): read-only is a property of a connection, enforced by the gateway.** Owner feedback 2026-11-13: GitHub cannot be connected read-only (classic OAuth `repo` is read+write, installation tokens carry every App permission). (a) `app_connections.access` is `read` or `write` (default `write`, so every existing connection is unchanged); chosen at connect time (`access` in the connect request, offered on every connect method, recommended for new connections where the app has a read/write tool split) and changeable later with `PATCH /v1/connections/{id}` by whoever can manage it. (b) The gateway enforces it **before any rule** (like `block_untooled`): on a `read` connection only tools of the app's `read` group pass; a write tool, and any request that matches no catalogue tool (it cannot be proven a read), is blocked (403 `blocked_by_policy`, `details.reason = "read_only_connection"`), whatever the grants, rules or approvals say; never at `CONNECT` time. It composes by the usual strictness order and never adds an allow. (c) Aurora's enforcement is generic (any app with a read/write split) and always on; where the vendor can narrow the credential itself, the token is narrowed too (GitHub App installation tokens at mint: `permissions` and `repositories`), so a bug on either side still leaves the other. (d) The effective view evaluates a read-only connection the same way, so write tools show `block`. | The owner asked for least privilege that is visible and does not depend on the vendor offering a read-only scope. Doing it in the gateway answers every vendor whose scopes are coarser than Aurora's tools; narrowing at the source is defence in depth, not the only line. `access` is separate from the OAuth `access_level` (an authorize-time parameter some vendors define), which stays as it is. |
| D-241 | **FIX-136 (phase 5): tools declare the scopes they need; permissions follow the token.** Owner feedback 2026-11-13 (Indico): grant forms listed tools the connection's token could not serve, which then failed upstream after a person had granted them. (a) `tools[].scopes` (manifest contract 1, additive, absent = no requirement) lists scope ids, **any of**, that the tool needs; the linter requires each to be declared by one of the app's connect methods (`tool_scope_unknown`). (b) What a connection **has**: an OAuth connection's granted scopes (already `app_connections.scopes`, no new column); for a pasted token a `fields` method may declare `scopes` (the ones the token may carry) and `probe_scopes` (one harmless `GET` per **read** scope; write scopes are never probed, `probe_scope_write`); `POST /v1/apps/{app}/connect` takes `scopes` (each one of the method's, else prefilled by the probes), and `PATCH /v1/connections/{id}` edits them for such a connection only (C-741). (c) The gateway refuses a request whose matched tool declares `scopes` while the connection's **known** scopes (non-empty) hold none of them: 403 `tool_scope_missing` (`details: tool_id, needs`), **before any rule and before injection**, so the token is not sent; not at `CONNECT` time. **Unknown scopes (empty) refuse nothing**: declared scopes are trusted to *refuse* only, never to widen what a token can do. (d) The grant forms (Access tab and Give agents access, one `GrantForm`, D-233) list such tools collapsed under "Not available with this connection's token" with the scope they need, unselectable; the effective view evaluates the same. | Owner's question was exactly that: fewer permissions to configure when the token cannot do them. A pasted token cannot tell Aurora its scopes, so a person declares them and read probes help; refusing only on **known** scopes keeps a wrong or missing declaration from ever blocking a working token, and nothing in the declaration can grant anything. |
| D-242 | **FIX-135 (phase 5): one catalogue entry per product — "app families" for the genuine D-91/D-92/D-93 splits.** Owner review 2026-11-13 of every split that D-93's "a tool names exactly one host" caused. Sentry was simply unnecessary (D-243); the rest are technically justified (GitHub.com needs two hosts per connection and GHES has the API under `/api/v3`; Atlassian Cloud is OAuth 3LO on `api.atlassian.com/ex/<product>/<cloudid>` with Jira v3 / Confluence v2 while Data Center is a PAT on `{instance}/rest` with other payloads, D-92(a); Jira and Confluence are different products; Google's three apps are different products, D-94) but the user saw two products for one. (a) **`about.family: { id, name, variant_label }`** (manifest contract 1, additive; `09 §3.4 about`): apps of one package that are one product in different deployments share it; the apps are unchanged underneath, so SEC-04/SEC-59, the gateway and the per-app tool catalogues are untouched. (b) The catalogue shows one tile per family; the connect window's first question is where the product runs (hosted variant, then each registered server of the family's instance-bound apps, "Register a server…" for instance admins); everywhere else an app is named "<family> · <variant>" through one shared helper and component (`web/src/lib/api/app-name.ts`, `AppName`). (c) `github` 2.2.0 (`github`, `github-ghes`) and `atlassian` 1.1.0 (`jira`/`jira-dc`, `confluence`/`confluence-dc`) declare families; presentation only, no permission widens. (d) **Rejected:** merging GitHub and GHES into one app with multi-host instances and per-instance path templates (changes the one-host-per-connection guarantee to host sets for the same visible result; revisit only if a family needs shared grants across variants); **allowing a credential on subdomains of its host** (the agent chooses the request host and subdomains are often third-party, dangling or user content, e.g. GHES `pages.`/`raw.`/`gist.`/`media.` — the safe generalization, if ever wanted, is a finite set of exact hosts declared by the manifest per instance, never a wildcard of an admin-registered host; static wildcard claims stay limited to vendor-owned namespaces, `09 §7`). (e) Presentation unified by families: D-91 and D-92 notes. | Merging the apps; subdomain credentials; leaving two tiles | One product, one tile, with the exact-host guarantee left exactly where it is. |
| D-243 | **FIX-134 (phase 5): one Sentry app; a vendor's region hosts are preset instances.** Owner feedback 2026-11-13: "why isn't that implemented simply as separate instances, like for gitlab?" D-93(a) split Sentry into four apps because a tool names one host; the self-hosted app already showed the alternative (`{instance}`). (a) **Manifest contract 1, additive: `instances.presets: [{ host, label }]`** — hosts the package ships as ready-made instances, static claims signed with the package (like `default_host`, same §7 conflict rules), plain DNS names inside a registrable domain, distinct from `default_host` and each other. (b) **Implemented as built-in instance rows**: the API seeds one instance-wide `app_instances` row per preset (`builtin = true`) in the activation transaction and refreshes it on each activation, so everything that already understands a registered instance (connect flow, connections, gateway exact-host injection, library fetch, BYOC clients, the picker) serves a preset unchanged and SEC-04/SEC-59 hold without a new code path; a built-in row is read-only to everyone (`instance_builtin`). (c) **`sentry` 2.0.0 is one app**: `default_host: sentry.io`, presets `us.sentry.io` ("US region") and `de.sentry.io` ("EU region (DE)"), every tool on `{instance}`; the connect window asks where the organization lives. (d) **`list_organizations` stays on all hosts**: Sentry documents it on sentry.io only, but a per-tool host restriction would need a new manifest field for one tool; the tool still reaches only the connection's own host, and the agent text says region hosts do not serve it (revisit when a second app needs it). (e) **Migration** reuses FIX-123's supersede step (C-743): connections of `sentry-us` / `sentry-de` move to `sentry` bound to the preset, `sentry-self-hosted` keeps its registered instance; ids, grants (tool ids unchanged) and history stay; an entry whose old app lives in the same package retires no package. This supersedes D-93(a). | Per-tool host restrictions; a `preset_host` column on connections with a second resolution path in the gateway, connect flow and library fetch; keeping four apps | One registered-instance mechanism for every case keeps the exact-host guarantee in one place; the vendor's regions are the package's own data, not an admin's registration, hence built in and read-only. |
| D-234 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5, P5-ADMDEL): instance admins can delete any organization and any workspace.** The one exception to "instance admin grants no access to org data" (`04 §Instance admins`): removal, plus the metadata needed to choose what to remove — per organization name, slug, frozen and ownerless state, member, workspace and agent counts; per workspace name, slug, personal marker and agent count (C-733) — never content (no member names, conversations, agent configuration, credentials). Guarded like the most destructive self-service action: session only, a recent OIDC re-authentication (600 s, as account deletion), the slug typed to confirm. The deletes call the owner routes' services unchanged (one implementation: deprovisioning, secret discard, gateway invalidation, sandbox teardown, ledgers kept) and work on frozen organizations. Deleted workspaces are dropped from the placement's shared workspaces in the same transaction, on every delete path. Audited `admin.org.delete` / `admin.workspace.delete`, security-critical, as instance-level rows so the instance Audit page shows them by default. No e-mail to the organization's people. Dashboard: Admin › Organizations gets counts, an Actions menu (Freeze, Workspaces, Delete organization…) and an organization page listing its workspaces with Delete workspace…. | Admins joining the org as owner to delete it (grants content access and leaves a membership trail); a soft delete with a grace period; an `aurora-api` CLI command only | Operators need to remove abandoned, ownerless or abusive tenants without reading them; reusing the owner services keeps one tested deletion path, and the re-auth + typed slug + critical audit match the weight of an irreversible delete. |
| D-235 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (phase 5 planning): answers to SPEC-SETTINGS' open classification questions and the order of the unspecified items.** (a) **Sandbox resource sizes and `max_sandboxes` stay in Helm** (runner configuration; the dashboard shows them read-only), not moved to the app. (b) **OIDC providers are editable in the app** (Admin › Sign-in): providers incl. role/group mappings and JIT are instance settings; client secrets are sealed in the secret store; a provider defined in Helm stays possible and shows as "managed by Helm" (locked); `auth.instance_admins` stays in Helm as the break-glass list. (c) **Spec first, in this order of interest: additional LLM providers (P4-LLM), SSH access (P4-SSH-1/2), approval park-and-resume and Web Push (P4-011B)**; the rest (Slack, S3, evals, narration cards, user tokens) after them. (d) **P5-CERN-ADDONS: LDAP lookups run through a gateway lane** (the gateway performs the query; no directory credentials or raw LDAP egress in the sandbox). | (a) moving sizes to the app, validated against the runner's limits; (b) providers in Helm only; (d) an `ldap_search` sandbox tool | (a) sizes interact with namespace quotas and node capacity, which the operator owns in Helm; (b) every IdP change would otherwise need a `helm upgrade`, and the break-glass list keeps a bad sign-in setting recoverable; (d) keeps credentials out of the sandbox and puts LDAP under policy and audit like other app calls. |
| D-236 | **SPEC-SETTINGS (phase 5, D-195(b)–(d)): the instance-settings framework.** (a) **Storage**: one row per section in `instance_settings` (`03 §Planned changes`): a flat jsonb object of stored values keyed by the dotted configuration key, a `generation` bumped by every write and compared-and-set by it (409 `stale_revision`), the last writer. Sections and keys come from one registry in `aurora-config` (type, application default, Helm key, list kind, secret flag, loosening order); a key that is not in the registry is not a setting (400 `setting_unknown`). (b) **Effective value** = the deployment configuration's value when the key is present there (the TOML Helm renders, or an `AURORA__…` override; source `helm`, **locked**), else the stored value (`dashboard`), else the application default (`default`). The default lives in the application, never in the chart. Locks are per leaf key; whole-list keys lock as a whole; keyed lists (`auth.oidc_providers`, `secrets.stores`, `llm.endpoints` by `id`; `ratelimit.api.classes` by class) lock per entry, so a Helm entry ("managed by Helm", locked) and dashboard entries with other ids coexist; a dashboard entry whose id is a Helm entry's is refused with a field error (422 `settings_invalid`, code `entry_id_in_helm`), and an entry Helm adds later with a stored entry's id replaces it at the next start (mirroring). (c) **Helm mirroring**: at every API start, under an advisory lock, the stored value of each locked key is set to the Helm value (system row `instance_settings.helm_sync`), so removing a key from Helm hands it to the dashboard **with the value Helm had** — every service, the gateway included, then reads that stored value; nothing reverts to the default and no service needs a copy step of its own. (d) **Validation**: every write is validated on the merged view of all sections with the same functions as startup validation (`aurora-config/src/validate.rs`, cross-section rules included); invalid → 422 `settings_invalid` with `details.fields` naming keys. (e) **Writes**: instance admin, session only, re-authentication within 600 s (412 `reauth_required`), security-critical audit with a before/after diff in the same transaction, e-mail to every instance admin after commit; a write to a locked key → 409 `setting_locked` `{ key, helm_key }`, enforced in the one settings service for every route, import and the CLI. A **preview** route returns the diff, the keys that loosen and the organizations affected, which the dashboard shows before saving. (f) **Live**: after commit `NOTIFY aurora_settings { section, generation }`; the API replicas and gateways re-read the section, validate the merged view and swap it atomically; a 30 s poll of the generations backs the notify up and a LISTEN reconnect reloads everything; a reload that fails validation keeps the previous settings (never the defaults). Values a sandbox receives at boot apply on its next boot (`restart_to_update`). (g) **Secrets** entered in the dashboard are sealed in the **inline** store (purpose `instance_setting`), whatever `secrets.default_store` says, so sign-in and mail never depend on an external store, and are never returned, exported or logged. (h) **Export / import** (YAML or JSON, no secret values) with a dry-run diff, all sections of one import in one transaction. (i) Org and workspace layering is unchanged (`08 §11.1`): only where the instance level is stored moves.
