| D-291 | **P4-EVAL: the configuration fingerprint, recorded per run for regression attribution.** (a) A run records, once at start and never afterwards, a canonical JSON object of the configuration the answers depend on (C-866): the instructions' hash (`agents.brief`), harness, model provider, endpoint and model, effort, the effective skill set (tier, name and content hash, `10 §Skills`), the sorted logical ids of the granted policy rules that apply to the agent, and the question-set version (hash of the enabled questions' ids and versions). Its SHA-256 is `fingerprint_hash`. (b) Hashes, never contents or secret values, are stored in the run. (c) A run page shows the field-level diff against the previous run and against any chosen run (Q-EVAL-8); attribution is that diff, not an automatic cause. (d) Known gap, stated in `10 §Evals`: agent memory is not in the fingerprint in phase 5 (it changes agent-wide and is not versioned per run); the follow-up is the memory revision high-water mark. | Fingerprint per turn (heavier, and a turn's configuration is already on the turn row); storing full instructions in each run (copies sensitive text into rows that are kept longer); automatic cause attribution (a diff is honest, a cause guess is not) | Regression attribution needs to know what changed between two runs; hashes give that without copying the brief or secrets into eval rows, and the canonical form makes equal configurations compare equal across replicas. |
| D-292 | **P4-EVAL: judged questions go through `PlatformLlm` with a granted, governed judge key, and fail closed.** (a) **`PlatformLlm`** is the platform's internal LLM service (`08 §Platform-internal LLM calls`, the single choke point the rule names); it is the only path a judge call takes, and it records usage. Its first purpose is `eval_judge`. (b) The **judge key** is an LLM secret named in the agent's eval settings with a model (C-863); it must be granted to the agent, usable (`08 §Model readiness`) and admitted by governance for its endpoint and scope at every call, not only when saved (Q-EVAL-4, recommended: workspace or org scope; the key owner's account pays). (c) **Minimal input** (SEC-115): the question, its criteria and the final answer of that question's own turn; no other conversation, no tool argument or result, no expected answer of another question. (d) **Strict output**: `{verdict, rationale}`; anything else is `judge_unparseable`. (e) **Fail closed, no fallback**: every judge failure (not configured, not granted, not permitted, unavailable, timeout, unparseable) is an `error` for that question; there is no second key, no second provider and no retry on another route; a judged question is never a pass by default. (f) Judge usage is summed into the run (`judge_usage`), never into `turns.usage`. | An org-wide judge without a grant (would let any agent's answers reach a key nobody granted to it); the agent's own model as judge by default (self-grading); a fallback judge (silent change of who sees the answer); a 1 to 5 scale with threshold 4 (Q-EVAL-5 alternative) | The judge is a second place where agent output leaves the platform, so it gets the same grant, governance and egress rules as any model call, and a failure must never look like a pass. |
| D-293 | **P4-EVAL: schedules, limits and regression notices.** (a) An **eval schedule** is a row of its own (`eval_schedules`, C-870) that fires through the schedules engine: the same lease, the creator or workspace anchor and fire-time authority (`04 §Fire-time authority`), frozen-skip (`skipped_frozen`), busy-skip (`skipped_busy`) and `SCHEDULE_FAILURE_DISABLE`; its runs count toward the agent's daily run cap, so scheduled evals are automations under the same caps and never a second scheduler. (b) Cadence: at least `EVAL_SCHEDULE_MIN_INTERVAL` (one hour, Q-EVAL-3). (c) Limits (`02 §Limits`): `EVAL_QUESTIONS_PER_AGENT` 100, `EVAL_RUNS_ACTIVE_PER_AGENT` 1, `EVAL_RUNS_PER_AGENT_DAY` 20, `EVAL_TURN_TIMEOUT` 900 s, `EVAL_JUDGE_*`, `EVAL_ANSWER_CHARS`, `EVAL_RUN_RETENTION` 90 days. (d) **Regression notice** (C-871): a finished run whose pass rate is at least `EVAL_REGRESSION_DROP` (10 points) below the previous finished run of the same question-set version shows a notice on the Evals page to eligible viewers, with counts only; nothing is posted to a chat platform, e-mailed or pushed in phase 5 (Q-EVAL-7). | A separate eval scheduler (a second authority and cap path to keep right); unlimited scheduled runs (cost without bound); notices that quote answers (they would reach shared spaces) | An eval schedule that is not an automation would escape the caps and the authority rules that every other scheduled turn obeys; counts-only notices keep answers in the agent's audience. |
| D-294 | **P4-EVAL: visibility, retention and what never leaves the agent's audience.** (a) **Audience**: questions, expected answers, answers, judge rationales and results are visible to `can_use` of the agent's workspace (Q-EVAL-9, recommended); `can_own` alone can delete a run and change the judge (`04` matrix). (b) **Never outside the audience** (SEC-116): expected answers and answers are not in the sandbox, its boot document, a gateway credential, a connector post or notice, an audit row's metadata, the request log, a log line or a URL; audit rows carry counts and ids. (c) **Retention**: eval conversations follow the org's conversation window like any other kind; runs and results are deleted after `EVAL_RUN_RETENTION` (90 days, Q-EVAL-6); deleting an agent cascades to its questions, runs, results and schedules; deleting a run deletes its eval conversations through the normal delete path, including the harness session purge (`10 §Archive, delete, redaction, retention, export`). (d) **Expected answers are data for people, not for the agent**: they are never sent to the agent in any turn, and an agent cannot read another question's expected answer. | Expected answers readable by `can_own` only (Q-EVAL-9 alternative: safer for a shared workspace, more friction for the people who write questions); keeping runs forever (every answer ever given, retained with no owner); sending expected answers in the question text | An eval's answer key is as sensitive as the answers it grades; keeping it inside the same audience as the agent's transcripts means no new trust boundary, and bounded retention limits how long old answers exist. |
| D-295 | **P5-GUIDE amendment: no plugin notes in the instruction file, and recipes** *[(b) and (c) amended by D-452 and D-453 (owner, 2026-11-22): recipes become app skills, which may include scripts]* (owner, 2026-11-15: "I don't like every app filling the context with tokens that may never be relevant"; `24 §3.1`, `§5.1`, `§6`). (a) **Always the index** (amends D-171(c)): the *connected apps* fragment is one paragraph plus one line per app (name, id, account count, `summary`), whatever the size of the notes; the essentials, accounts and links are only in `guide.md`. `AGENT_NOTES_INLINE_MAX` is removed. The inline fragment survives only as the fallback for an API older than P5-GUIDE-2. (b) **Recipes**: a guide entry may be `kind: recipe`, a Markdown procedure for one task a person asks for ("Find a free room and book it"), listed first in `guide.md` and found by `aurora-agent search`; the addon equivalent of a skill. Recipes are text, never run, carry no permission, are labelled third-party and are shown in the review and audited like other guide files (SEC-117). (c) Executable recipes (package scripts) are out of scope until their own decision. | Keeping the inline mode under 4 000 characters (D-171(c) as written); addon-contributed harness skills (the owner's first question, 2026-11-15); letting packages ship scripts | The inline notes cost every session whether or not the app is used, which is the cost Code Mode exists to remove; the index costs one file read in the conversations that use the app. Skills depend on the harness (D-171(a)); a recipe in the app's folder gives the same "how to do this task" knowledge on every harness, and keeping it text keeps a package's capability equal to what the admin approved. |
| D-296 | **P5-GUIDE-6: the *search* and *execute* half of Code Mode is `aurora-agent`, one command in the sandbox** (owner, 2026-11-15; `24 §3.5`, `§5.4`, `§10a`, SEC-118, SEC-120, C-640, C-651..C-653, C-656). *[Owner, 2026-11-15, on "Do we need both?": the separate `aurora-app` and `aurora-tool` of the first draft are one command, `aurora-agent`, whose targets are connected app ids and the reserved id `aurora` for platform tools (D-297); one `search` covers both.]* (a) A package may ship one **operations file** (`aurora.operations/1`: id, method, path template, typed params, top-level body fields, pagination, items path, optional tool id), validated by the linter against a closed schema (credential headers, foreign hosts and raw query strings refused); `import-openapi` generates it. (b) The core renders per agent and app an **`api.json`** (`aurora.app-api/1`) with accounts, operations (package ones plus one per unmapped catalogue tool), the catalogue tool each maps to (by the gateway's own matcher) and advisory access per account. (c) **`aurora-agent`** (`aurora-supervisor agent`, wrapper `/opt/aurora/bin/aurora-agent`) lists targets, searches operations, recipes and platform tools, describes one and calls it; for an app: account header, segment-safe path encoding, typed argument coercion, pagination with same-origin `next`, response saved under `/tmp/aurora-agent/`, a bounded preview on stdout, distinct exit codes for gateway refusals. The app id `aurora` is reserved for the platform target. (d) It is an ordinary client through the inherited proxy: no credential, no new privilege; the gateway decides. (e) The supervisor knows locally whether its sandbox has the command (`agent_cli`) and mentions it in the instructions only then. | A JavaScript isolate or Skarn (D-170); MCP meta-tools `search`/`execute` for apps; generated per-language client libraries; the core calling app APIs on the agent's behalf | The agent already runs code in a sandbox behind the gateway, so a command is all the "execute" half needs; one binary serves every language and harness; result discipline enforced by the tool (save + preview) is reliable where a paragraph of instructions is not; the operations file is data the admin reviews, and nothing the package ships ever runs. |
| D-297 | **P5-GUIDE-7: platform tools on demand** (owner, 2026-11-15: "spec out fixing the MCP bridge's issues the same way"; `24 §10b`, `12 §Platform tools`, SEC-119, C-654, C-655). (a) Registry entries declare `exposure` (`core` / `on_demand`, default on demand) and a `family`; the core set is at most `PLATFORM_TOOLS_CORE_MAX` (8) and starts as `send_file` and `complete_task`. (b) The MCP bridge lists only the core set; calls to any tool name are still served. (c) The instruction file gets a *platform tools* fragment: one line per family (purpose and tool names) and the `aurora-agent … aurora …` verbs. (d) **Target `aurora` of `aurora-agent`** (D-296) lists, searches, describes and calls any available tool over the existing socket, using `AURORA_SESSION`, which the supervisor sets for harness processes; results over the preview bound are saved under `/tmp/aurora-agent/`. (e) **Mode switch** `hosted.platform_tools` = `on_demand` (default) / `all` (today's behaviour), carried in the render inputs; `all` is forced for images without the command. (f) Idle sessions are released only when the core list or the rendered instruction files change. (g) Before the defaults ship, eval runs compare `all` and `on_demand` (P4-EVAL). | Keeping all ~38 tools in the MCP list (24 §12 as written on 2026-11-13); relying on a harness's own deferred tool loading (harness-specific; Codex has none); MCP meta-tools (`aurora_search` / `aurora_call`) instead of a command | 2 000–3 000 tokens of tool definitions in every session for tools most turns never use is the problem Code Mode describes; a command keeps calls composable in scripts and results out of the context, works on every harness, and goes through the same socket, registry and checks, so listing changes and authority does not. |

The branch's SEC-90 (agent guide content) is renumbered **SEC-117** because SEC-90 is SPEC-SETTINGS on master; new invariants SEC-118..SEC-120; contracts C-651..C-657 from the branch's reserved C-640..C-659 block (C-658, C-659 stay reserved). | D-301 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (Q-GUIDE-1): platform-tool calls are tied to the process that made them.** A call on `/run/aurora/tools.sock` acts for a conversation only when the calling process descends from the harness process the supervisor started for that conversation's active turn: the supervisor reads the peer PID with `SO_PEERCRED` at accept and walks `/proc` ancestry to a registered harness (PID plus start time). Built as FIX-151 (SEC-121), in lane `p5-apps` before P5-GUIDE-6. | A per-session token in the environment (readable by any same-uid process through `/proc/<pid>/environ`); a per-session socket path (any same-uid process can connect to it) | Only the kernel's peer credentials and the supervisor's own record of which process it started cannot be forged by agent code running as the same uid. |
| D-248 | **P4-LLM-1 (lane `p5-agent`, coordinator 2026-11-15): three contract adjustments found while building the provider framework.** (a) `GET /v1/llm/providers` keeps each provider's `auth_modes` as `string[]` (the dashboard's LLM form reads it that way) and adds `auth_mode_details[]` with `{ id, value_shape, recommended }`; C-802 amended. P4-LLM-6 reads the details. (b) The upstream-classification metric follows the gateway's naming: **`aurora_gateway_llm_upstream_total{provider,endpoint,class}`** (C-808, `08`, `18`, the brief amended). (c) An endpoint entry with a malformed `organization`, `project`, `auth_header` or `models_path` is refused **`value_invalid`** (added to C-800's refusal list). | Objects in `auth_modes` (breaks the shipped form); the unprefixed metric name of C-808 (the only gateway metric without `aurora_gateway_`); reusing `base_url_invalid` for every malformed field | Additive and consistent with what clients and dashboards already read. |

(b) A provider whose origin proof is its validation (Vertex: the mint that proves `project_id` and `client_email`) is called **once** at save, with or without a key-origin rule (`LlmProvider::validates_by_origin`); there is no separate listing call. | D-302 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (Q-P6-MOD-1..6 of the P6-MODELS proposal, which this batch replaces): the harness chooses models, built in phase 5.** (a) The agent's model is the **default** the harness starts each session with. The harness may use any model governance allows on the same endpoint, and the effective allow-list stays the only model control. (b) Roles get harness-neutral names: `large`, `medium`, `small`. (c) Fallback across providers goes through the LiteLLM router, as an operator recipe. No Aurora-native model chain is built. (d) Subagent definitions stay with the agent or the person: they are agent-owned files in the home, and the platform neither ships nor projects any. (e) Agents get no model allow-list of their own. (f) Everything is built **in phase 5**, after P4-LLM-5, as P5-MODELS-1..3. That includes the fix for `claude-code` turns leaving subagent tokens out of usage, which was Q-P6-MOD-7. | D-303 | **Model roles, subagent default and model metadata (P5-MODELS-1).** Specified in `08 §Model roles` and C-875..C-878 and C-881..C-884.<br><br>**Roles.** An endpoint (`llm.endpoints[].model_roles`) and an agent (`agents.model_roles`) may name a model for each role. The agent's entry is stamped with its endpoint, like an override. Every model id is checked against `MODEL_ID_PATTERN`, the allow-list and the endpoint's static `models`. An agent role that becomes unusable is dropped with a notice and never re-pointed. An endpoint role that is not allowed for an org counts as unset for that org's agents, with a warning. Resolved roles travel in `ModelChoice.roles`.<br><br>**Harness mapping.** Each harness declares `model_roles` per pinned version. `claude-code` maps `large`/`medium`/`small` to its `opus`/`sonnet`/`haiku` alias variables; `codex` declares none. `agents.subagent_model` (`inherit` or a role) maps to `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=<alias>`.<br><br>**Model metadata.** `llm.endpoints[].model_info` (`context_window`, `capabilities`; named so that the endpoint entry's credential-name check, which refuses keys such as `token`, does not trip) feeds the CLI's per-alias capability variables and its context-window override, for model ids the CLI does not recognise.<br><br>Roles never cross endpoints, because one harness process has one base URL. The platform preamble gains a fixed **Models** line. | Gateway enforcement of "the agent's model": it would break subagents and the CLI's own background requests, and the allow-list already bounds them. Roles on other endpoints: a harness process has one base URL. Unstamped agent roles: a changed default endpoint would point them at models that endpoint does not serve. `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL_FORCE`: it would override definitions the person wrote. | In practice the harness already chooses the model. Roles make that usable on non-Anthropic endpoints, and the existing override rules carry over unchanged. |
| D-304 | **Usage per model (P5-MODELS-2).** Specified in `10 §Usage and metering`, `08` (*Error classification and usage*), C-879 and C-880.<br><br>**What harnesses report.** Harnesses report `by_model` entries. `claude-code` takes them from the result's per-model report, which includes subagents. Until now the adapter read the result's main-loop `usage` instead, which leaves subagents out, so this fixes an under-count. Turn totals are the sum of the entries. The CLI's cost estimates are ignored.<br><br>**What the API accepts.** The API keeps stamping `provider` and `endpoint_id` from `ModelChoice`. It accepts an entry's `model` only when that model is the default, a resolved role, or on the effective allow-list. When no allow-list applies, any id that matches `MODEL_ID_PATTERN` is accepted. Any other entry is folded into the default model's entry, and the turn's usage is marked `unverified_models`.<br><br>**Audit.** `request_log.llm_model` records the model of each request with an injected key on a governed LLM endpoint. It is for audit only. | Counting tokens in the gateway: it would have to read and parse response streams, which `08` rules out. Accepting the harness's model claims unconditionally: code in the sandbox could misattribute cost. Reconciling with `request_log` at turn end: log writes are batched, so the result would depend on timing. | The gateway already refuses any model outside the allow-list. The allowed set therefore bounds what could really have been used, at no cost and with no race. |
| D-305 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: organization deletion keeps its re-authentication, and a failed re-authentication becomes usable and diagnosable (FIX-152, C-890).** The owner reported that deleting an organization failed on a CERN SSO (Keycloak) deployment. The fresh sign-in was refused `reauth_stale`, which sent the still-signed-in owner to the sign-in page. Decisions: (a) the re-authentication within 10 minutes (D-234) stays; (b) once the flow state has opened, a failed re-authentication returns to the page it started from with `reauth_error=<code>`, and that page shows the error; (c) the `reauth_stale` denial records the `auth_time` age or that the claim was missing. The 60 s window and the required claim (FIX-108) are unchanged until that evidence shows what Keycloak sends. The fix for the root cause is a follow-up. | Dropping re-authentication for admin deletes; widening the window or accepting a missing `auth_time` without evidence; trusting `acr` (`04` never assumes it) | Deleting an organization is irreversible and affects every member. The fix should rest on what the IdP actually sends, not a guess. |
| D-306 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: connecting and granting apps must be findable, and an app that needs no sign-in takes one step** (the owner could not find where to connect CERN Phonebook from the org dashboard; briefs `FIX-153`, `FIX-154`; C-891..C-897).<br><br>**(a) No dead ends (FIX-153).** Every dashboard link the gateway puts in an error points at a page that exists, built in one place: connect = the C-842 deep link `/o/<org>/w/<ws>/credentials?tab=apps&connect=<app>`, reconnect = `…/credentials?tab=connections&reconnect=<id>`, store a secret = `…/credentials?tab=secrets`, give access = the agent's Access tab `…/agents/<agent id>?tab=access` (the agent page accepts an id and replaces it with the handle). The `apps/<app>` and `secrets` paths the gateway emitted until now have no page. The org **Apps** page lists the installed apps with **Connect…**, which asks for one of the caller's owned workspaces and opens that workspace's connect dialog; the org Connections empty state links there.<br><br>**(b) LDAP refusals say what to do (FIX-153).** `ldap_no_connection` carries `details.reason`: `not_connected` (no connection of the app in scope; message and `connect_url`), `not_granted` (a connection exists, this agent has none granted; message and `access_url`) or `no_app` (the host is no installed LDAP app). Plain HTTP to an LDAP host is a separate `400 ldap_https_required`. `access_restricted` gains `details.access_url` and its message points there (`manage_url` stays).<br><br>**(c) Credential-free apps (FIX-154).** An app is **credential-free** when it declares no credentials, no token, no instances, no `secret_link` and no `database`, and every connect method is `kind: fields` with no fields and no scopes (`cern-phonebook`, `cern-maps`). `AppSummary`/`App` carry `credential_free`. For such an app the agent's Access tab lists the app itself with **Give access**, which calls `PUT /v1/agents/{agent}/grants/apps/{app}`: in one transaction, serialized per (workspace, app), it reuses the workspace's oldest active connection of that app or creates one (label from `connect_note.label_hint`, else the app name; audited `connection.created` with `via: "agent_grant"`), then upserts the grant exactly like `PUT …/grants/connections/{conn}`. The connect dialog for such an app has no form: it shows the note and goes straight to choosing agents. Gateway refusals for a credential-free app point at the Access tab, not at connecting.<br><br>**What stays.** D-251(d) and SEC-110 are unchanged: the gateway still answers an LDAP request only for a granted connection of the app, so no agent reads a directory by default. The connection still exists, is listed under Connections and can be revoked; it is created for the person instead of by them. | Dropping the connection requirement for credential-free apps and making access a plain per-agent permission (a second grant model beside connections for the same rules, approvals, request log and revocation; owner chose to keep the gate). Granting every agent at package approval (exposes the people directory by default; may come later as an explicit opt-in). Auto-creating a connection in every workspace at approval (connections nobody asked for, in workspaces that never use the app). | The gate is right; the cost was that people had to learn the word "connection" and find a page for an app with nothing to enter. Creating the connection inside the grant keeps one model and one audit trail, and makes the common case one click. |
| D-307 | **Coordinator, 2026-11-15 (FIX-124 security review): the Vault `kubernetes` login method is for Helm-declared secret stores only.** A store added in Admin › Secret stores must use AppRole (literal `role_id`, sealed `secret_id`); a dashboard entry with `auth.method = "kubernetes"` is refused 422 (`dashboard_reference_refused` on `auth.method`). Reason: the kubernetes login reads the API (or gateway) pod's own service-account token and POSTs it to the store's address, so a dashboard store pointing at an attacker's host would hand over the pod's cluster identity; the address guard (S1) cannot stop it because a public host passes. Helm entries keep `kubernetes` (the operator chose the address). Same rule as the other review fixes: dashboard stores never read the pod's environment or files (D-237(b)). The owner can widen this later (e.g. an operator allow-list of Vault addresses for which `kubernetes` is permitted). | Allow `kubernetes` for dashboard stores (exfiltrates the service-account token); allow it only for addresses also declared in Helm (more machinery for no current need) | The pod's identity must never leave for an address an admin typed. |
| D-308 | **SET-3 (lane `p5-apps`, 2026-11-15): the `llm` section's cross-rules and the live reload.** (a) `llm.provider_preference ⊆ llm.enabled_providers` is enforced on a settings write **only when the dashboard wrote either list**: a deployment configuration that already has a preference naming a provider it does not enable keeps working exactly as before (`LlmSection::effective_provider_preference` appends the enabled ones), so the rule can never fail a reload of an existing install. Every other `llm` rule is startup validation's, run on the merged view (`api_config`/`gateway_config`), so a write and a start can never disagree; a name in `enabled_providers` that this release cannot run → 422 `provider_unknown`, an endpoint whose provider is not enabled → `provider_not_enabled` on `/llm.endpoints/<id>/provider`. (b) Startup validation errors on a keyed-list entry (`llm.endpoints[1].allowed_paths`) are reported as `/llm.endpoints/<id>/<field>` (index → entry id), the stable code of C-800 where the message carries one (`<code> (<field>)`), else `invalid`. (c) A credential-like or unknown key in an entry JSON is classified **before** the entry is deserialized (`credential_in_entry`, `key_unknown`, also inside `key_origin`), because the typed configuration would only say "unknown field". (d) One reload swaps the API's `ProviderRegistry` (`AppState::llm()`), the instance ceiling that carries the endpoints and the known LLM hosts, and in the gateway the policy resolver's and the governance step's tables, in that order, then flushes as `all`; a registry that does not resolve keeps the previous one. (e) The dashboard-only destination rules of D-262 (`http_needs_private_host`, `endpoint_host_claimed`, the guard allowance by source) stay with P4-LLM-6; until it lands a dashboard endpoint is judged by the Helm rules and the gateway's start-time guard allowance. | Enforce the subset rule on every merged view (a Helm install with an inconsistent preference would stop reloading settings); validate a dashboard entry with its own second rule set | Startup and write validation must be one function; a rule that can reject a configuration the operator already runs breaks the "Helm keeps working" promise of D-236. |
| D-309 | **SET-4 (lane `p5-apps`, 2026-11-15): the `sign_in` and `email` sections — what keeps sign-in safe when an admin edits it.** (a) **Secrets.** A dashboard provider's `client_secret` and `email.smtp_password` are sealed in the inline store (D-236(g)) and write-only. No environment variable or file is ever *named* from the dashboard (SEC-89/SEC-90: a name would make the API send that server secret to an admin-chosen issuer): a submitted entry that carries `client_secret_env` is refused (`dashboard_reference_refused`) unless it is the very entry that already did (a provider Helm used to define, handed over when Helm drops it) **and** its issuer and client id are unchanged; sending a new secret drops the reference. `{ "$secret": "keep" }` is bound to the **issuer and client id only** (other fields — roles, domains, scopes — may change while the secret is kept); any other change needs the secret again (`secret_reentry_required`). Any key outside the provider shape is refused by name (`credential_in_entry` for anything named like a secret, token, file or path, else `key_unknown`); only `client_secret` may carry a secret marker. (b) **Destination.** A dashboard issuer is `https` (plain `http` only for an address literal or `localhost`) and it and every endpoint its discovery document names (authorization, token, JWKS) pass the destination guard (`gateway.allowed_private` opens private ranges); a new or re-pointed provider is discovered on save (422 `oidc_discovery_failed` with `reason`, `issuer_not_https`, `destination_refused`). The provider test is the same discovery with no write. Residual risk: the guard resolves names once at save, the sign-in's later requests to the discovered endpoints are not dial-guarded (the same level as FIX-124's Vault addresses). (c) **Never strand the admin.** A save by an admin session is refused (422 `removes_own_provider`) when every provider the admin has an identity at is removed or re-pointed (issuer or client id changed — subjects are issuer-scoped); the preview warns the same, plus `identities_orphaned` (count of linked accounts per provider that stops working) and `grants_instance_admin`. The shell (`aurora-api settings`, `Via::Cli`) and the development IdP identity are exempt. The "at least one provider" startup rule is the write's 422 `last_oidc_provider`. (d) **Loosening** is judged per entry (a provider added; a mapping to `instance_admin`, an org role or a workspace role added or widened; JIT added or widened; `trusted_email_domains` added; `link_existing_users` turned on; more e-mail domains allowed; `require_role` dropped) and by order for sign-up mode, org creation and the session and CLI key lifetimes (`auth.cli_key_ttl_days = 0` = never is the loosest). (e) **Live.** One swap replaces the sign-in settings (`AppState::auth()`: providers, sign-up mode, lifetimes, environment client secrets) and the mailer (`LiveMailer`, built in the background; mail in flight finishes with the old transport); a dashboard client secret is opened per use. Lifetimes: a session is live while `expires_at` **and** `created_at + auth.session_absolute_secs` are in the future, so a shorter lifetime ends existing sessions at their next request (the session cache is dropped on a swap) and a longer one extends none. (f) `GET /v1/admin/identity` is removed (the Identity page is replaced by Admin › Sign-in). (g) No re-authentication is added (D-317); SET-1's existing `require_reauth` call stays until FIX-157 removes it. | Allow `client_secret_env` from the dashboard (exfiltrates any server secret); `keep` bound to every field (every role edit would re-ask the secret); hard-refuse every change to the signed-in provider (an admin could never fix a typo in it); validate discovery of every dashboard provider on every save (one IdP outage would block unrelated edits) | Sign-in is the one setting whose mistake locks everyone out or lets the wrong people in, so each rule above closes one concrete way to do that. |
| D-310 | **SET-4 security fix (lane `p5-apps`, 2026-11-15): the SMTP password goes only where it was configured to go.** (a) `email.smtp_password` is bound to `email.smtp_url` and `email.smtp_username`: `{ "$secret": "keep" }` with a URL or user name different from the stored one is refused on save, preview and the test e-mail (422 `secret_reentry_required`; the password must be typed again). (b) While Helm supplies the password (`AURORA_SMTP_PASSWORD`), `email.smtp_url` and `email.smtp_username` cannot be changed from the dashboard (409 `setting_locked`, on save and on the test e-mail's proposed values): the operator's password never goes to a dashboard-chosen host; to change the server the operator changes Helm. (c) A server named on the page (save and test e-mail; not Helm's) passes the same destination guard as Vault stores and OIDC discovery (422 `destination_refused`; `gateway.allowed_private` opens private ranges, metadata addresses stay refused). (d) The OIDC provider test (`provider-test`) is discovery only: it uses no client secret, so a changed issuer cannot receive one; the write path's `keep` binding is D-309(a). | `keep` bound to the password only (the stored password could be sent to any host an admin types); letting the dashboard edit the server while Helm holds the password (the operator's credential goes to an admin-chosen host) | Same class as FIX-124's B2: a stored secret must never follow an address an admin can change. |
| D-311 | **P4-LLM-3 (lane `p5-agent`, 2026-11-15): Azure OpenAI credentials.** (a) **A mode is admitted by the provider, the origin rule decides the rest.** A save names any mode the provider has (`azure_openai`: `api_key`, `azure_entra`); an `api_key` under a `provider_account` rule (instance or organization) is saved by nobody: the key cannot report its account, so it is 422 `llm_key_unverifiable` (C-804). `auth_modes` of an endpoint in `GET /v1/llm/providers` (C-802) stays the dashboard's hint computed from the instance entry's rule; it is not an admission test, because the rule in force may be the organization's. (b) **Entra claims are read, not verified.** The gateway and the API take `tid` and `appid`/`azp` from the JWT the pinned authority just returned over TLS and do not check its signature (they are not the audience; the claims are origin evidence only, never authorization). A token whose `tid` is not the tenant the secret names is `Rejected` (`mint_rejected`). (c) **`tenant_id` is a GUID.** Domain names and the aliases `common`/`organizations`/`consumers` are `tenant_not_specific`; the client id must be a GUID too. (d) **Validation of an Entra application** is the mint followed by `GET <base>/openai/v1/models` with the token; the `provider_account` origin is a second mint (one per call of the trait, not shared), the resource key's validation is the models call with `api-key`. A refused mint is 422 `llm_key_invalid` `details.reason: "mint_rejected"` (`LlmError::MintRejected`); a models answer of 401/403 stays reason-less `llm_key_invalid`. | `api_key` refused at admission under a rule (hides the rule from the organization's rule); verifying the Entra token's signature (needs the tenant's JWKS: another outbound call and cache for evidence the gateway does not act on) | The brief and C-804 name `llm_key_unverifiable` for a key under `provider_account`; admission by the instance entry alone would answer a different code depending on where the rule lives. |
| D-312 | **P4-LLM-4 (lane `p5-agent`, 2026-11-15): the organization context of a key check.** (a) A provider call that depends on the organization (`aws_assume_role`: the org's own `external_id` and `RoleSessionName = aurora-<org_id>`) takes a **`KeyCheck { mode, org_id, org_external_id }`** in place of the bare auth mode: `LlmProvider::{verify_key_origin_as, list_models_strict_as, list_models_as}` (the plain methods stay as `KeyCheck::new(mode)` for modes with no organization context). The API fills it from the database (`organizations.external_id`, never the request or the secret); an assume-role check with no external id fails closed with no STS call. (b) **`assume_role_denied` is a reason of `llm_key_invalid`** (`LlmError::AssumeRoleDenied` → 422 `llm_key_invalid`, `details.reason = "assume_role_denied"`), not a code of its own: an STS `AccessDenied` is the trust policy refusing the source key or the external id; every other rejected mint (`InvalidClientTokenId`, `SignatureDoesNotMatch`, `ExpiredToken`) is `mint_rejected`. (c) A `provider_account` rule mints once more for the origin (one mint per trait call, as D-311(d)); the role's account id comes from the assumed-role ARN, a static key's from a signed `GetCallerIdentity`. | A second `*_assume_role` trait family; an `assume_role_denied` error code; reading the external id inside `aurora-llm` | The trait is shared by the gateway-less callers (API validation, listing, origin); the organization is the one datum assume-role adds, and it must come from the one place the confused-deputy guard trusts. |
| D-313 | **P4-LLM-4 (lane `p5-agent`, 2026-11-15): how Bedrock requests are signed.** (a) There is **one SigV4 signer**, in `aurora-llm::sigv4` (also used by the STS and control-plane calls and by the gateway's `aws_sigv4` finalizer, which re-exports it). For Bedrock inference the gateway buffers the body (≤ `LLM_SIGN_BODY_MAX`, decided before any secret is opened, 413 `llm_request_too_large`), removes every agent `Authorization`, `X-Amz-*` (prefix match) and `x-api-key`/`api-key`, signs service `bedrock` for the endpoint's region with **`x-amz-content-sha256` = the SHA-256 of the buffered body** among the signed headers, and sets `X-Amz-Security-Token` for session credentials; the response is never read (D-260(d)). (b) A VPC `base_url` of a `bedrock` endpoint may be **plain `http` to a loopback host** (tests and development, as `azure_openai`, D-311's config fix; `08 §Configuration`); every other host must be `https`. (c) `llm.dev_upstream_overrides` also accepts the `https://sts.<region>.amazonaws.com`, `https://bedrock-runtime.<region>.amazonaws.com` and `https://bedrock.<region>.amazonaws.com` origins (loopback targets only, D-260(e)). (d) **The 413 follows the policy verdict and precedes the secret**: the winner is only known once policy has allowed the request, so the body is buffered for signing only for an allowed request (a blocked or held one is never read for it); `llm_path_not_permitted` stays before policy. `06` step 6a said "before policy" for both; it is corrected. | A second signer in the gateway; signing with `UNSIGNED-PAYLOAD`; streaming the request body to sign it (the signature needs the whole body hash first) | The agent's body hash cannot be trusted and a chunked-signature scheme is not supported by Bedrock's runtime; buffering with a bound is the only way to sign a body the gateway did not produce. |
| D-314 | **Withdrawn 2026-11-15 by the owner (never effective; kept for the record).** Why: the web terminal runs as the agent uid with `HOME=/workspace` and `/workspace/.local/bin` first on `PATH`, so agent code can shadow `claude`, plant `~/.zshrc` or attach to the terminal shell. A non-agent writer for `harness.env` would close one of those doors and leave the others open. The supervisor's own memory is already out of reach (`dumpable=0`), and keeping the harness's own tool children out of the harness's memory is worth little. Instead the terminal is accepted as part of the agent's environment: `17` residual, FIX-155 (wrapper hygiene plus a pane notice). FIX-151 is unaffected. Original proposal: **run the supervisor and the agent's harness under a different, non-root uid than the agent's interactive shell and agent-spawned processes.** Give the sandbox two uids: a supervisor/harness uid and an agent uid. Today PID 1, the harness and every agent process all run as uid 1000 (`images/sandbox/Containerfile` `USER 1000:1000`; `process.rs` only drops a child uid when the supervisor is root, which it is not), so the one wall protecting supervisor memory is `dumpable=0`, and any agent process can `process_vm_readv`/`ptrace` the harness (red-team F10) and rewrite any file the supervisor owns — mode is moot when owner == attacker (F12, `harness.env` and `ca.pem`; the `/run/aurora` residual in `17`). **Confirmed live 2026-11-15:** `process_vm_readv` read the harness's memory with `ptrace_scope=0`, and both `harness.env` and `ca.pem` are agent-owned `0644` written by the uid-1000 supervisor, so root-owning them (the clean fix the follow-up report proposed) is impossible without this change — a non-root process cannot create root-owned files. A second uid makes those kernel-enforced rather than resting on `dumpable=0` and on FIX-151's per-call ancestry check (which same-uid injection can defeat — FIX-151 §Design(6)). Touches D-28/D-29 (bare pod, non-dumpable supervisor), D-30 (rootless posture), the Containerfile `USER`, and the `process.rs` uid-drop path. Owner must decide the privilege model (supervisor starts as root to drop the agent shell to a second uid, or two non-root uids arranged another way) and the tradeoff against the current rootless container. FIX-155 is the interim hardening (wrapper no longer trusts `harness.env`) until this lands. | Keep one uid with `dumpable=0` + FIX-151 as the only walls (leaves F10/F12 open: same-uid memory read/write of the harness, and an agent-writable file a human terminal sources) | `dumpable=0` and a per-call process check are same-uid mitigations; a uid boundary is the only kernel-enforced line between untrusted agent code and the supervisor/harness. The sandbox's whole job is to run untrusted code, so "only agent code is in the pod" is not a safe premise. |
| D-315 | **Coordinator, 2026-11-15 (Dependabot PR #22): the workspace MSRV rises from 1.88 (I-21) to 1.95.** `kube` 5.1 (needed with `k8s-openapi` 0.29) declares `rust-version` 1.95. The build machine runs Rust 1.99 and CI and `rust-toolchain.toml` use stable, so nothing that builds Aurora is below 1.95; the workspace `rust-version` is set in the same change as the kube bump. Amends I-21's MSRV only (its RUSTSEC notes stand). | Stay on kube 4 / k8s-openapi 0.28 and ignore the kube line (falls behind on Kubernetes API support) | No supported build environment is affected. |
| D-316 | **P5-FILES (phase 5, owner 2026-11-15): the agent's files in the dashboard — a Files page in the Computer section, over SFTP.** (a) **Transport**: the API is an SFTP client of the image's `sftp-server`, started by the supervisor as an `exec` in mode `sftp` (`12 §SSH channels`) over the runner's :4108 listener with a new single-use grant `auf_` (`16 §W21`, C-901); the sandbox gains no listener, no network path and no file-handling code of the platform's own — path resolution, permissions and atomic rename are OpenSSH's, as the agent uid. P5-FILES-1 builds the `exec.*` leg 2 and the pipes-only `sftp` mode ahead of P4-SSH-2, which then adds `shell`/`exec`/`tty` on the same engine. (b) **No gate of its own**: Files exists exactly where the web terminal does (`terminal.enabled`, hosted, `can_use`, session only); it is the terminal's privilege with a different interface (D-314 withdrawal: the terminal is part of the agent's environment). (c) **Scope `/workspace`**: requests and resolved paths must stay under `/workspace` (symlinks resolving outside are refused), `/workspace/.aurora` is read-only; the scope is presentation, not a security boundary (SEC-125). (d) **Frozen agent: no access**, like the terminal. (e) **Limits**: 512 MiB per upload, 1 MiB editable text, 5,000 entries per listing, 10,000 entries per recursive delete. (f) **Content never runs in the dashboard's origin**: downloads are attachments with `octet-stream`, `nosniff` and a sandbox CSP; image preview only for magic-byte-checked rasters as `data:` URLs; no SVG or HTML preview (SEC-126). (g) Editing is optimistic (`If-Match` with a SHA-256 etag, temp file + `posix-rename`), with Reload / Overwrite / Copy my text on conflict. (h) Placement: lane `p5-platform`, before P4-SSH-1/2. Contracts C-898..C-907. | (a) New `fs.*` frames with platform file code in the supervisor; (b) an operator switch `files.enabled`; (c) the whole filesystem the agent uid can reach; (d) read-only downloads while frozen | (a) OpenSSH's `sftp-server` is mature, already required by P4-SSH-2 and runs with exactly the terminal's privilege; our own file protocol would be new path-handling code in the sandbox to audit. (b) Anyone who has the terminal can already read and write every file, so a second switch adds configuration without adding protection. (c) The agent's work lives in `/workspace`; the rest is image content. (d) A freeze stops every interactive path. |
| D-317 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: no operation requires an OIDC re-authentication any more (supersedes D-305(a) and the re-authentication parts of RFE-004, D-132, D-234, SEC-90 and FIX-124; built as FIX-157).** **Evidence:** the FIX-152 log line on the owner's deployment read `auth_time_missing: true`. CERN SSO (Keycloak) sends no `auth_time` even when `max_age=0` is requested. Requesting the `basic` scope, which carries the claim, is refused `invalid_scope` for the client. A fresh sign-in therefore could never be proven, and every guarded operation was unusable. **The requirement is removed from:** (a) account deletion (`DELETE /v1/me`); (b) admin organization and workspace deletion; (c) SCIM enable and SCIM token creation; (d) instance settings writes and import (`PUT /v1/admin/settings/{section}`, `POST /v1/admin/settings/import`); (e) the admin secret-store writes. **Every other guard stays:** session only where it was (API keys, agent tokens and non-admins are still refused), typed-slug confirmation, compare-and-set, the critical audit rows, the admin e-mail, and Helm-locked keys. **The `reauth=1` sign-in flow stays in the API** (`start`, callback, `sessions.reauth_at`, C-890), unused by any operation, so a later step-up can reuse it. No migration. The dashboard drops its "Sign in again" steps. | (1) Trust the IdP's `prompt=login` and stop requiring `auth_time` per provider: it would keep the step-up and still need the CERN password. (2) Drop re-authentication only for the admin deletes. (3) Use Keycloak's `acr` as freshness proof (`04` never assumes `acr`). (4) Get the `basic` scope or an `auth_time` mapper assigned at CERN. | The owner's choice. With CERN SSO the check could never pass, and the owner judged a session plus explicit confirmation sufficient. **Accepted risk:** a stolen dashboard session of an instance admin or org owner can now perform these operations without the IdP password. The audit rows and admin e-mails remain the detection path (`17` residual risks). |
| D-318 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: the S3 blob store moves to phase 6; OPS-022 is cancelled ("we don't need it").** (a) **P4-S3** (S3-compatible `BlobStore` and `aurora-api blobs migrate`, D-120, SEC-63) leaves phase 5's wave-3 queue (lane `p5-platform`) and joins the phase-6 table. It keeps its id, its spec (D-120 and the sections it names, now tagged "phase 6 (D-318, moved from phase 5)") and its brief `docs/tasks/P4-S3.md`. Like the rest of phase 6 it waits for the owner's all-clear (D-278). Attachments stay in Postgres until then. Nothing was built, so nothing is removed. (b) **OPS-022** (multi-arch amd64 + arm64 images on native arm64 runners) is **cancelled**, not deferred. Images stay linux/amd64 only (`15 §Images`). The brief is kept for the record, marked cancelled. The phase-5 exit audit has no multi-arch criterion. | (a) Keep P4-S3 last in `p5-platform`. (b) Keep OPS-022 deferred | Owner's call on scope: phase 5 closes sooner, and no arm deployment exists or is planned. |
| D-319 | **Owner, 2026-11-15: on chat platforms the person can see the agent is working until the answer posts (built as FIX-158, C-908).** **Report:** on Mattermost, the 👀 receipt was removed before the first tool call of a 9-tool turn had finished. After that nothing showed until the answer posted; the dashboard showed the work. (a) **The receipt belongs to the message**: it is removed only when that thread link gets the answer or the failure line, or the turn is aborted, or the turn ends with nothing posted and no follow-on work pending. Follow-on work means an armed watch, a pending approval or hold, a joining follow-up, or an active turn. An orphan sweep clears receipts left open after `RECEIPT_ORPHAN_SECS`. A remove never runs before its add. (b) **Typing indicator**: while a turn for a thread link runs, the core sets the platform's working status (`status.set` / `status.clear`). Mattermost declares `native_status` and shows "is typing…" by refreshing `POST /users/{id}/typing` until the clear or a cap. The 👀 receipt stays as well. (c) The cause of the early removal is investigated first (FIX-158 Part A: deployment query and receipt log lines). | Keep turn-scoped receipts and add only a typing indicator; post a "Working…" message | The owner wants visible feedback for the whole wait. The receipt is the immediate signal and typing the ongoing one, and a working post would be noise in the thread. |
| D-320 | **Owner, 2026-11-15 (P5-WEBHOOK): an "Outgoing webhook" app lets agents call a webhook the person set up. The webhook's URL is the secret, and the agent fills in only the blanks of a template the person wrote.** Owner: "the full webhook URL would be the secret, so this should be handled similar to the ICS addon … the user will probably need to specify the remote URL to hit and the shape of the contents, so agents know what's expected on the other end." Answers to Q-P5-WH-1..10 (`docs/tasks/P5-WEBHOOK.md`): (a) **The send mode of the secret-link lane** (C-843 extended by C-909/C-910). The person enters a name and the full `https` URL (Q6: `https` only). The agent sends to `<connection_id>.link.aurora.internal`, and the gateway sends to the stored URL. There is no request at connect, because a webhook acts when called. (b) **The payload is a template with typed slots** (Q1, Idea C). The person writes the body (JSON, form or text), with `{{slot}}` blanks and fixed parts. The agent supplies only slot values, which the gateway validates and inserts in one pass with the content type's escaping. "Free-form JSON" is a template with one `json` slot. (c) **Secret values** (Q4): ≤ 4 sealed values, used as `{{secret:name}}` in headers or the body. **Signing** (Q5): an optional `X-Aurora-Signature` (HMAC-SHA256 over `<t>.<body>`), with the secret generated or pasted and shown once. Every send carries `X-Aurora-Delivery`. (d) **The default for `send` is Ask** (Q3). The approval card shows the webhook's name and host and the slot values, never the URL, the fixed parts or secrets. (e) **Redirects** (Q7): ≤ 3, `https`, guard and policy per hop; `307`/`308` keep method and body, `301`/`302`/`303` become a body-less `GET`. There is no automatic retry and no cache. (f) **Configuration in the dashboard** (Q8): presets shipped as data in the package (no code per provider, as D-273), an example picker that turns a pasted payload into a template, a raw editor, a server-rendered preview, and Send test. (g) **Everything goes through `aurora-agent`** (Q9, owner: "everything should be exposed through `aurora-agent`"). Each webhook is an operation of the `webhook` target, bound to its connection, with its slots as body fields (C-911). There is no separate `webhooks.json`; plain `curl` stays possible through `tools.md`. (h) **Configurable limits** (Q10): defaults of 30 a minute and 500 a day per connection. They are an instance setting (section `outgoing_webhooks`), an org may tighten them, and a person may lower them per connection; the effective limit is the lowest (C-912). The org switch `outgoing_webhooks.enabled` (default on) is as for `calendar_invitations.enabled`. (i) Phase 5 (Q2), in `p5-apps` after P5-ICS. | A description only, or a JSON Schema the agent's whole body must match (the agent would still write the fixed parts: channel, card layout, routing key); one package per service (rejected for ICS as not scalable); show the URL to the agent (it is a bearer secret); a built-in platform tool `webhook_send` (it would bypass the gateway's grants, approvals, request log and egress policy); a separate `webhooks.json` beside `aurora-agent` (two ways to learn the same thing) | The person fixes the destination and everything the agent may not change. The agent fills in typed blanks, and the secret never enters the sandbox. Presets and the example picker make "the shape of the contents" a few clicks for any receiver, with no core change per receiver. |

Instance admins are never named. (b) A manifest may mark a method `audience: admin` (C-956). Such a method is never suggested but stays under Advanced. The builtin GitHub App installation method is `admin`. (c) The suggested method is the first ready person method, in the order: recommended, oauth2, fields, hook. (d) `scope_presets` (C-956) turn "Read only / Read and change" into scopes, so people never pick raw scope ids unless they open Advanced. The flow object is already authenticated and bound to the user, and the re-check closes the window in which the caller's role or the agent could change during sign-in. |

Methods may declare `show_context: [org_external_id]` so the form shows the value with Copy (C-942), also on the Bedrock assume-role form (C-811). The external id stays non-secret (it is already readable by every org member, `GET /v1/orgs/{org}`; SEC-97 unchanged). | Fix the guides' wording only (they drift again); hide the external id from members (it is not a secret, and the AWS role cannot be made without it) | Six builtin guides named an "Admin, Apps, <app>" page that does not exist, and the one value the recommended AWS method needs was shown nowhere. |
| D-335 | *[Amended by D-350: health also comes from real use, for accounts of every kind; the route is `POST /v1/accounts/{type}/{id}/test`.]* **P5-UX-14: connections can be tested, and show their health.** `POST /v1/connections/{id}/test` (C-943) runs the method's probe, a gateway database login to `ReadyForQuery`, or a hook mint, and answers ok / rejected / unreachable / unexpected / not testable, never with upstream content; the result is stored (`last_check_at`, `last_check_result`, C-944) and shown next to "Last used". Methods without a probe are tested once right after connecting, as a warning. SEC-129. | Make every method declare a probe (impossible for a database lane and some hooks); test continuously in the background (cost, noise, rate limits upstream) | A typo'd database host was "connected" and "Never used" until an agent failed. An explicit, on-demand check closes that without background load. |
| D-336 | *[Amended by D-352: no agent name on New workspace; the rest is folded into P5-UX-2, -4, -11 and -13.]* **P5-UX-15: names and labels that tell things apart.** Multi-workspace agent pickers lead with the workspace ("Platform team · Daneel"); New workspace takes an optional agent name (default `agents.default_name`); OAuth client pickers label clients by the sign-in methods that use them (`used_by_methods`, C-946), never by `client_key`; GitLab's methods get labels and descriptions (C-949). | Rename every default agent per workspace automatically (breaks the shared-bot and personal-workspace conventions of `10 §Default agent`) | The walkthrough showed two identical "Daneel @daneel" rows and four GitHub clients told apart only by internal keys. |
| D-337 | *[Amended by D-344 and D-345: the access step is the one access choice; "Another website or API" is the last app tile; repository limits are not built.]* **P5-UX (phase 5, coordinator 2026-11-15, from the owner's first-run walkthrough): giving an agent an app is one journey that ends in a connection and a grant.** (a) The primary entry is the agent (Access › **Add an app**, "Give <Agent> <App>"); Welcome and gateway refusals link there (C-959); the Apps tab's Connect opens the same journey with an agent step. (b) Steps App → Account (reuse an existing connection, which saves only a grant) → What may <Agent> do (Read only, recommended; Read and change, asking you first; Read and change without asking, under More choices) → Sign in (only the suggested method) → Done. (c) The C-842 controls (method chooser, scopes, OAuth client, label) stay, under **Advanced**; Reconnect is unchanged. (d) "Asking you first" is a custom grant with read tools `allow` and write tools `ask`, so approvals carry the change and the person decides each one. Specified in `14 §Agents, accounts and access`, C-914, C-955..C-959. | Keep the workspace-first dialog and add only a "next step" link to the Access tab; a wizard per app written in each package; auto-granting every agent of the workspace | Generalizes D-306(c) (connection created inside the grant) to apps that need sign-in. People think "let Daneel use GitHub", not "make a connection, then a grant". One request makes both, so nothing is left half-wired, and the full controls stay for people who need them. |

Each topic is one page with tabs at Administration, organization and workspace level, following the route table of `14 §Information architecture`. Every old route redirects (C-960). "Governance", "Policy" and "Credentials" stop being page names; the vocabulary table of `14` binds all UI text (Service for a generic secret, Packages / Package sources / Publishers / Host conflicts for the catalogue parts; Self-hosted apps and OAuth clients keep their names). Members see Workspaces and read-only AI models, Internet access and Agents (C-961). Administration gets a labelled entry and every page a level badge; the org switcher lists organizations only (C-962). | Keep the current pages and add cross-links (four homes stay four homes); group only the Admin sidebar (the org and workspace levels keep different names for the same thing) | Internet access was on four pages under three names, BYO on three, retention on four, and Administration had 18 items plus 13 settings sections. One vocabulary across levels is what makes a setting findable the second time. |

| D-344 | *[Amended by D-353: only the organization's accounts have a limit, with the same three choices; Always allow keeps Ask before changes.]* **One access choice per agent and account** (review R1). For each account an agent can use, a person chooses **Read only**, **Ask before changes** or **Change without asking** (or Custom, per tool). An account used by one agent has no other access setting: one save sets the agent's access and the connection's `access` underneath (C-980). Only a **shared** account (the organization's, or one given to more than one agent) has a **limit**, shown on its row and set by whoever manages it; choices above it are not offered. A saved choice above a lowered limit reads as the limit. The gateway's enforcement is unchanged (D-240). Amends D-331 and D-333: no "can never run" state exists any more. | Keep connection access and the grant as two controls and explain conflicts (the earlier draft's `unreachable`) | "Four places decide whether Daneel may write" was the second walkthrough's main finding. For one agent and one account there is one decision, so there should be one control. |
| D-345 | **One list of accounts, three kinds** (review R4, R5, R12). It replaces the Secrets, LLM keys and Connections tabs, the Access tab's three sections and the "Overview" of the earlier draft. "Service" is not a concept: "Another website or API" is the last tile of the connect journey. In a workspace, AI keys are rows of this list; AI models is a topic only for organizations and Administration. The interface says "account" where it said connection, secret, service or credential; the person's own account is always "your account". Amends D-327(a) and D-338. | Keep a Services tab and an AI models page per workspace; keep "connection" | A one-agent workspace showed the same information in four places under four names. |

The Internet access page lists only the rules people wrote, with one line pointing to the agents' access. The "From agents' access" group and `derived_from` are dropped (C-935 not built). An account row on the Access tab expands to **What exactly?**: each tool with Allowed, Asks you or Blocked, and a reason only when something other than the agent's access decided it (C-934). Rule ids are shown nowhere (C-918 not built). Amends D-331. | Show compiled rules read-only (teaches an implementation detail) | People never need to know that access is compiled into rules; they need to know what the agent can do. |
| D-347 | **Internet access: little to decide in a workspace, one way to save** (review R3). A workspace's page is the summary, two Common changes (Ask me before changes on a website; Block a website), the rules people wrote, and the limits from above with the workspace's four controls behind **Tighten…**. "Only allow websites I list" is not a Common change. Every Internet access page has one pending-changes bar with Apply changes and Discard; "Save draft" is gone (C-989). The organization's page applies its limits and its rules with one button. Amends D-327(b). | Six open sections on the workspace page; two save models on one page | Most workspace owners should never touch the defaults; a page with "Save" and "Apply changes" side by side had two mental models. |
| D-348 | *[Amended by D-353: after Always allow the access stays Ask before changes with the change listed as always allowed; no nudge.]* **Approvals are the everyday experience; an agent can ask in the conversation** (review R6 and blind spots). (a) An approval reads as a sentence ("Daneel wants to create an issue in Linear") and has three answers: **Allow once**, **Always allow**, **Block**. Always allow updates the agent's access for that kind of change in that account, in one transaction with the approval, for people who may change that access, never above a shared account's limit (C-982, SEC-131). (b) Approvals appear in the conversation where the agent paused: the API now writes the transcript events the dashboard already renders (C-983). (c) An agent's request for an app is a card in the conversation with **Give <Agent> <App>**; when access is given the conversation says so and, if the turn had ended, the agent is started again with a fixed message (C-984, SEC-132). | Leave approvals as Approve/Deny on a page; leave requests as grey text with an unclickable link | "Ask before changes" is the recommended default, so approvals are what people do most; today no chat card is ever shown and each write asks for ever. Always allow removes the need to edit per-tool access up front. |
| D-349 | **One way to say who can fix something** (review R7). One server shape, `WhoCan` (action, role, up to three names, a path when the viewer can do it themselves), and one component render every "who can help" sentence: refusals, empty states, blocked connect methods, setup pages, guide links, ladder rows and shared accounts (C-981). Instance admins are never named. Replaces `Unlock` and `required_role` of D-321 and D-323. | Six separate mechanisms | The same question deserves the same answer everywhere. |
| D-350 | **Facts, not promises: one summary of what an agent can do, and health from real use** (review R8 and a blind spot). (a) One function produces the sentences that describe an agent ("Daneel can read GitHub and Linear. It asks you before changing anything in Linear."), shown on Welcome, on the Access tab and on Internet access; Welcome makes no promise the summary does not show (C-986). (b) An account's health comes from real use: the gateway records when an app refuses an account's credentials and when it works again; an expired sign-in, a refused token or an unreachable host is one line on the account's row, on the agent's header and above the message box, with Reconnect or Replace token (C-985, C-945). Test stays on demand (D-335). | A fixed safety sentence on Welcome; health only from a manual test | Welcome said the gateway "asks you before risky actions" while a fresh workspace asked nothing. An expired token surfaced only as an agent failure. |
| D-351 | **Structure, revised** (review R11, R12; amends D-338, D-339, D-342). Topic names are the same at every level: **People** (not "People & sign-in"), **Apps & accounts** (not "Apps & services"). No topic has more than four tabs. A workspace has no AI models page and no OAuth clients tab (an app's setup page holds the workspace's clients). Org members no longer see the organization's Agents topic. The ladder's "below" table exists only for Internet access. The model is stated in five sentences that Welcome, the tour and help repeat. The vocabulary table adds account, App, Website, AI key, access, limit, "Check what would happen" and "your account". Renames ship together, and people who used the dashboard before see a one-time "Pages have new names" notice (C-987). | Six tabs per page; different topic names per level; silent renames | The review counted six tabs on three pages and three names for the people page. |
| D-352 | *[Amended by D-353: the order inside each wave, and the display-name setting is cut.]* **Delivery: fixed waves, merged briefs, cuts and what waits** (review R9, R10, cuts). (a) Twenty briefs in four fixed waves, with no "if the other merged first" branches: wave 0 FIX-159 → P5-UX-2, P5-IA-1; wave 1 P5-UX-1 → P5-IA-2 and P5-UX-4, P5-UX-13; wave 2 P5-UX-3 → P5-UX-9 → P5-IA-3, P5-UX-3 → P5-UX-14 → P5-UX-16, P5-IA-4, P5-IA-5; wave 3 P5-UX-7 → P5-UX-5, P5-UX-6, P5-UX-10, P5-UX-17 → P5-UX-11. A brief starts when the briefs it names under "Wave and order" have merged; nothing is built twice. (b) P5-UX-8 is merged into P5-UX-2, P5-UX-12 into P5-UX-4 and P5-UX-13, P5-UX-15 into P5-UX-2, -4, -11 and -13; new P5-UX-16 (approvals) and P5-UX-17 (an agent asks). (c) **Release gate:** FIX-159 can ship alone. After it, master is not deployed between the first other merge of wave 0 and the end of wave 1, so nobody sees new names on old pages or links to pages that do not exist yet. (d) Cut: the starter-grant option in the organization's connect flow; an agent name on New workspace; Approver in Add people unless the workspace already has one; the Copy request button; the "below" table for BYO; the workspace OAuth clients tab; repository limits in the journey (`limit` manifest key). (e) Waiting, recorded in `14 §Agents, accounts and access` "Not in this phase": a landing page for people in several organizations, offboarding, "Same access as <agent>", a glossary, a history of access changes, what org owners see of personal conversations. | Twenty-one briefs with soft dependencies | Each conditional was a double implementation and an intermediate state. |
| D-353 | **Refinements after two independent reviews of the reworked set** (2026-11-16; one reviewer for cross-references, one for what a person sees; within the owner's "adopt all recommendations", D-343). (a) **A limit exists only on the organization's accounts, and it is one of the same three choices** (Read only, Ask before changes, Change without asking). A workspace's account has no limit, with one agent or several: the connection's `access` follows its agents. Lowering a limit lowers the agents above it and says so first (C-990, `app_connections.limit_level`; amends D-344). (b) **Always allow keeps "Ask before changes"**: the row lists the changes that are always allowed, each removable. Custom is only what is neither a level nor a level with such exceptions. The "allowed 3 times this week" nudge is not built (amends D-344, D-348). (c) **One table of an app's actions**: **What exactly?** and **Custom…** open the same table, where people who may change the access set each action and everyone sees what actually happens and why (amends D-346). (d) **One rule for the journey's buttons**: "Give <Agent> an app" or "Give <Agent> <App>" when an agent is known, "Connect" when none is; an existing account still asks for the access; all three choices are shown; a family assumes the hosted service unless a self-hosted server is registered. (e) **One wording per thing, written once in `14`**: the health lines and their fixes, the Done sentences, the summary sentences, the role phrases of `WhoCan`, **Turn off** and **Turn on**. Briefs quote them. (f) **What a person sees of something they cannot do**: a page's main action is replaced by the `WhoCan` sentence in the same place; other controls are not shown; values read as text. (g) Smaller: Add people says that Aurora sends no e-mail and gives the link; Administration › Overview shows when e-mail is not set up; an organization's AI key is added on the organization's Accounts; app names in Administration open the catalogue entry; personal workspaces are named after the person by a fixed rule, with no display-name setting (amends D-326); Welcome is the landing page until the guide is finished or skipped. (h) **Order inside the waves**: P5-UX-1 first in wave 1 (it builds `WhoCan`); P5-UX-3 → P5-UX-14 → P5-UX-16 (the journey's Done step, then the gateway files); P5-UX-17 → P5-UX-11 (both edit the access code); P5-IA-4 alone moves the workspace's BYO setting; P5-IA-2, not P5-IA-1, renames the labels inside the Internet access editor. The vocabulary test's list of files can only shrink. | Keep the limit as read or write with a note; show Custom after Always allow; a separate read-only "What exactly?" table next to a Custom editor; several button labels for one flow; a display-name template setting | The reviews found one flow under six names, three overlapping explainers, a limit that spoke another language than access, an answer (Always allow) that broke the three-choice model, and pairs of briefs editing the same files with no order. Each fix removes a concept or a second way of saying the same thing. |
| D-354 | **The redesign canvas is the design reference for P5-UX and P5-IA** (owner, 2026-11-16: “Make sure this artifact is mentioned in the specs so the UI designers use it for the redesign … the redesign is good to go”). The design canvas “Aurora — agents, accounts and access” (https://claude.ai/artifact/3UwmUgrhh6MLCsfqdhTFmQ; 64 boards, a spec note beside each) is the visual reference for every P5-UX and P5-IA brief and for dashboard screens built after them (`14 §Visual design` › “Redesign canvas (phase 5)”). It adds a navy navigation rail in both themes, a sentence opening every topic page, and one three-way access switch; colours, type and spacing stay the D-68 tokens. The text wins: where a board and `14` or `API_CONTRACTS` disagree, build the text and have the board corrected; sample text on boards is illustrative. A spec change that changes a screen updates its board in the same change. Each brief names its boards under “Linked requirements”. | Mock-ups in the repository (`docs/design/`); no visual reference | A screen per brief, drawn once with the spec beside it, lets twenty implementers build one product; the canvas is linked, not copied, so it can be kept current with the spec. |
| D-355 | **Owner, 2026-11-16 (FIX-160): pending Rust dependency updates are done in one bulk change instead of Dependabot's five-at-a-time PRs.** Choices made in it: (a) **MSRV 1.98** (amends D-315): wasmtime 50 declares 1.97 and mail-parser 1.0 declares 1.98, both already locked; the declared `rust-version` is kept truthful so the MSRV-aware resolver does not hold updates back. (b) **reqwest 0.13 trusts the OS store**: its `rustls` feature verifies with `rustls-platform-verifier` instead of 0.12's bundled webpki roots. Every image carries a CA store (distroless cc, AlmaLinux); `tls.extra_ca_bundle` still adds anchors, and a Vault store's `ca_file`/`ca_pem` still replaces them (`tls_certs_only`). (c) **OIDC keeps one reqwest**: openidconnect 4 / oauth2 5 are built for reqwest 0.12, so their `reqwest` feature is off and `OidcHttp` implements `AsyncHttpClient` over the workspace reqwest, which removes reqwest 0.12 from the tree. (d) **sqlx 0.9 `AssertSqlSafe`** wraps every dynamic SQL string; only constants, validated identifiers and code-generated values are interpolated (`docs/ops/dependencies.md` §Bulk updates). (e) **croner 4 with `sloppy_ranges(true)`**: saved schedules using the `5/5` shorthand keep evaluating. (f) **The wasm tooling follows wasmtime**: wasmparser = wasmtime's own version (0.261), wit-bindgen = the newest not ahead of it (0.62). (g) **rsa 0.10** in the GitHub hook (constant-time); the RUSTSEC-2023-0071 waiver of D-100 for that crate and the stale RUSTSEC-2024-0436 waiver of the root `deny.toml` are removed. | Let Dependabot deliver them five at a time (a full CI run per PR, weeks of backlog); keep reqwest 0.12 with a custom rustls config to keep the bundled roots (code in every client for no real gain); reject `5/5` (breaks saved schedules) | One review and one CI run; the trust store follows the operator's OS, which is what an operator adding a private CA expects. |
| D-356 | **SSH certificate check after the signature, not at the query (P4-SSH-2 Part C, coordinator 2026-11-16; amends `16 §W20` Authentication and SEC-92; owner approved 2026-11-17).** russh 0.65.2 hands the query-phase handler only the offered subject key and algorithm; the certificate reaches the handler only after the library verified the signature (and already checked the validity window and the certificate's own signature). So: (a) the query phase answers OK to any `ssh-ed25519` offer (russh reports a certificate offer and a plain ed25519 key as the same algorithm, so it cannot tell them apart; the answer is the same for every holder, so it reveals nothing) and refuses other algorithms; a plain key is refused after its signature, and only a certificate can authenticate; (b) the whole certificate law (CA, type `user`, window with 60 s skew, principals, no critical options, key-id shape) runs offline right after the signature phase and **before any API call** — still no session row and no API call before possession is proved; (c) every refusal stays the same `USERAUTH_FAILURE` listing `publickey` only after the same delay. Effect: a client learns less (it cannot tell whether a certificate would pass until it has signed); the cost is one extra round and one ed25519 verification for a plain key or a junk certificate, bounded by the pre-auth limits. Also: russh counts the `none` probe, so the terminator keeps its own count of real attempts (`SSH_AUTH_ATTEMPTS` 6) with russh's limit at 7 as a backstop; key exchange is `curve25519-sha256` (+`@libssh.org`) only, since russh has no `sntrup761x25519` (`16 §W20` allows "where the library offers it"). | The query-phase certificate check of `16 §W20`/SEC-92 as first written (needs a patched russh) | The library cannot show the certificate before the signature; checking after it, before any API call, keeps every guarantee that matters (no session and no API call before possession is proved, hint-free refusals) and leaks less. Patching russh was rejected (P4-SSH-2). |
| D-357 | **The parking cross-key rule applies only while parking is on (P4-011B-1 review, coordinator 2026-11-17; amends C-829; owner approved 2026-11-17).** `approvals.park.threshold_secs ≤ approvals.max_timeout_secs ≤ approvals.park.max_timeout_secs` is checked at startup and on a settings write only when `approvals.park.enabled` is true (and when a write turns it on). | The rule unconditional, as C-829 was first written | `approvals.max_timeout_secs` may be 300–14400 and the threshold defaults to 900: an existing deployment with a hold maximum below 15 minutes would fail to start after the upgrade although parking is off by default. With parking off the threshold decides nothing, so checking it then only breaks upgrades. |
| D-358 | **SSH requires the web terminal (owner, 2026-11-17: "Whether SSH should work without the web terminal enable. No, it shouldn't."; amends D-239(a), `04 §SSH access`, `11 §SSH access`, `15` `ssh.enabled`).** `ssh.enabled` is valid only with `terminal.enabled`: the chart already refuses the render (P4-SSH-2 Part D), and the API refuses to start with `ssh.enabled` true and `terminal.enabled` false (FIX-162), so a deployment outside Helm gets the same clear error instead of SSH sessions whose every exec is refused. The sandbox boot document keeps carrying the exec capability only with the terminal (`runner_plane/boot.rs`); the P4-SSH-2 follow-up "widen the boot-document gate" is dropped. | `ssh.enabled` independent of `terminal.enabled`, as D-239(a) was first written (needs the boot gate widened) | SSH is the web terminal's access over a second door: same rule, same exec path. An operator who has not opened the terminal has not opened the sandbox's shell, and an internet-facing SSH port should not be the first way in. |
| D-359 | **Workspace usage counts tokens and time, not money (owner, 2026-11-17: "let's forget cost for now and just track tokens and time"; P5-USAGE-1, C-1000..C-1002).** (a) **What is counted**, per workspace, agent, UTC day and model: the four token counters of `turns.usage` per model (D-304), the number of turns and their time (`finished_at − started_at`, control-plane clock), and sandbox awake time (`runtime_intervals` → `runtime_daily`). No cost: the harness's `costUSD`/`total_cost_usd` stay dropped and D-276(b) stands; a later price table would compute an estimate from these counters at read time, so nothing stored has to change. (b) **Tokens and turn time go to a ledger, `usage_daily`, at turn end**, in the same transaction as the fenced terminal transition (`turns::finish`), and are only ever incremented. They are not aggregated from `turns` at read time: turns go with their conversation (retention, deletion), and usage records outlive conversations, agents, workspaces and organizations (`14`, organization delete). Existing finished turns are backfilled once. (c) **Awake time stays the hourly singleton rollup of `05 §Background jobs`**, recomputing every UTC day touched by an interval that is open or changed since the previous run; `runtime_daily` gains `workspace_id`. (d) **Only reduced usage is counted**: every value the ledger counts has passed the D-304 trust rule, including a turn that ends without a terminal usage report (abort, dead dispatch) and keeps a streaming `usage` claim. (e) **Days are UTC** (as `runtime_daily`), and the route is readable by whoever can read the workspace's activity (`…/activity`), aggregates only (`05`, invisible direct conversations). | Aggregate `turns.usage` at read time (shrinks with retention and deletion; scans turns); an hourly token rollup with its own watermark (turn end is already a single fenced point); time from the harness's `duration_ms` (reported from inside the sandbox; the control plane already stamps both ends); cost from the CLI's `costUSD` (one harness, list prices, sandbox-reported; D-276(b)) | The owner asked for tokens and time. Counting at the one fenced transition gives exactly-once counts with no job; the ledger keeps history that retention would erase; every number comes from the control plane or from a claim already bounded by the trust rule. |
| D-360 | **Usage is shown at agent, workspace, organization and instance level (owner, 2026-11-17: "add dashboards, at agent, workspace, and instance level. No use collecting data nobody can see.", then "yes, add an organization-level view too"; P5-USAGE-1 for the instance and organization routes, P5-USAGE-2 for the views; C-1003..C-1006, `14 §Usage`).** (a) **One view, four places**: the organization's Data gains a fourth tab, **Usage**, for org owners and admins, over `GET /v1/orgs/{org}/usage` (top 50 workspaces and model, filterable to one workspace); the workspace's Activity gains a **Usage** tab beside **Requests**; an agent's Computer page gets a **Usage** card (last 30 days) whose **See usage** opens that tab filtered to the agent; Administration › Data gains a fourth tab, **Usage**, over a new instance route (`GET /v1/admin/usage`, by organization, top 50 workspaces and model). (b) **The agent's page gets no sixth section**: its five sections stay (D-338 structure), and the filtered workspace view is the agent's full usage. (c) **Charts are one measure at a time** (a switch between tokens, turns, turn time and awake time), with a table view, in two new shared primitives (`StatTile`, `BarChart`, inline SVG in the `accent` token; no chart library).
