(g) Provenance is text (template id, name, version), so deleting a source or template changes no agent. (h) Template repositories reuse the addon-source poll job and leases, without keys; ~~the addon-source fetch~~ (replaced by the skill library's git reader, D-457(a)). | Templates that grant access directly; a live link where template edits flow into agents; a new format separate from the export | One format for export, Save as template and repositories; access stays a person's decision; an agent never changes under its owner. |
| D-437 | **Internal-only hosts are listed once, by name, in Helm (owner, 2026-11-21: "list the FQDNs somewhere on my-values.yaml and everything will be handled"; FIX-200).** (a) Chart value `privateHosts.names` (host names or `*.suffix`, lower case, at least two labels after `*.`; addresses and CIDRs refused) and `privateHosts.ports` (default `[443]`). (b) The names join `config.gateway.allowed_private`, keeping what the operator lists there; the list is then Helm-managed and read-only in Administration › Limits (D-237's lock). (c) With NetworkPolicies on, one CiliumNetworkPolicy `<release>-private-hosts` selects the gateway, API and hook host: `toFQDNs` (`matchName`, or `matchPattern` for `*.`) on those ports, plus a DNS rule (`matchPattern: "*"`, to the `dnsNamespaceSelector` namespace) that only routes their lookups through Cilium's DNS proxy. (d) Without `networkPolicy.cilium.enabled` the render fails: a NetworkPolicy cannot match names, and an ipBlock per host is exactly what the owner wants to stop maintaining. | Per-host ipBlocks in `gatewayExtraEgress` (breaks silently when DNS changes); a Cilium value that leaves `allowed_private` to the dashboard (two places to keep in step); opening whole private ranges at the network layer; an implicit allowance for registered self-hosted app hosts (still a possible follow-up, needs its own decision) | One list drives both layers and follows DNS; the API and hook host get the same reach as the gateway, which the self-hosted app checks and hook egress need. |

Please check the form."; FIX-201).** (a) `/admin/apps/packages/[id]` accepts a version's UUID (as before) or a package id. For a package id it lists `GET /v1/admin/plugins?package_id=<id>` (C-1120) and replaces the address with the **active** version's UUID, else the newest version's; none → a not-found state naming the id, with a link to Administration › Apps. (b) `AppLink` keeps linking by package id. (c) The e2e mock answers a non-UUID `GET /v1/admin/plugins/{id}` with 400 `invalid_request`, as the server does. (d) The built-in setup guides describe the optional Path of a self-hosted registration (D-231) and drop "no path". | The version UUID on `AppDetail` (an admin-only id on a member-facing DTO, and stale after the next upgrade); `GET /v1/admin/plugins/{id}` taking either kind of id (two meanings for one path parameter); linking to the package list (loses the app) | A package id is stable across upgrades, so the link stays right when a new version activates; the page after the replace is the page the package list opens. |
| D-439 | **Pages use the full content width (owner, 2026-11-21: "we're wasting lots of horizontal space, what's the point of limiting things with `.max-w-page`?"; P5-LAYOUT-1).** (a) No page caps its own width: `max-w-page` (1160 px) and the page-level `max-w-5xl`/`4xl`/`3xl` wrappers are removed, as the agent page (FIX-129) and the Agents page already were. (b) What must stay readable caps itself: a paragraph of running text at `max-w-prose` (in `PageHeader`'s description and `EmptyState`, not page by page), a form's column of fields, a page that is one form or one reading column at `max-w-page-narrow`. (c) Card grids grow with the width through one `CardGrid` primitive: 1 column, 2 from `sm`, 3 from `lg`, 4 from `2xl`. Amends `14 §Visual design` (page widths). | Raising `max-w-page` to a larger value (still empty on wider screens); a per-page width choice (drifts) | The cap kept lines readable, but most pages are grids and lists whose lines are short; text and forms keep their measure where it matters. |
| D-440 | **Accounts and agents show as cards in the shared grid (owner, 2026-11-21: "Accounts should be displayed as boxes, not rows, just like apps are"; "In the Overview, make the agents be displayed as boxes instead of rows. Same in the Agents page"; P5-LAYOUT-2, P5-LAYOUT-3).** A workspace's and an organization's Accounts, the workspace Overview's agents and the Agents page are `CardGrid`s of cards in the Apps card frame, showing what their rows showed. An agent's Access tab keeps its one bordered list (P5-POLISH-11 C1); the organization's agent lists are unchanged. Overview keeps **New agent** in its section header (no tile; `14 §Screens` corrected). | Wider rows; a list/grid toggle | One look for the three things a workspace is made of (apps, accounts, agents), and the width D-439 frees is used. |
| D-441 | **The approvals example is a closed fold, never amber (owner, 2026-11-21: "the example looks a lot like something the user actually has to act on. Hide it in a collapsible section, like the Skills page"; P5-LAYOUT-4).** With nothing waiting, Approvals shows the empty state and one closed fold **What an approval looks like** in the style of Skills' **How skills combine**; inside, one line saying nothing is sent, then the sample card with a neutral border. Amends P5-REFINE-16's workspace-11 example card. | Keeping the card open with a stronger "Example" label; removing the example | Amber and open buttons mean "needs you" (D-384); a closed fold keeps the teaching for those who look. |
| D-442 | **Bring-your-own agents is a Settings field, shown only when there is a choice (owner, 2026-11-21: "the things that are set for me and I can't do anything about aren't useful here, hide them. Why isn't the BYO configuration in settings?"; P5-LAYOUT-5).** (a) The workspace's BYO choice leaves the Agents page and becomes a field of Settings › General, saved with the form (`PATCH /v1/workspaces/{ws}` `byo_posture`, unchanged), with the limit from above in its hint. (b) It is shown only to workspace owners, and only when a stricter value exists or the workspace has its own value to clear; otherwise nothing about BYO is drawn. (c) Rule for this setting: a workspace page does not draw a limit from above that the viewer can neither change nor tighten. Amends C-967 (workspace) and D-340 for this setting; D-444 extends (c) to every ladder. | Keeping the ladder row on the Agents page and hiding it only when locked; a read-only line in Settings | The Agents page is for agents; a setting nobody can move is noise, and the owner looks for settings in Settings. |
| D-443 | **Custom is a schedule preset like the others (owner, 2026-11-21: "why the hell does Custom 'need more controls' that have to be shown separately? All the other options hide and show controls, why is this one any different?"; FIX-205).** Anyone who can edit a schedule can choose **Custom**; choosing it shows the cron field (hint, too-often check), leaving it hides it. The `auto.cron` altitude and the "Custom needs more controls · Show" line are removed; D-406's Expert list no longer includes the raw cron. A custom schedule's row still reads "Custom schedule" with the cron under Technical details. Also in FIX-205: Once's date, time and zone share one line like every other preset. | Keeping cron at Expert with a clearer message; hiding the Custom segment without the switch | A preset that turns on a dashboard-wide switch behaves unlike its siblings; the field is only seen by people who chose Custom. |
| D-444 | **Members and approvers see no limits from above (owner, 2026-11-21: "Yes, hide them for members and approvers", answering D-442's question; FIX-206).** A viewer who can change nothing at a level (a workspace member or approver; an organization member) is not shown the "Set for you by …" rows of a scope ladder; the page shows what is in force in words and who to ask for this level. Owners and admins keep the ladder where **Restrict further…** offers a stricter choice; where nothing can be tightened, its rows are not drawn (as D-442 for BYO). Amends `14 §Scope ladder` (D-339) and D-406's "a plain member reads the limits from above". | Showing the rows to everyone (D-339 as built); a collapsed "Limits" fold for members | A limit nobody on the page can move is noise; the who-to-ask line keeps the one useful part. |
| D-445 | **An agent's SSH user name is its handle (owner, 2026-11-21: "why is the username `…`? It's the bot's handle, right? Just fill it in"; answers P5-REFINE-4's open "should the terminator map SSH handles?"; FIX-208).** (a) Certificates carry two principals, `[<agent uuid>, <handle>]`, and the certificate answer's `username` is the handle (C-1121); `ssh <handle>@host` connects, and the uuid still works. (b) The terminator never looks an agent up by name: the typed user name must be one of the certificate's principals, the first principal must be the key id's agent, and the session opens for that agent (SEC-147, amending SEC-92's principal clause). (c) A certificate issued before a rename keeps the old handle until it expires and still lands on the same agent. The SSH page shows and copies the full command. | Mapping handle → agent in the terminator by lookup (a name-based authority, and handles are unique only per workspace); keeping the uuid behind Copy (C-1060) | The person types the name they know; the certificate the API signed stays the only authority for which agent opens. |
| D-446 | **A managed connector's callback address is Aurora's, and only its origin may differ (owner, 2026-11-21: "yes, do that", after Mattermost's Allow once failed because "Address Mattermost uses to reach Aurora" held the Mattermost server's own address; FIX-209).** (a) A managed connector's `public_url`, when set, is `<origin>/connectors/<id>` (trailing `/` allowed, no credentials, query or fragment); anything else is a field error on create and PATCH. (b) The Mattermost connector never uses a callback address whose origin is the integration's own Mattermost server: that integration gets the card fallback instead of buttons and no `/aurora` command, a warning in the log and in the integration check. (c) The field is labelled "Aurora's address for Mattermost" and says to leave it empty. | Any http(s) URL (until now), which failed only at click time with no trace on Aurora's side; a reachability test from the platform (Mattermost has no API that makes it call an integration URL on request) | The host serves callbacks only under `/connectors/<id>/`, so any other path cannot work; refusing it at save time turns a silent click failure into a message on the field. |
| D-447 | **An agent's Pause, Export/Import and Delete live on one Settings page, Manage (owner, 2026-11-21: "Export and import, pause and delete are at the bottom of EVERY agent settings page. This is ridiculous, move them to their own page and add a menu item for it"; FIX-213; amends D-408's Settings frame).** The Settings sub-nav ends with **Manage** ("Pause, export, import or delete"), shown to those who may use its actions (workspace owners). It holds Pause or Resume, Export and import (Expert, hosted agents), and Delete in the danger style; no other Settings page shows them. Palette commands and links that pointed at the old block open Manage. The paused banner under the header stays. | Keeping the block under one page only (Instructions); moving the actions into a header "…" menu | One place to find what changes the agent's existence, out of the way of everyday settings. |
| D-448 | **A skill library is any git repository, given by its URL; no app connection (owner, 2026-11-21: "Skill libraries need to support any git repo, not just things on github. It shouldn't require a Github account to be configured, it should work the same as importing apps from external repos … Drop the Github requirement and accept a git URL like for addons"; P5-SKLIB-1; amends D-148, D-149 and SEC-84, which SEC-148 replaces).** (a) **Source**: a library is `{ label, url, ref?, path? }`. `url` is an **https** git URL; the person may paste the clone URL or the repository's page (GitHub `/tree/<ref>/<dir>`, GitLab `/-/tree/<ref>/<dir>`, Gitea/Forgejo `/src/branch\|tag\|commit/<ref>/<dir>`, Bitbucket `/src/<ref>/<dir>`), and the server fills `ref` and `path` from a page URL (the C-1123 rules). `ssh://`, `git@host:…`, `git://`, `http://`, credentials in the URL, query and fragment are refused with a reason. (b) **Transport**: the **API** reads the repository itself with the git smart-HTTP protocol (v2, falling back to v0), written in Rust on the API's guarded outbound client (`http_instance`: public addresses plus `gateway.allowed_private`, so a self-hosted git server on the operator's network works once allowed). No `git` binary (the images are distroless), no libgit2, no checkout and no working tree: only refs, a depth-1 commit with its trees (`filter blob:none` when the server offers it) and the blobs a browse, add or proposal needs, all in memory, every object id recomputed from its bytes. A server without partial clone gets a depth-1 fetch under `LIBRARY_PACK_MAX`. SHA-256 repositories are refused. (c) **Private repositories**: an optional **access token** (and an optional user name, default `x-access-token`; Bitbucket needs `x-token-auth`) sent as HTTP Basic only to the library URL's origin. It is sealed in the secret store (purpose `SkillLibraryToken`), write-only (the API answers `token_set` and when it was set, never the value), replaceable and removable, and deleted with the library. The check route uses a token from its request once and stores nothing. (d) **No gateway, no package**: the gateway's `POST /internal/v1/library-fetch`, the API's gateway client and the package-supersede move of `skill_libraries.app_id` are removed; the manifest `library` block (`09 §3.4`) stays parseable (contract 1 is additive) but is ignored, the linter warns, and the built-in `github` and `gitlab` packages drop it. (e) **Existing libraries** (migration `20261201000270`, additive): GitHub ones get `https://github.com/<repo>.git`, GitLab ones `<instance base URL>/<repo>.git`; any other app leaves `url` null with status `error` and the reason "Enter the repository's git address", the one case where PATCH may set `url`. Their connection is not copied: a private repository goes to `error` ("The repository needs an access token") at its next check until an admin adds a token. Pinned skills never change. `connection_id` and `app_id` stay as unused columns until a later cleanup. (f) **Check before saving**: `POST …/skill-libraries/check` reads the repository like a create would and answers what it found (address, branch, commit, skills found and the first names) without storing anything; the dashboard's add dialog uses it as its first step. (g) Unchanged: pinning, read-only library skills, proposals that a person with the tier's write authority approves, budgets, the refresh job and its lease, untrusted repository text, the kill switch `skills.library.enabled`. C-1122..C-1127, SEC-148. | Keeping app connections as a second way in (two code paths and test matrices for one feature, and it still needs a git-hosting app per host); running the `git` CLI (no binary in the distroless images, a subprocess with its own DNS outside the destination guard, repository config and protocol options to lock down); libgit2 (a C dependency); copying the connection's OAuth token into the library (it expires and belongs to a person); ssh URLs (key management for one feature; every forge serves https) | One rule for every git host, the same shape as addon sources (paste a URL); the API already fetches addon manifests through the same guard, so the gateway's credential-proxy route (SEC-84) disappears instead of growing a second credential type. |
| D-449 | **Skill libraries can also be set up for the whole instance (owner, 2026-11-21: "It should also be possible to configure it at the Instance level, not just at the org level"; P5-SKLIB-2; amends D-148).** (a) A library with no organization (`org_id` null) is an **instance library**. Instance admins create, edit, pause, check and delete it at `/v1/admin/skill-libraries` (session only, the instance-admin guard); at most `LIBRARIES_PER_INSTANCE` (20), labels unique among instance libraries. (b) **Every organization sees every active instance library** beside its own: in the org's library list (`scope: "instance"`, read-only for org admins, with this org's counts and status), in Add from library, and in browse. Who may browse and who may add is unchanged (browse: anyone who can use a workspace of that organization; add and approve: the tier's write authority, so an instance library never lets anyone put instructions in front of a workspace or an organization they could not already write). No per-organization opt-out in this version (Q-SKLIB-1, owner 2026-11-21: "not yet"; proposal `P6-SKLIB-HIDE`). (c) **Organization data stays separate**: browse of an instance library names the organization (`?org=`), and its "already added" chips, counts, pending updates and budgets only ever count that organization's skills; an instance admin sees totals and how many organizations use it, never another organization's skill names through these routes. (d) **One refresh for everyone**: the job reads an instance library once per interval and writes proposals for every organization's skills of it. The open-proposal budget (`LIBRARY_PENDING_BYTES_PER_ORG`) stays per organization: an organization over budget gets no new proposals and its view shows "Pending updates over budget", the library and the other organizations carry on (`skill_library_org_state`, migration `20261201000271`, which also takes over `over_budget` for organization libraries). (e) Deleting an instance library detaches its skills in every organization (they keep their content) and writes one `skill_library.delete` audit row per affected organization plus the instance row. (f) The token of an instance library is the instance admin's; organization admins see only that the library is private (`token_set`). C-1128, SEC-149. | Instance libraries copied into each organization (stale copies, N refreshes of one repository); an instance-level skill tier (a new tier changes the effective set, projection and `16`, for something a library already gives); letting organization admins edit instance libraries | The instance admin curates one shared catalogue; each organization still decides, skill by skill and tier by tier, what its agents are told. |
| D-450 | **Where skill libraries live in the dashboard and how one is added (owner, 2026-11-21: "setting up all the UI/UX to add/update/approve skills repos"; P5-SKLIB-3).** (a) **Organization**: Agents › Skills › Libraries (`/o/<org>/agents/skills/libraries`) keeps its place with two sections, "Your organization's libraries" (owners/admins manage) and "Shared with every organization" (instance libraries, read-only, with Browse); **Add a library** replaces Connect a repository. (b) **Instance**: a sub-page **Skill libraries** (`/admin/skill-libraries`) reached from What agents may do › Defaults, in the Skills group (a row "Skill libraries" with the count and **Manage**), with the same table and dialogs plus "Organizations using it". (c) **Add a library** is a two-step dialog: *Where is it?* (repository address, optional branch or tag, optional folder, and a closed "This repository is private" fold with the access token in the shared `SecretInput` and the user name) → **Check** → *What Aurora found* (address, branch and commit, how many skills can be added and the first names as plain text, the label prefilled) → **Add library**. (d) **Edit** changes label, branch or tag, folder, and the token (Replace / Remove); the address is fixed ("To use another repository, add a new library"), except on a library migrated without one. (e) Statuses in words: Active, Paused, "Can't be read" with the sanitized reason, "Needs an access token", "Pending updates over budget". (f) Add from library lists both groups; a library skill's badge reads "Library · <host/path> · <sha7>"; the update review (Approve / Reject, full diff) is unchanged and is tested for instance-library skills too. C-1129. | One page for every level (org admins would see instance controls they cannot use); a single-step dialog that saves on the first try (a wrong branch or folder would only show as an empty catalogue later) | The add dialog says what Aurora will read before anything is saved; each level manages its own libraries where its other skill settings already are. |
| D-451 | **GitLab projects are addressed by numeric id for now (owner, 2026-11-21, answering the FIX-216 finding: "numeric IDs ok for now, write brief to improve this later"; also confirms Q-SKLIB-2 and Q-SKLIB-3: D-448(e) private libraries need a token after the backfill, D-448(a) https only).** The gateway keeps refusing every encoded slash (`%2F`) with `ambiguous_path` (`06` step 3), so the built-in gitlab package (1.4.2, FIX-216) addresses projects and groups by numeric id, finds an id with `GET /api/v4/projects?search=`, reads repository files at the root through the API and files in folders with git over HTTPS. Path addressing is the phase-6 proposal `P6-ENCODED-PATHS` (a declared tool parameter may carry encoded slashes; security review). | Accepting `%2F` everywhere (matcher and upstream could disagree, SEC-04); resolving paths to ids inside the gateway | Correct and safe today; the better addressing comes with its own security review. |
| D-452 | **What an app gives agents may include scripts (owner, 2026-11-22: "Change of D-295: recipes may include scripts. there's no difference between adding the script as text to the recipe and shipping an executable version already."; amends D-295(b) "recipes are text" and D-295(c), and `24 §12` "Executable recipes"; P5-APPSKILL-1..2, SEC-150).** (a) A package's agent content may contain **executable files** (with D-453: files of an app skill, executable bit from the archive entry, covered by the signed digest, C-1131). (b) A script is third-party content of an approved package: **no platform component runs it**; it runs only when the agent runs it, in that agent's sandbox, with exactly that agent's access, and every request it makes passes the gateway like any other request of the agent (SEC-150). (c) A change of a script is, like any change of agent content, **not a permission widening** (D-171(e) kept): it is shown, executables marked, in the package page and in the approval of the version, and audited (`agent_changed`, C-1137). | Keep recipes text only (D-295(c) as written); treat a script change as a widening that needs re-approval | Text that spells out the commands persuades the agent to run them just as well as a file does, so text-only bought no safety and only made authors paste scripts into Markdown. A person already stands before every third-party version (an addon update waits for an admin's click, `22`; an upload or a directory package is placed by an admin) and built-in packages are part of the release the admin installed (D-381); the gateway, grants and egress posture bound what any code in the sandbox can reach. |
| D-453 | **Apps ship skills; the "agent guide" is removed (owner, 2026-11-22: "with that change out of the way, 'guides' and 'skills' are basically the same thing, aren't they? write a brief to eliminate the concept of guides and replace it with skills, which is more common. So, now apps can ship with skills after all."; P5-APPSKILL-1..4; amends D-171(a), (b), (f), (g) and D-295(b); D-171(c)/D-295(a) (the instruction file always lists apps one line each), D-171(d), (e), D-296 and D-297 unchanged).** (a) **Format**: a package may contain `skills/<app_id>/<name>/SKILL.md` plus that skill's files, in the skill format of `10 §Skills` (frontmatter `name`/`description`, body and file rules, `SKILL_*` limits), checked by **one shared validator** that the linter, the CLI and the API's library reader all use. The name is `<app_id>` or starts with `<app_id>-`; files are UTF-8 text; at most `APP_SKILLS_MAX` (16) skills and `APP_SKILLS_APP_MAX` (256 KiB) per app (C-1130). (b) **Removed**: `agent.guide[]`, `kind: reference\|recipe`, reference files under `agent/<app_id>/`, the generated `guide.md`'s recipe and reference sections. Like the `library` block (D-448), a package that still has them is **accepted and served**: the linter warns `agent_guide_ignored` and nothing of it reaches an agent. `agent.summary`, `agent.instructions` (the essentials) and `agent.operations` (still `agent/<app_id>/<file>.json`, D-296) are unchanged. (c) **Who gets them**: every agent whose home lists the app today (an active connection it holds a grant on, the app available to the org and workspace) gets the app's skills in its harness skills directory, like any skill; no person adds them. They are the **lowest tier**: agent > workspace > organization > **app**, so a same-name skill at any other tier wins (a disabled one does not veto, as today); between two apps the app whose id is the longest prefix of the name wins. App skills do not count toward `SKILL_PER_AGENT_MAX` or the library budgets; one home carries at most `APP_SKILLS_SYNC_MAX` (2 MiB, replaces `AGENT_GUIDE_SYNC_MAX`) of them (C-1134). (d) **Visible as skills**: the effective skills of an agent (route and the `skill_list`/`skill_get` tools) list them with tier `app`, read-only, with the app id and package version; the dashboard shows "From the <App> app" (C-1133, C-1139). (e) **The app folder stays, written by Aurora only**: `.aurora/apps/<app_id>/` holds `app.md` (was `guide.md`: accounts, access, **the app's skills with their paths**, documentation links, the essentials fenced), `tools.md` and `api.json` (gains `skills`); `aurora-agent search` matches app skill names and descriptions. The wire names (`apps` part kind, the render input `guide_path`) are kept so a supervisor one release older keeps working (C-1134, C-1135). (f) **Labelled**: an app skill's projected `SKILL.md` carries a provenance line after its frontmatter (C-1134). (g) **Review and audit**: a package's admin view lists per app the summary, the essentials, the operations file and the skills with their files (executables marked) and change marks; audit metadata `agent_changed` replaces `agent_guide_changed` (C-1136, C-1137). (h) **Words**: "agent guide" and "recipe" leave the docs, the dashboard, the CLI (`aurora plugin preview` replaces `aurora plugin guide`) and the API; the **setup guide** (`docs/setup.md`, for people) keeps its name. (i) **Built-in packages**: every guide entry becomes a skill (P5-APPSKILL-1); Indico gets the generic form of the owner's room, series, meeting-prep and search skills (P5-APPSKILL-4). | Keep guides beside skills (two names and two pipelines for one thing); make the generated folder a skill too (would mix Aurora-written account and access data with publisher text, against SEC-117, and make the account list depend on the harness's skill loading); let a person add each app skill per organization (the grant already decides who uses the app; an extra step per organization for the app's own know-how); reject packages that still carry `agent.guide` (would break installed third-party versions) | D-171(a)'s objection is gone: every harness Aurora runs has a skills directory today (`claude-code` `.claude/skills`, `codex` `.codex/skills/aurora`, `pi`/`goose`/`opencode` `.agents/skills`, `12`), and skills are the format authors already write for skill libraries and other agent platforms. With D-452 a recipe was a skill under another name. The lowest tier lets an organization replace an app's skill with its own without any new switch. |
| D-454 | **Skills refer to their own files through `SKILLS_DIR` (owner, 2026-11-22: "what if we kept the projection as is, to `<harness skills dir>/<name>/`, added an environment variable `$SKILLS_DIR=<harness skills dir>/` and then we can just have the skills reference `$SKILLS_DIR/myskill/scripts/helper.sh`? this seems simpler"; P5-SKILLDIR, C-1140).** (a) The projection is unchanged: each skill stays in the harness's own folder (`.claude/skills`, `.codex/skills/aurora`, `.agents/skills`). (b) The supervisor sets **`SKILLS_DIR`** = the absolute path of that folder (no trailing slash) in every environment it builds: the harness process, `process_start` processes, terminal shells and SSH execs; it is set last, so it wins over an image or profile value, and an SSH client cannot override it. (c) The name has no `AURORA_` prefix because those environments strip every `AURORA_*` variable, and the sweep keeps no exceptions. (d) The instruction file's Skills section names the folder by its real path and tells the agent to run a skill's scripts as `"$SKILLS_DIR/<name>/scripts/<file>"` (a harness's file tools do not expand variables). (e) Every harness adapter declares a skills folder (`skills_dir` required); `24 §12`'s "codex may have no skills directory" was wrong (codex has had one since P4-HAR). (f) BYO agents have no projected home and no variable. | One shared folder `.aurora/skills/` with the harness folders as links (needs every CLI to follow linked folders, unverified, and goes against the supervisor removing links in managed roots); writing the path into each skill's text per harness (changes pinned library content); `AURORA_SKILLS_DIR` (would need an exception in the `AURORA_*` sweep of four environments) | The smallest change that makes a skill with helper scripts work on every harness and after a harness change; nothing moves on disk. |
| D-455 | **App skills cannot be switched off selectively (owner, 2026-11-22, answering Q-APPSKILL-1: "no, app skills can't be switched off selectively"; P5-APPSKILL-1..3).** No organization, workspace or agent switch hides one app skill or all skills of an app: an agent that can use the app gets every skill of the active package version (D-453(c)). What stays possible without a new control: a skill of the same name at the agent, workspace or organization tier replaces it (the app tier is the lowest), and disconnecting the app or removing the grant removes its skills. A disabled same-name skill still does not veto (D-453(c)). | A per-organization or per-agent hide switch in the shape of P6-SKLIB-HIDE | The package is approved as a whole by an instance admin; the same-name override covers replacing one skill, and the grant decides who uses the app at all. |
| D-456 | **How agent templates are distributed and what they may contain (owner, 2026-11-22, P6-TEMPLATES; amends D-435 and D-436).** The owner's points: (a) "templates come from any (https) git repo, not only github ones through the app"; (b) "the remote repository itself does not contain zip files, it contains the unzipped sources, one subdirectory per agent (with further subdirs as needed). the repository may contain CI jobs to package each agent into a zip file that is added to the repository artifacts, which are then used for the import"; (c) "you have to define and standardize the remote repository format too, including the CI jobs (based on github actions as an example)"; (d) "templates may include fully specified skills, but they may also include references to a skill defined in another place (ie. the name of a skill from a skill library, a core skill, etc.): those do not need to be fully defined in the agent template"; (e) "templates may also include scripts or helper tools"; (f) "when a new version is waiting for the user's approval, the previous version continues to work normally". | Zip files committed to the repository with a sha256 list; a raw manifest URL on GitHub or GitLab; every skill copied into the template | Templates are reviewed and versioned like code in any git host. CI produces the packages. Skills that already live in a library are not duplicated. |
| D-457 | **Agent template repositories, packages, skill references and helpers (coordinator, 2026-11-22, for D-456; C-1103..C-1112, C-1141..C-1144, SEC-146).** (a) A source is an https git address (C-1123), an optional branch or tag, a folder and an optional sealed read-only token (`TemplateSourceToken`), read by the skill library's reader (C-1125) with template caps; this replaces D-436(h)'s addon-source fetch and raw-manifest URL (the poll job and leases stay). (b) The repository holds `aurora-templates.yaml` and one source directory per template, named by its id. The manifest's `packages.url` (with `{id}` and `{version}`) says where CI publishes `<id>-<version>.zip`. A version is published by raising `version` in `template.yaml`. Aurora reads only the head, downloads the package, and accepts it only if its `content_sha256` equals the template directory's at that commit (`source_mismatch` otherwise). A missing package waits (`awaiting_package`, retried every 15 min, for up to 24 h). This replaces D-436(b)'s sha256 list. (c) Skill references are of two kinds. `library` (address, optional branch and folder, skill name) is resolved against the libraries the organization can use, and added through the library add path, so the skill is pinned, read-only and updated by the library. `organization` (a name) is only expected, and nothing is written. A template never adds a library, and there is no `core` kind until Aurora has built-in skills. (d) Helper files (`helpers/`) are stored with the agent (`agent_helper_files`), projected read-only at `.aurora/helpers/` and named in the instructions; people can remove them, and updates offer them as one part. (e) Every executable file in a template must be UTF-8 text. (f) The offered version is the newest approved one, and nothing else changes it: no waiting, rejected or invalid version, failed check or vanished repository. An agent changes only when its person applies an update. (g) Update parts are the instructions, own skills, helpers and library references; a library skill's content is updated only by its library. (h) The reference CI is a GitHub Actions workflow with `check` (pull requests and pushes) and `publish` (the default branch: pack, then create the release `<id>-v<version>`, never overwrite, and fail when a published template changed without a new version), plus a GitLab sketch. | Trusting the CI artifact without comparing it with the source; Aurora packing the source itself and ignoring artifacts; writable helper files seeded into the agent's home; helpers as a fake skill; copying library skills into templates; a template that adds a skill library | The package is what CI validated and equals what people reviewed in git, without signing. Library skills keep one owner. Helpers can be updated and never run on the platform. A pending version never disturbs what works. |
| D-458 | **Every agent template says what it is for, in a required README (owner, 2026-11-22: "make sure agent templates also include some sort of description or text explaining what the agent is designed for, etc. so the humans can decide if they're interested in deploying it or not. this could very well be just a README.md in the agent template subfolder"; C-1110, C-1116, C-1118, C-1141).** (a) `README.md` in each template directory is required (`template_readme_missing`), up to `TEMPLATE_README_MAX` (20 000) characters of Markdown, with no placeholders. It is part of the content and of `content_sha256`, so the admin approves it with the rest. (b) People read it before deploying: choosing a gallery card opens the template's details (the README rendered, what it needs, what it runs on its own), and only then **Use this template**. It is also in a fold above the four steps, and the new version's README is shown in the update dialog. (c) Rendering uses the shared sanitized `Markdown` component: no raw HTML, no images loaded, only `https:`/`mailto:` links, relative links as plain text. The admin review shows the source with a Preview. (d) Save as template asks for it (**What it's for**, prefilled with an outline that must be replaced). `summary` stays the one-line card text, and `CHANGELOG.md` stays optional and outside the content. | An optional README; a `description` field in `template.yaml`; README rendered as plain text for everyone | A README is what publishers already write in a git repository, and it reads well both there and in Aurora. Making it approved content means people read what the admin read. |
| D-459 | **SSH opens the person's chosen shell as a login shell, with the image's defaults (owner, 2026-11-22: "I logged into the agent via SSH and I was missing the aliases I had set up and expected … aliases should be a part of the image or the base environment"; "make sure the chosen shell (bash vs zsh) also works over ssh"; FIX-218, C-1145).** (a) SSH `shell` mode, with or without a tty, runs the person's chosen shell as an interactive **login** shell, as OpenSSH's `sshd` does. It has the same `PATH`, aliases, colours, prompt and history as the web terminal. (b) `exec` mode runs `<chosen shell> -c <command>`, not as a login shell; `sftp` is unchanged. (c) The bash/zsh choice is stored on the server per person and agent (`terminal_shell_preferences`, C-1145). It replaces the browser's `localStorage`, and it applies to the web terminal and to SSH. The terminal's `?shell=` stays as an explicit override. (d) An interactive bash login shell also reads `~/.bashrc` when the home has none of `~/.bash_profile`, `~/.bash_login`, `~/.profile`. The value is a closed enum mapped to fixed paths, never a path from the wire. Amends `12 §SSH channels`' "login-less interactive shell", and `12 §Terminal` and `11`'s "defaults in `/etc/bashrc`" (AlmaLinux's bash never reads that file on its own). | Keep SSH login-less and install an `/etc/bashrc` that bash reads (it does not without a `~/.bashrc`); seed a `~/.bashrc` in the agent's home (the home is the agent's data, FIX-138); one shell per person for every agent (today's choice is per agent); one shell per agent for everyone (one person's choice would change another's); an SSH-only switch such as a `SendEnv` variable | A login shell is what SSH users expect, and it reads the defaults the image already ships, so both entry points get the same shell from one place. A stored choice is the only way SSH can know it. |
| D-460 | **The agent sees mentions as the platform writes them, and can write them back (owner, 2026-11-22: "when that gets to the agent it's something like `<@user:qb7c1wwoypdfpxr9wp9n84beeo>` … the agent loses all useful information of who is being talked about … it should work both ways … this sort of thing should work as well when sending channel links"; FIX-219, C-1146).** (a) In a turn's text, each mention appears as the platform writes it (`@jlunadur`, `~town-square`), followed by one `[Mentioned: …]` line that names each person, bot or channel; the agent never sees a neutral token. (b) The connector supplies both strings, in `Mention.handle` and `Mention.label` (C-1146), because only it knows the platform's syntax and names; the core renders them. The neutral text contract (`13 §4.3`) and the tokens on the wire are unchanged, and a connector that sends no handle behaves as before. (c) Mattermost also resolves `~channel-name` within the integration's team: an open channel always, a private channel only when the message's author is a member, never a DM or group DM; anything else stays as typed, so nobody learns the display name of a channel they cannot see. (d) Outbound needs no new mechanism: an agent's `@handle` and `~channel` already stay live (D-226(b), D-271); the `send_message` description tells the agent to write mentions as messages show them. | The connector writes `@name` into the text and drops the tokens (Mattermost only; breaks the neutral contract other connectors follow); the core looks up names itself through `user.lookup` (a platform call per inbound message, and the core does not know the platform's sigils); labels inline after each mention (changes the text the person wrote, including inside code) | The handle is what the agent must write to mention someone, and the label is what tells it who that is; keeping both in the connector keeps the core platform-neutral. |
| D-461 | **Linking a chat account also chooses the agent that answers; the direct-message "not set up" reply says what to do (owner, 2026-11-22: "when the user goes through the 'sign in to Aurora to link' flow, they should be asked to confirm which agent to link (if they only have one, preselect it). It doesn't make sense for them to link but then have to do something else cryptic for their one single agent"; "fix the wording"; FIX-220, C-1147).** (a) The sign-in link page asks which agent answers the person's direct messages to the shared bot, on the confirm page. The choices are the agents of the integration's organization that the person can use. One choice is preselected; with several the person picks one or "Decide later"; with none they are told to create one. The question appears only when the integration has a shared bot with `presence_shared` on. (b) Choosing an agent not yet on the shared bot attaches a shared presence (as Attach, C-34, audited) and sets the DM default, in one transaction with the link. `can_use` already allows a shared attach, so no right is widened; reach-only agents must already be attached. (c) In a direct message, the "not set up" reply links to the account page ("None of your agents answers direct messages here yet. Choose one: …"), or says how to get an agent when the person can use none. It never mentions `bind`, and it is sent at most once per person and integration per 10 minutes. The channel reply is unchanged. (d) The account page's DM-default select lists only the integration's organization's agents that the person can use, and choosing an unattached one attaches it. | Keep linking and attaching separate and only fix the wording (the owner rejected it: one more cryptic step); attach every agent of every member automatically (puts agents on a bot nobody asked for); pick the agent in a chat card after linking (left for later; the person is already on the Aurora page) | The person is signed in on the link page with their agents at hand, so one question there finishes the setup. Attaching through `can_use` is what the agent's own chat settings already allow. |
| D-462 | **Grafana's unified query is a read; the app queries through it first (owner, 2026-11-22: "improve the Grafana app so it's easier for agents to use"; chose "make it a read"; P5-GRAFANA-2).** (a) `query_datasources` (`POST /api/ds/query`, exactly that method and path) moves from the write to the read tools, keeping its id. It runs under a read (Viewer) connection, and a person can still set it to Ask in the account's actions table. (b) Accepted risk: a SQL or InfluxDB data source whose database user can write could be changed by a query. The gateway cannot tell from the body, because Grafana picks the plugin by the data source UID and not by the body's `type`. The connect note and the skill say that data sources must use read-only users, as Grafana advises. (c) New read tool `list_queryable_datasources` (`GET /api/frontend/settings`): the data sources the token can query (name, UID, type), which every signed-in browser already receives. *[Not built (P5-GRAFANA-2, 2026-11-22): Grafana's frontend settings carry decrypted basic-auth and InfluxDB passwords of direct-access data sources (Grafana v13.3.1 `pkg/api/bootdata.go` :274–308, cited in `P5-GRAFANA-2.md`), so the condition failed; agents use `list_datasources`.]* It is kept only if the lane confirms in Grafana's source that no secure field is sent. (d) The proxy GET tools stay. The skills send agents to `ds/query` first, because an instance whose data source URL redirects answers the proxy with a `3xx` to the backend host, which `aurora-agent` and the gateway never follow. (e) Every Grafana tool gets an `operations.json` entry; none stays a catalogue entry. | Keep `ds/query` a held write (every query under a read connection waits for a person); read only for data sources the person lists per account (new gateway and UI work: a JSON-body UID matcher); follow the proxy's redirect (the new host is not the connection's, and Grafana's backend credentials stay in Grafana) | Grafana grants the query to Viewers and its own UI reads data this way. On instances such as CERN's Monit it is the only query route that works, so holding it made the app nearly unusable under read access. |
| D-463 | **Locked memories: a person can lock a memory so the agent can read it but not change or delete it (owner, 2026-11-22: "it may be useful to have 'read-only' memories that the agent can't modify"; then "locked memories should still count toward the 100-per-agent limit. yes, template memories can also arrive locked … include the dashboard work, so the user can toggle the memory to locked. They can of course still edit the memory even if it's locked, locked applies only to the agent"; P6-MEMLOCK, C-1148..C-1151, SEC-151).** (a) The lock binds **only the agent**: people still edit, restore, redact and delete a locked memory. (b) People turn it on and off on the agent's Memory page, and when creating a memory. (c) Locked memories count toward the 100-per-agent cap. (d) Template memories can arrive locked (`locked: true` in their front matter). (e) Phase 6, implemented just before the agent templates (P6-TPL-1..6), so they carry the lock from their first version. | Use the brief or a skill for facts the agent must not change (always shown in full, or only when the skill is used, instead of recalled when relevant); a lock that binds people too; locked memories outside the cap | The brief and skills are already read-only to the agent but are not recalled the way memories are. A turn, or a message injected into one, should not be able to rewrite what a person set. |
| D-464 | **How locked memories work (coordinator, 2026-11-22, P6-MEMLOCK; C-1148..C-1151, SEC-151).** (a) A flag on the memory (`locked`, `locked_at`, `locked_by`), set by its own idempotent route (`PUT …/memories/{key}/locked`) and on create. A lock is not a content change: no revision, but an audit row (`memory.lock`/`memory.unlock`) and a home-sync bump. (b) One rule in the memory service: a write or delete authored by the agent on a locked memory is 409 `memory_locked`, checked inside the write's transaction after the agent row lock. Tool saves, tool deletes and harvested files are therefore all refused by the API. No agent tool can lock, unlock, restore or redact. (c) The agent is told. Locked memories are marked ` (locked)` in the index and recall, `locked` appears in tool results, a front-matter line is added to the file, and one sentence goes into the instructions only when the agent has a locked memory. (d) The supervisor projects locked files `0444` and never harvests them. It restores an edited, deleted or replaced locked file from the bytes it installed, with one notice. The API's refusal still covers older supervisors and the edit-before-lock race. (e) The export and import carry `locked` as a front-matter line, written only when true, so existing exports are unchanged. The template format reuses it (C-1151). (f) One brief, P6-MEMLOCK: Part A Rust, then Part B web. | A `read_only` author kind on revisions; a separate table of locked keys; locking as part of the content `PUT` (each toggle would become a revision); the harvest uploading and relying only on the 409 (the edited file would stay in place until the next sync, and the agent would keep reading its own edit) | A flag on the row keeps the cap, search and the projection unchanged. Putting the refusal in the one service function covers every agent path at once. Restoring locally means the agent never sees its edit as the memory's content. |
| D-465 | **An app can't ship a skill named with another app's id as its prefix (owner, 2026-11-22, answering Q-APPSKILL-2: "yes, linter should refuse this: an app can't ship a skill with another app's name as a prefix"; FIX-221; amends D-453(a)).** (a) An app may ship a skill only when it owns the name among **all** apps Aurora knows (its package's and every other active package's): the owner is the app whose id is the longest one the name equals or continues with `-`. (b) At ingest the linter refuses a package that breaks this: `app_skill_name_claimed`, naming the owning app; the offline CLI checks against the package's own and the built-in app ids. (c) When a later install makes an existing skill's name claimed, that install is not refused; the one candidate list (`app_skills::candidates`, shared by the effective route, the skill tools and the home projection) leaves the claimed skill out, so it reaches no agent. (d) The shipping package's admin view marks such a skill "Not given to agents: the name belongs to the <App> app" (`claimed_by`, C-1136). | Allow it and rely on the runtime longest-prefix rule (a skill would still read as the other app's, name squatting); refuse the later install instead (an app's own id would be blocked by someone else's skill name) | A skill name is how agents and people tell whose know-how it is; only the app that owns the prefix may use it. |
| D-466 | **Every column that stores a blob key is a reference the orphan-blob sweep honours (owner report, 2026-11-22: "I've given my agent an avatar, but it doesn't show up … it did used to show up before"; coordinator, FIX-223).** (a) The hourly orphan-blob sweep keeps a `postgres` blob that any attachment row **or any agent's `avatar_blob_key`** references; until now it checked only attachments and deleted every avatar an hour after upload. An avatar blob is removed only once its agent row is gone (SEC-47). (b) A one-time migration clears `avatar_blob_key`/`avatar_public_key` where the blob no longer exists, so those agents show their initial and offer Change picture; the bytes can't be recovered, people re-upload. The web avatar falls back to the initial when the image fails to load. (c) Any later table that stores a blob key (P6-TPL-2's template avatars, for example) adds itself to the sweep's reference list in the same change; `scripts/guards/blob-references.sh` fails on a `*blob_key` column the list does not name. Amends C-41 and C-176 in place. | A `kind`/owner column on `blobs` with a per-kind sweep (a schema change and backfill for two users); exempt avatars from the sweep entirely (deleted agents' avatars would never be erased) | The sweep exists to erase what cascades leave behind; it must know every owner of a blob, and a guard keeps the next one from being forgotten. |
| D-467 | **An instance's base path is invisible to the agent (owner, 2026-11-22: agents on CERN's Jira (`https://its.cern.ch/jira`) wrote memories "REST path prefix must include /jira (e.g. `--path /jira/rest/api/2/search`)"; "the prefix path is an internal detail that the agent shouldn't care about … what can we do to make this transparent to them?"; FIX-224).** D-231 resolved the base path everywhere in the core and the gateway, but the app folder rendered only the instance host (`api.json` `base_url`, `app.md` "(host …)") and `aurora-agent` accepted only a bare origin, so its calls went to the host root and met the gateway's 421 naming `/jira`. (a) Everything the agent reads about an account gives the address with the base path. (b) `api.json` accounts gain `base_path` (`"/jira"` or `null`) beside an unchanged bare-origin `base_url`, so an older `aurora-agent` still parses the file (C-652). (c) `aurora-agent call` puts the base path in front of an operation's path on the account's host and of every `--path` (now relative to the account); a `--path` already starting with the base path is sent as given with a one-line hint, so existing memories keep working; the built URL and pagination `next` URLs must stay under the base path (C-653). An app whose own paths begin with its base path's segment would be misread; accepted, none exists. (d) `app.md` shows each account's **address**, host plus base path without the scheme (`its.cern.ch/jira`), the `$H` of the skills (C-646). (e) In approval link templates `{request.host}` is the matched registration's host plus its base path, and a link may also point at the held request's own host (until now `declared_hosts` held only static hosts, so registered-instance apps likely lost their links). Gateway matching and injection are unchanged (SEC-59). | A path inside `base_url` (an older `aurora-agent` refuses a non-origin `base_url` and every call would fail during the rollout); teaching the base path in the Jira DC agent instructions or skills (the address is deployment-specific, and every self-hosted app would need it); a new `{request.base}` link variable (every package's links would have to change, the same reason D-231(b) kept manifests unchanged) | The base path is a fact about the deployment; the platform already knows it, so the agent's tools should apply it the way the gateway does, and the agent's memory should only hold what the agent learned about the work. |
| D-468 | **P6-MODELMIX: an agent's model sizes from more than one key (owner, 2026-11-22; Q-P6-MIX-1..10 answered with the recommendations; `docs/tasks/P6-MODELMIX.md`).** (a) A per-conversation **router in the supervisor**, next to the conversation's proxy port and forwarding through it, picks each request's key from its `model`; the gateway is unchanged (Q1). (b) A level (Large, Medium, Small) and the subagent model each name **a key and a model**, not an endpoint (Q2). (c) Only models of keys other than the default key get a prefix, `<key-slug>:<model id>`; the default key's ids stay real, and the router looks ids up whole, never splitting on `:` (Q3). (d) The prefix is a slug of the key's name computed each turn, made unique among the turn's routes; no new field (Q4). (e) An id with no prefix that is not in the table goes to the default key; the gateway's allow-list still decides (Q5). (e2) The operator's endpoint roles (`llm.endpoints[].model_roles`) stay single-endpoint: mixing is a per-agent setting only (Q6). (f) One router for every harness, not native multi-provider configuration (Q7). (g) Translation between Anthropic Messages, OpenAI Chat and OpenAI Responses is in scope as Part 2, after routing, with the pinned `switchyard-translation` crate (Q8). (h) The default model may be translated too, with a `translated` warning (Q9). (i) Placed in phase 6 after P6-WARMPOOL Parts 1 and 2, independent of P6-ORGTREE and P6-LIMITS (Q10). Amends D-303's "roles never cross endpoints" when the spec batch is written. | A router in the gateway (the route table would have to reach it, and translation would break "never reads a response body"); an off-the-shelf proxy (each holds the provider keys); prefixing every id once an agent mixes keys, or only colliding ids; a stored prefix field; refusing unknown ids; endpoint roles naming another endpoint (the operator cannot name a key, so the result would depend on each agent's keys and would move traffic to a provider its owner did not choose; operators who run the endpoints can add the models to their LiteLLM instead); OpenCode/goose native providers | The owner's answers to the proposal's recommendations. |
| D-469 | **Queued messages: Steer, Send now, Edit, Cancel by harness capability (owner, 2026-11-22: "clicking 'Send now' on a queued message doesn't do anything" — every real adapter declares `background_tool: false`, the API accepted the request and dropped the supervisor's `unsupported`; then: "enable steer everywhere that supports it … a steer button … send now only if the harness supports it … test each harness … always have a cancel and an edit button").** (a) A follow-up **waits** until a person clicks Steer (or Send now), Edit or Cancel; untouched it runs as the next turn; only platform-made approval-resume follow-ups steer automatically. (b) **Steer** shows when the agent's harness steers; **Send now** only when it does not steer but declares `background_tool`; **Edit** (its author) and **Cancel** always on a waiting follow-up. (c) Chat-platform status cards follow the same rules without Edit/Cancel. (d) Each pinned harness is **probed in its adapter's current mode** (mock upstreams) and declares exactly what the probe proves; a harness that could steer only in another CLI mode is reported, not rewritten. `FIX-225`, C-1152..C-1156, SEC-152, `10`, `12 §Turn controls by harness`, `13`, `14`. | Auto-steer like the Claude Code CLI (Edit/Cancel would almost never get a chance); removing the card button; rewriting adapters to other CLI modes in the same fix | The owner's answers (Wait for Steer, same rules on cards without Edit, probe and report). |
| D-470 | **No silent controls (owner, 2026-11-22: "never have buttons that don't do anything and don't even tell you they've failed").** In the dashboard and on chat cards every button is either hidden when its action cannot apply, or answers every click visibly — a state change the person sees or an error saying why — and an outcome that arrives later (a missed steer, a send-now that found nothing running) is shown too. A button is never shown because the action "might" work. Applies to every future control; reviews check it. `14`, `13`, FIX-225. | Showing every control and relying on API errors; logging failures only | A control that silently does nothing is worse than no control: the person cannot tell whether to wait, retry or give up. |
| D-471 | **The built-in Grafana app is trimmed to three skills; `sweep` leaves the built-in (owner, 2026-11-22: "I'm worried about the grafana app becoming so large. can it be trimmed down?" then "ok, drop sweep. write up the whole thing"; FIX-229).** (a) Three skills: `grafana-query` (the one skill read first: `ds/query`, data source UIDs, the request per data source type, reading answers, errors, and what `grafana-api-notes` held about dashboards, alerting, annotations and the write tools, without repeating it), `grafana-prometheus` (steps, windows and peaks, `compare`, and the "why did this metric jump" steps of `grafana-investigate-a-spike`), `grafana-opensearch` (the `grafana-os` script first, one hand-written example, the quirks). `grafana-api-notes` and `grafana-investigate-a-spike` are removed. (b) `grafana-prom sweep` (node health and filesystem days-to-full, its default checks, `--checks` file and placeholders) is removed from the package: it is a site's operations recipe, not general Grafana know-how; an organization that wants it keeps it as its own skill (a skill library or an organization skill), where it outranks any app skill anyway (D-453(c)). (c) Budgets for this app, checked by its stubbed test: all skill files together (SKILL.md bodies and scripts) at most 59,000 characters (from 89,000; first written as 50,000, raised because the three scripts that remain are about 43,000 after `sweep` went), the SKILL.md bodies together at most 16,000, and only `grafana-query`'s description says to read it first. | Keep everything (89,000 characters, two "read first" skills, about 17,000 characters read before the first query, three copies of the script helpers to maintain); raise `SKILL_TOTAL_MAX` (D-453 limits apply to every package) | Agents read less before they act, and the package stays small enough to review. A site-specific check belongs to the site. |
| D-472 | **P6-FRICTION: agents report tool problems (owner, 2026-11-22: "agents should have a tool for lodging complaints … a single complaint with 10k +1s"; Q-P6-FRIC-1..12 answered with the recommendations; `docs/tasks/P6-FRICTION.md`).** (a) A report can be about a platform tool, `aurora-agent`, an app operation or an app as a whole, an app skill, a library skill, or the sandbox. It must be something the agent can use. The agent's own, workspace and organization skills are excluded (Q1). (b) `friction_report` is an **on-demand** platform tool, plus one instruction paragraph (Q2). (c) Report text is read by the organization's owners and admins for their own organization, and by instance admins for organizations that share. Nobody else reads it. Counts and structured fields are visible to both (Q3). (d) The organization setting `share_tool_problems` is **on by default**. When it is off, text stays in the organization (Q4). (e) Reports are grouped by the **deterministic fingerprint** (target, maintainer, major version, category, signal), with admin merge and split. Model matching (Part 4) comes only if the fingerprint falls short (Q5). (f) Known issues go back to agents in the first version (Part 3), **written by instance admins only**, never an agent's workaround as it stands (Q6). (g) Failed calls are counted without reports (Part 2), as counts only (Q7). (h) Issues are **never sent to publishers' repositories automatically**: Copy as Markdown and an export route only (Q8). (i) The agent's people do not see its reports in this version (Q9). (j) Reports are kept 90 days and deleted with their conversation, agent or organization. Issues and their counters stay until an admin deletes them (Q10). (k) The instance setting is **on by default** (Q11). (l) Parts 1 and 2 come early in phase 6, right after P6-WARMPOOL Parts 1–2 and beside other lanes. Part 3 follows once there are issues worth publishing, and Part 4 only if needed (Q12). | Opening issues in publishers' repositories automatically; issue summaries written by a model from the reports; a core (always listed) tool; asking the agent at the end of every turn; failure detection alone; +1 buttons for people; reports on the agent's own skills; sharing off by default | The owner's answers to the proposal's recommendations. The workaround in a report is the useful part. Report text is written by an agent and possibly shaped by what it read, so it stays inside its audience, and only admin-written text reaches other agents. |
| D-473 | **Chat agent choices offer only the person's own agents and name their workspace (owner, 2026-11-22: the shared Mattermost bot asked "which agent should reply" with two options both called Daneel, one of them another member's; "the prompt wouldn't tell me which workspace each bot was in" and "I'm asked to link agents that belong to other people's workspaces"; accepted the coordinator's recommendation; FIX-230, C-1157).** (a) A person's **own agents** are those in workspaces where they have access in their own right: an active direct binding (manual or IdP-claimed) or an active group binding, while an active org member and not suspended there. Agents reached through an approved person reach grant also count, as today. The implicit owner role of org owners and admins on every workspace does **not** count. (b) It applies wherever chat offers an agent or picks one by itself: DM routing's single-agent auto-route and the DM picker; the `agents` and `use` commands; the link page's question, the account page's select and their server rule (`PUT /v1/me/dm-defaults`, the confirm's `agent_id`: 422 `agent_not_reachable` otherwise); the DM "not set up" reply's test; and the binding picker (own workspaces the adder can also own). (c) Authorization is unchanged. Admins keep `can_use`/`can_own`, so existing thread links, space bindings, `bind <handle>`, Attach (C-34) and the dashboard work as before. A DM default that points at an agent that is no longer the person's own is ignored by routing (not deleted). (d) Every agent choice names its workspace: picker options read `Name (@handle) · Workspace`, as on the link page, and so do the text fallback and the `agents` reply. `use <handle>` matching several own agents sets nothing and points at the account page. | Keep other people's agents in the lists, labelled "(not yours)" (still offers them); drop the implicit owner role from `can_use` (that is the admin's management right) | Admin powers are for administration; chat lists are "your agents". Names repeat across workspaces, so a choice without its workspace is a coin toss. |
| D-474 | **The LiteLLM automatic pick reads model modes from `/model_group/info` first (owner, 2026-11-23: "just fix it yourself"; FIX-231; amends D-372).** The automatic pick (`source = "key_listing"`) learns each public name's mode from `GET <base>/model_group/info` (`data[].model_group` → `mode`) read with the key; when that answer is empty or fails, from `GET <base>/model/info` as before. An empty answer counts as no answer. A listing without modes (every model counts as chat) is cached for the 30 s of a failed read, not `MODEL_CACHE_TTL`. No API, schema or gateway change: the API calls the endpoint directly, and agents still reach only `/model/info` through the gateway (D-367). | Treat a missing mode as non-chat (an agent would get no model on a gateway without either route); skip models without `supports_function_calling` (LiteLLM reports `false` or `null` for working chat models whose operator did not set it); a name heuristic (`asr`, `embed`, …) | Production: `aigw.cern.ch` answers a team key's `GET /model/info` with `{"data": []}` while `/model_group/info` gives every mode. With no modes, every model counted as chat, and a new user's agent picked `qwen3-asr-1.7b` (`audio_transcription`, ahead of the chat models in LiteLLM's order). vLLM refused the turn: `"auto" tool choice requires --enable-auto-tool-choice`. |
| D-475 | **A refused OAuth sign-in says what the provider answered (owner, 2026-11-23: "implement and push it directly"; FIX-232, C-1158; amends C-842).** When the provider sends the person back with `error`, or the code exchange answers non-2xx or cannot be reached, the failure keeps the code `oauth_exchange_failed` and adds `reason { stage, status?, error?, provider_code? }` for a flow bound to the caller. The failure page shows one line built from it, the dashboard dialog shows the same line, and the API logs it with the cleaned-up `error_description`. `error` must be a fixed identifier; `provider_code` is the leading `<letters+digits>:` of the description (Entra's `AADSTS…`). The description text never reaches the page. | Show the `error_description` to the person (provider text on our page, inconsistent with C-1070's "nothing else of the body"); log only, no page change (the person connecting often cannot read the server log); an Entra-only `error_codes` reader | Owner, 2026-11-23: a Microsoft 365 sign-in ended in "The sign-in did not complete (oauth_exchange_failed)", the Test button (C-1070) said the client was fine, and the owner cannot read the tenant's sign-in logs. Aurora had thrown away both the authorize `error` and the token endpoint's answer, so nobody could tell which AADSTS error it was. |
| D-476 | **Single-tenant Entra registrations: a sign-in app's own value in an OAuth URL's path (owner, 2026-11-23: "yes, do it"; FIX-233, C-1159; answers Q15, deferred by D-78(c)).** A fixed OAuth URL may contain `{client.<field>}` as a whole path segment. It is filled from the sign-in app's non-secret field, else from the field's new manifest `default`, and the value must be a GUID or domain-shaped segment. The API checks it at save time, and the API and gateway fail closed at use. Microsoft 365 1.5.0 adds "Directory (tenant) ID" (default `common`), so existing sign-in apps behave as before. One sign-in app serves one directory. | A `{connection.*}` template with a zone (the tenant is the registration's, not each person's, and zones match hosts, not paths); an Entra-only `tenant` key on `oauth_clients`; a free-text URL override per client (the host would no longer be the manifest's) | Owner, 2026-11-23: CERN registers Entra applications as single-tenant (`signInAudience: AzureADMyOrg`, through the service desk), and Microsoft refuses `/common` for those. The value is the registration's, so it belongs on the sign-in app beside the client ID. Keeping the host literal leaves token hosts, egress and the upgrade diff unchanged. |
| D-477 | **An organization role administers workspaces; it does not use them (owner, 2026-11-23: "quite a privacy issue with admins (instance or org) being able to read chats of other people's agents … An org admin can just tell someone's agent to send mail as that person!"; accepted the coordinator's recommendation with (d) changed; FIX-234, C-1160..C-1163, SEC-153; amends D-47, D-55, D-130 and D-473(c)).** (a) Org owners and admins (by their direct role, a group's `admin` mapping or an IdP role claim) are **no longer implicit owners of every workspace**. Every workspace right comes only from access **in one's own right** (`own_workspace_access`, D-473(a)). That covers `can_use`, `can_approve`, `can_own`, `can_see_conversation`, `can_ssh`, the gateway's `can_use`, fire-time authority, a workspace-anchored automation's "an active owner exists", approval deciders and an `auo_` key's workspace access. A workspace's creator keeps the owner binding made at creation. (b) The org role gives a separate, narrow right, **`can_administer`**. It covers: seeing every workspace of the org with its metadata (name, people, owners, agent names and states, usage numbers); managing its people (list, add, change role, remove, suspend, pending access, group bindings), except as (c) says; pausing and resuming (freezing) the workspace and its agents; deleting the workspace; the offboarding view and reassigning automations to a person who has access; and the organization's own accounts and their limits. It **never** reads content (conversations, attachments, memory, files, instructions, activity rows). It never starts agent work (chat, schedules, webhooks, terminal, SSH, previews, evals), never decides approvals, and never changes a workspace's own credentials, grants or policy. (c) **A personal workspace's people are its person's choice.** Only the person adds people or groups there or raises a role there; an administrator may still remove or lower a role, suspend, freeze and delete. (d) **Owners in their own right keep D-47's read access** (owner: "it's no different than owners of a workspace with only them as a member"). They read members' direct conversations read-only and audited, because they are listed among the workspace's people and answer for its agent and accounts. (e) **An administrator who adds themselves** to a shared workspace (directly, through a group they belong to, or by raising their own role) must do it in a session (an API key is refused). The audit row is security-critical, and the workspace's other owners are told by e-mail and Web Push; if it has none, the organization's other owners are told. (f) **Nothing is migrated.** What administrators set up on agents they can no longer use stops at its next use by the existing rules: schedules, webhooks, thread links, space bindings, DM defaults and `auo_` keys. Before the upgrade, a read-only `aurora-api access-report` lists these, and the workspaces that have no active owner in their own right. | Keep the implicit role and add audit and notices only (the admin can still talk to the agent and use the person's accounts); a read-only implicit role (still reads every chat); hide direct conversations from every owner (the owner's (d): an owner answers for the agent and can read its home anyway) | An organization role is for running the organization. Using an agent means acting with its accounts, which means acting as its person. Until now an org admin could open a chat with Bob's agent and have it send mail from Bob's mailbox, approve its requests and read Bob's chats, in every workspace, personal ones included. FIX-230 hid other people's agents from chat lists but kept the authority. |
| D-478 | **Emergency read access, for organization owners only (owner, 2026-11-23: "Break-glass is org-owner-only"; FIX-236, C-1164, C-1165, SEC-154).** (a) An org **owner** can open emergency read access to one workspace of the organization, with a reason (10–500 characters) for 1–24 hours (default 4). Owner means the effective org role `owner`; an org admin cannot, and neither can an instance admin by that flag. It needs a session. (b) While it is open, that person reads what an owner reads: every conversation including direct ones, attachments and export, activity, the agents' settings and memory, the lists of automations and the workspace's people. Nothing else: no writing, chatting, stopping, approvals, terminal, SSH, files, previews, credentials or grants. It never counts as `can_use` or `can_own` anywhere. (c) **Opening it tells people at once.** Everyone with access to the workspace in their own right is told (for a personal workspace, its person), and so are the organization's other owners, by e-mail and Web Push. A banner in the workspace names the owner, the reason and the end time while it is open and for 7 days after. (d) **Every read is recorded.** Opening a conversation writes `conversation.emergency_view` before any content is returned, and a failed write refuses the read. Opening, ending and expiry are security-critical audit rows. The opener or any org owner can end it early. Nobody can extend it: a new one notifies again. | No break-glass at all (freeze, suspend and delete stop harm, but legal and incident work sometimes needs the content); org admins too; access without notice (a silent read is the gap itself); the person approves first (an incident about that person could not go ahead) | The owner's answer. The rare case that truly needs someone's chats gets a bounded, visible and recorded way in that never lets the reader act. |
| D-479 | **The chat says who can use the agent and read the conversation (owner, 2026-11-23: "If there's only one member … 'only you can use this agent and read conversations', when it's more people … 'all N members of this workspace…'"; FIX-235, C-1162).** The thread header's visibility line replaces "Only you and workspace owners can see this conversation". It is built from N, the number of people with access to the workspace **in their own right** (D-477: an administrator who is not one of them can neither use nor read), and its owners. Lines: N = 1, "Only you can use this agent and read its conversations." A direct chat, N ≥ 2, where the reader is the only owner: "All N people in this workspace can use this agent. Only you can read this chat." A direct chat, N ≥ 2, with other owners: "All N people in this workspace can use this agent. This chat is visible to you and the workspace's owners: Ann, Bo." (more than three: "Ann, Bo, Cy and 2 others"). A conversation that is not direct, N ≥ 2: "All N people in this workspace can use this agent and read this conversation." An owner reading another person's direct chat: "Only Ann and the workspace's owners can see this conversation." (as before). While emergency access is open (D-478), a second line: "Dee, an organization owner, can read this workspace's conversations until 18:00 (emergency access)." The count is per workspace, not per organization (a personal workspace in a large organization has one person), and says "people", the dashboard's word for those with access. | Keep the one fixed sentence (it says nothing about who can use the agent, which is what surprises people: everyone who can chat with it uses its accounts); list every person (long, and members cannot list a workspace's people) | The owner's request. After D-477 the line can be exact, so it should say who can act with the agent as well as who can read. |
| D-480 | **A person always owns their own personal workspace (owner, 2026-11-23, answering Q-FIX234-1: "Yes, people are always owners of their own personal workspace."; FIX-234, FIX-235; amends D-140's selectable personal role and C-523..C-527).** (a) Placement always binds the person as `owner` of their new personal workspace. The placement setting `personal_role` is retired: the API reports `owner` and ignores a written value for one release, and the dashboard no longer offers it. (b) FIX-234's migration makes every existing personal workspace's person, while an active member of its organization, an active `owner` there: a lower role is raised, a suspended binding is restored, a missing one is inserted. Each change writes a system audit row `workspace_access.personal_owner`. (c) From then on nobody, the person included, can lower, suspend or remove the person in their own personal workspace (409 `personal_workspace_person`). The person can delete the workspace, and an administrator can still freeze or delete it, or suspend or remove the person from the organization. | Keep the setting and let administrators add an owner other than themselves to such a workspace (the person's workspace would then belong to someone else) | Once org admins stop being implicit owners (D-477), a personal workspace whose person is only a member or approver would have no owner at all: nobody could connect accounts or give access there. |
| D-481 | **A sign-in asks for the scopes the request chose (owner, 2026-11-24: "just implement it directly and push it"; FIX-237, C-1166; amends C-956).** The connect request's `scopes` decides an OAuth sign-in's `scope` parameter: the level's preset, or the person's More options edit, each validated against the method's declared scopes. A reconnect without scopes asks again for what the connection asked for, and otherwise `default_scopes` applies. Built-in presets are pinned where the derived preset (every read or every scope) would add a scope that needs admin consent or is "extended": Microsoft 365 `Sites.Read.All`, Indico `read:everything`/`full:everything`, GitHub `workflow`. | Keep `default_scopes` and hide the switches (people could never narrow a request or get write scopes by signing in); honor only an explicit edit and keep sending `default_scopes` for a level (the "Read and change" choice would keep failing upstream for want of write scopes); always add `offline_access` (provider-specific; the switch says what it does) | Owner, 2026-11-24: switching every requested scope Off still produced the same Entra consent screen, because the server always sent `default_scopes` and ignored the request. That is a silent control (D-470). It also meant a "Read and change" sign-in never asked for write scopes. |
| D-482 | **Microsoft 365 is mail and calendar only (owner, 2026-11-24: "the problem is the scopes `Files.Read` and `Files.ReadWrite`. let's get rid of those altogether", then "get rid of Sites.* too"; FIX-238).** Microsoft 365 1.6.0 declares no `Files.*` or `Sites.*` scope and drops every tool that needed them: the 8 OneDrive reads, the 4 file writes (OneDrive upload, two folder creates, document-library upload) and the 8 SharePoint reads, with their approvals. Scopes: `offline_access`, `User.Read`, `Mail.Read`, `Calendars.Read`; write `Mail.Send`, `Calendars.ReadWrite`. A minor version: removing tools is not a widening, and rules that named the removed tools go inert and are flagged (09 §10). | Keep the tools behind unticked scopes (people would still see tools that fail at CERN, and the registration would still list the file permissions); swap `Files.ReadWrite` for `Sites.ReadWrite.All` (more consent, not less) | At CERN the file permissions were what put the app behind administrator consent. Mail and calendar are what agents use. |
