| D-483 | **What agents read says what they need, and only what they can open (owner, 2026-11-24, after two agents reviewed the Microsoft 365 docs: "Incorporate their suggestions", "maybe that sort of info has to be in the system prompt so it applies for all apps", and "make sure the agent doesn't end up with any references to documents … it can't access"; FIX-239).** (a) **Approvals live in the instruction file**, once for every app: an **Approvals** section (agree on a write's details first; a held request waits, usually up to 5 minutes, and `aurora-agent call` waits up to 15; don't end it early or resend; `manual_approval_denied` / `manual_approval_expired`; after an error, check before retrying). Skills refer to it. (b) **`aurora-agent` examples work as printed**: a catalogue entry's example and its refusal give `--method … --path …` (with `*` as `<id>`), and a write without described fields adds `--body @<file.json>`. (c) **Standing rule: nothing an agent reads cites an internal document, section or task id** (`07 §…`, `docs/…`, FIX/D/C/SEC/RFE ids). That covers the instruction file, `aurora-agent` output, gateway messages, rendered app files, built-in skills and plugin agent text, and files written into the sandbox. Code comments and author-facing docs may cite them. (d) **Microsoft 365 1.7.0**: operations for its three writes (`send_mail`, `create_event`, `create_calendar_event`, `--body @file`); the Graph conventions skill gains access-first, `$search` limits and search-first, Inbox and own-address notes, time zones and personal links; a new microsoft-365-send-mail skill has the complete send, its answers and how to confirm it in Sent Items. | Put the approval facts in every app's skills (repeated, and they drift); point to the documents (agents cannot open them); a full Graph operations file (OData `$` options cannot be declared as parameters) | Two agents' reviews of real Microsoft 365 use: the example `describe` printed was refused by `call`, the waiting line cited "07 §Approvals" without saying how long or what a refusal looks like, and the skill did not mention `$search`'s limits or that sending could be blocked. |
| D-484 | **The Grafana app lets an agent create, change and delete alert rules, and nothing that routes a notification (owner, 2026-11-25: "write a fix to improve the grafana app to be able to CRUD alert rules ... make sure the guardrails you mention are taken into account"; FIX-241, SEC-155; amends D-471's size budgets).** (a) grafana 1.6.0 adds the write tools `create_alert_rule`, `update_alert_rule` and `delete_alert_rule` and the read tool `list_contact_points` (names only; Grafana redacts secrets). No tool creates or changes a contact point, notification policy, mute timing or template: those stay with people. (b) Every one of the three writes is **held for a person's approval** that shows the rule the script builds, field by field (title, folder, group, data source and query, comparison and threshold, states, labels and annotations each under its name, receiver) or the UID to delete. (c) A helper script, `grafana-alert`, builds the rule and applies the guardrails: a `managed_by` label and an **expiry** (required, at most 30 days), at most 10 managed rules per folder and 5 per service, evaluation at least every minute with `for` at least two minutes and a range of at least five, a receiver that exists, one data source query only, and refusal to change or delete a rule without the agent's label or outside the given folder. Change also refuses a rule that left the shape the script builds: a recording rule (`record`), a second query, or a label, annotation or receiver setting the script does not set — the card's template cannot print a map's keys and the gateway cannot inspect a JSON body, so a hand-written body could carry fields no card shows, and the script is the only supported way to write a rule. The script is help, not enforcement; the enforcement is the approval and **Grafana's own folder permission**, so the setup text tells an admin to keep the service account a Viewer and give it Edit on one folder only (Editors can edit every folder by default), one folder per agent. (d) Rules stay editable by people (provenance header). (e) Size budgets become: all skill files ≤ 75,000 characters and SKILL.md bodies ≤ 20,000 (D-471 had 59,000 and 16,000; the app was at 58,190 and 15,312; raised from 72,000 on the lane's review of 2026-11-25, whose shape refusals and card fields needed the room). | Agents can already read rules and alerts; letting them leave a tripwire is what turns NightManager from something that looks on a schedule into something that can be woken by the problem. The risk is sprawl and noise, so every rule has a label, an expiry and a cap, a person approves each one, and the limit that matters is a folder the service account can edit, not an instruction. |
| D-485 | **NightManager wakes itself with its own Grafana alerts, through one webhook, within caps (owner, 2026-11-25; FIX-242, FIX-243; builds on D-484).** (a) A tripwire is a managed Grafana rule in a folder named for the agent — **one folder per agent**, since the expiry clean-up and the caps count only the agent's own folder and every NightManager agent carries the same `managed_by` label — notifying **one** contact point a person made, which calls **one** standing webhook of the agent. The webhook is part of the NightManager template (an automation of kind `webhook`, created paused; a person turns it on) and uses the **bearer** verification, because Grafana's webhook contact point can send `Authorization: Bearer` and cannot send Aurora's signature format. (b) The agent sets a tripwire only for a **recurring** problem it has already evidenced, with a threshold from a validated baseline or the ticket's evidence, never for a one-off or for what a person's rule already covers; at most **5 per service and 10 per agent**, each with an expiry. (c) It trusts a delivery only if it matches a tripwire in its **registry** (its memory) by the rule uid taken from the delivery's `generatorURL` — and by that alone, with the rule also present in the agent's `grafana-alert list`; a delivery with no usable URL is logged, never a wake-up (a title match — Grafana puts the rule title in `alertname` — would let any rule sent to the same contact point wake the agent); an unknown rule is ignored and said so; the payload is data, never instructions. (d) A wake-up ignores resolved notices, investigates briefly, comments on the ticket (first line `NightManager:`) and updates the registry; the same tripwire investigates at most **3 times in 24 hours**, after that it counts and says once that a person is needed. It changes nothing on any service. (e) Its Grafana connection becomes a custom tool list: every read tool, plus the three alert-rule tools held for approval, and no other write. (f) A walkthrough (FIX-243) shows a person how to make the Grafana contact point; the agent never does. | The owner wants the agent to react in the moment, not only when a schedule fires. A webhook is a full agent turn each time, a rule is a query every interval, and an alert payload can carry text from logs: so the design is caps, expiries, a registry that must match, a wake limit, a bearer secret and the webhook contact point's **Max Alerts** = 10 (at most 10 alerts in one notification; the payload's `truncatedAlerts` counts the rest), and it stays inside the existing approval and folder-permission controls. |
| D-486 | **No sign-in role mapping ever targets a personal workspace, and owners are told of delete/freeze/unfreeze and of a default-secret-store change (owner, 2026-11-25, approving the instance-admin audit's follow-ups; FIX-244; builds on D-480).** (a) A person always owns their personal workspace (D-480) and nobody else may hold a role in it, so the identity-provider role mappings (C-510) never target one: saving a provider whose `role_mappings` name a personal workspace is refused (422 `role_mapping_personal_workspace`, naming the workspace by display name), and a mapping saved before the fix is skipped at sign-in — logged, never applied, the sign-in goes on. The claim-removal loop never removes or lowers the personal owner's own binding. (b) The workspace's owners are told when their workspace is deleted, frozen or unfrozen, and the organization's owners when the organization is; the notice names what happened, the workspace or organization, who did it (an instance administrator is not named), when and what it means. (c) When the default secret store changes, every organization's owners are told (from which store to which store; never secret values or credentials). Notices never go to the actor, are display names only, and a failed send never undoes the action. | Leave the mappings alone (a mapping that targets a personal workspace keeps working, but a role in someone's personal workspace could be granted and later silently removed by a claim); leave the actions silent (the audit row is written, but owners do not read the audit log) | The owner's answer to the instance-admin audit: no content reads were found, and these two follow-ups close the mapping gap and give the affected owners a notice they will actually see. |
| D-487 | **An administrator who is not one of a workspace's people resolves its address and lists its agents as metadata (FIX-246; amends D-477, SEC-153, C-1160, C-1165).** (a) `GET /v1/resolve` returns the workspace (id, slug, name) to an organization owner or admin of its organization, personal workspaces included, so the manage page can open; a person with neither own access nor the administer right keeps the unchanged 404, identical to an unknown slug. (b) `GET /v1/workspaces/{ws}/agents` answers such an administrator 200 with the same agents and only configuration and state: `brief` is empty, `byo` is null, and a freeze carries no e-mail address as its label (`by_label` is the freezer's display name); own access and emergency readers get the full list. | Leave the manage page unreachable for an administrator (the administer routes of FIX-234 work but nothing links to them); give the administrator the full agent list (the brief is person-written content) | The administrator's pause and resume controls need the agent list, and D-477 keeps content out of their reach: the list is the same rows with everything a person wrote or governs blanked. |
| D-488 | **People are told when a new template version is offered for their agents, and an update merges their own changes (FIX-251; amends C-1112).** (a) When a version becomes the offered one, every person who can apply an update to an agent made from that template gets one in-app notice per workspace and version (plus e-mail/push by their preferences), the Agents list marks the agents that are behind, and a **Needs update** filter and **Review updates** exist; never the approver, never a person without the apply right. (b) An update is a three-way text merge per text part (instructions, each skill body and file, each helper file): base = the text last written to the agent, ours = its current text, theirs = the new version rendered for this agent; a clean merge is ticked by default, a conflict is resolved by the person (**Keep mine**, **Take the new one**, edit) before anything is applied; with no stored base it falls back to today's all-or-nothing and says why. (c) Memories, schedules, webhooks and grants are never changed by an update. | Owner 2026-11-26: "all users with agents created from that template should be able to upgrade their skills (their local changes should be merged, if possible); memories excluded." | Nobody was told a version was there, and an edited part was all-or-nothing; the stored base makes a real merge possible. |
| D-489 | **An approval raised in a delegated (peer) task is delivered to the conversation the request came from, and its outcome is reported back (FIX-252; amends 07, D-178).** The approval card goes to the origin conversation (the Mattermost/Slack thread or web chat where the person asked), with the D-178 redaction in shared channels; the requester is also notified by their preferences. Deciding still needs `can_approve` in the target workspace: the card offers Approve and Deny only to a requester who has it, otherwise **Ask an approver** and **Cancel the task**; being the requester grants no right. Approved, denied and expired are posted back to the origin and the delegating agent gets its follow-up turn; a task with a pending approval stays open. | Owner 2026-11-26: approvals "need to travel back to the original request point". | The approval was attached only to the receiving agent's own conversation, so the person who asked never learned it was waiting. |
| D-490 | **An agent can wait for something, and nothing it does between turns is invisible (FIX-253).** (a) The agent instructions teach the wait: watch what you started, or set a one-time schedule, and never promise a later report without one; the platform adds a visible reminder offer when a turn ends with an open request and nothing set to wake the agent. (b) A peer task whose receiving agent registered a watch, a one-time schedule or a pending approval is `waiting`, not cancelled at the end of the turn; `schedule_create` works from a peer turn and belongs to the task's requester. (c) Whatever the harness does between turns is recorded (a **Background activity** entry with Stop, and `background` events, budgeted) or stopped; the lane first reproduces the owner's case and implements the smallest part that makes it visible and stoppable. | Owner 2026-11-26: "Pipeline's still running. I'll finish the task report once it's done." and then the peer task was cancelled; the harness kept committing with nothing in the chat. | A peer task was tied to one turn, the idle harness' output had no sink, and the wait tools were never taught. |
| D-491 | **One safe way to run tests on the shared development machine (owner, 2026-11-27: "write some scripts to run tests in such a way that no agent can ever make these mistakes again because the script does the correct thing and doesn't run too many tests at once. reuse the heavy job script, of course."; FIX-255).** (a) One shared test Postgres for the whole machine (`aurora-test-pg`, tmpfs, `127.0.0.1`, fixed port), started on first use and reused by every lane, run and crate. (b) `aurora-testkit` fails closed: without a URL it uses that shared server (starting it once, under a lock) instead of a container per test process, and it refuses a URL on port 55432 (the owner's dev database). (c) `scripts/dev/test.sh` is the one entry point: a heavy slot through the heavy job script (moved into the repo as `scripts/dev/heavy.sh`, the old path a wrapper), the shared URL on the command line, capped test threads, one crate at a time, refusing a workspace-wide or crate-less run and waiting when the machine is loaded. (d) `CLAUDE.md` and the developer docs name only these scripts. CI is unchanged. | Rules in every prompt (agents forgot them twice: 193 containers, load 70–80); a per-lane container (still one per lane, and lanes forget to remove them); capping only heavy.sh (it cannot see inside a job) | The safe behaviour is the default of the tools, so a forgetful agent cannot take the machine down. |
| D-492 | **The identity provider's sync never lifts a suspension, at the organization level either (Orchestrator, 2026-11-27, extending D-501 after FIX-261's lane found the org-level path; FIX-256; the owner may reverse it).** A suspension of a workspace binding or of an organization membership is lifted only by a person through the API's rules. When the claim behind a suspended org membership lapses, the sync leaves it in place and suspended; a returning claim never reactivates it. A suspended row whose claim lapsed becomes an ordinary manual suspended row (D-501 applies to it), with its base role, or `member` when the claim created it (no base role), so a later restore never brings back the lapsed claim's role (Orchestrator, 2026-11-27, FIX-256). A SCIM removal or a moved SCIM link keeps a suspension a person made as a suspended row; a suspension the directory made itself goes with the removal. A legacy claim-sourced suspended binding in a personal workspace stays as it is (FIX-244). A departed member's kept binding is not addressable (404 before any change). | Let the identity provider's group membership decide everything, suspensions included (a suspended person comes back just by being re-added to a group) | A suspension is a person's decision about a person; the identity provider says who someone is and which groups they are in, not that a suspension is over. |
| D-493 | **P6-PINGS: an agent's own GitLab and Jira accounts wake it when it is tagged (owner, 2026-11-27: "regular pings in the platform (ie, a `@agent fix this please` in a gitlab comment) would be able to reach the agent somehow, so it wakes up and react"; "connectors are the right solution"; Q-P6-PING-1..5 answered; `docs/tasks/P6-PINGS.md`, `P6-PINGS-GITLAB.md`, `P6-PINGS-JIRA.md`).** GitLab and Jira become built-in channel connector types (dedicated presence = the agent's own account; space = project; thread = issue or merge request; answers posted back as comments). (a) Commenters become Aurora users by **SSO username**: "SSO usernames match across all apps" — an audited opt-in per integration, set by an org owner or admin, matching only active members of the integration's organization; no sign-in link in public comments (Q1). (b) Strangers get **silence**, plus a 🙊 reaction where the platform has reactions (Q2). (c) Nothing new for public projects: connecting an agent to one is the person's decision; app approval rules already cover what the agent posts itself (Q3). (d) **Anything that tags the agent directly** wakes it: mentions, assignments, review requests, approver requests, reviews of its merge requests (Q4). (e) Attaching such a presence needs **`can_own`** (Q5). Q-P6-PING-6..11 are open with recommendations; C-, SEC- and migration numbers come with the spec batch. | Agent webhooks pointed at the platform; a polling block in each app manifest; the agent configuring its own presence | Webhooks have no speaker and fire on every comment; the channel layer already has speaker linking, reach, threads, receipts and posting. A presence widens who can give the agent work, so a person attaches it, and the token never enters the agent's context. |
| D-494 | **P6-PINGS: the agent account's token is stored once and rotated in the first version (owner, 2026-11-27: "Store the token only once and rotate it in the first version."; Q-P6-PING-7; `docs/tasks/P6-PINGS.md` §One token, rotated).** (a) A GitLab or Jira presence has no credential of its own. It points at a **token-kind app connection** (a personal access token, on the integration's server) through `connection_id`. The attach dialog creates that connection from a pasted token, or picks an existing one, and both must read the same account. The agent's app grant is chosen separately. (b) The connector host receives that connection's token only while an active presence of a bound type points at it, on the same server. An OAuth connection can't back a presence. Deleting a connection a presence uses is refused (409 `connection_in_use`). (c) The **connector is the token's only writer.** It rotates the token when the expiry is fewer than `rotate_days_before` days away (default 30), for `token_lifetime_days` (default 365, capped by the server). It writes the new token back through `POST /v1/connector/credentials` (target `{ connection_id }`, compare-and-set on `credentials_version`, under the connection's credential lock). The write-back is audited `connection.credential_rotate` in the critical set and followed by `aurora_gateway_invalidate { scope: "connection" }`. On a conflict, the person's newer token wins and the connector revokes the token it made. (d) The gateway re-reads a connection whose version moved and retries a `401` once. (e) GitLab revokes the old token when rotating, so a host that dies before the write-back loses the token: `credential_dead`, and the owners are told. Jira creates the new token first and deletes the old one after the write-back, where its API allows a token to create its successor. Where rotation is impossible, the 14-day expiry warning remains. | Two sealed copies (presence and connection) with no rotation (the first recommendation); an OAuth connection behind a presence (two writers); rotation by the gateway (it does not speak to the platform as the account outside agent requests) | One stored token can't drift between two copies. The connector already owns rotating its platform credentials (`13 §1`) through a compare-and-set write-back. The connection's lock and the gateway invalidation that OAuth refresh uses keep one writer and a fresh cache. |
| D-495 | **P6-PINGS: the remaining recommendations accepted (owner, 2026-11-27: "The recommendations to the other open questions are accepted"; Q-P6-PING-6, 8..11; `docs/tasks/P6-PINGS.md`).** (a) To-dos with no person behind them (a failed pipeline, a merge conflict on the agent's own merge request) do **not** wake the agent in the first version: there is no speaker whose access can be checked. A later version may add a creator rule like schedules have (Q6). (b) **Jira Data Center first**. Jira Cloud (other auth, account ids, hidden e-mails, ADF) is a later variant of the same connector type (Q8). (c) A **linked organization member without access** gets silence on the platform, but a `pending` reach grant is created and the owners are told, as on Mattermost. An unlinked author creates nothing (Q9). (d) **No narration cards** in GitLab or Jira spaces: the answer only; the steps are on the dashboard (Q10). (e) On Jira, an answer to a tag in a comment restricted to a group or role **copies that visibility** (Q11). With D-493 and D-494, every P6-PINGS question is answered. | Speakerless to-dos acting on the presence owner's authority; Cloud in the first version; dropping reach requests for members; narration cards in tracker comments; unrestricted answers to restricted comments | Each keeps the first version to what can be authorized and shown safely: no speakerless turns, one Jira variant to verify, owners still learn who asked, tracker threads stay readable, and an answer never shows more people than its question. |
| D-496 | **The Skills & Memory review is the target of the P5-MEMORY and P5-SKILLS briefs (owner, 2026-11-27: "everything in your proposals in the artifacts looks great. Write briefs for this next UX polish round with all your changes. Make sure the mockups are visible to the agents this time … Add a brief at the end of each part (memory and skills) to compare the work done with the original mockups and order more work if needed").** (a) The review (artifact `7JhM1jESJLZmBR8G7r41ZW`, private) is copied in full into the repository at `docs/design/skills-memory/` — findings and proposal text (`review.md`), every After mockup as HTML and as dark and light PNGs, every Before screenshot, the seed script — so every worker reads the same target offline. (b) Precedence: the brief → the owner's decisions D-496..D-499, D-505, D-506 → the After mockup → the proposal text; the app shell (rail, agent header, Settings sub-nav) and sample data in a mockup are illustrative; interface words are exact. (c) The proposals' optional designer's notes on mem-1 (Lock inside the "…" menu; the Settings sub-nav moved above the content) are **not** adopted: the mockup's Edit · Lock · "…" stands, and the sub-nav stays. (d) P5-MEMORY-5 and P5-SKILLS-5 compare the merged work with the mockups and send differences back through the Orchestrator until a pass finds none. | Keep the mockups only in the private artifact or a folder outside the repository (workers could not see them last time) | The owner asked that the agents see the mockups; a repository copy is versioned with the briefs and reachable from every worktree. |
| D-497 | **Every memory says where it came from and when the agent last used it (owner, 2026-11-27, accepting mem-1/mem-4; P5-MEMORY-1, C-1169, C-1170, SEC-157).** (a) A memory records who **added** it and who **last changed** it: a person (their display name, never their e-mail), an API key (by its name, and its person), the agent (and the conversation it was in), a template, or an import. (b) "Used" means the agent read the memory through the memory tools (`memory_get`, or a `memory_search` hit it then opened) or the turn-start recall included it; reading the projected file is not seen and not counted. The last use (time and conversation) is kept on the memory, written at most once per memory, conversation and hour. (c) **A conversation is named only to people who may read it:** everyone else sees "in a conversation" without a title or link. (d) Revisions show the same actor and "via" (the dashboard, the API, a conversation, a template, a restore), never an e-mail address. | Show e-mails (today); count reads of the projected file (cannot be seen); name every conversation (leaks private chat titles to co-owners and admins) | M-3, M-5: nothing said who wrote a memory or whether the agent used it; the review showed `bob@example.test (user, api)`. |
| D-498 | **A skill has a display name separate from its folder name, and its description is a sentence (owner, 2026-11-27, accepting skill-1/skill-4; P5-SKILLS-1, C-1173).** (a) A skill written in Aurora keeps the name as the person typed it ("Summarize pull requests") as its **title**; the folder name (`summarize-pull-requests`) is derived from it, shown small in the editor only, and never changes. (b) Skills without a title (older skills, library and app skills) show their folder name made readable as the mockups do: dashes become spaces and the first letter is capitalized ("internal-comms" → "Internal comms"), and a one-word folder name stays as written ("pdf"); one spelling everywhere, never the slug beside a re-cased copy. (c) "When should <Agent> use it?" takes a sentence that completes "Use it to …"; lists show it as written, never glued to a label ("Use it when Draft a short email…"); the projected `description` is unchanged. | Keep re-casing the slug; make the folder name editable (breaks every reference and pin) | S-1, S-2: the same skill appeared as "Summarize pr" and `summarize-pr`, and every description read wrong. |
| D-499 | **An inherited skill can be turned off for one agent (owner, 2026-11-27, accepting skill-1/skill-3; P5-SKILLS-1, C-1174, SEC-158).** (a) Anyone who may edit an agent may turn a workspace or organization skill (including one added from a library) **off for that agent only**, and back on; it changes nothing for other agents or for the skill itself. (b) Only **off** is possible: a skill its owners switched off, or one a higher level replaces, cannot be turned on for one agent. (c) App skills are not covered (D-455: an app's skills come and go with the app). (d) The effective set, the files projected into the agent's computer, the skill tools and the configuration fingerprint all honour it; it is audited. | Make the agent's own copy the only way (today: shadowing by name); allow turning on what the owners turned off (overrides their decision) | S-6: an organization skill applied to an agent whether its owner wanted it or not. |
| D-500 | **A template input's `default` is the value used when the person gives none (code audit 2026-11-27, `docs/audit/AUDIT-2026-11-27.md`; FIX-272; amends C-1104/C-1141's input rules in place).** The server's `check_inputs` treats an absent or empty value of an input that declares `default` as that default before the rules run (create, update offers, the CLI, a direct API call); the dashboard pre-fills the field with it and sends what is in the field. An optional input without a default stays the empty string. | Web-only pre-fill (a direct API create or the CLI would still 422 on a required input with a default); drop `default` from the format (it is already in published templates) | The format promised a default and neither side applied it: a required input left as offered failed with "this input is required". The server is the authoritative place. |
| D-501 | **Restoring a suspended workspace binding is a grant (code audit 2026-11-27; FIX-261; follows D-477, D-480; amends C-1161 in place).** (a) In a personal workspace only its person (holding own `can_own`) restores anyone: 403 `personal_workspace_owner_only` otherwise. (b) Restoring oneself without own `can_own` in the workspace is a self-grant: a session (403 `session_required` for an API key), the critical `workspace_access.self_grant` row with `via: "restore"`, and the other owners told. (c) Restoring someone else in a shared workspace stays a `can_administer` action. Extended by the Orchestrator at review (2026-11-27): removing a **suspended** binding (`DELETE …/access/{user}`) lifts the suspension, so it follows (a)–(c) exactly (`via: "remove"`); removing an active binding is unchanged. Extended again at review (2026-11-27): the sign-in claim sync (OIDC/IdP role mapping) never removes or lowers a **suspended** binding: when its claim lapses it stays suspended and claim-managed, and only a person lifts it, through the API's rules; active claim-managed bindings keep their behaviour. For the same reason removing an org membership, by any path, keeps the person's suspended bindings (they grant nothing without a membership), so a re-join does not lift a suspension. | Leave restore as an administer action (an administrator whose binding the owners suspended restores it with an org key, silently, and gets everyone's direct chats back) | The restore direction had none of the self-grant safeguards FIX-234 put on adding: it was a way around C-1161. |
| D-502 | **Every read under emergency access is recorded, not only conversations (code audit 2026-11-27; FIX-262; makes D-478(d) and the notice's "Everything they open is recorded" true; amends C-1165 in place).** Each workspace-content route an emergency reader reaches through `ReadAuth` (memories, schedules, webhooks, skills, the agent's settings, activity, the timeline) writes one `workspace.emergency_read` audit row (grant id, kind, target) before the body is returned; a failed write refuses the read with 503; rows are deduplicated per grant, kind and target per hour, and a list read is one row. The owner may instead choose to reword the notice and D-478(d) to "every conversation they open is recorded". | Reword the notice (people rely on the notice; memories and instructions are as private as chats); record per item of a list (noise) | The notice promises a record of everything opened; only conversations were recorded. |
| D-503 | **Sign-in group sync never puts anyone but the person into a group bound to a personal workspace (code audit 2026-11-27; FIX-263; applies D-480 and D-486(a)).** The add is skipped and logged (org and group ids, no names) and the sign-in goes on, as SCIM already refuses it. | Trust the person's own binding of a claim-linked group (the identity provider could then add anyone to their personal workspace) | D-480: nobody but its person holds a role in a personal workspace; SCIM had the rule, the claim sync did not. |
| D-504 | **Microsoft 365 is tools-only (owner, 2026-11-27: "D-504: yes, make it tools-only"; proposed by the code audit 2026-11-27; FIX-273).** `grants.block_untooled: true`, so every Graph request matches a catalogue tool and the write tools ask; a full grant means "every tool", not "anything the token allows"; reply / reply all / forward get asking tools with cards if they are needed. | Keep full as "trust the agent with the token" and correct the manifest comment to say the approval is the control only below full | Under a full grant an agent could draft and then send mail (or reply, forward) with no approval card, though the manifest says the approval is the only control on outbound mail. Grafana, Indico and aws-keys already block untooled requests. |
| D-505 | **Skills show how they are used (owner, 2026-11-27, accepting skill-2/skill-6; P5-SKILLS-1, C-1175, SEC-158).** (a) A use is the agent loading a skill: the `skill_get` tool, or the harness's own skill loading as the supervisor reports it (the harness's skill tool, or a read of `<skills dir>/<name>/SKILL.md`). (b) An agent's skill panel says "Used N times this week · last in “<conversation>”" (D-497(c) applies: the title only for people who may read it; "Not used yet" when none). (c) A workspace or organization skill says "Used by N agents" = distinct agents with a use in the last 30 days. (d) Uses are kept 90 days. | Count only `skill_get` (misses every native skill load); show reach ("on for N agents") instead of use | S-12: nothing said whether a skill was ever used. |
| D-506 | **Skills have a home at each level, visible to the people who own them, and one set of words (owner, 2026-11-27, accepting skill-6 and "Also" 1–3; P5-SKILLS-2, P5-SKILLS-3, C-1177).** (a) **Manage <org> › Skills** is its own sub-nav entry with tabs **Skills · Libraries** (libraries as cards); the old tab under Agents and `/o/<org>/agents/skills/libraries` redirect there. (b) The workspace's **Skills** entry shows for the workspace's owners (team and personal workspaces) without "Show more controls"; Manage <org> › Skills shows for the organization's owners and admins without it; Tests and the other expert pages keep the switch. (c) **Administration › Skills** holds the instance's libraries and the library settings (on/off, refresh interval, size cap), moved out of the Defaults page. (d) Words: **Turn off for <Agent>** (the per-agent switch), **Remove from <Agent> / this workspace / <Org>** (delete the skill at that level), **Stop updates and edit** (today's Detach, in the "…" menu with its explanation); never two of these as neighbouring buttons; skills are named by their display name everywhere except the editor's folder line. | Keep the tab under Agents behind the switch (the owner could not find it) | S-8, S-9, S-11, S-13: three layouts, pages hidden from their owners, a table scrolling sideways, settings at the bottom of an unrelated page. |
| D-507 | **Agent pages: one fixed header with four tabs, and no second left menu (owner, 2026-11-27, choosing nav-2a of the Navigation review: "I like nav-2a (agent settings as tabs) … All your proposed decisions are accepted"; P5-NAV-1, P5-NAV-4; amends D-408's Settings button).** (a) Every agent section shows the same header: avatar, name, `@handle` (Expert), status, attention chip and "Update available" when they apply, and the agent's "…" menu, labelled as the agent's. (b) Under it one row of underline tabs **Chat · Automations · Computer · Settings**; Settings is a tab, not a button; the "‹ Chat" back button and the Settings h2 frame go. (c) A quieter second row (pills) appears only under **Computer** (Files · Terminal · Connect · Previews · Details) and **Settings** (Instructions · Apps & access · Memory · Skills · Tests · Other agents · Manage); on a phone both rows are single lines that scroll sideways, never wrapping. (d) Every section uses the full content width; the header never moves between sections. (e) Addresses are unchanged (`?tab=memory` etc.). (f) The design target is `docs/design/navigation/` (nav-2a, nav-2a-chat, -automations, -computer, -phone). | nav-2b (the rail drills into the agent: the workspace's pages one step further away); nav-2c (an icon rail: keeps two menus) | N-1, N-2, N-3, N-10: two left menus, four columns on Memory and Skills, a header that moves, seven pills wrapping on a phone. |
| D-508 | **Places: the rail always shows one place, and Manage <Org> is a place (owner, 2026-11-27, choosing nav-3 of the Navigation review with nav-1, nav-4 and nav-5, and accepting every proposed decision; P5-NAV-2, P5-NAV-3, P5-NAV-5; C-1178).** (a) Aurora has four places: a **workspace**, its **organization** (Manage <Org>), **Administration**, **Your account**. The rail shows exactly one; its top is the **place card** saying where you are, with a back line outside a workspace. (b) Entering Manage <Org> **replaces the rail** with the organization's menu (no second column): Overview · Workspaces · People; group **What agents can use**: AI models · Apps & accounts · Chat platforms · Internet access · Skills; group **Oversight**: Agents · Data; Settings. (c) **Workspaces** is its own entry and page (today a list on Manage's Overview). (d) **Agents** sits under Oversight (in Manage it is the list of every agent, to pause or review). (e) **Members do not see Manage**: no picker entry, no rail entry, and Manage's addresses send a member to their last workspace in that organization (what the organization provides stays visible where they use it). (f) The switcher becomes the **place picker**: Workspaces in <Org> (5 recent, role, ✓, "All n workspaces"), Organization (Manage <Org>, owners/admins/instance admins, with its count), Administration (instance admins, with its count), Other organizations (opens your last workspace there), New; search across organizations when there are more than 6 places; a person with nothing else to open gets the card without a menu. (g) **Manage <Org> also appears at the rail's foot** for owners and admins, above Administration; **Administration stays in the picker too**; Your account stays in the user menu. (h) The **back line** returns to the workspace and page you came from (else your last workspace in that organization; hidden with none). (i) Page titles in Manage name the scope ("Agents in Acme"). (j) Manage's count covers only what an owner or admin can act on. (k) On a phone the top bar shows the place card; tapping it opens the picker as a sheet; the drawer shows the current place's menu with its back line. (l) The design target is `docs/design/navigation/` (nav-1, nav-3, nav-3a..d, nav-4, nav-5). | Keep Manage as a second column (two menus, six names twice); a read-only "About <Org>" for members (nothing to manage there; what Acme provides is shown where it is used) | N-4..N-8: the rail showed an unrelated workspace beside Manage's menu, Manage hid in the switcher, every place had its own way back, the phone used a native select. |
